Q: What is machine learning?
A: Machine learning is a branch of artificial intelligence where algorithms learn patterns from data to make predictions or decisions without being explicitly programmed for each task. It enables systems to improve automatically through experience by identifying statistical regularities in data rather than following hand-crafted rules.

Q: How does machine learning differ from traditional programming?
A: Traditional programming requires developers to write explicit rules that map inputs to outputs. Machine learning instead learns these mappings automatically from examples, allowing systems to handle complex patterns that would be impractical or impossible to encode as rules manually.

Q: What are the main types of machine learning?
A: The main types are supervised learning, which trains on labeled data; unsupervised learning, which finds structure in unlabeled data; semi-supervised learning, which combines both; reinforcement learning, where agents learn from environmental rewards; and self-supervised learning, which generates its own labels from data structure.

Q: What is supervised learning?
A: Supervised learning trains a model on labeled input-output pairs so it learns to map inputs to correct targets. The model minimizes prediction error on training examples and generalizes this mapping to unseen data, enabling tasks like classification and regression.

Q: What is unsupervised learning?
A: Unsupervised learning discovers hidden structure in unlabeled data without predefined targets. Algorithms identify clusters, latent representations, density estimates, and association rules purely from the data distribution, making it valuable when labeled data is scarce or expensive.

Q: What is semi-supervised learning?
A: Semi-supervised learning leverages a small labeled dataset combined with a large unlabeled dataset during training. It uses the labeled data for supervision and the unlabeled data to better model the input distribution, improving generalization when annotation is costly.

Q: What is self-supervised learning?
A: Self-supervised learning generates supervisory signals automatically from the data itself without human annotation. Examples include predicting masked tokens in BERT, predicting the next frame in video, or predicting image rotations, producing powerful representations that transfer to downstream tasks.

Q: What is reinforcement learning?
A: Reinforcement learning trains an agent to make sequential decisions by interacting with an environment, receiving scalar reward signals, and learning a policy that maximizes cumulative reward. It applies to robotics, game playing, and decision-making under uncertainty.

Q: What is the difference between classification and regression?
A: Classification predicts discrete categorical labels such as spam or not spam, while regression predicts continuous numerical values such as house prices or temperature. Both are supervised learning tasks but differ in the nature of their output space and the loss functions used.

Q: What is a training set?
A: A training set is the portion of data used to fit model parameters during the learning process. The model observes examples from the training set and adjusts its weights to minimize the difference between its predictions and the true labels.

Q: What is a validation set?
A: A validation set is a held-out portion of data used to evaluate model performance during training and guide hyperparameter selection. It provides an unbiased estimate of model quality on data not seen during parameter optimization, helping detect overfitting.

Q: What is a test set?
A: A test set is a final held-out dataset used only once to evaluate the trained model's generalization performance. It provides an honest estimate of how the model will perform on truly unseen real-world data and must never influence model training or selection.

Q: What is overfitting?
A: Overfitting occurs when a model learns the training data too specifically, capturing noise and irrelevant patterns rather than the true underlying signal. This causes excellent training performance but poor generalization to new unseen data, a critical failure mode in machine learning.

Q: What is underfitting?
A: Underfitting occurs when a model is too simple to capture the underlying relationships in the data, producing high error on both training and test sets. It typically results from insufficient model capacity, too few training iterations, or excessive regularization.

Q: What is the bias-variance tradeoff?
A: The bias-variance tradeoff describes how model complexity affects generalization error. High-bias models underfit by making strong assumptions, while high-variance models overfit by being too sensitive to training data. Optimal performance is achieved by balancing both sources of error.

Q: What is model capacity?
A: Model capacity refers to the range of functions a model can represent, determined by its architecture and number of parameters. Higher capacity allows fitting more complex patterns but increases overfitting risk. Matching capacity to problem complexity is essential for good generalization.

Q: What is the curse of dimensionality?
A: The curse of dimensionality describes how data becomes increasingly sparse in high-dimensional spaces, making distance-based methods unreliable and requiring exponentially more data to achieve the same statistical coverage as low-dimensional problems. Feature selection and dimensionality reduction mitigate this issue.

Q: What is a feature in machine learning?
A: A feature is an individual measurable property or characteristic of the phenomena being observed, used as input to a machine learning model. Good features capture information relevant to the prediction task and are the primary determinant of model performance alongside sufficient data.

Q: What is feature selection?
A: Feature selection identifies and retains only the most informative input variables for a model, removing redundant or irrelevant features. It reduces dimensionality, speeds up training, prevents overfitting, and often improves interpretability and model generalization performance.

Q: What is feature extraction?
A: Feature extraction transforms raw data into a lower-dimensional representation that captures the most relevant information. Unlike feature selection which picks existing variables, feature extraction creates new features, often through dimensionality reduction techniques like PCA or learned neural network representations.

Q: What is normalization in machine learning?
A: Normalization scales input features to a standard range, typically zero to one or negative one to one. It ensures features with large magnitudes do not dominate gradient updates, speeds up gradient descent convergence, and improves numerical stability during training.

Q: What is standardization?
A: Standardization transforms features to have zero mean and unit variance by subtracting the mean and dividing by the standard deviation. Unlike min-max normalization, it does not bound values to a fixed range and is more robust to outliers in the data.

Q: What is a loss function?
A: A loss function quantifies the discrepancy between model predictions and true target values, providing a scalar signal that guides parameter optimization. Common loss functions include mean squared error for regression and cross-entropy for classification tasks.

Q: What is mean squared error?
A: Mean squared error is a loss function for regression that computes the average of squared differences between predictions and true values. Squaring amplifies large errors, making the model penalize significant deviations more heavily, and ensures the loss is always non-negative.

Q: What is cross-entropy loss?
A: Cross-entropy loss measures the difference between the predicted probability distribution and the true label distribution for classification. It equals the negative log probability assigned to the correct class, strongly penalizing confident wrong predictions and encouraging calibrated probability estimates.

Q: What is a hyperparameter?
A: A hyperparameter is a configuration setting defined before training that controls the learning process rather than being learned from data. Examples include learning rate, number of layers, regularization strength, and batch size. They are tuned using validation performance.

Q: What is the difference between parameters and hyperparameters?
A: Parameters are values learned from data during training, such as neural network weights. Hyperparameters are settings chosen by the practitioner before training that control the learning process, such as learning rate and model architecture. Parameters are optimized automatically while hyperparameters require manual or automated search.

Q: What is an epoch in training?
A: An epoch is one complete pass through the entire training dataset during model optimization. Multiple epochs are typically needed for convergence. The model's weights are updated after each mini-batch, and performance on validation data is monitored after each epoch.

Q: What is a mini-batch?
A: A mini-batch is a subset of training examples processed together before updating model parameters. Using mini-batches balances the noisy but fast updates of stochastic gradient descent with the more stable but slower updates of full-batch gradient descent.

Q: What is early stopping?
A: Early stopping monitors validation loss during training and halts training when it stops improving, preventing overfitting. It acts as an implicit regularizer by limiting the number of optimization steps the model takes, often used in conjunction with restoring the best checkpoint.

Q: What is data leakage?
A: Data leakage occurs when information from outside the training set inappropriately influences model training or evaluation, producing misleadingly optimistic performance metrics. Common causes include preprocessing on the full dataset before splitting and using future information to predict past events.

Q: What is class imbalance?
A: Class imbalance occurs when one class has significantly more examples than others, causing models to bias toward the majority class. Solutions include oversampling minority classes, undersampling majority classes, generating synthetic examples with SMOTE, and using class-weighted loss functions.

Q: What is SMOTE?
A: SMOTE, or Synthetic Minority Oversampling Technique, generates new synthetic training examples for the minority class by interpolating between existing minority samples and their nearest neighbors in feature space, balancing the class distribution without simple duplication.

Q: What is data preprocessing?
A: Data preprocessing transforms raw data into a format suitable for model training. It includes handling missing values, encoding categorical variables, scaling numerical features, removing duplicates, and splitting data into train, validation, and test sets.

Q: What is one-hot encoding?
A: One-hot encoding represents categorical variables as binary vectors where only the position corresponding to the category's value is one and all others are zero. It allows categorical data to be used in models that require numerical inputs without imposing ordinal relationships.

Q: What is label encoding?
A: Label encoding assigns a unique integer to each category of a categorical variable. While compact, it can incorrectly imply ordinal relationships between categories. It is appropriate for tree-based models but can mislead distance-based algorithms.

Q: What is imputation?
A: Imputation fills in missing values in a dataset using statistical estimates such as the mean, median, or mode of observed values, or using predictions from a separate model trained on observed features. It allows models to use incomplete data without discarding samples.

Q: What is outlier detection?
A: Outlier detection identifies data points that deviate significantly from the majority of observations due to measurement errors, fraud, or rare events. Methods include statistical approaches like z-scores, distance-based methods like isolation forests, and density-based approaches like LOF.

Q: What is the no free lunch theorem?
A: The no free lunch theorem states that no single machine learning algorithm universally outperforms all others across all possible problems. Any algorithm that performs well on some problems will necessarily perform poorly on others, justifying the practice of comparing multiple models.

Q: What is Occam's razor in machine learning?
A: Occam's razor suggests preferring simpler models over complex ones when both explain the data equally well. Simpler models are less likely to overfit, more interpretable, and more likely to generalize. This principle motivates regularization techniques that penalize model complexity.

Q: What is cross-entropy in information theory?
A: Cross-entropy from information theory measures the average number of bits needed to encode events from a true distribution using a different predicted distribution. In machine learning, minimizing cross-entropy loss is equivalent to maximizing the likelihood of the true labels under the model's probability distribution.

Q: What is KL divergence?
A: Kullback-Leibler divergence measures how one probability distribution differs from a reference distribution. It is always non-negative and equals zero only when distributions are identical. KL divergence is used in variational autoencoders, knowledge distillation, and detecting distribution shift in model monitoring.

Q: What is Jensen-Shannon divergence?
A: Jensen-Shannon divergence is a symmetric and smoothed version of KL divergence that measures the similarity between two probability distributions. It is bounded between zero and one when using logarithm base two, making it useful as a metric for comparing distributions.

Q: What is mutual information?
A: Mutual information measures the amount of information one variable contains about another, quantifying statistical dependence. In machine learning it is used for feature selection to identify variables that share the most information with the target, regardless of whether the relationship is linear.

Q: What is a decision boundary?
A: A decision boundary is the surface in feature space that separates predictions of different classes. Linear classifiers produce flat hyperplane boundaries, while nonlinear models like neural networks and SVMs with kernels produce complex curved boundaries that better separate non-linearly separable data.

Q: What is the margin in classification?
A: The margin is the distance between a decision boundary and the nearest training examples from each class, called support vectors. Maximizing the margin, as in support vector machines, produces classifiers that are more robust and generalize better to new data.

Q: What is a kernel function?
A: A kernel function implicitly maps data into a high-dimensional feature space where classes become linearly separable, without computing the explicit coordinates in that space. Common kernels include polynomial, radial basis function, and sigmoid, enabling nonlinear decision boundaries in SVMs.

Q: What is the kernel trick?
A: The kernel trick allows algorithms that rely only on dot products between data points to operate in high-dimensional feature spaces efficiently by substituting a kernel function for the explicit dot product. This avoids the computational cost of explicitly computing high-dimensional feature vectors.

Q: What is a support vector machine?
A: A support vector machine finds the hyperplane that maximizes the margin between classes in the training data. It uses the kernel trick to handle nonlinear boundaries and L2 regularization to balance margin maximization with misclassification tolerance, producing strong classifiers with good theoretical guarantees.

Q: What is logistic regression?
A: Logistic regression applies a sigmoid function to a linear combination of features to produce probability estimates for binary classification. Despite its name, it is a classification algorithm trained by minimizing cross-entropy loss, interpretable through its coefficients as log-odds ratios.

Q: What is linear regression?
A: Linear regression models the relationship between input features and a continuous target as a weighted sum of feature values. It minimizes the sum of squared residuals between predictions and true targets, and the learned weights represent the marginal effect of each feature.

Q: What is a decision tree?
A: A decision tree recursively partitions feature space by selecting thresholds on individual features that best separate the data at each node, according to criteria like information gain or Gini impurity, until leaf nodes contain sufficiently pure class distributions.

Q: What is information gain in decision trees?
A: Information gain measures the reduction in entropy of the target variable achieved by splitting on a particular feature at a particular threshold. Decision trees select splits that maximize information gain, greedily choosing features that most reduce uncertainty about the target.

Q: What is Gini impurity?
A: Gini impurity measures the probability of incorrectly classifying a randomly chosen element from a node if it were labeled according to the class distribution at that node. CART decision trees minimize Gini impurity when selecting splits, which is computationally simpler than computing entropy.

Q: What is a naive Bayes classifier?
A: Naive Bayes applies Bayes' theorem to classify data by assuming all features are conditionally independent given the class label. Despite this unrealistic assumption, it often performs well in practice, particularly for text classification, and is computationally efficient to train.

Q: What is a k-nearest neighbors algorithm?
A: K-nearest neighbors classifies a new example by finding the k most similar training examples in feature space and assigning the majority class label. It is a non-parametric, lazy learning algorithm that stores all training data and defers computation to prediction time.

Q: What is the elbow method?
A: The elbow method selects the optimal number of clusters for k-means by plotting within-cluster sum of squares against the number of clusters. The optimal k is identified at the point where the curve bends sharply, resembling an elbow, indicating diminishing returns from adding more clusters.

Q: What is k-means clustering?
A: K-means clustering partitions data into k groups by iteratively assigning each point to the nearest centroid and updating centroids as the mean of assigned points until convergence. It minimizes within-cluster variance and is sensitive to initialization and the choice of k.

Q: What is hierarchical clustering?
A: Hierarchical clustering builds a dendrogram of nested clusters either bottom-up by merging closest clusters agglomeratively or top-down by recursively splitting clusters divisively. It does not require specifying the number of clusters in advance and reveals the hierarchical structure in data.

Q: What is DBSCAN?
A: DBSCAN is a density-based clustering algorithm that groups points that are closely packed together while labeling outliers as noise. It discovers clusters of arbitrary shape, automatically determines the number of clusters, and is robust to outliers without requiring them to belong to any cluster.

Q: What is anomaly detection?
A: Anomaly detection identifies rare data points, events, or observations that deviate significantly from expected patterns. Applications include fraud detection, network intrusion detection, equipment failure prediction, and medical diagnosis, using statistical, proximity-based, reconstruction-based, or density-based methods.

Q: What is an isolation forest?
A: Isolation forest detects anomalies by building random trees that isolate data points through recursive random splits. Anomalies require fewer splits to isolate because they are rare and different from the majority of data, resulting in shorter average path lengths in the trees.

Q: What is principal component analysis?
A: PCA identifies the orthogonal directions of maximum variance in high-dimensional data and projects data onto these principal components. It reduces dimensionality while retaining the most informative variation, used for visualization, noise reduction, and decorrelating features before modeling.

Q: What is t-SNE?
A: t-SNE, or t-distributed Stochastic Neighbor Embedding, is a nonlinear dimensionality reduction technique that maps high-dimensional data to two or three dimensions for visualization. It preserves local neighborhood structure by modeling pairwise similarities with Student-t distributions in the low-dimensional space.

Q: What is UMAP?
A: UMAP, or Uniform Manifold Approximation and Projection, is a dimensionality reduction technique that preserves both local and global data structure. It is faster than t-SNE, scales better to large datasets, and can be used for both visualization and as a preprocessing step for downstream modeling.

Q: What is an autoencoder?
A: An autoencoder is a neural network trained to compress input data into a low-dimensional bottleneck representation and then reconstruct the original input. The bottleneck forces learning of compact, meaningful representations, useful for dimensionality reduction, denoising, and anomaly detection.

Q: What is a denoising autoencoder?
A: A denoising autoencoder is trained to reconstruct clean inputs from corrupted versions, forcing the model to learn robust representations that capture the underlying data structure rather than memorizing noise. The corruption process can include Gaussian noise, masking, or random dropout of features.

Q: What is transfer learning?
A: Transfer learning uses a model pretrained on a large dataset as a starting point for a new related task. Fine-tuning the pretrained weights on a smaller domain-specific dataset allows the model to leverage learned representations and achieve strong performance with less data and compute.

Q: What is domain adaptation?
A: Domain adaptation addresses the distribution shift between a source domain where labeled data is abundant and a target domain where it is scarce. Techniques align feature distributions between domains, allowing models trained on source data to generalize to target domain examples.

Q: What is multi-task learning?
A: Multi-task learning trains a single model on multiple related tasks simultaneously, sharing representations across tasks. Shared layers learn general features applicable to all tasks while task-specific layers specialize, improving generalization through implicit regularization from the diversity of training signals.

Q: What is online learning?
A: Online learning updates model parameters incrementally as each new training example arrives, rather than processing the entire dataset at once. It is suited for streaming data, adapts to changing distributions over time, and has low memory requirements compared to batch learning.

Q: What is active learning?
A: Active learning iteratively selects the most informative unlabeled examples for human annotation, maximizing the information gained per labeled example. By querying the model-uncertain or diverse examples, it achieves strong performance with significantly fewer labeled samples than random sampling.

Q: What is few-shot learning?
A: Few-shot learning trains models to recognize new classes or adapt to new tasks from only a handful of labeled examples per class. It relies on strong inductive biases, metric learning, or meta-learned initialization to generalize rapidly from minimal supervision.

Q: What is zero-shot learning?
A: Zero-shot learning enables models to make predictions for classes or tasks not seen during training by leveraging auxiliary information such as semantic descriptions, attributes, or knowledge graphs that relate known and unknown categories in a shared embedding space.

Q: What is meta-learning?
A: Meta-learning or learning to learn trains models across many tasks so they can adapt to new tasks quickly with few examples. The model learns an inductive bias that facilitates rapid learning, including gradient-based approaches like MAML and metric-based approaches like prototypical networks.

Q: What is federated learning?
A: Federated learning trains machine learning models across distributed data sources without centralizing the raw data. Clients train locally and share only model updates, which are aggregated on a central server, preserving privacy and reducing communication costs in applications like mobile devices.

Q: What is continual learning?
A: Continual learning, also called lifelong learning, trains models sequentially on new tasks without forgetting previously learned knowledge. Catastrophic forgetting, where new learning overwrites old representations, is the core challenge, addressed through regularization, memory replay, and progressive network architectures.

Q: What is curriculum learning?
A: Curriculum learning trains models on examples ordered from easy to hard, mimicking how humans learn. Starting with simpler examples helps the model learn basic patterns before encountering complex cases, often improving convergence speed and final generalization performance.

Q: What is a neural network?
A: A neural network is a computational model inspired by biological neural systems, consisting of interconnected layers of units called neurons. Each neuron computes a weighted sum of its inputs and applies a nonlinear activation function, enabling the network to learn complex mappings from inputs to outputs.

Q: What is a neuron in a neural network?
A: An artificial neuron computes a weighted linear combination of its inputs, adds a bias term, and passes the result through a nonlinear activation function. This unit is the basic computational building block of neural networks, and layers of neurons arranged hierarchically form deep networks.

Q: What is a weight in a neural network?
A: Weights are learnable parameters that scale the input signals to each neuron. During training, gradient descent adjusts weights to minimize the loss function, allowing the network to learn the appropriate strength of each connection for mapping inputs to correct outputs.

Q: What is a bias in a neural network?
A: A bias is an additional learnable parameter added to the weighted sum before applying the activation function. It shifts the activation function horizontally, allowing the network to fit data that does not pass through the origin and increasing representational flexibility.

Q: What is an activation function?
A: An activation function introduces nonlinearity into neural networks, allowing them to represent complex functions beyond linear transformations. Without activation functions, stacking multiple layers would be equivalent to a single linear transformation regardless of network depth.

Q: What is the sigmoid activation function?
A: The sigmoid function maps any real input to a value between zero and one using the formula one over one plus e to the negative x. It is used in output layers for binary classification but causes vanishing gradients in deep networks when saturated near its extremes.

Q: What is the tanh activation function?
A: The hyperbolic tangent function maps inputs to values between negative one and one, is zero-centered unlike sigmoid, and outputs negative values for negative inputs. Like sigmoid it suffers from vanishing gradients at saturation but is generally preferred over sigmoid for hidden layers.

Q: What is the ReLU activation function?
A: Rectified Linear Unit outputs the input directly if positive and zero otherwise, computed as the maximum of zero and the input. It avoids vanishing gradients for positive values, is computationally efficient, and is the most widely used activation function in deep networks.

Q: What is the dying ReLU problem?
A: The dying ReLU problem occurs when neurons permanently output zero because their weights are updated such that the weighted sum is always negative. These dead neurons contribute nothing to learning. It is mitigated by using Leaky ReLU or initializing weights carefully.

Q: What is Leaky ReLU?
A: Leaky ReLU allows a small negative slope for inputs below zero, typically 0.01 times the input, instead of outputting exactly zero. This prevents the dying ReLU problem by ensuring all neurons have nonzero gradients and can participate in learning regardless of their activation state.

Q: What is the ELU activation function?
A: Exponential Linear Unit uses exponential decay for negative inputs, producing smooth activation and negative outputs that push mean activations toward zero. Compared to ReLU, ELU reduces the bias shift, improves convergence speed, and produces more robust representations.

Q: What is the GELU activation function?
A: Gaussian Error Linear Unit multiplies the input by the cumulative distribution function of the standard normal, approximating a smooth stochastic regularizer. It is used in transformer-based models like BERT and GPT, offering better empirical performance than ReLU in many NLP tasks.

Q: What is the softmax function?
A: The softmax function converts a vector of arbitrary real numbers into a probability distribution where all values are positive and sum to one. It is used in the output layer of multi-class classifiers to produce interpretable class probabilities from the network's raw logit outputs.

Q: What is a fully connected layer?
A: A fully connected layer, also called a dense layer, connects every neuron in the current layer to every neuron in the previous layer. Each connection has a learnable weight, making fully connected layers the most parameter-intensive but most expressive components of neural networks.

Q: What is backpropagation?
A: Backpropagation computes gradients of the loss function with respect to every weight in the network by applying the chain rule of calculus backward from the output layer to the input. These gradients indicate how each weight should change to reduce the loss.

Q: What is the chain rule in backpropagation?
A: The chain rule states that the derivative of a composite function equals the product of the derivatives of its components. Backpropagation uses this rule to efficiently compute gradients layer by layer, multiplying local Jacobians backward through the computation graph.

Q: What is weight initialization?
A: Weight initialization sets the starting values of neural network parameters before training begins. Poor initialization can cause vanishing or exploding gradients, preventing learning. Xavier initialization scales weights by the inverse square root of fan-in for sigmoid and tanh, while He initialization scales by square root of two over fan-in for ReLU.

Q: What is Xavier initialization?
A: Xavier initialization, also called Glorot initialization, sets weights from a distribution with variance equal to two divided by the sum of the number of input and output units. It is designed to maintain signal variance across layers when using sigmoid or tanh activations.

Q: What is He initialization?
A: He initialization sets weights from a distribution with variance equal to two divided by the number of input units. It is specifically designed for ReLU activations, accounting for the fact that ReLU zeroes out half of its inputs, maintaining appropriate signal variance throughout deep networks.

Q: What is vanishing gradient?
A: Vanishing gradients occur when gradients become exponentially small as they are backpropagated through many layers, making it difficult for early layers to learn. It is caused by repeated multiplication of small gradient values and is addressed with ReLU activations, residual connections, and normalization.

Q: What is exploding gradient?
A: Exploding gradients occur when backpropagation produces exponentially large gradients, causing weight updates that destabilize training. It is common in recurrent networks processing long sequences and is addressed using gradient clipping, which scales gradients down when their norm exceeds a threshold.

Q: What is gradient clipping?
A: Gradient clipping prevents exploding gradients by scaling the gradient vector when its norm exceeds a predefined maximum. Clipping by norm divides the entire gradient by its magnitude when it exceeds the threshold, preserving gradient direction while controlling update magnitude.

Q: What is batch normalization?
A: Batch normalization normalizes the activations of a layer across the current mini-batch to have zero mean and unit variance, then applies learnable scale and shift parameters. It stabilizes training, allows higher learning rates, reduces dependence on initialization, and provides mild regularization.

Q: What is layer normalization?
A: Layer normalization normalizes activations across the feature dimension for each sample independently, rather than across the batch dimension. It is more suitable for variable-length sequences and recurrent architectures, and is the standard normalization approach in transformer models.

Q: What is group normalization?
A: Group normalization divides feature channels into groups and normalizes within each group independently for each sample. It performs well with small batch sizes where batch normalization statistics are unreliable, making it useful for detection and segmentation tasks with high-resolution images.

Q: What is instance normalization?
A: Instance normalization normalizes each channel of each sample independently, removing instance-specific contrast information. It is particularly effective for style transfer tasks where stylistic information is captured in the feature statistics and content information is preserved in their spatial patterns.

Q: What is the role of the learning rate?
A: The learning rate controls the step size of parameter updates in gradient descent. A learning rate that is too large causes oscillation or divergence, while one that is too small results in very slow convergence. Appropriate learning rate selection is critical for effective optimization.

Q: What is learning rate scheduling?
A: Learning rate scheduling adjusts the learning rate during training according to a predetermined schedule or adaptive rule. Common strategies include step decay, cosine annealing, and warmup followed by decay, allowing large steps early in training and fine-grained refinement near convergence.

Q: What is warmup in learning rate scheduling?
A: Warmup gradually increases the learning rate from a small value to the target rate at the start of training. It prevents large parameter updates when gradients are unreliable early in training, stabilizing optimization especially for transformer models trained with adaptive optimizers.

Q: What is cosine annealing?
A: Cosine annealing decays the learning rate following the shape of a cosine function from its initial value to a minimum. It provides smooth decay and can be combined with warm restarts that periodically reset the learning rate, helping the model escape local minima.

Q: What is momentum in optimization?
A: Momentum accumulates a velocity vector in the direction of persistent gradients across successive parameter updates, dampening oscillations in high-curvature directions and accelerating progress along low-curvature directions. It helps gradient descent navigate ravines and escape shallow local minima.

Q: What is Nesterov momentum?
A: Nesterov momentum computes the gradient at the anticipated future position after applying the current velocity rather than the current position, producing a look-ahead correction that provides better convergence and less oscillation than standard momentum in smooth optimization landscapes.

Q: What is the RMSprop optimizer?
A: RMSprop maintains a moving average of squared gradients to normalize parameter updates, dividing each gradient by the root of this average. This adapts the effective learning rate per parameter, reducing learning rates for frequently updated parameters and increasing rates for infrequent ones.

Q: What is the Adam optimizer?
A: Adam combines first-order momentum with second-order RMSprop adaptation, maintaining exponential moving averages of both gradients and squared gradients. Bias correction in early iterations prevents underestimation, making Adam robust to sparse gradients and a popular default optimizer.

Q: What is AdaGrad?
A: AdaGrad accumulates the sum of squared past gradients to adapt learning rates per parameter, aggressively reducing rates for frequently updated parameters. While effective for sparse data, the monotonically increasing accumulation eventually makes learning rates negligibly small, limiting its use for non-convex problems.

Q: What is dropout regularization?
A: Dropout randomly sets a fraction of neuron outputs to zero during each forward pass of training, sampled independently for each example. At inference, all neurons are active but their outputs are scaled by the dropout probability, approximating an ensemble of many subnetworks.

Q: What is L1 regularization in neural networks?
A: L1 regularization adds the sum of absolute values of all weights to the loss function, multiplied by a regularization coefficient. It encourages sparsity by driving small weights to exactly zero, effectively performing feature selection and producing simpler, more interpretable models.

Q: What is L2 regularization in neural networks?
A: L2 regularization adds the sum of squared weights to the loss function, penalizing large weights and encouraging smaller distributed values. Also called weight decay, it prevents any single weight from dominating predictions and is the most common regularization technique in neural networks.

Q: What is weight decay?
A: Weight decay is a regularization technique that multiplies weights by a factor slightly less than one at each update step, equivalent to L2 regularization in standard gradient descent. In adaptive optimizers like Adam, weight decay must be decoupled from gradient adaptation to be effective.

Q: What are residual connections?
A: Residual connections, introduced in ResNet, add the input of a layer directly to its output as a shortcut path. They allow gradients to flow directly through the network without passing through many nonlinear transformations, enabling training of very deep networks of hundreds of layers.

Q: What is a skip connection?
A: A skip connection passes the output of one layer directly to a later layer, bypassing intermediate layers. Residual and dense connections are specific types of skip connections that improve gradient flow, facilitate feature reuse, and enable the training of much deeper networks.

Q: What is a ResNet?
A: ResNet, or Residual Network, uses skip connections that add the input of each block to its output, enabling training of networks over a hundred layers deep. The residual formulation makes it easier to learn identity mappings and passes gradients cleanly to earlier layers.

Q: What is DenseNet?
A: DenseNet connects each layer to every subsequent layer within a dense block, concatenating all preceding feature maps as input. This maximizes feature reuse, strengthens gradient flow, reduces the total number of parameters compared to ResNet, and enables compact yet powerful networks.

Q: What is a bottleneck layer?
A: A bottleneck layer reduces the dimensionality of feature representations using a convolutional or linear layer with fewer output channels, then expands dimensionality afterward. Bottleneck designs reduce computational cost while maintaining representational capacity, used in ResNets and transformer feed-forward blocks.

Q: What is the universal approximation theorem?
A: The universal approximation theorem states that a feedforward neural network with at least one hidden layer and sufficient neurons can approximate any continuous function on a compact subset to arbitrary precision. It provides theoretical justification for the expressiveness of neural networks.

Q: What is depth versus width in neural networks?
A: Depth refers to the number of layers while width refers to the number of neurons per layer. Depth allows learning hierarchical representations with exponentially more expressiveness per parameter, while width increases capacity within each layer. Deep networks generally outperform equivalently parameterized shallow networks.

Q: What is the inductive bias of neural networks?
A: Inductive bias is the set of assumptions baked into a model's architecture that determine what kinds of functions it can efficiently represent. CNNs assume translation invariance and local structure, RNNs assume sequential dependency, and transformers assume arbitrary pairwise interactions.

Q: What is a multilayer perceptron?
A: A multilayer perceptron is a fully connected feedforward neural network with one or more hidden layers between the input and output. It is trained with backpropagation and can theoretically approximate any continuous function, serving as the baseline architecture for many tasks.

Q: What is weight sharing?
A: Weight sharing uses the same weights at multiple positions in a model, reducing the total number of parameters and imposing translational invariance. Convolutional networks apply the same filter across all spatial positions, and attention mechanisms share projection matrices across all positions.

Q: What is parameter efficiency?
A: Parameter efficiency refers to how much performance a model achieves relative to its number of learnable parameters. Architectures with strong inductive biases like CNNs are more parameter efficient than dense networks for structured data, learning better representations from the same amount of data.

Q: What is a siamese network?
A: A siamese network uses two identical branches with shared weights to encode two inputs into a common embedding space, then computes a similarity or distance between the embeddings. It is used for face verification, signature verification, and one-shot learning tasks.

Q: What is a capsule network?
A: A capsule network replaces scalar neuron outputs with vectors called capsules that represent both the probability of an entity's presence and its pose properties. Routing by agreement aggregates lower-level capsules into higher-level ones, better modeling part-whole spatial relationships than CNNs.

Q: What is a Hopfield network?
A: A Hopfield network is a recurrent network where units are connected symmetrically and update asynchronously to minimize an energy function. It functions as an associative memory that can store patterns and retrieve them from partial or noisy inputs.

Q: What is a Boltzmann machine?
A: A Boltzmann machine is a stochastic recurrent network where units have binary states determined probabilistically by the states of connected units. It learns a probability distribution over training data and can generate new samples, serving as an early generative model.

Q: What is a restricted Boltzmann machine?
A: A restricted Boltzmann machine restricts connections to between visible and hidden units with no within-layer connections, enabling efficient training via contrastive divergence. It learns compact binary feature representations and was a key building block in early deep learning before backpropagation became dominant.

Q: What is a radial basis function network?
A: A radial basis function network uses neurons with radial basis functions such as Gaussians as activation functions, where output depends on the distance between the input and a learned center. It approximates functions and classifies data using local, distance-based representations.

Q: What is hypernetwork?
A: A hypernetwork is a neural network that generates the weights for another target network. By learning to produce weight configurations rather than fixed weights, hypernetworks enable dynamic adaptation to inputs, weight sharing across tasks, and compact parameterization of ensembles.

Q: What is mixture of experts?
A: Mixture of experts uses multiple specialized subnetworks called experts, each trained on different portions of the input space, with a learned gating network that routes each input to the most appropriate expert or weighted combination of experts.

Q: What is sparse mixture of experts?
A: Sparse mixture of experts activates only the top-k expert networks for each token or input, keeping most experts inactive during any single forward pass. This enables massive model capacity while maintaining constant computational cost per input, used in large language model scaling.

Q: What is gradient checkpointing?
A: Gradient checkpointing reduces memory consumption during training by recomputing intermediate activations during the backward pass rather than storing them all during the forward pass. It trades extra computation for significantly lower memory usage, enabling training of larger models on limited hardware.

Q: What is mixed precision training?
A: Mixed precision training stores and computes most operations in half-precision floating point while keeping master weights in full precision. It reduces memory usage by half, doubles throughput on modern accelerators, and requires loss scaling to prevent underflow in small gradient values.

Q: What is model parallelism?
A: Model parallelism distributes different layers or components of a model across multiple devices, allowing models too large to fit on a single device to be trained. It requires communication between devices when activations pass from one device to another during forward and backward passes.

Q: What is data parallelism?
A: Data parallelism replicates the full model across multiple devices, each processing a different mini-batch. Gradients are aggregated across devices before updating parameters, scaling training speed nearly linearly with the number of devices for large datasets.

Q: What is tensor parallelism?
A: Tensor parallelism distributes individual weight matrices or attention heads across multiple devices, splitting the computation of each layer. It is particularly effective for transformer models with very large weight matrices and requires efficient all-reduce communication within each layer's forward pass.

Q: What is pipeline parallelism?
A: Pipeline parallelism assigns consecutive groups of layers to different devices and processes different micro-batches through each stage simultaneously. Like an assembly line, it keeps all devices active but introduces pipeline bubbles that reduce efficiency near the start and end of each batch.

Q: What is the lottery ticket hypothesis?
A: The lottery ticket hypothesis proposes that large randomly initialized networks contain small sparse subnetworks called winning tickets that can be trained to the same accuracy as the full network. Finding these subnetworks could enable training much smaller models from scratch.

Q: What is neural architecture search?
A: Neural architecture search automatically finds optimal model architectures for a given task and dataset using search strategies including reinforcement learning, evolutionary algorithms, and differentiable relaxations. It removes the need for manual architecture design but requires significant computational resources.

Q: What is knowledge distillation?
A: Knowledge distillation trains a small student model to mimic the soft probability outputs of a larger teacher model, not just the hard labels. The soft targets encode inter-class relationships and uncertainty, providing richer supervision that often produces students that outperform models trained on hard labels alone.

Q: What is model pruning?
A: Model pruning removes weights, neurons, or attention heads that contribute least to model performance, producing compact sparse models. Magnitude pruning removes small weights, while structured pruning removes entire channels or layers, enabling deployment of large models on resource-constrained devices.

Q: What is network quantization?
A: Quantization reduces the precision of neural network weights and activations from 32-bit floats to 8-bit integers or fewer bits. This reduces memory usage, speeds up inference through integer arithmetic, and enables deployment on edge devices while maintaining most of the model's accuracy.

Q: What is post-training quantization?
A: Post-training quantization applies quantization to an already trained model without retraining, using calibration data to determine optimal quantization ranges. It is fast and convenient but may produce more accuracy degradation than quantization-aware training for very aggressive precision reduction.

Q: What is quantization-aware training?
A: Quantization-aware training simulates the effects of quantization during the training process by inserting fake quantization operations in the forward pass. This allows the model to adapt its weights to the reduced precision, resulting in better accuracy after deployment compared to post-training quantization.

Q: What is weight quantization?
A: Weight quantization converts continuous floating-point model weights to a lower-precision representation such as INT8 or INT4. Since weights are static after training, they can be quantized very aggressively, often producing minimal accuracy loss while achieving significant memory savings.

Q: What is activation quantization?
A: Activation quantization converts the outputs of neural network layers to lower precision during inference. Unlike weight quantization, activations vary with each input, making their range harder to determine statically and typically requiring more careful calibration to avoid accuracy degradation.

Q: What is model compression?
A: Model compression reduces model size and computational requirements through techniques including pruning, quantization, knowledge distillation, and low-rank factorization. The goal is to produce models that meet resource constraints of deployment environments while retaining as much accuracy as possible.

Q: What is low-rank factorization?
A: Low-rank factorization decomposes large weight matrices into products of two or more smaller matrices with lower rank, reducing the number of parameters and computational cost. It exploits the observation that trained weight matrices often have low intrinsic rank due to redundancy in learned representations.

Q: What is a convolutional neural network?
A: A convolutional neural network applies learnable convolutional filters across spatial dimensions to detect local patterns. Pooling layers downsample feature maps, and multiple convolutional layers build hierarchical representations from edges to textures to objects, making CNNs highly effective for image understanding.

Q: What is a convolutional filter?
A: A convolutional filter is a small matrix of learnable weights slid across the input to produce a feature map highlighting specific patterns. Each filter learns to detect a particular feature such as horizontal edges, corners, or textures, and multiple filters per layer detect diverse patterns.

Q: What is a feature map?
A: A feature map is the output of a convolutional operation applied to an input, representing the spatial activation pattern of a particular filter. Stacking multiple feature maps across many convolutional layers builds hierarchical representations that encode increasingly abstract and semantic information.

Q: What is pooling in CNNs?
A: Pooling reduces the spatial dimensions of feature maps by summarizing local regions with summary statistics. Max pooling takes the maximum value in each region, selecting the strongest activation, while average pooling takes the mean, providing translation invariance and reducing computational cost.

Q: What is global average pooling?
A: Global average pooling averages the entire spatial extent of each feature map into a single scalar, reducing spatial feature maps to a vector without fully connected layers. It drastically reduces parameters, improves regularization, and produces spatial average descriptions used in modern classification architectures.

Q: What is padding in convolutions?
A: Padding adds values, typically zeros, around the edges of the input before applying convolution. It controls the spatial dimensions of the output, with valid padding reducing size and same padding maintaining the input dimensions, preventing loss of information at the boundaries.

Q: What is stride in convolutions?
A: Stride determines how many positions the convolutional filter moves between applications. A stride of one applies the filter at every position, while larger strides skip positions, reducing output spatial dimensions and computational cost, similar to pooling but with learnable behavior.

Q: What is a depthwise separable convolution?
A: Depthwise separable convolution factorizes a standard convolution into a depthwise convolution applied independently per channel followed by a pointwise one-by-one convolution. This reduces computation by roughly a factor of the number of output channels, used in MobileNet for efficient inference.

Q: What is dilated convolution?
A: Dilated convolution inserts spaces between filter elements to increase the receptive field without increasing parameters or reducing resolution. With dilation rate d, filter elements are spaced d positions apart, enabling exponentially larger receptive fields with only linear parameter growth.

Q: What is a receptive field?
A: The receptive field of a neuron is the region of the input space that influences that neuron's output. Deeper layers in CNNs have larger receptive fields, allowing them to integrate information from wider spatial contexts and detect larger patterns and objects.

Q: What is VGGNet?
A: VGGNet is a deep CNN architecture using exclusively three-by-three convolutional filters stacked in increasing depth. The simplicity of using small uniform filters demonstrated that network depth using very small convolutions is the critical component for achieving strong performance on large-scale image recognition.

Q: What is GoogLeNet or Inception?
A: GoogLeNet introduces inception modules that apply convolutional filters of multiple sizes in parallel and concatenate the outputs, capturing multi-scale spatial information efficiently. One-by-one convolutions reduce dimensionality before expensive large convolutions, achieving high accuracy with significantly fewer parameters than VGGNet.

Q: What is EfficientNet?
A: EfficientNet uniformly scales network depth, width, and resolution using compound scaling coefficients determined by neural architecture search. This principled scaling strategy achieves better accuracy and efficiency than scaling only one dimension, producing a family of models across a wide range of resource constraints.

Q: What is a recurrent neural network?
A: A recurrent neural network processes sequences by maintaining a hidden state vector updated at each time step based on the current input and previous hidden state. This allows it to capture temporal dependencies and learn sequential patterns for tasks like language modeling and time series forecasting.

Q: What is the hidden state in an RNN?
A: The hidden state in an RNN is a vector that summarizes information from all previous time steps in the sequence. It is updated at each step by combining the current input with the previous hidden state through learned weight matrices and a nonlinear activation function.

Q: What is a sequence-to-sequence model?
A: A sequence-to-sequence model uses an encoder to compress an input sequence into a fixed-length context vector and a decoder that generates an output sequence conditioned on this context. It is used for machine translation, summarization, and dialogue systems.

Q: What is teacher forcing?
A: Teacher forcing is a training strategy for sequence-to-sequence models that feeds the true target token at each decoding step rather than the model's previous prediction. It stabilizes training by preventing error accumulation but can create a mismatch between training and inference behavior.

Q: What is beam search?
A: Beam search is a decoding algorithm that maintains the top-k most probable partial sequences at each step rather than greedily choosing the single best token. The beam width k controls the tradeoff between exploration and computational cost in generating sequences from language models.

Q: What is the attention mechanism in sequence-to-sequence models?
A: Attention in sequence-to-sequence models allows the decoder to dynamically focus on different encoder states when generating each output token, rather than compressing the entire input into a single fixed vector. This dramatically improves translation quality for long sequences.

Q: What is the Transformer model?
A: The Transformer entirely replaces recurrence with multi-head self-attention mechanisms, enabling modeling of all pairwise interactions in a sequence simultaneously. Combined with positional encodings and feed-forward layers, it enables highly parallelizable training and achieves state-of-the-art results across NLP tasks.

Q: What is multi-head attention?
A: Multi-head attention applies scaled dot-product attention multiple times in parallel with different learned linear projections of queries, keys, and values. Each head can attend to different aspects of the input, and their outputs are concatenated and linearly projected to produce rich contextual representations.

Q: What is scaled dot-product attention?
A: Scaled dot-product attention computes compatibility scores between queries and keys, scales by the square root of the key dimension to prevent softmax saturation, applies softmax to produce attention weights, and outputs a weighted sum of values. It is the core operation in transformer attention.

Q: What is positional encoding in transformers?
A: Positional encoding adds sinusoidal or learned vectors to token embeddings to inject information about each token's position in the sequence. Since transformers lack recurrence, positional encodings allow the model to distinguish tokens based on their sequential order.

Q: What is BERT?
A: BERT, Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers, is pretrained using masked language modeling and next sentence prediction. Its bidirectional attention allows it to condition each token on both left and right context simultaneously, producing powerful contextual representations for fine-tuning on downstream NLP tasks.

Q: What is masked language modeling?
A: Masked language modeling, used in BERT pretraining, randomly replaces a fraction of input tokens with a mask token and trains the model to predict the original tokens from surrounding context. This forces the model to learn deep bidirectional representations using both left and right context.

Q: What is next sentence prediction?
A: Next sentence prediction is a binary classification pretraining task where the model predicts whether a second sentence follows the first in the original document or is randomly sampled. It is used in BERT pretraining to learn inter-sentence relationships useful for downstream tasks requiring sentence pair reasoning.

Q: What is GPT?
A: GPT, Generative Pretrained Transformer, is a unidirectional transformer pretrained on next-token prediction. It generates text autoregressively by predicting each token conditioned on all previous tokens, making it highly effective for text generation tasks and few-shot learning through prompting.

Q: What is autoregressive language modeling?
A: Autoregressive language modeling trains a model to predict the probability of each token given all preceding tokens in a sequence. At inference, tokens are sampled or selected one at a time from the predicted distribution and appended to the growing sequence.

Q: What is temperature sampling?
A: Temperature sampling controls the diversity of text generation by dividing logits by a temperature parameter before applying softmax. Higher temperatures flatten the distribution producing more diverse outputs, while lower temperatures sharpen it toward the most probable tokens, with zero producing greedy decoding.

Q: What is top-k sampling?
A: Top-k sampling restricts generation to only the k most probable next tokens, redistributing probability mass among them before sampling. It prevents sampling of very unlikely tokens while maintaining diversity, avoiding the degenerate behavior of always selecting the single most probable token.

Q: What is top-p or nucleus sampling?
A: Top-p sampling selects the smallest set of tokens whose cumulative probability mass exceeds a threshold p, then samples from this nucleus. Unlike top-k, the nucleus size adapts to the distribution's shape, using fewer tokens when the distribution is peaked and more when it is flat.

Q: What is RoBERTa?
A: RoBERTa retrains BERT with larger batches, more data, longer training, and without next sentence prediction, demonstrating that BERT was significantly undertrained. These modifications improve performance across GLUE benchmarks, showing the importance of training details beyond architectural choices.

Q: What is XLNet?
A: XLNet uses a permutation-based autoregressive pretraining objective that considers all permutations of the factorization order, enabling bidirectional context without the mask tokens that create a training-inference mismatch in BERT. It outperforms BERT on several NLP benchmarks.

Q: What is T5?
A: T5, Text-to-Text Transfer Transformer, frames all NLP tasks as text-to-text problems where both inputs and outputs are strings. This unified formulation allows training a single model on diverse tasks like translation, summarization, and question answering using a shared encoder-decoder architecture.

Q: What is a vision transformer?
A: A Vision Transformer, or ViT, applies the standard transformer architecture to images by dividing the image into patches, linearly embedding each patch, and processing the sequence of patch embeddings with transformer encoder layers. It achieves strong image classification performance with sufficient pretraining data.

Q: What is DALL-E?
A: DALL-E is a transformer model trained to generate images from text descriptions by treating both text tokens and image tokens as a unified sequence. It demonstrates that large-scale autoregressive models can learn complex cross-modal mappings between language and visual content.

Q: What is CLIP?
A: CLIP, Contrastive Language-Image Pretraining, jointly trains an image encoder and a text encoder to produce aligned representations where matching image-text pairs have high similarity. The contrastive objective on hundreds of millions of web image-text pairs produces powerful zero-shot visual classifiers.

Q: What is contrastive learning?
A: Contrastive learning trains representations by pulling embeddings of similar pairs closer and pushing dissimilar pairs apart in embedding space. Models like SimCLR create positive pairs through data augmentation and use the other batch samples as negatives, learning representations without manual labels.

Q: What is SimCLR?
A: SimCLR is a self-supervised contrastive learning framework that applies two different augmentations to each image, encodes both views, and trains the encoder to maximize agreement between the same image's views while minimizing agreement with all other images in the batch.

Q: What is MoCo?
A: Momentum Contrast maintains a large queue of negative examples encoded by a momentum-updated encoder, enabling large effective batch sizes for contrastive learning without requiring the negatives to be recomputed every step. This makes contrastive pretraining more efficient and scalable.

Q: What is a diffusion model?
A: A diffusion model learns to generate data by learning to reverse a gradual noising process. During training it predicts the noise added at each step, and during generation it iteratively denoises random noise through many small steps, producing high-quality images that rival GANs.

Q: What is a denoising diffusion probabilistic model?
A: A denoising diffusion probabilistic model is a specific implementation of diffusion models that trains a neural network to predict the noise added at each step of a Markov chain that gradually converts data to pure noise. Reversing this chain generates new samples from the learned distribution.

Q: What is classifier-free guidance?
A: Classifier-free guidance trains a conditional generative model alongside an unconditional version by randomly dropping conditioning information during training. At inference, predictions are interpolated between conditional and unconditional outputs with a guidance scale, trading diversity for fidelity to the condition.

Q: What is stable diffusion?
A: Stable diffusion performs the diffusion process in a compressed latent space encoded by a variational autoencoder rather than pixel space, dramatically reducing computational cost. It enables fast, high-quality text-to-image generation on consumer hardware using cross-attention to condition on text embeddings.

Q: What is a GAN?
A: A Generative Adversarial Network trains a generator to produce realistic synthetic samples and a discriminator to distinguish real from generated samples simultaneously. The adversarial training dynamic drives the generator toward the true data distribution without an explicit likelihood objective.

Q: What is mode collapse in GANs?
A: Mode collapse occurs when a GAN generator produces only a limited variety of outputs rather than the full diversity of the real data distribution. The generator discovers a small number of outputs that consistently fool the discriminator, ignoring large portions of the data distribution.

Q: What is Wasserstein GAN?
A: Wasserstein GAN replaces the Jensen-Shannon divergence used in standard GANs with the Wasserstein distance as the training objective, providing more meaningful gradients even when distributions have disjoint support. It uses gradient penalty or weight clipping to enforce the Lipschitz constraint on the critic.

Q: What is a conditional GAN?
A: A conditional GAN conditions both the generator and discriminator on additional information such as class labels, producing targeted generation of specific categories rather than unconditional sampling. This enables controlled image synthesis, image-to-image translation, and other guided generation tasks.

Q: What is CycleGAN?
A: CycleGAN performs unpaired image-to-image translation by training two generators and two discriminators with a cycle consistency loss ensuring that translating an image to the target domain and back recovers the original, enabling style transfer without requiring paired training examples.

Q: What is a variational autoencoder?
A: A variational autoencoder encodes inputs into a distribution over latent variables rather than a single point, parameterized by a mean and variance. The decoder samples from this distribution, and the ELBO training objective combines reconstruction loss with a KL divergence regularizing the latent space.

Q: What is the reparameterization trick in VAEs?
A: The reparameterization trick allows backpropagation through stochastic sampling by expressing the sampled latent vector as a deterministic function of the parameters plus a separate noise variable. Writing z as mu plus sigma times epsilon, where epsilon is standard normal, enables gradients to flow through mu and sigma.

Q: What is graph neural network?
A: Graph neural networks extend neural networks to graph-structured data by iteratively aggregating and transforming features from each node's neighbors. This message-passing framework allows learning representations that respect the relational structure of the data for tasks like node classification and link prediction.

Q: What is message passing in GNNs?
A: Message passing is the core operation in graph neural networks where each node collects messages from its neighbors, aggregates them with a permutation-invariant function like sum or mean, and updates its own representation using the aggregated message combined with its current state.

Q: What is graph convolutional network?
A: A graph convolutional network generalizes convolution to irregular graph domains by computing each node's new representation as a normalized sum of its own and its neighbors' features, multiplied by learned weights. Stacking GCN layers propagates information across increasingly large neighborhoods.

Q: What is graph attention network?
A: A graph attention network replaces the fixed normalized aggregation of GCNs with learned attention weights that determine the importance of each neighbor's contribution. Self-attention over the local neighborhood allows different neighbors to be weighted differently for each query node.

Q: What is a point cloud network?
A: A point cloud network processes unordered sets of three-dimensional points representing object surfaces, learning permutation-invariant features for tasks like 3D shape classification and segmentation. PointNet processes each point independently and aggregates with a global max pool, while PointNet++ uses local grouping.

Q: What is a neural ordinary differential equation?
A: A neural ODE models the hidden state dynamics of a network as a continuous-time ODE parameterized by a neural network, solving it with a numerical ODE solver during the forward pass. This enables adaptive computation depth, memory-efficient training via the adjoint method, and modeling of continuous dynamics.

Q: What is gradient descent?
A: Gradient descent is an iterative optimization algorithm that updates parameters by subtracting the product of the learning rate and the gradient of the loss with respect to each parameter. It drives parameters toward values that minimize the objective function over many update steps.

Q: What is stochastic gradient descent?
A: Stochastic gradient descent computes parameter updates using the gradient from a single randomly selected training example or small mini-batch. The noisy gradient estimates introduce variance that can help escape sharp minima and often converges faster than full-batch gradient descent on large datasets.

Q: What is mini-batch gradient descent?
A: Mini-batch gradient descent updates parameters using gradient estimates computed from a small subset of training data. It balances the low variance of full-batch gradients with the computational efficiency of stochastic updates, using vectorized operations efficiently on modern parallel hardware.

Q: What is the AdaGrad optimizer?
A: AdaGrad maintains a running sum of squared past gradients and divides each parameter's gradient by the square root of this sum. Parameters that have received large gradients historically get smaller updates, making it well-suited for sparse gradient problems but prone to excessive learning rate decay.

Q: What is the AdaDelta optimizer?
A: AdaDelta addresses AdaGrad's diminishing learning rates by replacing the accumulation of all squared gradients with a decaying average of recent squared gradients. It also eliminates the need for a global learning rate by normalizing updates by the recent parameter update magnitudes.

Q: What is the AdamW optimizer?
A: AdamW decouples weight decay from the gradient adaptation in Adam, applying weight decay directly to the parameters rather than to the gradients. This produces more theoretically sound regularization and empirically improves generalization compared to standard Adam with L2 regularization.

Q: What is the LAMB optimizer?
A: LAMB, or Layer-wise Adaptive Moments, scales Adam's per-parameter adaptive update by the ratio of parameter norm to update norm for each layer. This layer-wise trust ratio normalization enables training with very large batch sizes by stabilizing updates across layers with different scales.

Q: What is the Lookahead optimizer?
A: Lookahead maintains two sets of weights: fast weights updated by an inner optimizer and slow weights that take a step toward the fast weights every k steps. This look-ahead and correction mechanism improves training stability and often achieves better generalization than inner optimizer alone.

Q: What is a learning rate finder?
A: A learning rate finder systematically increases the learning rate from a very small value to a large value during a short training run, recording the loss at each step. The optimal learning rate is chosen just before the loss stops decreasing and begins to diverge.

Q: What is the one-cycle learning rate policy?
A: The one-cycle policy linearly increases the learning rate to a maximum over the first half of training, then decreases it to a minimum over the second half, followed by a brief annealing phase. This schedule often achieves better performance in fewer iterations than constant or step-based schedules.

Q: What is loss surface visualization?
A: Loss surface visualization plots the loss as a function of model parameters in low-dimensional projections, revealing the geometry of the optimization landscape including flat regions, sharp minima, and saddle points. It provides intuition about why certain optimizers or training strategies work better.

Q: What is a saddle point?
A: A saddle point is a critical point in the loss landscape where the gradient is zero but the point is neither a local minimum nor maximum, having positive curvature in some directions and negative in others. First-order methods slow down near saddle points but can escape them with sufficient noise.

Q: What is a sharp minimum?
A: A sharp minimum is a narrow valley in the loss landscape where small perturbations to parameters cause large increases in loss. Models converged to sharp minima often generalize poorly because they are sensitive to noise in the input. Flat minima correlate with better generalization.

Q: What is sharpness-aware minimization?
A: Sharpness-aware minimization explicitly seeks parameters in flat regions of the loss landscape by minimizing the worst-case loss within a small neighborhood of the current parameters. This two-step optimization procedure consistently improves generalization across diverse architectures and tasks.

Q: What is the role of batch size in training?
A: Batch size determines how many examples are processed before updating parameters. Larger batches produce more accurate gradient estimates and better GPU utilization but may converge to sharper minima with worse generalization. Scaling the learning rate proportionally to batch size is a common practice.

Q: What is gradient accumulation?
A: Gradient accumulation simulates a larger effective batch size by accumulating gradients over multiple forward and backward passes before performing a parameter update. This allows training with large effective batch sizes when memory is insufficient to process the desired batch in a single step.

Q: What is second-order optimization?
A: Second-order optimization uses the curvature of the loss surface, captured in the Hessian matrix or its approximations, to compute more accurate and efficient parameter updates than first-order gradient descent. Methods like L-BFGS and K-FAC use curvature information to adaptively scale updates.

Q: What is the Fisher information matrix?
A: The Fisher information matrix characterizes the curvature of the log-likelihood with respect to model parameters, measuring how much information the data provides about each parameter. Natural gradient methods use the inverse Fisher matrix to scale gradient updates, equating to second-order optimization in parameter space.

Q: What is natural gradient descent?
A: Natural gradient descent preconditions gradient updates by the inverse Fisher information matrix, adapting to the geometry of the parameter space. It converges faster than standard gradient descent for many models but computing and inverting the full Fisher matrix is computationally prohibitive for large networks.

Q: What is K-FAC?
A: K-FAC, Kronecker-Factored Approximate Curvature, approximates the Fisher information matrix as a Kronecker product of two smaller matrices for each layer, enabling efficient computation and inversion. It provides second-order optimization benefits with much lower computational cost than full natural gradient methods.

Q: What is gradient noise?
A: Gradient noise refers to the variance in gradient estimates arising from using mini-batches rather than the full dataset. Controlled noise can help optimization escape sharp local minima and saddle points, and annealing the noise by reducing learning rate or increasing batch size helps fine-tune near minima.

Q: What is cyclical learning rates?
A: Cyclical learning rates periodically increase and decrease the learning rate between minimum and maximum bounds during training. The cyclical variation helps escape local minima during increasing phases and stabilizes near good solutions during decreasing phases, often improving final performance.

Q: What is the relationship between optimization and generalization?
A: Optimization concerns minimizing training loss while generalization concerns test performance. Models that optimize training loss perfectly may overfit, and the relationship between optimization trajectory, convergence basin, and test performance is complex. Regularization, early stopping, and optimizer choice all affect both.

Q: What is flat loss landscape and why does it generalize better?
A: Flat regions of the loss landscape correspond to parameters where a wide range of values produce similarly low loss. Models in flat minima are more robust to small perturbations in weights, which may correspond to distribution shift between training and test data, explaining their better generalization.

Q: What is stochastic weight averaging?
A: Stochastic weight averaging computes the average of model weights visited during training at different points, typically using a cyclical learning rate schedule to explore multiple local minima. The averaged weights often lie in a wider, flatter region of the loss landscape with better generalization.

Q: What is neural network scaling laws?
A: Scaling laws describe empirical power law relationships between model performance and scale factors such as the number of parameters, training data size, and compute budget. They predict that larger models trained on more data with more compute consistently achieve lower loss, guiding resource allocation decisions.

Q: What is the Chinchilla scaling law?
A: The Chinchilla scaling law, derived from large-scale experiments, found that optimal training requires roughly twenty training tokens per model parameter. Previous models like GPT-3 were found to be over-parameterized relative to their training data, and compute-optimal models are significantly smaller but trained on more data.

Q: What is a learning rate warmup period?
A: A warmup period gradually increases the learning rate from near zero to its target value over the first portion of training. It stabilizes early optimization when gradients are noisy and the adaptive statistics of optimizers like Adam have not yet converged to reliable estimates.

Q: What is gradient flow?
A: Gradient flow refers to how gradient signals propagate backward through a network during training. Good gradient flow ensures all layers receive informative gradient signals and learn effectively. Residual connections, normalization, and careful initialization improve gradient flow in deep networks.

Q: What is a compute budget?
A: A compute budget specifies the total amount of floating-point operations or time available for training. Given a fixed compute budget, scaling laws inform decisions about optimal model size and dataset size, trading off between fewer parameters trained longer or more parameters trained shorter.

Q: What is efficient training?
