A: Efficient training maximizes model performance achieved per unit of compute, memory, and time. Techniques include mixed precision training, gradient accumulation, checkpointing, data loading optimization, and using hardware-optimized operations to eliminate bottlenecks in the training pipeline.

Q: What is distributed training?
A: Distributed training splits the training workload across multiple processors or machines to enable handling larger models or datasets. Common parallelism strategies include data parallelism, model parallelism, tensor parallelism, and pipeline parallelism, often used in combination.

Q: What is the ring-allreduce algorithm?
A: Ring-allreduce is a communication algorithm for data-parallel distributed training where devices exchange gradients in a ring topology, with each device simultaneously sending and receiving data. It achieves optimal communication bandwidth utilization and scales efficiently to large numbers of devices.

Q: What is gradient compression?
A: Gradient compression reduces the communication overhead in distributed training by transmitting compressed gradients that approximate the true gradients. Techniques include sparsification, which transmits only the largest gradient values, and quantization, which uses lower-precision representations.

Q: What is asynchronous training?
A: Asynchronous training allows workers to update parameters without waiting for other workers to complete their gradient computations. While it reduces idle time and increases throughput, stale gradients from slow workers can slow convergence and require careful handling.

Q: What is elastic training?
A: Elastic training dynamically adjusts the number of training workers, scaling up when resources become available and scaling down when they are needed elsewhere or when workers fail. It improves fault tolerance and resource utilization in large-scale distributed training systems.

Q: What is hyperparameter search?
A: Hyperparameter search systematically explores the space of hyperparameter configurations to find the combination that maximizes validation performance. Methods include grid search over a fixed set of values, random search which samples configurations randomly, and Bayesian optimization which models the performance landscape.

Q: What is Bayesian hyperparameter optimization?
A: Bayesian optimization models the relationship between hyperparameters and validation performance using a surrogate probabilistic model, typically a Gaussian process. It uses this model to intelligently select the next configuration to evaluate, balancing exploration of uncertain regions and exploitation of promising ones.

Q: What is random search for hyperparameters?
A: Random search samples hyperparameter configurations uniformly from specified ranges and evaluates each independently. It is surprisingly effective compared to grid search because it samples more diverse configurations across the most important dimensions, efficiently covering the search space.

Q: What is population-based training?
A: Population-based training maintains a population of models training simultaneously with different hyperparameter configurations. Poorly performing models can copy the weights and hyperparameters of better-performing ones and then mutate the hyperparameters, efficiently discovering good configurations while sharing learned representations.

Q: What is AutoML?
A: Automated Machine Learning automates the end-to-end process of applying machine learning, including data preprocessing, feature engineering, model selection, hyperparameter optimization, and ensembling. It democratizes machine learning by enabling non-experts to build competitive models with minimal manual intervention.

Q: What is neural architecture search?
A: Neural architecture search automatically discovers optimal model architectures using reinforcement learning, evolutionary algorithms, or gradient-based differentiable relaxations. While computationally expensive, it has discovered architectures like NASNet and EfficientNet that outperform human-designed counterparts.

Q: What is differentiable neural architecture search?
A: Differentiable neural architecture search relaxes the discrete architecture choices to a continuous supernetwork where operations are weighted by learned architecture parameters. Gradient descent simultaneously optimizes the weights and architecture parameters, enabling efficient search that avoids costly separate evaluation of candidate architectures.

Q: What is warm starting?
A: Warm starting initializes a new model with weights from a previously trained model rather than random initialization, providing a better starting point for optimization. It is used when fine-tuning pretrained models, resuming interrupted training, or adapting models to new data distributions.

Q: What is weight averaging?
A: Weight averaging combines the parameters of multiple model checkpoints from the same training run by computing their element-wise mean. Averaged weights often lie in flatter loss regions with better generalization than any individual checkpoint, improving test performance at no additional inference cost.

Q: What is exponential moving average for model weights?
A: Exponential moving average maintains a shadow copy of model weights updated as a weighted combination of the current shadow weights and the latest trained weights. The EMA weights provide smoother, more stable parameter estimates used for evaluation and deployment while training continues on the primary weights.

Q: What is loss spike in training?
A: A loss spike is a sudden large increase in training loss, typically caused by a bad mini-batch, numerical instability, or learning rate that is too large. Gradient clipping, loss scaling, and monitoring gradient norms help detect and mitigate loss spikes before they destabilize training.

Q: What is numerical stability in training?
A: Numerical stability ensures that floating-point operations during training do not produce infinities, NaNs, or catastrophic cancellation that disrupts optimization. It is maintained through careful initialization, gradient clipping, normalization, mixed precision with loss scaling, and monitoring of intermediate values.

Q: What is a checkpoint in training?
A: A training checkpoint saves the model weights, optimizer state, and training progress at a particular step, enabling training to resume after interruption and preserving model snapshots for analysis. Checkpointing is essential for long training runs and enables selecting the best model seen during training.

Q: What is the role of random seeds in machine learning?
A: Random seeds initialize pseudorandom number generators to produce reproducible sequences of random numbers, ensuring that stochastic operations like weight initialization, data shuffling, and dropout produce identical results across runs. Fixing seeds is essential for reproducibility but can obscure variance in results.

Q: What is training instability?
A: Training instability manifests as erratic loss behavior, diverging gradients, or consistently poor convergence. It is caused by inappropriate learning rates, poor initialization, lack of normalization, or architectural choices that impede gradient flow. Identifying and addressing the root cause requires careful diagnosis.

Q: What is natural language processing?
A: Natural language processing enables computers to understand, analyze, and generate human language. It encompasses tasks like tokenization, parsing, sentiment analysis, machine translation, question answering, and text generation, leveraging statistical models, deep learning, and linguistic knowledge.

Q: What is tokenization?
A: Tokenization splits raw text into smaller units called tokens, which may be words, subwords, or characters. Modern tokenizers use byte-pair encoding or unigram language model algorithms to learn a subword vocabulary that balances vocabulary size with the ability to represent any word.

Q: What is byte-pair encoding?
A: Byte-pair encoding iteratively merges the most frequent pair of adjacent symbols in a corpus, starting from characters and building up a vocabulary of subword units. It represents rare words as multiple subword tokens and frequent words as single tokens, balancing vocabulary coverage and size.

Q: What is the vocabulary size of language models?
A: Vocabulary size determines how many unique tokens a language model recognizes. Larger vocabularies represent more words as single tokens but increase the size of the embedding and output projection layers. Modern language models typically use vocabularies of 30,000 to 100,000 subword tokens.

Q: What is word embedding?
A: A word embedding maps discrete tokens to dense continuous vectors in a high-dimensional space where semantically similar words have similar representations. Learned from large text corpora, embeddings capture syntactic and semantic relationships including analogy relationships between words.

Q: What is context in language models?
A: Context refers to the surrounding text that a language model conditions on when predicting or representing a token. Larger context windows allow models to use more information from the document history, improving coherence and enabling long-range dependencies to be captured.

Q: What is context window length?
A: Context window length is the maximum number of tokens a language model can process in a single forward pass, determining how much preceding text it can attend to. GPT-2 uses 1024 tokens, while modern models extend to 128,000 or more using techniques like rotary position embeddings.

Q: What is perplexity in language modeling?
A: Perplexity is the exponential of the average cross-entropy loss per token, measuring how surprised a language model is by a held-out test set. Lower perplexity indicates better predictions. It is the primary intrinsic metric for language model quality before task-specific evaluation.

Q: What is a language model?
A: A language model is a probability distribution over sequences of tokens, assigning high probability to likely sequences and low probability to unlikely ones. It can be used for text generation, scoring candidate sequences, feature extraction, and as a foundation for fine-tuning on downstream NLP tasks.

Q: What is instruction tuning?
A: Instruction tuning fine-tunes a pretrained language model on examples that pair natural language instructions with desired outputs, teaching the model to follow human instructions. Models trained this way generalize to a wide range of tasks described in natural language without task-specific fine-tuning.

Q: What is reinforcement learning from human feedback?
A: RLHF fine-tunes language models using human preference data collected by asking raters to compare model outputs. A reward model trained on these preferences scores model outputs, and PPO reinforcement learning optimizes the language model to produce outputs that maximize the reward while staying close to the original model.

Q: What is PPO in RLHF?
A: Proximal Policy Optimization is a policy gradient reinforcement learning algorithm used in RLHF to fine-tune language models. It updates the policy to maximize reward while constraining the update magnitude using a clipped surrogate objective, preventing large destructive policy changes during fine-tuning.

Q: What is a reward model?
A: A reward model in RLHF is a neural network trained to predict human preference scores for model outputs, fine-tuned from the same pretrained language model. It provides a differentiable proxy for human judgment, enabling the language model to be optimized via reinforcement learning.

Q: What is constitutional AI?
A: Constitutional AI is an approach from Anthropic that trains AI systems to be helpful, harmless, and honest using a set of principles instead of purely human preference labels. The model critiques and revises its own outputs according to the principles, then a preference model is trained on the revisions.

Q: What is direct preference optimization?
A: Direct preference optimization is an alternative to RLHF that directly optimizes the language model policy on paired preference data without training a separate reward model. It frames the reward maximization as a binary cross-entropy loss on preferred versus rejected response pairs, simplifying the training pipeline.

Q: What is a prompt in language models?
A: A prompt is the text input provided to a language model that conditions its generation. Prompts can include instructions, examples, context, and the beginning of the desired output, and the model generates a completion that continues from where the prompt leaves off.

Q: What is prompt engineering?
A: Prompt engineering designs the text provided to language models to elicit desired behaviors or improve performance on specific tasks. Techniques include chain-of-thought prompting, few-shot examples, structured output formatting, and role assignment to guide model responses without fine-tuning.

Q: What is chain-of-thought prompting?
A: Chain-of-thought prompting provides examples or instructions that encourage a language model to generate intermediate reasoning steps before producing a final answer. Showing the model how to think through problems step-by-step dramatically improves performance on mathematical, logical, and commonsense reasoning tasks.

Q: What is few-shot prompting?
A: Few-shot prompting provides a language model with a small number of input-output examples in the prompt to demonstrate the desired task format and behavior. The model infers the pattern from the examples and generalizes to the actual query without updating any parameters.

Q: What is zero-shot prompting?
A: Zero-shot prompting describes a desired task to a language model in natural language without providing any examples. Large enough language models can perform many tasks zero-shot by understanding the task description and applying knowledge acquired during pretraining.

Q: What is in-context learning?
A: In-context learning is the ability of language models to adapt to new tasks by conditioning on examples provided in the prompt at inference time, without gradient updates to the model parameters. It emerges in large language models and improves with scale.

Q: What is hallucination in language models?
A: Hallucination occurs when a language model generates text that is fluent and plausible-sounding but factually incorrect or not grounded in the provided context. It arises because models are trained to produce likely continuations rather than verified facts, making factual accuracy a critical challenge.

Q: What is retrieval-augmented generation?
A: Retrieval-augmented generation combines a language model with a retrieval system that fetches relevant documents from an external knowledge base at inference time. The retrieved documents are provided as context to the model, enabling it to generate accurate, grounded responses using up-to-date information.

Q: What is a vector database?
A: A vector database stores dense embedding vectors alongside their source documents and enables efficient approximate nearest neighbor search. In retrieval-augmented generation, query embeddings are compared against stored document embeddings to retrieve the most semantically relevant documents quickly.

Q: What is approximate nearest neighbor search?
A: Approximate nearest neighbor search finds vectors in a database that are close to a query vector without exhaustive comparison to all entries, using data structures like HNSW or FAISS that index vectors for fast retrieval. It enables scalable similarity search over millions or billions of embeddings.

Q: What is FAISS?
A: FAISS, Facebook AI Similarity Search, is a library for efficient similarity search and clustering of dense vectors. It implements highly optimized approximate nearest neighbor algorithms that leverage GPU acceleration, enabling fast retrieval from embedding databases containing billions of vectors.

Q: What is fine-tuning a language model for classification?
A: Fine-tuning for classification adds a classification head on top of a pretrained language model and trains the entire system end-to-end on labeled examples. The pretrained model provides rich language representations while the head learns task-specific decision boundaries with minimal labeled data.

Q: What is parameter efficient fine-tuning?
A: Parameter efficient fine-tuning adapts pretrained language models to new tasks by modifying only a small fraction of parameters while keeping most pretrained weights frozen. Methods like LoRA, prefix tuning, and adapter layers achieve competitive performance while storing and updating far fewer parameters.

Q: What is LoRA?
A: Low-Rank Adaptation freezes pretrained model weights and injects trainable low-rank decomposition matrices into each transformer layer. By representing weight updates as products of two small matrices, LoRA achieves task adaptation with a fraction of the parameters of full fine-tuning at minimal performance cost.

Q: What is prefix tuning?
A: Prefix tuning prepends a sequence of learnable continuous token embeddings to the input of each transformer layer, adapting the model's behavior without modifying its weights. Only the prefix parameters are updated during fine-tuning, requiring far fewer parameters than full model adaptation.

Q: What is adapter tuning?
A: Adapter tuning inserts small bottleneck modules with approximately 0.5 to 8 percent of the original parameter count between transformer layers. Only adapter weights are trained while the base model is frozen, enabling efficient task-specific adaptation while keeping the original model unchanged for other tasks.

Q: What is prompt tuning?
A: Prompt tuning learns a small set of continuous task-specific prompt embeddings prepended to the input while keeping the entire language model frozen. It simplifies multi-task deployment since the same frozen model serves all tasks with different learned prompt vectors, reducing storage requirements significantly.

Q: What is the GLUE benchmark?
A: GLUE, the General Language Understanding Evaluation benchmark, is a collection of nine NLP tasks designed to evaluate the breadth of language understanding including textual entailment, sentiment analysis, question answering, and linguistic acceptability. Fine-tuned BERT substantially advanced the state of the art on GLUE.

Q: What is the SuperGLUE benchmark?
A: SuperGLUE is a more challenging successor to GLUE designed after BERT approached human performance, containing harder tasks requiring multi-sentence reasoning, coreference resolution, word sense disambiguation, and question answering that require deeper language understanding.

Q: What is named entity recognition?
A: Named entity recognition identifies and classifies mentions of named entities in text into categories like person, organization, location, date, and product. It is a fundamental information extraction task used in knowledge base construction, document understanding, and downstream question answering.

Q: What is part-of-speech tagging?
A: Part-of-speech tagging assigns grammatical categories such as noun, verb, adjective, and adverb to each token in a text. It is used as a preprocessing step for downstream NLP tasks that benefit from syntactic information, and is now often performed implicitly by pretrained language models.

Q: What is dependency parsing?
A: Dependency parsing analyzes the grammatical structure of a sentence by identifying syntactic dependencies between words, connecting each word to its head and labeling the dependency relation. The result is a dependency tree representing the predicate-argument structure and modifier attachments in the sentence.

Q: What is coreference resolution?
A: Coreference resolution identifies all expressions in a text that refer to the same real-world entity, grouping them into coreference clusters. It is essential for discourse understanding, question answering, and information extraction tasks that require tracking entities across multiple sentences.

Q: What is relation extraction?
A: Relation extraction identifies semantic relationships between entities mentioned in text, such as person works at organization or city located in country. It converts unstructured text into structured knowledge that can populate knowledge graphs and support question answering systems.

Q: What is text summarization?
A: Text summarization automatically produces a shorter version of a document that preserves its most important information. Extractive summarization selects and arranges key sentences from the source, while abstractive summarization generates new text that condenses the main points.

Q: What is machine translation?
A: Machine translation automatically translates text from one natural language to another. Neural machine translation using sequence-to-sequence transformers with attention has replaced statistical phrase-based approaches, achieving near-human quality for high-resource language pairs.

Q: What is BLEU score?
A: BLEU, or Bilingual Evaluation Understudy, measures translation quality by computing the precision of n-gram overlaps between a candidate translation and reference translations. A brevity penalty discourages overly short translations. While widely used, BLEU correlates imperfectly with human translation quality judgments.

Q: What is ROUGE score?
A: ROUGE, Recall-Oriented Understudy for Gisting Evaluation, measures summarization and translation quality by computing recall of n-gram overlaps between system output and reference summaries. ROUGE-N measures unigram and bigram recall, while ROUGE-L measures the longest common subsequence.

Q: What is BERTScore?
A: BERTScore computes similarity between reference and generated text using contextual BERT embeddings, matching tokens by their cosine similarity in the embedding space rather than exact string matches. It correlates better with human judgments than BLEU for tasks where paraphrases are acceptable.

Q: What is sentiment analysis?
A: Sentiment analysis classifies the emotional polarity of text as positive, negative, or neutral, and more fine-grained approaches identify specific emotions or aspect-level sentiments about particular attributes. It is widely used for monitoring brand reputation, product reviews, and social media analysis.

Q: What is question answering?
A: Question answering systems automatically answer natural language questions using information from documents, knowledge bases, or the model's parameters. Extractive QA identifies answer spans in a provided document, while generative QA and open-domain QA generate answers from world knowledge.

Q: What is textual entailment?
A: Textual entailment determines whether a hypothesis logically follows from a premise, is contradicted by it, or is neutral with respect to it. It is a fundamental NLP task that requires language understanding including inference, common sense reasoning, and world knowledge.

Q: What is semantic role labeling?
A: Semantic role labeling identifies the predicate-argument structure of sentences, labeling each verb and its arguments with semantic roles such as agent, patient, instrument, and location. It captures who did what to whom, when, and where, providing semantic structure beyond syntactic analysis.

Q: What is word sense disambiguation?
A: Word sense disambiguation determines which sense of a polysemous word is intended in a given context. For example, distinguishing the bank of a river from a financial bank requires contextual reasoning, and language models learn to perform this disambiguation implicitly from their training data.

Q: What is a language model's context length limitation?
A: Context length limitations restrict the maximum amount of text a model can process in one forward pass. Within-context attention is quadratic in sequence length, and positions beyond the training length may receive poor position encodings. Techniques like rotary embeddings and sliding window attention extend effective context.

Q: What is rotary position embedding?
A: Rotary position embedding encodes absolute position information into query and key vectors by applying a rotation matrix that depends on position. The resulting dot product naturally encodes relative position differences, enabling effective generalization to longer sequences than those seen during training.

Q: What is a mixture of experts language model?
A: A mixture of experts language model replaces dense feed-forward layers with a collection of expert networks and a learned routing mechanism that activates a small subset of experts per token. This dramatically increases total parameter count without proportionally increasing computation per forward pass.

Q: What is speculative decoding?
A: Speculative decoding accelerates language model inference by using a small draft model to generate multiple candidate tokens cheaply, then verifying them in parallel with the full model. Tokens that the full model accepts are used, and the process restarts from the first rejected token.

Q: What is key-value cache?
A: The key-value cache stores the attention key and value matrices computed for past tokens during autoregressive generation, avoiding recomputing them at each new step. This reduces inference time from quadratic to linear in the number of generated tokens, making generation much faster.

Q: What is flash attention?
A: Flash attention is a memory-efficient exact attention algorithm that reorders computation to minimize memory reads and writes between GPU HBM and SRAM. It achieves the same mathematical result as standard attention while being significantly faster and using much less memory for long sequences.

Q: What is sparse attention?
A: Sparse attention reduces the quadratic complexity of full self-attention by computing attention only between a subset of token pairs, such as local windows, strided patterns, or globally important tokens. It enables transformers to process much longer sequences efficiently at modest accuracy cost.

Q: What is a language model's emergent ability?
A: Emergent abilities are capabilities that appear suddenly in language models only above certain scale thresholds, absent in smaller models and then suddenly present in larger ones. Examples include multi-step arithmetic, chain-of-thought reasoning, and multilingual translation emerging without explicit training.

Q: What is model alignment?
A: Model alignment is the process of ensuring AI systems behave in accordance with human values, intentions, and preferences. Aligned models are helpful, following instructions; honest, avoiding deception; and harmless, refusing dangerous requests. RLHF and constitutional AI are practical alignment techniques.

Q: What is the safety problem in AI?
A: The AI safety problem concerns ensuring that AI systems reliably pursue intended goals, avoid harmful behaviors, and remain under meaningful human control as they become more capable. It includes technical challenges like specification, robustness, and interpretability, and governance challenges.

Q: What is token prediction in language models?
A: Token prediction is the core training objective of autoregressive language models, where the model learns to predict the next token in a sequence given all previous tokens. Minimizing cross-entropy loss across billions of tokens on diverse internet text produces models with broad language understanding.

Q: What is model evaluation?
A: Model evaluation assesses how well a trained model performs on the target task using held-out data not seen during training. Proper evaluation requires appropriate metrics, unbiased test sets, statistical significance testing, and analysis across different data subgroups to ensure comprehensive assessment.

Q: What is accuracy as a metric?
A: Accuracy is the fraction of correctly classified examples among all examples. While intuitive, it can be misleading for imbalanced datasets where predicting the majority class always gives high accuracy. It should be supplemented with precision, recall, and F1 for imbalanced problems.

Q: What is precision in classification?
A: Precision, also called positive predictive value, is the fraction of predicted positive examples that are truly positive. High precision indicates the model makes few false positive predictions and is important in applications where false alarms are costly, such as medical screening.

Q: What is recall in classification?
A: Recall, also called sensitivity or true positive rate, is the fraction of actual positive examples correctly identified by the model. High recall means few actual positives are missed and is critical in applications where missing a positive case is costly, such as disease detection.

Q: What is the F1 score?
A: The F1 score is the harmonic mean of precision and recall, providing a single metric that balances both. It is particularly useful for imbalanced classification problems where accuracy is misleading. The F-beta score generalizes this by weighting recall beta times more heavily than precision.

Q: What is specificity?
A: Specificity, or true negative rate, measures the proportion of actual negative examples correctly identified as negative. High specificity is important when false positives have serious consequences, such as flagging healthy patients for invasive procedures.

Q: What is the ROC curve?
A: The receiver operating characteristic curve plots the true positive rate against the false positive rate as the classification threshold varies from zero to one. It visualizes the tradeoff between sensitivity and specificity and enables threshold selection appropriate for the application's tolerance for each error type.

Q: What is AUC-ROC?
A: The area under the ROC curve, or AUC, summarizes classifier performance across all possible thresholds as a single number between zero and one. An AUC of 0.5 indicates random performance, while 1.0 is perfect. It is threshold-independent and useful for comparing classifiers on imbalanced data.

Q: What is the precision-recall curve?
A: The precision-recall curve plots precision against recall as the classification threshold varies, providing an informative view of classifier performance for imbalanced datasets where the positive class is rare. Average precision, the area under this curve, summarizes classifier quality for detection tasks.

Q: What is average precision?
A: Average precision computes the area under the precision-recall curve by integrating precision over recall values. It summarizes classifier performance across all thresholds into a single score, heavily used in object detection and information retrieval where the distribution of positives and negatives is very unequal.

Q: What is mean average precision?
A: Mean average precision averages the average precision computed separately for each class or query in a multi-class or retrieval setting. It provides a single performance summary across all categories, used widely in object detection evaluation on benchmarks like COCO and PASCAL VOC.

Q: What is regression evaluation?
A: Regression models are evaluated with metrics including mean squared error, which penalizes large errors heavily; mean absolute error, which treats all errors linearly; root mean squared error, in the same units as the target; and R-squared, which measures the fraction of variance explained by the model.

Q: What is R-squared?
A: R-squared, or the coefficient of determination, measures the proportion of variance in the target variable explained by the model, ranging from negative infinity to one. A value of one indicates perfect prediction, zero indicates the model is no better than predicting the mean, and negative values indicate worse.

Q: What is mean absolute error?
A: Mean absolute error computes the average absolute difference between predictions and true values. Unlike MSE, it is robust to outliers because large errors are not squared, providing a more interpretable measure of average prediction error in the same units as the target variable.

Q: What is root mean squared error?
A: Root mean squared error is the square root of mean squared error, expressing prediction error in the same units as the target variable. It penalizes large errors more than MAE due to squaring before averaging, making it sensitive to outliers but more mathematically tractable for optimization.

Q: What is Huber loss?
A: Huber loss behaves like mean squared error for small errors and mean absolute error for large errors, providing robustness to outliers while maintaining smooth gradients. The transition point, delta, is a hyperparameter controlling how large an error must be to switch from quadratic to linear regime.

Q: What is model calibration?
A: Model calibration measures whether predicted probabilities match empirical frequencies. A perfectly calibrated model predicting 80 percent confidence should be correct 80 percent of the time. Calibration is assessed using reliability diagrams and summary statistics like expected calibration error.

Q: What is expected calibration error?
A: Expected calibration error measures the average difference between model confidence and actual accuracy across bins of predicted probabilities. Well-calibrated models have low ECE, important for applications where decision-making depends on accurate uncertainty quantification rather than just classification accuracy.

Q: What is Platt scaling?
A: Platt scaling trains a logistic regression model on the raw model outputs to calibrate probabilities. By fitting a sigmoid function to the model's scores using a held-out validation set, it can correct systematic over- or under-confidence in the original model's probability estimates.

Q: What is isotonic regression for calibration?
A: Isotonic regression is a nonparametric method for probability calibration that fits a monotonically increasing step function to the validation set's reliability data. It is more flexible than Platt scaling and can correct non-monotonic miscalibration patterns but requires more calibration data.

Q: What is statistical significance testing?
A: Statistical significance testing assesses whether observed performance differences between models are likely due to random variation or reflect true systematic differences. Methods like paired bootstrap resampling and McNemar's test compare model outputs to determine if observed improvements are reliable.

Q: What is cross-validation for evaluation?
A: Cross-validation estimates model generalization by splitting data into k folds, training on k minus one folds and evaluating on the held-out fold, repeating for all folds. The average performance across folds provides a less biased and lower variance estimate than a single train-test split.

Q: What is stratified cross-validation?
A: Stratified cross-validation ensures each fold contains approximately the same proportion of examples from each class as the full dataset. This is particularly important for imbalanced classification problems where random splitting could produce folds with very different class distributions.

Q: What is leave-one-out cross-validation?
A: Leave-one-out cross-validation uses a single example as the validation set, training on all remaining examples, and repeats this for every example. It provides an almost unbiased estimate of generalization but is computationally expensive for large datasets.

Q: What is holdout evaluation?
A: Holdout evaluation splits the dataset into a training set used for model development and a test set used only once for final evaluation. The test set must never influence model development to provide an unbiased estimate, and performance on it represents expected generalization to new data.

Q: What is error analysis?
A: Error analysis systematically examines examples on which a model makes mistakes to understand failure patterns, bias toward certain types of examples, and opportunities for improvement. It guides data collection, feature engineering, and model design more efficiently than global metrics alone.

Q: What is a baseline model?
A: A baseline model is a simple reference model against which more sophisticated approaches are compared. Common baselines include majority class classifiers, mean predictors, rule-based systems, and pretrained models without task-specific fine-tuning. A model that cannot beat a baseline needs more development.

Q: What is a leaderboard in machine learning?
A: A leaderboard ranks competing models by their performance on a held-out evaluation set for a benchmark task. Public leaderboards motivate progress but can lead to overfitting through repeated submission and test set exploitation, which is why the best benchmarks maintain private test sets.

Q: What is benchmark contamination?
A: Benchmark contamination occurs when test set examples or their paraphrases appear in a model's training data, artificially inflating benchmark performance. It is a growing concern for large language models trained on internet data and makes reported benchmark numbers unreliable indicators of true generalization.

Q: What is behavioral testing?
A: Behavioral testing, or CheckList, evaluates NLP models by specifying expected behaviors using structured test cases covering minimum functionality, invariance to paraphrasing, and directional expectations. It identifies systematic failures not revealed by aggregate accuracy on standard evaluation sets.

Q: What is human evaluation in NLP?
A: Human evaluation assesses model outputs using human judges rating quality dimensions such as fluency, coherence, factual accuracy, and helpfulness. While more reliable and nuanced than automatic metrics for generative tasks, it is expensive, slow, and subject to inter-annotator disagreement.

Q: What is inter-annotator agreement?
A: Inter-annotator agreement measures consistency between human annotators labeling the same data, quantified with Cohen's kappa or Fleiss' kappa for categorical data. Low agreement indicates ambiguous annotation guidelines or inherently subjective tasks, and high agreement is required for reliable ground truth labels.

Q: What is A/B testing in ML?
A: A/B testing compares two model versions by randomly assigning users to each and measuring downstream outcome metrics like click-through rate, conversion, or user satisfaction. It provides causal evidence of improvement in the actual deployment environment rather than offline proxy metrics.

Q: What is offline versus online evaluation?
A: Offline evaluation assesses model performance on static held-out datasets before deployment, using proxy metrics. Online evaluation measures real-world impact metrics after deployment through A/B tests or shadow mode testing. Offline metrics often imperfectly predict online improvements due to distribution shift.

Q: What is Goodhart's law in machine learning?
A: Goodhart's law states that when a measure becomes a target, it ceases to be a good measure. In machine learning, optimizing a proxy metric too aggressively can improve the metric while degrading the true objective, as seen when language models optimize for high BLEU scores while generating poor translations.

Q: What is semantic similarity evaluation?
A: Semantic similarity evaluation measures how well model outputs capture the intended meaning rather than surface form, using embedding-based cosine similarity scores. Models like BERTScore and Sentence-BERT produce embeddings that correlate with human similarity judgments better than n-gram overlap metrics.

Q: What is MLOps?
A: MLOps, or Machine Learning Operations, applies DevOps principles to the machine learning lifecycle, covering data versioning, experiment tracking, model training pipelines, deployment, monitoring, and retraining. It enables reliable, reproducible, and scalable production machine learning systems.

Q: What is model deployment?
A: Model deployment is the process of making a trained machine learning model available to serve predictions in a production environment. It involves containerizing the model, setting up a serving infrastructure, managing compute resources, and establishing monitoring to detect performance issues.

Q: What is a model serving infrastructure?
A: Model serving infrastructure accepts prediction requests, loads the model, performs inference, and returns results. Key considerations include latency, throughput, scalability, versioning, and reliability. Common solutions include REST APIs, gRPC services, and specialized serving systems like TensorFlow Serving.

Q: What is model versioning?
A: Model versioning tracks different versions of trained models along with their associated code, data, hyperparameters, and performance metrics. It enables rollback to previous versions if a new deployment regresses, systematic comparison of model generations, and reproducibility of experiments.

Q: What is a model registry?
A: A model registry is a centralized repository for storing, versioning, and managing trained machine learning models along with their metadata, lineage, and lifecycle status. It facilitates collaboration, enables auditing, and provides a governed path from experiment to production.

Q: What is an experiment tracking system?
A: An experiment tracking system records hyperparameters, metrics, artifacts, and code versions for every training run, enabling systematic comparison of experiments. Tools like MLflow and Weights and Biases log rich metadata that allows practitioners to understand what changes produced improvements.

Q: What is feature store?
A: A feature store is a centralized repository for storing, computing, and serving machine learning features. It ensures consistency between features used during training and inference, enables feature reuse across models, and handles point-in-time correct feature retrieval to prevent data leakage.

Q: What is data versioning?
A: Data versioning tracks changes to datasets over time, enabling reproducibility of model training runs and understanding how data changes affect model behavior. Tools like DVC integrate with Git to version large dataset files and provide checksum-based tracking of data lineage.

Q: What is a training pipeline?
A: A training pipeline is an automated workflow that orchestrates data ingestion, preprocessing, model training, evaluation, and artifact logging in a reproducible manner. Pipelines codify the training process, reduce manual errors, and enable systematic retraining when new data becomes available.

Q: What is continuous integration for ML?
A: Continuous integration for ML automatically runs tests, data validation, model training, and evaluation checks whenever code changes are committed. It catches regressions early, ensures training scripts remain functional, and validates that new model versions meet minimum performance thresholds.

Q: What is continuous deployment for ML?
A: Continuous deployment for ML automatically promotes model versions that pass evaluation criteria to production, integrating deployment into the development workflow. It reduces the manual overhead of deployment and enables rapid iteration while enforcing quality gates that prevent regressed models from reaching users.

Q: What is canary deployment?
A: Canary deployment gradually routes a small fraction of production traffic to a new model version while maintaining the existing version for the majority. It allows monitoring the new model's behavior and metrics in production before full rollout, reducing risk of widespread issues.

Q: What is shadow mode deployment?
A: Shadow mode runs a new model alongside the production model, routing all traffic to both but only returning responses from the production model to users. The shadow model's predictions and performance are logged for analysis without impacting users, enabling safe evaluation in the production environment.

Q: What is model monitoring?
A: Model monitoring continuously tracks a deployed model's performance metrics, input data distributions, and output distributions in production. It alerts when metrics degrade beyond thresholds, enabling rapid detection of data drift, model drift, or infrastructure issues that require intervention.

Q: What is input data drift?
A: Input data drift occurs when the statistical distribution of features provided to a deployed model shifts away from the distribution it was trained on. Detection methods include statistical tests like KS test and PSI applied to feature distributions, comparison of current versus training data moments.

Q: What is prediction drift?
A: Prediction drift occurs when the distribution of model outputs changes over time without a corresponding change in inputs, possibly indicating model degradation or distributional shift. Monitoring the histogram or moments of predicted probabilities and alerting on significant deviation enables timely intervention.

Q: What is label drift?
A: Label drift occurs when the distribution of ground truth labels in production shifts from the training distribution, reducing model performance even if inputs have not changed. It often indicates a change in the real-world concept being modeled and may require retraining on recent labeled data.

Q: What is the population stability index?
A: Population Stability Index measures the degree of shift between a reference distribution and a current distribution of a single feature, widely used in credit scoring and financial monitoring. PSI values below 0.1 indicate minimal shift, between 0.1 and 0.25 indicate moderate shift, and above 0.25 require action.

Q: What is model retraining?
A: Model retraining periodically updates a deployed model on new data to counteract performance degradation from distribution drift. Retraining strategies include scheduled periodic retraining, trigger-based retraining when performance drops below thresholds, and continuous training on streaming data.

Q: What is online learning in deployment?
A: Online learning in deployment continuously updates model parameters as new labeled examples arrive in production, allowing the model to adapt to distribution shifts without requiring full retraining. It requires careful management of learning rates and catastrophic forgetting of previously learned patterns.

Q: What is model latency?
A: Model latency is the time from receiving a prediction request to returning a response. Low latency is critical for user-facing applications. It is reduced through model quantization, caching, batching, hardware acceleration, model distillation, and efficient serving frameworks.

Q: What is model throughput?
A: Model throughput measures the number of prediction requests a model serving system can process per unit time. Maximizing throughput requires efficient batching, hardware utilization, and parallelism while maintaining acceptable latency, balancing the competing demands of responsiveness and efficiency.

Q: What is inference optimization?
A: Inference optimization reduces the computational cost and latency of producing model predictions. Techniques include model quantization, pruning, kernel fusion, operator optimization, batching dynamic requests, caching intermediate computations, and using hardware-specific accelerators.

Q: What is ONNX?
A: ONNX, Open Neural Network Exchange, is an open format for representing machine learning models that enables interoperability between frameworks. Models trained in PyTorch or TensorFlow can be exported to ONNX and deployed efficiently across diverse runtimes and hardware backends.

Q: What is TensorRT?
A: TensorRT is NVIDIA's inference optimization SDK that compiles neural networks into optimized execution plans for GPU deployment. It performs layer fusion, precision calibration, kernel auto-tuning, and graph optimization to maximize throughput and minimize latency on NVIDIA hardware.

Q: What is a serving framework?
A: A model serving framework manages the deployment and serving of machine learning models at scale, handling request routing, batching, model versioning, health checking, and autoscaling. Examples include TensorFlow Serving, TorchServe, Triton Inference Server, and Ray Serve.

Q: What is dynamic batching?
A: Dynamic batching collects multiple concurrent prediction requests and processes them together as a batch, improving GPU utilization and throughput. Requests that arrive within a short time window or until a maximum batch size is reached are grouped, trading small additional latency for significantly higher throughput.

Q: What is a microservice for ML?
A: A microservice for ML packages a single model or preprocessing step as an independently deployable service with a well-defined API. Microservice architectures allow individual components to be scaled, updated, and debugged independently, improving system reliability and development agility.

Q: What is containerization for ML?
A: Containerization packages a model, its dependencies, and runtime environment into a portable container using Docker. Containers ensure reproducibility across development and production environments, simplify dependency management, and integrate with orchestration platforms like Kubernetes.

Q: What is Kubernetes for ML?
A: Kubernetes orchestrates containerized ML workloads, automatically managing deployment, scaling, load balancing, and self-healing of model serving pods. It enables reliable scaling to handle variable prediction request volumes and simplifies management of complex multi-model serving infrastructure.

Q: What is a data pipeline?
A: A data pipeline automates the collection, cleaning, transformation, validation, and routing of data through a series of processing steps. In ML systems, reliable data pipelines ensure models receive consistently formatted, high-quality inputs during both training and inference.

Q: What is Airflow in MLOps?
A: Apache Airflow is a workflow orchestration tool that schedules and monitors directed acyclic graph pipelines. In ML systems, it orchestrates data preprocessing, model training, evaluation, and deployment tasks with dependencies, retries, and alerting, ensuring automated and reliable pipeline execution.

Q: What is Kubeflow?
A: Kubeflow is a machine learning toolkit for Kubernetes that provides components for distributed training, hyperparameter tuning, pipeline orchestration, model serving, and experiment tracking. It enables running scalable ML workloads on Kubernetes clusters with integrated tooling for the full ML lifecycle.

Q: What is data quality monitoring?
A: Data quality monitoring validates that production data meets expected schemas, statistical properties, and completeness requirements before it reaches a model. It detects missing values, unexpected categories, out-of-range features, and format errors that would cause incorrect predictions.

Q: What is an ML platform?
A: An ML platform is an integrated system that provides tools and infrastructure for data management, experiment tracking, model training, deployment, and monitoring. Examples include Google Vertex AI, AWS SageMaker, and Azure ML Studio, abstracting infrastructure complexity from ML practitioners.

Q: What is model governance?
A: Model governance encompasses the policies, processes, and controls that ensure machine learning models are developed, validated, deployed, and monitored responsibly. It includes documentation, approval workflows, audit trails, performance monitoring, and ethical impact assessments.

Q: What is responsible AI?
A: Responsible AI is a framework for developing and deploying AI systems that are transparent, accountable, fair, reliable, private, and beneficial. It involves technical practices like bias testing and interpretability tools alongside organizational governance processes and stakeholder engagement.

Q: What is a prediction confidence score?
A: A prediction confidence score is the probability estimate associated with a model's predicted class or output, indicating how certain the model is about its prediction. Well-calibrated confidence scores are essential for risk-sensitive applications and for deciding when to defer to humans.

Q: What is uncertainty quantification?
A: Uncertainty quantification measures and communicates the confidence in model predictions, distinguishing aleatoric uncertainty from irreducible data noise and epistemic uncertainty from model ignorance. Methods include Bayesian approaches, ensembles, Monte Carlo dropout, and conformal prediction.

Q: What is conformal prediction?
A: Conformal prediction provides statistically guaranteed prediction sets that contain the true label with at least a specified probability, without assumptions about the data distribution. It wraps any model to produce valid uncertainty estimates by calibrating coverage on a held-out calibration set.

Q: What is model documentation?
A: Model documentation provides comprehensive information about a model's intended uses, limitations, performance metrics across subgroups, training data, evaluation methodology, and ethical considerations. Standardized formats like model cards enable transparent communication between developers and users.

Q: What is a model card?
A: A model card is a short document that describes a machine learning model's purpose, performance characteristics, intended use cases, limitations, and potential risks. Introduced by Google, model cards promote transparency and help users make informed decisions about whether a model is appropriate for their use case.

Q: What is A/B testing for model deployment?
A: A/B testing for model deployment routes different fractions of production traffic to a baseline model and a candidate model, measuring impact on business metrics. Statistical analysis determines whether observed differences exceed the noise threshold, providing causal evidence that the new model improves outcomes.

Q: What is rollback in model deployment?
A: Rollback reverts a production model to a previous version when a newly deployed model causes performance degradation, errors, or unexpected behavior. Automated rollback triggered by monitoring alerts minimizes user impact from problematic deployments, requiring good model versioning and deployment tooling.

Q: What is a model ensemble in deployment?
A: Deploying a model ensemble combines predictions from multiple models, improving robustness and accuracy at the cost of increased latency and compute. Ensemble members can run in parallel, and the serving infrastructure must aggregate predictions efficiently to meet latency requirements.

Q: What is edge deployment?
A: Edge deployment runs machine learning inference on devices close to where data is generated, such as smartphones, cameras, or IoT sensors, rather than sending data to cloud servers. It reduces latency, preserves privacy, and enables offline operation but requires highly compressed, efficient models.

Q: What is concept drift?
A: Concept drift is a change in the statistical relationship between input features and the target variable over time, causing a model's predictions to become less accurate. It can occur suddenly, gradually, or recurrently, requiring detection and adaptation mechanisms to maintain model performance.

Q: What is covariate shift?
A: Covariate shift is a specific type of distribution shift where the marginal distribution of input features changes between training and deployment, but the conditional distribution of labels given inputs remains the same. It can be corrected using importance weighting techniques.

Q: What is prior probability shift?
A: Prior probability shift occurs when the marginal distribution of class labels changes between training and test time while the conditional distribution of features given class remains the same. It affects classifiers whose decisions depend implicitly on class frequencies in training data.

Q: What is dataset shift?
A: Dataset shift is a broad term encompassing any mismatch between the joint distribution of inputs and outputs at training time and at test time. It is a fundamental challenge in deploying machine learning models to production environments that differ from training conditions.

Q: What are types of concept drift?
A: Concept drift types include sudden drift where distributions change abruptly, gradual drift where the change happens slowly over time, incremental drift involving small steady changes, recurring drift where past distributions reappear, and blip drift representing temporary transient deviations.

Q: What is drift detection method?
A: The Drift Detection Method monitors classification error rates over time and signals drift when the error rate increases significantly beyond a learned baseline. It uses statistical control charts to distinguish random variation from genuine concept drift requiring model retraining.

Q: What is the ADWIN algorithm?
A: ADWIN, Adaptive Windowing, maintains a variable-length window of the data stream and detects drift by testing whether the statistics in any two sub-windows are significantly different. When drift is detected, the old portion of the window is dropped, adapting the model to the current distribution.

Q: What is a statistical process control chart in drift detection?
A: Statistical process control charts monitor whether a process is in control by plotting measurements over time and flagging points that fall outside control limits derived from baseline statistics. In model monitoring, they detect when prediction error rates or feature statistics deviate significantly from expected values.

Q: What is population stability index for drift?
A: Population Stability Index compares the distribution of a feature or prediction between a reference period and a current period, summing the signed differences in proportions weighted by their logarithm ratio. PSI is widely used in financial services to detect meaningful distributional shifts in credit models.

Q: What is the Kolmogorov-Smirnov test for drift?
A: The Kolmogorov-Smirnov test measures the maximum difference between the empirical cumulative distribution functions of two samples, determining whether they could have been drawn from the same distribution. It detects distributional changes in continuous features without requiring assumptions about the distribution shape.

Q: What is the chi-squared test for drift?
A: The chi-squared test compares the observed frequency distribution of a categorical feature to an expected reference distribution, detecting significant deviations. In model monitoring, it identifies when the distribution of categorical input features has shifted away from the training distribution.

Q: What is Maximum Mean Discrepancy?
A: Maximum Mean Discrepancy measures the distance between two distributions by comparing the means of their embeddings in a reproducing kernel Hilbert space. It provides a non-parametric, kernel-based test for distribution shift that can detect multivariate drift in feature spaces.

Q: What is domain drift in language models?
A: Domain drift in language models occurs when the language patterns, vocabulary, or topics of production queries shift away from the training distribution, degrading generation quality and semantic alignment. It is detected by measuring embedding-space distance between current and reference query distributions.

Q: What is semantic drift score?
A: Semantic drift score quantifies how far model responses have drifted from the intended domain by computing cosine similarity between response embeddings and reference embeddings representing the target domain. Consistently low scores across many turns indicate the model is generating off-topic or misaligned responses.

Q: What is embedding-based drift detection?
A: Embedding-based drift detection encodes both training data and production data into a shared embedding space using a pretrained encoder, then computes statistical distances between the resulting embedding distributions. It can detect semantic shifts not captured by simple feature statistics.

Q: What is label-free drift detection?
A: Label-free drift detection identifies distribution shift using only model inputs and outputs without requiring ground truth labels, which are often unavailable quickly in production. Methods include monitoring prediction confidence distributions, feature statistics, and embedding distances as proxy indicators of drift.

Q: What is a drift detection threshold?
A: A drift detection threshold defines the level of measured change that triggers an alert indicating significant drift. Thresholds must be calibrated to balance sensitivity to genuine drift against false alarm rates from natural data variability, often using historical data to estimate expected variation.

Q: What is retraining trigger?
A: A retraining trigger is a condition that initiates model retraining, such as a drift detection alert, a drop in monitored accuracy below a threshold, a scheduled time interval, or an accumulation of sufficient new labeled examples. Automated retraining triggers reduce the manual overhead of production model maintenance.

Q: What is continuous monitoring?
A: Continuous monitoring tracks model performance, data quality, and system health metrics in real time throughout a model's production lifetime. Dashboards, alerts, and automated responses enable rapid detection and remediation of issues before they significantly impact users or business outcomes.

Q: What is a monitoring dashboard?
A: A monitoring dashboard visualizes model and data metrics over time, providing an at-a-glance view of system health. Key panels include prediction distribution histograms, feature distribution comparisons, error rate trends, latency percentiles, and drift detection alerts, enabling rapid diagnosis of production issues.

Q: What is ground truth delayed feedback?
A: Ground truth delayed feedback refers to the lag between model predictions and the availability of true labels for evaluating prediction accuracy. In applications like credit scoring, labels may not be available for months. This delay complicates monitoring and requires proxy indicators of model health.

Q: What is a reference dataset for monitoring?
A: A reference dataset used in model monitoring represents the expected distribution of inputs and predictions, typically sampled from the training or validation data. Production distributions are compared against this reference to identify deviations that indicate potential drift or data quality issues.

Q: What is window-based drift detection?
A: Window-based drift detection compares statistics computed over a recent window of production data against a reference period, detecting shifts as the window updates. Sliding windows provide continuous monitoring while tumbling windows compare non-overlapping fixed intervals, each offering different sensitivity and latency characteristics.

Q: What is alerting in MLOps?
A: Alerting in MLOps sends notifications to appropriate teams when monitored metrics exceed defined thresholds, enabling rapid response to model degradation, data quality issues, or infrastructure problems. Effective alerts include sufficient context for diagnosis and are calibrated to minimize false alarms.

Q: What is an SLA in ML systems?
A: A Service Level Agreement for ML systems defines expected performance characteristics including prediction latency percentiles, uptime availability, throughput capacity, and model accuracy floors. SLAs set contractual obligations and guide infrastructure investment and monitoring alert thresholds.

Q: What is feedback loop in ML systems?
A: A feedback loop occurs when model predictions influence the data used to train future models, potentially amplifying biases or creating self-fulfilling prophecies. For example, recommender systems can trap users in filter bubbles if training data is collected only from model-influenced behavior.

Q: What is data freshness?
A: Data freshness refers to how recently the training data used to build a model was collected relative to the current deployment period. Stale training data from months or years ago may poorly represent current patterns, requiring regular retraining to maintain model relevance and accuracy.

Q: What is feature importance in monitoring?
A: Feature importance monitoring tracks which features have the greatest influence on model predictions over time. Shifts in relative feature importance can indicate distributional changes in the underlying data or changes in the relationships between features and the target variable.

Q: What is anomaly detection in monitoring?
A: Anomaly detection in model monitoring identifies unusual patterns in input data, model predictions, or system metrics that deviate significantly from historical norms. It provides an unsupervised signal for data quality issues, model degradation, and infrastructure problems before labeled feedback is available.

Q: What is responsible model monitoring?
A: Responsible model monitoring extends standard performance tracking to include fairness metrics, ensuring that model accuracy remains equitable across demographic subgroups over time. Disparate impact may emerge or worsen after deployment as data distributions shift differently across subpopulations.

Q: What is AI fairness?
A: AI fairness ensures machine learning models treat individuals and groups equitably, not systematically disadvantaging protected characteristics like race, gender, or age. It involves defining appropriate fairness criteria, measuring disparities, and applying technical and organizational interventions to reduce bias.

Q: What is demographic parity?
A: Demographic parity requires that the proportion of positive predictions be equal across demographic groups. For example, a hiring model satisfies demographic parity if it recommends equal proportions of candidates from each gender group, regardless of the group's true qualification rates.

Q: What is equalized odds?
A: Equalized odds requires both equal true positive rates and equal false positive rates across demographic groups. It ensures the model makes the same quality of decisions for all groups, treating false negatives and false positives as equally costly regardless of group membership.

Q: What is individual fairness?
A: Individual fairness requires that similar individuals receive similar predictions, formalizing the intuition that treating like cases alike is fair. It requires defining a meaningful task-specific similarity metric between individuals, which is often difficult to specify in practice.

Q: What is counterfactual fairness?
A: Counterfactual fairness requires that a prediction would remain the same in a counterfactual world where an individual's protected attribute was different but everything causally unrelated to that attribute remained the same. It is grounded in causal inference and accounts for systemic relationships between attributes.

Q: What is disparate impact?
A: Disparate impact occurs when a neutral-seeming model or practice has a disproportionately negative effect on a protected group without business justification. Legal standards often require that the selection rate for any group not fall below 80 percent of the rate for the highest selected group.

Q: What is algorithmic bias?
A: Algorithmic bias is the systematic unfairness in model outputs arising from biased training data, problem formulation, or optimization objectives. It can perpetuate or amplify existing societal biases, causing discriminatory outcomes in consequential decisions like hiring, lending, and criminal justice.

Q: What is representation bias in data?
A: Representation bias occurs when certain groups or perspectives are underrepresented in training data relative to their presence in the population. Models trained on such data may perform poorly for underrepresented groups and encode implicit assumptions that disadvantage them.

Q: What is measurement bias?
A: Measurement bias occurs when the proxy variable used as a label in training systematically measures the true concept differently across groups. For example, using arrest rates as a proxy for criminal behavior reflects discriminatory policing patterns rather than actual behavior differences.

Q: What is historical bias?
A: Historical bias arises when training data reflects past human decisions that were themselves biased, causing models to perpetuate and amplify these historical inequities. Even with accurate data collection, optimizing for historical decisions can encode discrimination into automated systems.

Q: What is aggregation bias?
A: Aggregation bias occurs when a model treats different subgroups as a single homogeneous group, applying the same function across groups with different underlying distributions. This can produce accurate average performance while being systematically wrong for specific subgroups.

Q: What is evaluation bias?
A: Evaluation bias occurs when benchmark datasets do not represent all subgroups adequately, causing reported performance metrics to mask disparate accuracy across groups. A model may achieve high overall accuracy while performing poorly on underrepresented groups that are invisible in standard benchmarks.

Q: What is interpretability in machine learning?
A: Interpretability is the degree to which a model's predictions and decisions can be understood by humans. It encompasses global interpretability of the overall model behavior and local interpretability of individual predictions, enabling trust, debugging, regulatory compliance, and identification of failure modes.

Q: What is explainability in AI?
A: Explainability provides human-comprehensible explanations for AI system decisions, enabling users to understand why a particular prediction was made. Explanations can take the form of feature attributions, counterfactuals, natural language rationales, or visualization of relevant input regions.

Q: What is SHAP?
A: SHAP, SHapley Additive exPlanations, uses game theory to attribute the contribution of each feature to a model's prediction relative to a baseline. SHAP values satisfy desirable properties like consistency and local accuracy, providing both local instance-level and global model-level explanations.

Q: What is LIME?
A: LIME, Local Interpretable Model-agnostic Explanations, explains individual predictions by training a simple interpretable surrogate model on perturbed versions of the input, weighted by their proximity to the original example. The surrogate model's coefficients reveal which features drove the prediction locally.

Q: What is a saliency map?
A: A saliency map visualizes which input regions or features had the greatest influence on a neural network's output by computing or approximating the gradient of the output with respect to the input. It provides visual explanations for image classification decisions that highlight which pixels were most important.

Q: What is gradient-weighted class activation mapping?
A: Grad-CAM produces visual explanations for CNN decisions by computing the gradient of the class score with respect to the final convolutional feature maps and weighting the maps by these gradients. The resulting heatmap highlights the image regions most relevant to the predicted class.

Q: What is attention visualization?
A: Attention visualization displays the attention weights in a transformer model as a heatmap showing which tokens or positions attend strongly to which others. While suggestive of explanations, attention weights do not always faithfully represent which inputs causally influenced the output.

Q: What is a surrogate model?
A: A surrogate model is a simpler, interpretable model such as a decision tree or linear model trained to approximate the predictions of a complex black-box model. Analyzing the surrogate provides global insights into the black-box model's behavior while sacrificing some fidelity.

Q: What is rule extraction?
A: Rule extraction derives human-readable if-then rules from trained neural networks or other complex models, summarizing the model's decision logic in a form that can be inspected, validated, and explained. Rules sacrifice some predictive accuracy for interpretability and regulatory compliance.

Q: What is a black-box model?
A: A black-box model makes predictions through a complex, opaque process that is difficult for humans to interpret directly, such as deep neural networks or large ensembles. Black-box models often achieve higher accuracy than interpretable models, creating a tension between performance and explainability.

Q: What is a white-box model?
A: A white-box model has a transparent structure that is directly interpretable by humans, such as linear regression, decision trees, or rule lists. The decision process can be traced and understood step by step, enabling trust, debugging, and explanation without additional post-hoc tools.

Q: What is model transparency?
A: Model transparency means the mechanisms and computations underlying a model's predictions can be examined and understood by practitioners and stakeholders. It is distinct from explainability in that transparency refers to the inherent openness of the model rather than post-hoc explanations of a black box.

Q: What is counterfactual explanation?
A: A counterfactual explanation describes the minimal change to an input that would have altered the model's prediction, such as what would have needed to change for a loan application to be approved. Counterfactuals help users understand actionable changes and provide recourse in high-stakes decisions.

Q: What is algorithmic recourse?
A: Algorithmic recourse identifies actionable steps an individual can take to change an unfavorable model prediction, respecting constraints on which features can realistically be changed. It is a fairness concern ensuring that people denied beneficial outcomes have a meaningful path to obtain them.

Q: What is privacy in machine learning?
A: Privacy in machine learning ensures that models do not reveal sensitive information about training data individuals and that predictions cannot be used to infer private attributes. Privacy techniques include differential privacy, federated learning, and secure multi-party computation.

Q: What is differential privacy?
A: Differential privacy provides a mathematical guarantee that the inclusion or exclusion of any individual's data in the training set has a negligible effect on the model's outputs. It is implemented by adding carefully calibrated noise to gradients or data, controlled by the privacy budget epsilon.

Q: What is membership inference attack?
A: A membership inference attack determines whether a specific example was included in a model's training set by analyzing the model's outputs. Models that overfit tend to be more vulnerable because they have higher confidence on training examples than unseen data.

Q: What is model inversion attack?
A: A model inversion attack reconstructs sensitive training examples from a model's predictions, extracting private information from the training data. An attacker can, for example, reconstruct recognizable face images from a facial recognition model by optimizing inputs that maximize class probabilities.

Q: What is data minimization?
A: Data minimization is the principle that only the minimum data necessary for the intended purpose should be collected, stored, and processed. Applying it to machine learning reduces privacy risks, attack surfaces, and regulatory exposure while potentially improving model fairness by removing sensitive attributes.

Q: What is the right to explanation?
A: The right to explanation, enshrined in regulations like the GDPR, gives individuals the right to receive meaningful explanations of automated decisions that significantly affect them. It drives demand for interpretable models and post-hoc explanation methods in regulated industries like finance and healthcare.

Q: What is AI regulation?
A: AI regulation establishes legal requirements for how AI systems can be developed and deployed, addressing fairness, transparency, safety, and accountability. Examples include the EU AI Act which categorizes AI systems by risk level and imposes requirements for high-risk systems like facial recognition and credit scoring.

Q: What is the EU AI Act?
A: The EU AI Act is a comprehensive regulatory framework classifying AI systems by risk level. Unacceptable risk systems like social scoring are prohibited. High-risk systems like credit scoring and hiring tools face requirements for transparency, human oversight, and conformity assessment before deployment.

Q: What is model documentation for fairness?
A: Model documentation for fairness records the intended use cases, training data demographics, disaggregated performance metrics across subgroups, known limitations, and mitigation strategies for identified biases. Datasheets for datasets and model cards are standardized formats for this documentation.

Q: What is bias mitigation?
A: Bias mitigation techniques reduce unfair disparities in model outcomes across demographic groups. Pre-processing methods modify training data, in-processing methods add fairness constraints during training, and post-processing methods adjust predictions for deployed models. No single technique is universally optimal.

Q: What is reweighting for fairness?
A: Reweighting assigns different importance weights to training examples to balance the influence of different demographic groups, compensating for underrepresentation or historical bias. It is a pre-processing fairness intervention that requires no changes to the model architecture or training algorithm.

Q: What is adversarial debiasing?
A: Adversarial debiasing simultaneously trains a classifier to predict the target label and an adversary to predict the protected attribute from the classifier's representations. The classifier is trained to fool the adversary while maintaining accuracy, learning representations that are not predictive of the protected attribute.

Q: What is calibrated fairness?
A: Calibrated fairness requires that predicted probabilities match empirical outcomes equally well across demographic groups. A credit model satisfies calibrated fairness if a predicted default probability of 30 percent corresponds to actual default rates of 30 percent for all demographic groups.

Q: What is the impossibility theorem of fairness?
A: The impossibility theorem of fairness proves that except in degenerate cases, it is mathematically impossible for a classifier to simultaneously satisfy demographic parity, equalized odds, and calibration. Practitioners must choose which fairness criteria to prioritize based on context and values.

Q: What is ethical AI?
A: Ethical AI encompasses principles and practices for developing AI systems that are beneficial, fair, transparent, accountable, and safe. It includes technical methods for fairness and interpretability alongside organizational governance, stakeholder engagement, and ongoing monitoring of real-world impacts.

Q: What is a generative model?
A: A generative model learns the joint probability distribution over inputs and outputs, enabling it to generate new samples from the learned distribution. Examples include GANs, VAEs, diffusion models, and autoregressive language models, with applications in image synthesis, text generation, and data augmentation.

Q: What is a discriminative model?
A: A discriminative model learns the conditional probability of outputs given inputs, directly modeling the decision boundary between classes rather than the full data distribution. Discriminative models like logistic regression and neural classifiers are typically more data efficient for prediction tasks than generative models.

Q: What is image synthesis?
