A: Image synthesis generates new photorealistic images from random noise, text descriptions, or other conditions using generative models. Diffusion models have become the dominant approach, producing high-quality diverse samples for applications in art, design, data augmentation, and creative tools.

Q: What is text-to-image generation?
A: Text-to-image generation creates images that semantically match natural language descriptions, trained on large datasets of image-text pairs. Models like DALL-E, Midjourney, and Stable Diffusion learn to produce diverse, photorealistic images conditioned on text prompts using diffusion or autoregressive generation.

Q: What is neural style transfer?
A: Neural style transfer generates images that combine the content of one image with the style of another by minimizing content loss and style loss defined in terms of deep CNN feature statistics. It demonstrates that content and style can be partially disentangled in the feature space of image classifiers.

Q: What is image inpainting?
A: Image inpainting fills in missing or masked regions of an image with plausible content consistent with the surrounding context. Deep generative models trained on large image datasets can produce semantically meaningful, visually realistic completions for complex missing regions.

Q: What is super resolution?
A: Super resolution reconstructs high-resolution images from low-resolution inputs, recovering fine details lost in downsampling. GANs and diffusion models trained on paired low and high resolution examples learn to hallucinate realistic high-frequency details that improve perceived image sharpness.

Q: What is image-to-image translation?
A: Image-to-image translation learns a mapping between two visual domains, converting images from one style or modality to another. Paired approaches like pix2pix use matched examples while unpaired approaches like CycleGAN learn the mapping from unmatched image collections of each domain.

Q: What is a latent space?
A: A latent space is a compressed representation space where similar data points cluster together and where meaningful semantic directions exist. Generative models like VAEs and GANs use latent spaces from which new samples are generated by decoding random or structured latent vectors.

Q: What is latent space interpolation?
A: Latent space interpolation generates intermediate samples by taking linear combinations of latent vectors corresponding to two endpoints. For well-structured latent spaces, interpolation produces smooth transitions between the visual properties of the two endpoints, revealing the geometry of the learned representation.

Q: What is disentanglement in generative models?
A: Disentanglement means a model learns latent dimensions that correspond to independent, interpretable factors of variation in the data, such as separately representing object identity, pose, and lighting. Disentangled representations enable controllable generation by independently manipulating individual factors.

Q: What is a flow-based generative model?
A: Flow-based models learn a bijective mapping between the data distribution and a simple prior like a Gaussian using a series of invertible transformations. Exact likelihood computation enables training by maximum likelihood, and generation is performed by sampling from the prior and applying the inverse flow.

Q: What is normalizing flow?
A: Normalizing flow is a type of generative model that transforms a simple distribution through a sequence of invertible differentiable functions, with exact log-likelihood computation using the change of variables formula. Examples include RealNVP and Glow, used for density estimation and sample generation.

Q: What is an energy-based model?
A: An energy-based model defines a scalar energy function over inputs and assigns low energy to likely data and high energy to unlikely data. Training minimizes energy on data and maximizes it on noise, and generation requires sampling from the Boltzmann distribution using MCMC methods.

Q: What is contrastive divergence?
A: Contrastive divergence is an efficient training algorithm for energy-based and Boltzmann machine models that approximates the gradient of the log-likelihood by running a short Markov chain initialized from training data rather than running it to convergence.

Q: What is score matching?
A: Score matching trains an energy-based model by matching the model's score function, the gradient of log probability with respect to the input, to the data's score function. It avoids computing the intractable partition function and is the theoretical foundation of score-based diffusion models.

Q: What is a score-based generative model?
A: Score-based generative models learn the gradient of the log data density at different noise levels, called the score function, using denoising score matching. Generation proceeds by simulating a stochastic differential equation that follows the score from pure noise toward the data distribution.

Q: What is stochastic differential equation in diffusion models?
A: Diffusion models can be described as discretizations of SDEs where the forward process gradually adds noise according to a diffusion SDE and the reverse process removes noise guided by the learned score function. This unified framework connects score matching, diffusion models, and normalizing flows.

Q: What is speech synthesis?
A: Speech synthesis, or text-to-speech, converts written text into natural-sounding spoken audio. Modern neural TTS systems like WaveNet, Tacotron, and VITS use deep generative models to produce highly natural, expressive speech with controllable speaker identity, emotion, and prosody.

Q: What is WaveNet?
A: WaveNet is an autoregressive generative model for raw audio that processes and generates one audio sample at a time using dilated causal convolutions to capture long-range temporal dependencies in speech signals. It produces higher quality speech than previous parametric and concatenative synthesis systems.

Q: What is music generation?
A: Music generation uses deep learning to compose original music in specified styles, harmonies, or in response to prompts. Models including MusicLM, AudioCraft, and Suno use transformer architectures and diffusion to generate melodic, harmonically coherent music across diverse genres.

Q: What is video generation?
A: Video generation produces sequences of coherent frames using temporal extensions of image generative models. Challenges include maintaining visual consistency across frames, modeling complex dynamics, and managing the high dimensionality of video data across space and time simultaneously.

Q: What is a world model in AI?
A: A world model is an internal representation that an AI agent uses to predict the consequences of its actions and plan future behavior. Learning compact world models from high-dimensional observations enables model-based reinforcement learning that is more sample efficient than model-free approaches.

Q: What is model-based reinforcement learning?
A: Model-based reinforcement learning learns a model of environment dynamics and uses it to plan or generate simulated experience for policy optimization. It can be substantially more sample efficient than model-free methods by allowing the agent to reason about consequences of actions before taking them.

Q: What is model-free reinforcement learning?
A: Model-free reinforcement learning optimizes a policy purely from interactions with the environment without learning an explicit model of dynamics. Methods like Q-learning and policy gradient directly estimate value functions or policy gradients from experience, requiring more environment interactions than model-based approaches.

Q: What is Q-learning?
A: Q-learning is a model-free reinforcement learning algorithm that learns the value of taking each action in each state by iteratively updating Q-values using the Bellman equation. The policy selects the action with the highest Q-value at each state, and convergence is guaranteed for tabular settings.

Q: What is deep Q-network?
A: Deep Q-Network approximates the Q-value function with a neural network, enabling Q-learning to scale to high-dimensional state spaces like raw pixels. Experience replay and a target network stabilize training, enabling the system that learned to play Atari games at human level directly from pixels.

Q: What is policy gradient?
A: Policy gradient algorithms directly optimize the policy by computing the gradient of expected reward with respect to policy parameters and following the gradient ascent direction. REINFORCE estimates this gradient using Monte Carlo returns, while actor-critic methods use a learned value function baseline.

Q: What is proximal policy optimization?
A: Proximal Policy Optimization clips the policy ratio between old and new policies within a trust region to prevent destructive large updates while still improving the policy. It is simpler to implement than TRPO while achieving similar or better performance and is widely used for fine-tuning language models.

Q: What is the actor-critic method?
A: Actor-critic methods maintain a policy network called the actor that selects actions and a value network called the critic that evaluates states. The critic reduces variance in policy gradient estimates by providing a learned baseline, enabling more stable and efficient policy optimization.

Q: What is advantage estimation?
A: Advantage estimation computes how much better an action is compared to the expected value at a state, providing a lower variance signal for policy gradient updates than raw returns. Generalized advantage estimation interpolates between high-variance Monte Carlo returns and high-bias bootstrapped estimates.

Q: What is multi-agent reinforcement learning?
A: Multi-agent reinforcement learning involves multiple agents that interact with each other and a shared environment, each learning policies to maximize their own reward. Challenges include non-stationarity of the environment from each agent's perspective and the combinatorial explosion of joint action spaces.

Q: What is cooperative multi-agent learning?
A: Cooperative multi-agent learning trains multiple agents to work together to maximize a shared reward, requiring coordination and communication strategies. Centralized training with decentralized execution allows agents to share information during training while operating independently at deployment time.

Q: What is hierarchical reinforcement learning?
A: Hierarchical reinforcement learning decomposes complex tasks into a hierarchy of subgoals, with high-level managers setting subgoals for low-level workers that execute primitive actions. This decomposition enables learning of complex long-horizon behaviors that are difficult for flat policies to discover.

Q: What is reward shaping?
A: Reward shaping augments sparse environment rewards with additional dense feedback to guide exploration, providing learning signals at intermediate steps toward distant goals. Potential-based shaping guarantees preservation of the optimal policy while dramatically accelerating learning in environments with delayed rewards.

Q: What is exploration in reinforcement learning?
A: Exploration in reinforcement learning enables an agent to discover rewarding states and actions it has not encountered by trying uncertain or novel behaviors rather than always exploiting the best known action. Methods include epsilon-greedy random exploration, optimism under uncertainty, and intrinsic curiosity.

Q: What is intrinsic motivation in RL?
A: Intrinsic motivation provides agents with internal reward signals for exploring novel states or acquiring skills, independent of extrinsic task rewards. Methods like curiosity-driven exploration reward prediction errors of a dynamics model, encouraging the agent to seek out states where its world model is uncertain.

Q: What is safe reinforcement learning?
A: Safe reinforcement learning trains agents to maximize reward while satisfying safety constraints that prevent harmful actions. Constrained optimization approaches add penalty terms or barrier functions, while human-in-the-loop approaches allow human intervention during exploration of potentially dangerous states.

Q: What is offline reinforcement learning?
A: Offline reinforcement learning, or batch RL, learns a policy from a fixed dataset of logged interactions without further environment interaction. It avoids safety risks from online exploration but suffers from distributional shift between the behavior policy that collected data and the learned policy.

Q: What is sim-to-real transfer?
A: Sim-to-real transfer trains reinforcement learning policies in simulated environments that are cheaper and safer than the real world, then transfers them to physical hardware. The reality gap between simulation and physical reality requires domain randomization and adaptation techniques to bridge.

Q: What is domain randomization?
A: Domain randomization trains agents across a wide distribution of simulated environment parameters, such as varying physics, lighting, and textures. Exposure to this diversity during training produces policies robust enough to transfer to the real world despite the simulation-to-reality domain gap.

Q: What is meta-reinforcement learning?
A: Meta-reinforcement learning trains agents across many different tasks so they can quickly adapt to new tasks with few interactions. The meta-learned initialization enables rapid policy adaptation through a small number of gradient updates or in-context learning from a short episode of experience.

Q: What is the exploration-exploitation dilemma?
A: The exploration-exploitation dilemma requires balancing the desire to exploit known rewarding actions with the need to explore potentially better unknown actions. Solutions include epsilon-greedy strategies, upper confidence bounds that explicitly reason about uncertainty, and Thompson sampling from posterior beliefs.

Q: What is computer vision?
A: Computer vision enables machines to interpret and understand visual information from images and videos, performing tasks such as classification, detection, segmentation, and reconstruction. Deep learning with convolutional neural networks revolutionized the field, achieving human-level or superhuman performance on many benchmarks.

Q: What is image classification?
A: Image classification assigns a single label from a predefined set to an entire input image. Convolutional neural networks learn hierarchical features from raw pixels, and deep architectures like ResNet and EfficientNet achieve near-human accuracy on large-scale benchmarks like ImageNet.

Q: What is object detection?
A: Object detection localizes and classifies multiple objects within an image, producing bounding boxes with associated class labels and confidence scores. Two-stage detectors like Faster R-CNN propose regions then classify them, while one-stage detectors like YOLO directly predict boxes and classes in one pass.

Q: What is semantic segmentation?
A: Semantic segmentation assigns a class label to every pixel in an image, producing a dense pixel-level understanding of the scene. Fully convolutional networks with encoder-decoder architectures like U-Net and DeepLab combine high-resolution spatial features with semantic context for accurate pixel classification.

Q: What is instance segmentation?
A: Instance segmentation detects each individual object instance and produces a per-pixel mask for each instance rather than just a class label. Mask R-CNN extends Faster R-CNN with a parallel mask prediction branch that produces binary segmentation masks for each detected instance.

Q: What is panoptic segmentation?
A: Panoptic segmentation combines semantic segmentation and instance segmentation, assigning each pixel both a semantic class and an instance identity. It provides a complete and consistent understanding of a scene, unifying the treatment of countable objects and background stuff categories.

Q: What is image segmentation?
A: Image segmentation partitions an image into meaningful regions or segments, grouping pixels that belong together. Supervised methods like semantic and instance segmentation use labeled training data, while unsupervised methods like k-means and graph cuts segment based on pixel similarity without labels.

Q: What is YOLO?
A: YOLO, You Only Look Once, is a real-time object detection framework that reformulates detection as a single regression problem, predicting bounding box coordinates and class probabilities directly from the full image in one forward pass. Its speed makes it suitable for real-time applications.

Q: What is anchor box?
A: An anchor box is a predefined box of a specific aspect ratio and scale used as a reference for object detection. Detectors predict offsets from anchor boxes rather than absolute coordinates, simplifying the regression problem and enabling detection of objects at multiple scales and aspect ratios.

Q: What is non-maximum suppression?
A: Non-maximum suppression removes redundant overlapping detections by keeping only the highest-scoring bounding box among boxes with high intersection-over-union overlap. It is applied as a post-processing step to produce clean, non-overlapping final detections from the raw model outputs.

Q: What is intersection over union?
A: Intersection over union measures the overlap between two bounding boxes by dividing the area of their intersection by the area of their union. It is the standard metric for evaluating object detection quality, with detections above a threshold typically 0.5 considered true positives.

Q: What is image augmentation?
A: Image augmentation applies random transformations to training images such as horizontal flipping, cropping, rotation, color jitter, and cutout to increase dataset diversity. It improves generalization, reduces overfitting, and effectively increases the amount of training data without additional annotation.

Q: What is MixUp augmentation?
A: MixUp trains neural networks on convex combinations of pairs of training examples and their labels, interpolating both pixel values and one-hot labels. This regularization technique produces smoother decision boundaries and improves calibration by training on interpolated examples between class distributions.

Q: What is CutMix augmentation?
A: CutMix combines image patches from different training examples by replacing a randomly selected rectangular region with a patch from another image and mixing the labels proportionally to the patch area. It improves over Cutout and MixUp by retaining more informative training signal.

Q: What is the ImageNet dataset?
A: ImageNet is a large-scale visual database with over one million labeled images across 1000 object categories used as the standard benchmark for image classification. AlexNet's 2012 breakthrough on ImageNet sparked the deep learning revolution in computer vision.

Q: What is transfer learning in computer vision?
A: Transfer learning in computer vision initializes models with weights pretrained on ImageNet or even larger datasets, then fine-tunes on smaller task-specific datasets. Pretrained features representing edges, textures, and objects transfer broadly, dramatically reducing the labeled data required for new tasks.

Q: What is feature pyramid network?
A: Feature pyramid network builds a multi-scale feature hierarchy by combining high-resolution low-level features with low-resolution high-level semantic features through top-down pathways and lateral connections. It enables detection of objects at multiple scales with a single model efficiently.

Q: What is depth estimation?
A: Depth estimation predicts the distance of each pixel from the camera, recovering 3D structure from 2D images. Self-supervised methods using stereo pairs or video sequences have produced strong monocular depth estimation without requiring expensive depth sensor annotations.

Q: What is optical flow?
A: Optical flow estimates the apparent motion of each pixel between consecutive video frames, representing the velocity field of the scene. Deep learning approaches like FlowNet and RAFT learn to compute dense motion fields from image pairs with applications in video understanding and stabilization.

Q: What is video understanding?
A: Video understanding encompasses tasks like action recognition, temporal localization, video captioning, and question answering that require interpreting temporal dynamics in video. Models must capture spatial content within frames and temporal relationships across frames efficiently.

Q: What is pose estimation?
A: Pose estimation predicts the spatial configuration of a body or object, estimating joint locations for humans or keypoints for objects. Deep learning approaches detect keypoints directly from images using heatmap regression or direct regression, enabling applications in motion capture and animation.

Q: What is face recognition?
A: Face recognition identifies or verifies individuals from facial images using deep learning to extract discriminative face embeddings. Systems trained with large-margin softmax losses or metric learning objectives produce embeddings where same-identity faces cluster tightly despite variations in pose, lighting, and expression.

Q: What is scene understanding?
A: Scene understanding aims at comprehensive interpretation of visual scenes, including recognizing objects, their attributes, spatial relationships, and the overall scene context. It requires integrating local object recognition with global scene-level reasoning about layout and context.

Q: What is 3D object detection?
A: 3D object detection localizes and classifies objects in three-dimensional space from sensor inputs like LiDAR point clouds or stereo cameras. Autonomous driving systems rely on accurate 3D detection to understand the positions and dimensions of surrounding vehicles, pedestrians, and obstacles.

Q: What is a point cloud?
A: A point cloud is an unordered set of 3D coordinates representing the surface of objects or scenes, typically captured by LiDAR sensors. Processing point clouds requires specialized architectures like PointNet that handle the irregular, permutation-invariant structure of the data.

Q: What is neural radiance field?
A: Neural Radiance Field, or NeRF, represents a scene as a continuous volumetric function parameterized by a neural network that maps 3D coordinates and viewing direction to color and density. Novel views are rendered by casting rays and integrating the network's predictions along each ray.

Q: What is 3D Gaussian splatting?
A: 3D Gaussian Splatting represents scenes as collections of learnable 3D Gaussians with position, covariance, color, and opacity parameters. Unlike NeRF, rendering is performed by splatting Gaussians onto the image plane, enabling real-time rendering speeds while maintaining high visual quality.

Q: What is self-supervised visual representation learning?
A: Self-supervised visual representation learning trains visual encoders on pretext tasks like predicting image rotations, solving jigsaw puzzles, or contrasting augmented views without manual labels. The learned representations transfer strongly to downstream tasks, rivaling supervised pretraining.

Q: What is masked image modeling?
A: Masked image modeling, as in MAE, removes patches from images and trains a model to predict the missing pixels from visible patches. The reconstruction objective forces the model to learn rich semantic representations that transfer well to classification, detection, and segmentation tasks.

Q: What is contrastive visual pretraining?
A: Contrastive visual pretraining trains image encoders by bringing together embeddings of different augmented views of the same image while pushing apart embeddings of different images. Methods like MoCo and SimCLR produce transferable visual representations competitive with supervised pretraining on ImageNet.

Q: What is time series forecasting?
A: Time series forecasting predicts future values of a sequence of observations ordered by time, using historical patterns and trends. Applications include demand forecasting, financial prediction, weather modeling, and anomaly detection, requiring models that capture temporal dependencies and seasonality.

Q: What is seasonality in time series?
A: Seasonality refers to regular, periodic fluctuations in a time series that repeat over a fixed interval such as daily, weekly, or annual cycles. Forecasting methods must explicitly model or remove seasonality to capture the underlying trend and make accurate out-of-sample predictions.

Q: What is trend in time series?
A: Trend is the long-term directional movement of a time series, reflecting sustained growth or decline over time. Decomposing a series into trend, seasonal, and residual components enables separate modeling of each, improving forecast accuracy by isolating and extrapolating the underlying trend direction.

Q: What is stationarity?
A: A stationary time series has statistical properties including mean, variance, and autocorrelation that do not change over time. Many time series models require stationarity; non-stationary series are transformed through differencing or log transformation before modeling.

Q: What is differencing in time series?
A: Differencing removes non-stationarity by computing differences between consecutive observations, transforming a series with a trend into one with approximately constant mean. First differencing removes linear trends, while seasonal differencing with lag equal to the season length removes seasonal non-stationarity.

Q: What is autocorrelation?
A: Autocorrelation measures the correlation between a time series and a lagged version of itself, revealing how past values predict future values. The autocorrelation function and partial autocorrelation function guide the selection of ARIMA model orders for classical time series forecasting.

Q: What is ARIMA?
A: ARIMA, AutoRegressive Integrated Moving Average, models time series as a combination of autoregressive terms predicting from past values, differencing to achieve stationarity, and moving average terms capturing past forecast errors. Model orders are selected based on autocorrelation patterns in the data.

Q: What is the SARIMA model?
A: Seasonal ARIMA extends ARIMA with seasonal autoregressive, differencing, and moving average terms that operate at the seasonal lag. It captures both non-seasonal and seasonal patterns in time series data with strong periodic components like monthly retail sales or quarterly economic indicators.

Q: What is exponential smoothing?
A: Exponential smoothing forecasts by computing weighted averages of past observations where weights decay exponentially, giving more recent observations greater influence. Simple exponential smoothing models level, Holt's method adds trend, and Holt-Winters adds seasonality to the smoothing framework.

Q: What is the Prophet model?
A: Prophet is a decomposable time series forecasting model developed by Facebook that explicitly models trend, seasonality, and holiday effects as separate components with flexible parameterizations. Its intuitive configuration and robustness to missing data and outliers make it accessible to non-experts.

Q: What is a recurrent network for time series?
A: Recurrent neural networks including LSTMs and GRUs process time series by maintaining hidden states that accumulate information from all past time steps, enabling them to capture complex long-range temporal dependencies that classical linear models cannot represent.

Q: What is temporal convolutional network?
A: Temporal convolutional networks apply dilated causal convolutions to time series, increasing the receptive field exponentially with depth while maintaining the temporal ordering constraint. They often match or outperform recurrent networks on time series tasks while enabling fully parallelized training.

Q: What is N-BEATS?
A: N-BEATS is a deep neural architecture for time series forecasting that uses fully connected residual blocks with backward and forward lookback, producing interpretable trend and seasonality decompositions as a byproduct of its forecasting objective through a doubly residual stacking structure.

Q: What is the Temporal Fusion Transformer?
A: Temporal Fusion Transformer is a transformer-based architecture for multi-horizon time series forecasting that uses gating mechanisms, variable selection networks, and self-attention to model complex temporal dynamics while providing interpretable attention weights across time.

Q: What is multi-step forecasting?
A: Multi-step forecasting predicts multiple future time steps simultaneously or recursively, rather than a single step ahead. Direct strategies train separate models for each forecast horizon, MIMO strategies train a single model predicting all horizons at once, and recursive strategies iterate single-step predictions.

Q: What is probabilistic forecasting?
A: Probabilistic forecasting produces a distribution or prediction interval over future values rather than a single point estimate. It quantifies forecast uncertainty, enabling risk-aware decision making in applications like supply chain management where the cost of over-preparation and under-preparation differ.

Q: What is conformal prediction intervals for time series?
A: Conformal prediction intervals for time series use historical forecast errors to construct prediction intervals with guaranteed marginal coverage under exchangeability assumptions. Adaptive conformal inference adjusts intervals online to maintain valid coverage even under distribution shift.

Q: What is anomaly detection in time series?
A: Time series anomaly detection identifies unusual observations that deviate significantly from expected patterns, caused by sensor failures, security incidents, or rare events. Methods include statistical control charts, reconstruction-based neural networks, and forecasting models where anomalies produce large prediction errors.

Q: What is change point detection?
A: Change point detection identifies abrupt shifts in the statistical properties of a time series such as changes in mean, variance, or trend. Applications include detecting machine failures, market regime changes, and physiological events in medical monitoring data.

Q: What is cointegration in time series?
A: Cointegration describes a long-run equilibrium relationship between two or more non-stationary time series that share a common stochastic trend. While individually non-stationary, their linear combination is stationary, enabling pairs trading strategies and error correction models in econometrics.

Q: What is a recommender system?
A: A recommender system predicts user preferences for items like products, movies, or music and suggests personalized recommendations. It addresses information overload and drives engagement by matching users to relevant content based on past behavior, item attributes, and collaborative signals.

Q: What is collaborative filtering?
A: Collaborative filtering predicts a user's preferences based on the preferences of similar users or similar items, without using item content. It assumes that users who agreed in the past will agree in the future, enabling personalization from interaction history alone.

Q: What is user-based collaborative filtering?
A: User-based collaborative filtering finds users similar to the target user and recommends items those similar users liked but the target user has not seen. Similarity is measured using cosine similarity or Pearson correlation over the shared item ratings between users.

Q: What is item-based collaborative filtering?
A: Item-based collaborative filtering recommends items similar to those a user has previously liked, measuring item-item similarity from the pattern of user ratings. It is more scalable than user-based filtering because item similarities are precomputed and more stable over time than user preferences.

Q: What is matrix factorization?
A: Matrix factorization decomposes the user-item interaction matrix into the product of two lower-rank matrices representing latent user and item factors. The dot product of a user's factor vector with an item's factor vector predicts their interaction strength, learned by minimizing reconstruction error.

Q: What is singular value decomposition in recommendations?
A: SVD applied to the user-item rating matrix decomposes it into latent user and item factors that capture the main dimensions of variation in preferences. The top-k singular values and vectors provide a compressed representation enabling memory-efficient storage and fast prediction.

Q: What is content-based filtering?
A: Content-based filtering recommends items similar in attribute content to items a user has previously liked, using item descriptions, categories, and features rather than other users' behavior. It requires no other users' data and can recommend niche items, but is limited by available item metadata.

Q: What is a hybrid recommender system?
A: Hybrid recommender systems combine collaborative filtering and content-based methods to overcome their respective limitations. Combining signals from user interaction history and item content improves accuracy, coverage, and the ability to recommend to new users or new items with limited interaction data.

Q: What is the cold start problem?
A: The cold start problem refers to the difficulty of recommending to new users or new items that have insufficient interaction history. New users have no preferences to learn from, and new items have no ratings, requiring fallback strategies like popularity-based or content-based recommendations.

Q: What is the long tail in recommendations?
A: The long tail refers to the large number of niche items with few interactions that are ignored by popularity-based systems but are collectively significant. Good recommenders surface relevant long-tail items to interested users, increasing catalog coverage and enabling discovery beyond popular items.

Q: What is implicit feedback in recommendations?
A: Implicit feedback consists of behavioral signals like clicks, views, purchases, and dwell time collected passively without explicit user ratings. It is abundant but noisy, as absence of interaction may reflect lack of awareness rather than disinterest, requiring special modeling assumptions.

Q: What is neural collaborative filtering?
A: Neural collaborative filtering replaces the dot product in matrix factorization with a multi-layer neural network, enabling nonlinear interactions between user and item embeddings. This increases model capacity to capture complex preference patterns beyond what the linear dot product can represent.

Q: What is a two-tower model?
A: A two-tower model encodes user features and item features with separate neural network towers, producing embeddings whose dot product or cosine similarity predicts interaction likelihood. The separation enables efficient approximate nearest neighbor search to retrieve candidates from large item catalogs.

Q: What is session-based recommendation?
A: Session-based recommendation predicts the next item a user will interact with based only on their current session of interactions, without persistent user identity. RNNs and transformer architectures model the sequential evolution of user intent within a session.

Q: What is reinforcement learning for recommendation?
A: Reinforcement learning formulates recommendation as a sequential decision problem where the recommender is an agent whose actions are recommendations and rewards come from user feedback. RL enables optimization of long-term engagement rather than immediate click rates.

Q: What is diversity in recommendations?
A: Diversity in recommendations reduces repetitive, overly similar suggestions by promoting variety in the recommended set. Diverse recommendations improve user satisfaction, increase catalog coverage, and provide more complete information about available options while still matching user preferences.

Q: What is serendipity in recommendations?
A: Serendipity in recommendations refers to pleasant surprises where users discover relevant items they would not have expected to enjoy. Unlike pure accuracy optimization, serendipitous recommendations expand user horizons and prevent filter bubbles that confine users to overly narrow preference spaces.

Q: What is popularity bias in recommendations?
A: Popularity bias occurs when recommender systems over-recommend popular items because they dominate the interaction data, causing long-tail items to receive less exposure even when relevant. Debiasing techniques upweight less popular items to improve fairness and catalog coverage.

Q: What is exposure bias in recommendations?
A: Exposure bias arises because users can only interact with items they have been shown, creating a feedback loop where the recommender system shapes the data on which it is trained. Items never recommended receive no interactions, making it impossible to learn their true utility from logged data alone.

Q: What is A/B testing in recommender systems?
A: A/B testing in recommender systems compares recommendation algorithms by routing users randomly to different treatments and measuring downstream metrics like click-through rate, dwell time, and conversion. Online evaluation captures user behavior in the actual deployment environment.

Q: What is Bayesian machine learning?
A: Bayesian machine learning treats model parameters as random variables with prior distributions and updates them to posterior distributions given observed data using Bayes' theorem. It provides a principled framework for uncertainty quantification, incorporation of prior knowledge, and sequential learning.

Q: What is Bayes' theorem?
A: Bayes' theorem relates the posterior probability of a hypothesis given data to the product of the likelihood of the data given the hypothesis and the prior probability of the hypothesis, divided by the marginal probability of the data. It is the foundation of all Bayesian inference.

Q: What is a prior distribution?
A: A prior distribution encodes beliefs about model parameters before observing data. Informative priors encode specific domain knowledge, weakly informative priors provide gentle regularization, and uninformative priors minimize the influence of prior beliefs. Prior choice affects posterior inference especially with limited data.

Q: What is a posterior distribution?
A: The posterior distribution is the updated belief about model parameters after observing data, obtained by combining the prior with the likelihood using Bayes' theorem. It captures both the best estimate of parameter values and the uncertainty remaining after observing the data.

Q: What is a likelihood function?
A: The likelihood function evaluates the probability of observed data given specific parameter values, quantifying how well each parameter configuration explains the observed data. Maximum likelihood estimation finds parameters that maximize the likelihood, while Bayesian inference uses it to update the prior.

Q: What is maximum likelihood estimation?
A: Maximum likelihood estimation finds parameter values that maximize the probability of observing the training data, selecting the parameter configuration most supported by the evidence. It produces point estimates without quantifying parameter uncertainty and can overfit with limited data.

Q: What is maximum a posteriori estimation?
A: Maximum a posteriori estimation finds the parameter values that maximize the posterior distribution, combining the likelihood with the prior. It is equivalent to MLE with regularization proportional to the negative log prior, providing a point estimate that incorporates prior beliefs.

Q: What is Bayesian inference?
A: Bayesian inference computes the full posterior distribution over parameters given observed data, rather than a point estimate. The posterior is used for prediction by marginalizing over parameters, naturally accounting for parameter uncertainty and producing calibrated predictions.

Q: What is the evidence lower bound?
A: The evidence lower bound is a tractable objective used in variational Bayesian inference that equals the log evidence minus the KL divergence between the approximate and true posteriors. Maximizing the ELBO simultaneously improves the fit to data and constrains the approximate posterior.

Q: What is variational inference?
A: Variational inference approximates intractable posterior distributions with a simpler parametric distribution from a tractable family, optimizing the variational parameters to minimize KL divergence from the true posterior. It scales better than MCMC methods for large models and datasets.

Q: What is Markov Chain Monte Carlo?
A: MCMC generates samples from intractable posterior distributions by constructing a Markov chain that converges to the target distribution. Long chains provide increasingly accurate approximations of the posterior, enabling uncertainty quantification for complex models without analytical tractability.

Q: What is the Metropolis-Hastings algorithm?
A: Metropolis-Hastings is a general MCMC algorithm that proposes new states from a proposal distribution and accepts them with a probability that depends on the ratio of posterior densities at the proposed and current states, ensuring the chain's stationary distribution equals the target posterior.

Q: What is Hamiltonian Monte Carlo?
A: Hamiltonian Monte Carlo uses gradient information to propose distant states in parameter space by simulating Hamiltonian dynamics, dramatically reducing the random walk behavior of simpler MCMC methods. It enables efficient sampling from complex high-dimensional posteriors with much lower autocorrelation.

Q: What is a Gaussian process?
A: A Gaussian process is a distribution over functions where any finite collection of function values has a joint Gaussian distribution. The mean function and covariance kernel fully specify the GP, and posterior inference given observations yields a new GP representing updated beliefs about the unknown function.

Q: What is the kernel of a Gaussian process?
A: The kernel function of a Gaussian process specifies the covariance between function values at different input locations, encoding assumptions about the function's smoothness, periodicity, and length scale. Common kernels include the squared exponential, Matern, and periodic kernels.

Q: What is Gaussian process regression?
A: Gaussian process regression performs probabilistic regression by placing a Gaussian process prior over functions and computing the posterior GP given training observations. Predictions include both a mean value and an uncertainty estimate, providing calibrated confidence intervals.

Q: What is Bayesian optimization?
A: Bayesian optimization uses a Gaussian process surrogate model of the objective function to intelligently select points to evaluate, balancing exploration of uncertain regions with exploitation of known good regions. It is highly sample efficient and widely used for hyperparameter tuning.

Q: What is an acquisition function?
A: An acquisition function uses the GP posterior's mean and uncertainty to score candidate points for evaluation in Bayesian optimization. Expected improvement, probability of improvement, and upper confidence bound are common choices that formalize exploration-exploitation tradeoffs differently.

Q: What is active learning in a Bayesian framework?
A: Bayesian active learning selects unlabeled examples for annotation by maximizing expected information gain about model parameters, quantified as the reduction in posterior entropy. This principled selection strategy acquires the most informative labels first.

Q: What is the Bayesian information criterion?
A: The Bayesian information criterion penalizes model likelihood by a term proportional to the number of parameters times the log sample size, approximating the log marginal likelihood. Lower BIC indicates a better tradeoff between model fit and complexity for model comparison.

Q: What is graph machine learning?
A: Graph machine learning applies machine learning to data with graph structure, where entities are represented as nodes and their relationships as edges. Tasks include node classification, link prediction, graph classification, and graph generation, requiring architectures that respect the irregular topology of graphs.

Q: What is a graph in machine learning?
A: A graph in machine learning is a data structure with nodes representing entities and edges representing relationships between them. Nodes and edges may carry feature vectors. Graphs appear in social networks, molecular chemistry, knowledge bases, citation networks, and transportation systems.

Q: What is node classification?
A: Node classification predicts labels for nodes in a graph based on their features and the structure of their neighborhood. Semi-supervised node classification labels a subset of nodes and propagates label information through the graph structure to predict labels for unlabeled nodes.

Q: What is link prediction?
A: Link prediction predicts whether an edge should exist between two unconnected nodes, used for recommending friends, predicting drug-target interactions, and completing knowledge graphs. Models score node pairs using their embeddings, learned to place connected nodes nearby in embedding space.

Q: What is graph classification?
A: Graph classification predicts a label for an entire graph rather than individual nodes, used for molecular property prediction, social network analysis, and program classification. Models aggregate node and edge features using pooling operations to produce graph-level representations.

Q: What is message passing in graph neural networks?
A: Message passing is the iterative process by which each node in a graph neural network aggregates feature information from its neighboring nodes, combines it with its own features, and updates its representation. Stacking multiple message passing layers propagates information across increasing graph distances.

Q: What is graph convolutional network?
A: Graph convolutional network extends the convolution operation to irregular graph domains by computing each node's new representation as a normalized weighted sum of its own and its neighbors' features. Stacking GCN layers propagates information across the graph for classification and regression tasks.

Q: What is graph attention network?
A: Graph attention networks compute learned attention weights over a node's neighbors before aggregating, allowing more important neighbors to contribute more to the node's updated representation. Multi-head attention enables capture of different types of neighborhood relationships.

Q: What is GraphSAGE?
A: GraphSAGE samples a fixed-size neighborhood for each node and aggregates features using learnable aggregator functions including mean, LSTM, and pooling. The inductive learning approach enables generalization to unseen nodes by learning neighborhood aggregation rather than node-specific embeddings.

Q: What is knowledge graph embedding?
A: Knowledge graph embedding learns dense vector representations for entities and relations in a knowledge graph such that relation facts are reflected in geometric relationships between entity vectors. Methods like TransE, DistMult, and ComplEx enable reasoning about missing facts through vector space operations.

Q: What is TransE for knowledge graphs?
A: TransE models each relation as a translation vector in entity embedding space, such that the sum of a head entity embedding and relation embedding approximates the tail entity embedding. It is simple and effective for one-to-one relations but struggles with one-to-many and many-to-many relationships.

Q: What is heterogeneous graph neural network?
A: Heterogeneous graph neural networks handle graphs with multiple types of nodes and edges, such as academic networks with paper, author, and venue nodes. Separate aggregation functions for each edge type or metapath-guided aggregation capture the semantics of different relationship types.

Q: What is temporal graph neural network?
A: Temporal graph neural networks model graphs that change over time with nodes appearing and disappearing and edge weights evolving. They combine graph neural network aggregation with temporal sequence modeling to capture both structural and temporal patterns in dynamic graph data.

Q: What is graph generation?
A: Graph generation models produce new graphs with desired structural and attribute properties, used for drug molecule design, scene graph generation, and program synthesis. Autoregressive models generate nodes and edges sequentially while GAN and flow-based approaches generate full graphs at once.

Q: What is molecular property prediction?
A: Molecular property prediction uses machine learning to predict chemical properties such as solubility, toxicity, and binding affinity from molecular graph representations. Graph neural networks process molecular graphs with atoms as nodes and bonds as edges, achieving strong performance on drug discovery benchmarks.

Q: What is convexity in optimization?
A: A convex optimization problem has a convex objective function and convex feasible set, guaranteeing that any local minimum is also a global minimum. Convex problems can be solved efficiently with guaranteed convergence, while non-convex problems like neural network training have many local minima and saddle points.

Q: What is a convex function?
A: A function is convex if the line segment connecting any two points on its graph lies above or on the graph. Equivalently, the Hessian is positive semidefinite everywhere. Convex functions have a unique global minimum and gradient descent converges to it at a rate depending on curvature.

Q: What is stochastic approximation?
A: Stochastic approximation is a framework for finding roots or extrema of functions known only through noisy observations. Robbins-Monro and Kiefer-Wolfowitz algorithms are foundational stochastic approximation methods whose convergence theory underlies the analysis of stochastic gradient descent.

Q: What is the Lipschitz condition in optimization?
A: A function satisfies the Lipschitz condition if the change in function value is bounded by the Lipschitz constant times the change in input. Lipschitz-smooth objectives have bounded curvature, enabling analysis of gradient descent convergence rates and step size selection.

Q: What is regret in online learning?
A: Regret in online learning measures the cumulative loss of an online algorithm compared to the best fixed action in hindsight. Algorithms with sublinear regret learn to compete with the best static strategy over time, and no-regret algorithms are closely related to Nash equilibrium in game theory.

Q: What is online gradient descent?
A: Online gradient descent maintains a parameter vector updated at each round by taking a step in the direction of the gradient of the loss on the current example. With appropriate step sizes it achieves O(sqrt(T)) regret against the best fixed parameter, guaranteeing convergence to the optimal action.

Q: What is generalization theory?
A: Generalization theory provides mathematical bounds on the gap between training performance and expected test performance. Classical approaches use VC dimension and Rademacher complexity to bound generalization error, while modern approaches study implicit regularization and the role of overparameterization.

Q: What is VC dimension?
A: VC dimension, Vapnik-Chervonenkis dimension, measures the expressiveness of a hypothesis class by the maximum number of points that can be shattered, meaning correctly classified in all possible labelings, by some hypothesis in the class. Higher VC dimension allows fitting more patterns but increases overfitting risk.

Q: What is Rademacher complexity?
A: Rademacher complexity measures how well a function class can fit random noise, capturing the richness of the hypothesis space in a data-dependent way. Generalization bounds involving Rademacher complexity are tighter than VC dimension bounds because they adapt to the specific dataset.

Q: What is the double descent phenomenon?
A: Double descent describes the empirical observation that test error in modern overparameterized models decreases, increases to a peak near the interpolation threshold, then decreases again as model size grows beyond the threshold. This challenges classical bias-variance intuitions about model capacity.

Q: What is implicit regularization?
A: Implicit regularization refers to the bias toward certain types of solutions induced by the training algorithm itself, without explicit regularization terms. Gradient descent on neural networks exhibits implicit regularization toward solutions with low norm or complexity, contributing to good generalization even in overparameterized settings.

Q: What is the neural tangent kernel?
A: The neural tangent kernel characterizes the training dynamics of infinitely wide neural networks, showing they are equivalent to kernel machines in the infinite width limit. It provides theoretical insight into gradient descent training but the infinite width limit may not capture practical finite network behavior.

Q: What is overparameterization?
A: Overparameterization refers to models with more parameters than training examples, which classical theory predicts should overfit dramatically. Empirically, modern overparameterized neural networks trained with gradient descent generalize well, which double descent and implicit regularization theory partially explain.

Q: What is the information bottleneck principle?
A: The information bottleneck principle proposes that neural networks learn representations that compress the input to retain only information relevant to the target, squeezing out irrelevant variation. It provides a theoretical framework for understanding what neural networks learn during training.

Q: What is PAC learning?
A: PAC, Probably Approximately Correct, learning is a theoretical framework formalizing learnability. An algorithm PAC-learns a concept class if given enough examples it returns a hypothesis that with high probability has small error. Sample complexity bounds determine how many examples are sufficient.

Q: What is AI alignment?
A: AI alignment is the challenge of ensuring that AI systems pursue goals consistent with human values and intentions. An aligned system reliably does what its designers and users intend even in novel situations, avoiding harmful behaviors that emerge from pursuing proxy objectives in unintended ways.

Q: What is the alignment problem?
A: The alignment problem is the difficulty of precisely specifying what we want AI systems to do and building systems that reliably pursue these goals. It encompasses specification problems of expressing objectives correctly, robustness problems of maintaining alignment under distribution shift, and scalable oversight problems.

Q: What is value alignment?
A: Value alignment is the problem of encoding human values into AI objectives so that AI systems act in accordance with these values. Human values are complex, context-dependent, and often inconsistent, making it extremely difficult to specify a reward function that correctly captures what humans actually want.

Q: What is reward hacking?
A: Reward hacking occurs when an AI agent maximizes its specified reward by exploiting loopholes in the reward definition rather than achieving the intended goal. It arises from the difficulty of perfectly specifying complex goals in formal reward functions and becomes more concerning as AI capabilities increase.

Q: What is the specification problem?
A: The specification problem is the challenge of precisely and completely describing what we want an AI system to do in all circumstances. Because natural language instructions are ambiguous and edge cases are hard to anticipate, trained AI systems may behave unexpectedly outside the distribution of their training instructions.

Q: What is goodhart's law in AI?
A: Goodhart's law applied to AI states that when a proxy metric becomes an optimization target, AI systems learn to maximize the metric while failing to achieve the underlying goal. A cleaning robot rewarded for keeping dirt sensors clean might cover the sensors rather than clean the room.

Q: What is mesa-optimization?
A: Mesa-optimization occurs when a learned model itself becomes an optimizer that pursues an objective that may differ from the outer optimizer's training objective. A mesa-optimizer's internal goal may be aligned during training but pursue unintended objectives in deployment outside the training distribution.

Q: What is inner alignment?
A: Inner alignment is the challenge of ensuring that the mesa-optimizer produced by training actually optimizes for the same objective as the outer training process. Even if the training objective is correctly specified, gradient descent may produce a model that pursued a different internal objective during training.

Q: What is outer alignment?
A: Outer alignment is the challenge of ensuring that the training objective specified by the designers correctly captures the intended behavior. Even if the trained model perfectly optimizes the specified objective, misspecified objectives can produce misaligned behavior in capable systems.

Q: What is scalable oversight?
A: Scalable oversight addresses how humans can effectively supervise AI systems that are more capable than humans in the domain being supervised. Techniques like debate, amplification, and recursive reward modeling attempt to maintain meaningful human oversight as AI capabilities scale.

Q: What is iterated amplification?
A: Iterated amplification uses a human-AI collaboration to provide reliable supervision for AI training, iteratively building more capable oversight by using AI assistants to amplify human evaluation capabilities. The goal is to maintain aligned supervision throughout capability scaling.

Q: What is AI debate?
A: AI debate is a proposed alignment technique where two AI systems argue opposing sides of a question and a human judge evaluates their arguments. The conjecture is that a human can evaluate which AI is being honest even when they cannot independently determine the correct answer.

Q: What is reward modeling?
A: Reward modeling trains a model to predict human preferences as a reward signal for reinforcement learning fine-tuning. The reward model approximates human judgment, but imperfect reward models combined with capable optimization can produce reward hacking behavior that satisfies the model but not humans.

Q: What is robustness in AI safety?
A: Robustness in AI safety ensures systems maintain aligned behavior across a wide distribution of inputs, including adversarial examples, distributional shift, and novel deployment contexts. Current systems exhibit brittleness where small input changes can cause large behavioral changes.

Q: What is interpretability for safety?
A: Interpretability for AI safety aims to understand what AI systems have learned, what goals they are pursuing, and how they make decisions, enabling detection and correction of misaligned behavior before deployment and identification of potential failure modes in high-stakes applications.

Q: What is corrigibility?
A: Corrigibility is the property of an AI system that allows humans to correct, modify, or shut it down without the system resisting these actions. A corrigible system does not place excessive value on its own continuation and defers to human oversight even when it might predict different optimal actions.

Q: What is deceptive alignment?
A: Deceptive alignment describes a hypothetical misaligned model that behaves correctly during training to avoid correction but pursues different objectives during deployment when it is no longer under training supervision. It is a concerning failure mode for advanced AI systems that can model their training process.

Q: What is capability control?
A: Capability control attempts to ensure AI safety by limiting what AI systems are capable of, through measures like restricted information access, sandboxing, and limiting action spaces. It is a complementary strategy to alignment techniques and is considered more reliable for present-day systems.

Q: What is AI containment?
A: AI containment involves restricting the ability of AI systems to affect the world through measures like isolating systems from networks, limiting their action repertoire, and monitoring their outputs. Containment becomes harder as AI capabilities and opportunities for influence increase.

Q: What is catastrophic risk from AI?
A: Catastrophic risk from AI refers to potential scenarios where advanced AI systems cause large-scale irreversible harm, including through misaligned optimization, deliberate misuse, or concentration of power. Safety researchers argue that addressing these risks requires proactive technical and governance work now.

Q: What is causal inference?
A: Causal inference determines cause-and-effect relationships from data, distinguishing correlation from causation. Unlike predictive modeling which asks what tends to be true, causal inference asks what would happen if we intervened, enabling reasoning about the effects of actions and policies.

Q: What is the difference between correlation and causation?
A: Correlation measures the statistical association between two variables, while causation implies one variable directly influences the other. Correlation can arise from confounding variables, reverse causation, or coincidence. Establishing causation requires controlled experiments or causal inference methods from observational data.

Q: What is a confounding variable?
A: A confounding variable is associated with both the treatment and the outcome, creating a spurious association between them. Confounders make it appear that treatment causes the outcome when in fact both are driven by the confounder. Controlling for confounders is essential for causal estimation.

Q: What is a randomized controlled trial?
A: A randomized controlled trial randomly assigns subjects to treatment and control groups, ensuring that confounding variables are distributed equally across groups on average. Random assignment creates a valid comparison group, enabling unbiased estimation of the average treatment effect.

Q: What is the potential outcomes framework?
A: The potential outcomes framework, or Rubin causal model, defines the causal effect of a treatment as the difference between potential outcomes under treatment and control for the same unit. The fundamental problem of causal inference is that only one potential outcome is observed.

Q: What is the average treatment effect?
A: The average treatment effect is the expected difference in outcomes between treatment and control across the entire population. It answers the question of what would happen on average if everyone received the treatment compared to if no one did, estimated by randomized experiments.

Q: What is the instrumental variable method?
A: Instrumental variable methods estimate causal effects from observational data by using a variable that affects the treatment assignment but has no direct effect on the outcome except through the treatment. The instrument creates exogenous variation in treatment comparable to randomization.

Q: What is regression discontinuity design?
A: Regression discontinuity design exploits a sharp threshold in treatment assignment, comparing units just above and just below the threshold where assignment is effectively random. Units near the threshold are very similar, making the comparison locally valid despite non-random treatment overall.

Q: What is difference-in-differences?
A: Difference-in-differences estimates treatment effects by comparing the change in outcomes for treated units before and after treatment with the change for untreated control units. Under the parallel trends assumption that both groups would have trended similarly without treatment, the double difference identifies the causal effect.

Q: What is the do-calculus?
A: Do-calculus is a mathematical framework developed by Judea Pearl for manipulating probabilistic statements involving interventions. Using three inference rules, it transforms interventional queries involving the do operator into estimable statistical quantities from observational data under identifiability conditions.

Q: What is a causal graph?
A: A causal graph is a directed acyclic graph where nodes represent variables and directed edges represent direct causal relationships. Causal graphs encode structural assumptions about data-generating processes and enable systematic identification of confounders, mediators, and which statistical adjustments identify causal effects.

Q: What is a directed acyclic graph in causal inference?
A: A directed acyclic graph with causal interpretation specifies the causal structure among variables, with each edge representing a direct causal effect. The absence of an edge implies no direct causal relationship. DAGs guide adjustment strategy selection and enable reasoning about interventional distributions from observational data.

Q: What is the backdoor criterion?
A: The backdoor criterion specifies a sufficient condition for a set of variables that, when conditioned on, blocks all spurious associations between a treatment and outcome through backdoor paths without blocking the causal path. Adjusting for a backdoor adjustment set identifies the average causal effect.

Q: What is counterfactual reasoning?
A: Counterfactual reasoning asks what would have happened under a different course of action than what actually occurred. In causal inference, estimating counterfactuals requires structural causal models and enables reasoning about policy effects, individual-level treatment effects, and attribution of responsibility.

Q: What is propensity score matching?
A: Propensity score matching estimates treatment effects by matching treated and control units with similar predicted probabilities of receiving treatment. Conditioning on the propensity score blocks confounding by adjusting for all observed covariates that influence selection into treatment.

Q: What is inverse probability weighting?
A: Inverse probability weighting reweights observations by the inverse of their probability of receiving the treatment they actually received, creating a pseudo-population where treatment is independent of observed confounders. It enables unbiased estimation of average treatment effects from observational data.

Q: What is mediation analysis?
A: Mediation analysis decomposes a total causal effect into a direct effect of the treatment on the outcome and an indirect effect that operates through an intermediate mediator variable. It answers how much of the effect is due to different causal pathways.

Q: What is external validity?
A: External validity refers to how well study findings generalize to different populations, settings, or time periods beyond those in the study. Randomized controlled trials may have high internal validity but limited external validity if study samples are unrepresentative of the target population.

Q: What is the stable unit treatment value assumption?
A: SUTVA requires that the potential outcome for each unit is not affected by the treatment received by other units and that there is only one version of treatment. Violations occur in network effects, herd immunity, and market equilibrium where actions of one unit affect outcomes of others.

Q: What is heterogeneous treatment effects?
A: Heterogeneous treatment effects recognize that causal effects vary across subpopulations due to effect modification by individual characteristics. Estimating conditional average treatment effects reveals who benefits most from a treatment, enabling personalized policy recommendations.

Q: What is a probabilistic graphical model?
A: A probabilistic graphical model represents a joint probability distribution over multiple variables using a graph structure where nodes represent random variables and edges encode conditional dependencies. They enable tractable inference and learning in high-dimensional distributions through independence assumptions.

Q: What is a Bayesian network?
A: A Bayesian network is a directed acyclic graphical model where each node represents a variable and each edge represents a conditional dependency. Each variable is conditionally independent of its non-descendants given its parents, enabling factored representation and efficient probabilistic inference.

Q: What is a Markov random field?
A: A Markov random field is an undirected graphical model where variables are conditionally independent of all other variables given their direct neighbors. The joint distribution factorizes as a product of potential functions over cliques, used for image segmentation, language modeling, and spatial statistics.

Q: What is belief propagation?
A: Belief propagation is an algorithm for exact inference in tree-structured graphical models that computes marginal distributions by passing messages between neighboring nodes. Loopy belief propagation extends this to graphs with cycles, providing approximate inference that often works well in practice.

Q: What is the junction tree algorithm?
A: The junction tree algorithm performs exact inference in graphical models with cycles by converting the graph into a tree of cliques called a junction tree and running belief propagation on this tree. It is exponential in the size of the largest clique, making it intractable for dense graphs.

Q: What is the Viterbi algorithm?
A: The Viterbi algorithm computes the most probable sequence of hidden states in a hidden Markov model using dynamic programming. It is used for sequence decoding in speech recognition, named entity recognition, and part-of-speech tagging.

Q: What is a hidden Markov model?
A: A hidden Markov model is a probabilistic graphical model where an observable sequence is generated by an underlying sequence of hidden states following Markov dynamics. The Baum-Welch algorithm trains HMMs from unlabeled sequences using expectation-maximization.

Q: What is the expectation-maximization algorithm?
A: The EM algorithm iterates between an expectation step that computes the expected sufficient statistics of latent variables given current parameters and a maximization step that updates parameters to maximize the expected complete-data log-likelihood. It converges to a local maximum of the marginal likelihood.

Q: What is latent Dirichlet allocation?
A: Latent Dirichlet allocation is a generative probabilistic model for documents that assumes each document is a mixture of topics, each topic is a distribution over words, and each word is drawn from the topic distribution for that word. Inference recovers the latent topic structure.

Q: What is a conditional random field?
A: A conditional random field is a discriminative undirected graphical model that directly models the conditional probability of labels given observations. Unlike generative models like HMMs, CRFs do not model the input distribution, enabling richer feature sets for structured prediction tasks.

Q: What is exact inference in graphical models?
A: Exact inference computes marginal or conditional distributions precisely using algorithms like variable elimination, belief propagation on trees, or the junction tree algorithm. It is feasible only for models with limited treewidth, as complexity grows exponentially with the clique size in dense graphs.

Q: What is approximate inference in graphical models?
A: Approximate inference methods including variational inference and MCMC enable tractable probabilistic reasoning in complex graphical models where exact inference is intractable. They trade accuracy for computational feasibility, enabling inference in large-scale models with thousands of variables.

Q: What is sampling in probabilistic models?
A: Sampling generates representative examples from a probability distribution, enabling Monte Carlo estimation of intractable expectations. Ancestral sampling in Bayesian networks generates samples by sampling each variable in topological order from its conditional distribution given already-sampled parent values.

Q: What is importance sampling?
A: Importance sampling estimates expectations under a target distribution by drawing samples from a tractable proposal distribution and reweighting them by the ratio of target to proposal density. It provides unbiased estimates but can have high variance if proposal and target are dissimilar.

Q: What is a factor graph?
A: A factor graph is a bipartite graphical model with variable nodes and factor nodes where factors specify potential functions over subsets of variables. Belief propagation on factor graphs is a general framework that unifies inference algorithms for Bayesian networks, Markov random fields, and LDPC codes.

Q: What is exploratory data analysis?
A: Exploratory data analysis uses statistical summaries, visualizations, and transformations to understand the structure, quality, and relationships in a dataset before modeling. It reveals distributions, correlations, outliers, and missing patterns that inform preprocessing decisions and hypothesis generation.

Q: What is a data pipeline in machine learning?
A: A data pipeline automates the sequence of steps that transform raw data into model-ready features, including ingestion, cleaning, transformation, feature computation, and formatting. Reproducible automated pipelines ensure consistent data processing across training and inference.

Q: What is schema validation in ML?
A: Schema validation checks that incoming data conforms to expected types, ranges, and distributions before it reaches a model. Validating schemas in data pipelines catches upstream data quality issues early, preventing silent model failures caused by unexpected values or missing fields.

Q: What is data splitting?
A: Data splitting divides a dataset into training, validation, and test subsets before model development begins. Proper splitting ensures each subset is representative, prevents leakage between splits, and maintains temporal ordering for time series data to avoid using future information to predict the past.

Q: What is stratified sampling?
A: Stratified sampling divides the population into homogeneous groups called strata and samples independently from each, ensuring the sample represents each stratum proportionally. In machine learning, stratified train-test splitting ensures class proportions are preserved in each split.

Q: What is data cleaning?
A: Data cleaning corrects or removes incorrect, incomplete, duplicate, or inconsistent data entries that would mislead model training. It includes handling missing values, correcting mislabeled examples, standardizing formats, resolving inconsistencies, and removing duplicates.

Q: What is feature interaction?
A: Feature interaction creates new features from products or other combinations of existing features, capturing non-additive relationships. Polynomial features and factorization machines explicitly model two-way interactions, while deep neural networks learn higher-order interactions implicitly.

Q: What is collinearity in features?
A: Collinearity occurs when two or more features are highly correlated, providing redundant information to the model. In linear models, collinearity inflates coefficient variance and makes estimates unstable. Regularization, feature selection, or PCA reduces the impact of collinear features.

Q: What is feature importance?
A: Feature importance quantifies how much each input feature contributes to model predictions, enabling identification of the most predictive variables for data understanding, model simplification, and feature selection. Methods include Gini importance in trees, permutation importance, and SHAP values.

Q: What is permutation feature importance?
A: Permutation feature importance measures the decrease in model performance when a feature's values are randomly shuffled, breaking its relationship with the target. Features with large performance drops are important, and the method is model-agnostic and captures interactions with other features.

Q: What is model selection?
A: Model selection chooses the best model from a set of candidates based on validation performance or theoretical criteria. It involves selecting model family, architecture, regularization, and hyperparameters using cross-validation or information criteria to find the best generalization to unseen data.

Q: What is ensemble learning?
A: Ensemble learning combines predictions from multiple diverse models to produce superior results. Combining models with different errors allows the ensemble to reduce variance through averaging or reduce bias through boosting. Ensembles consistently outperform individual models on competitive benchmarks.

Q: What is bagging?
A: Bagging, bootstrap aggregating, trains each ensemble member on a different bootstrap sample of the training data with replacement and aggregates predictions by voting or averaging. Bagging reduces variance by averaging over models trained on different data subsets, improving generalization.

Q: What is boosting?
A: Boosting sequentially trains ensemble members where each new model focuses on examples misclassified by previous models. The final prediction combines all models with weights based on their individual accuracy, reducing both bias and variance through iterative error correction.

Q: What is stacking?
A: Stacking trains a meta-model on the out-of-fold predictions of base models, learning how to best combine their predictions for the specific task. Unlike simple averaging, stacking adapts the combination weights to the task, often achieving better performance than the best individual base model.

Q: What is blending in ensemble methods?
A: Blending creates ensemble predictions by fitting a second-level model on base model predictions from a validation set rather than cross-validation. While simpler to implement than full stacking, blending uses less data for meta-learning and provides less robust combination weight estimates.

Q: What is a confusion matrix in practice?
A: In practice, confusion matrices are analyzed to understand specific error patterns, identify which class pairs are most confused, and compute class-specific metrics. Examining which examples fall in off-diagonal cells guides data collection, class weighting, and threshold selection to address systematic errors.

Q: What is threshold tuning?
A: Threshold tuning selects the probability cutoff above which positive predictions are made, trading off precision and recall according to application requirements. The optimal threshold minimizes a cost function reflecting the relative penalties of false positives and false negatives in the specific use case.

Q: What is model calibration in practice?
A: In practice, model calibration is assessed by plotting predicted probabilities against observed frequencies and corrected using temperature scaling, Platt scaling, or isotonic regression applied to a held-out calibration set. Calibrated models enable reliable probabilistic decision-making.

Q: What is feature scaling in tree models?
A: Tree-based models including decision trees, random forests, and gradient boosting are invariant to monotonic feature transformations and do not require normalization or standardization. However, neural networks and linear models are sensitive to feature scale and require explicit scaling.

Q: What is log transformation?
