A: Log transformation applies the natural logarithm to features with right-skewed distributions, compressing the long tail and making the distribution more symmetric. It is commonly applied to monetary values, population counts, and other quantities that vary over orders of magnitude.

Q: What is power transformation?
A: Power transformations including the Box-Cox and Yeo-Johnson transformations parameterically transform features to be more normally distributed by finding the optimal exponent. They are applied in preprocessing to satisfy distributional assumptions of linear models and improve neural network optimization.

Q: What is target encoding?
A: Target encoding replaces categorical variable levels with the mean of the target variable for observations belonging to that level, capturing the relationship between category and target. Cross-validated encoding prevents leakage by encoding each fold's training data using out-of-fold target statistics.

Q: What is frequency encoding?
A: Frequency encoding replaces categorical variable levels with their frequency of occurrence in the training data, providing a meaningful numerical representation that captures category rarity. It preserves information about common versus rare categories without expanding dimensionality as one-hot encoding does.

Q: What is embedding for categorical variables?
A: Entity embeddings learn dense vector representations for categorical variable levels during neural network training, placing similar categories nearby in embedding space. Unlike one-hot encoding, embeddings reduce dimensionality, capture inter-category relationships, and often improve model performance.

Q: What is the train-test distribution shift problem?
A: Train-test distribution shift occurs when the distribution of test data differs from training data, degrading model performance. It can arise from non-representative training data collection, temporal change, or deployment in a different context than training, requiring careful evaluation and domain adaptation.

Q: What is stratified k-fold cross-validation?
A: Stratified k-fold cross-validation ensures each fold contains approximately the same proportion of each class as the full dataset, providing more reliable performance estimates than unstratified splits, especially for imbalanced classification problems where random splits produce highly variable class distributions.

Q: What is nested cross-validation?
A: Nested cross-validation uses an outer loop for performance estimation and an inner loop for hyperparameter selection, providing unbiased estimates of generalization performance when hyperparameter tuning is performed. The inner loop selects hyperparameters and the outer loop evaluates the resulting model.

Q: What is group k-fold cross-validation?
A: Group k-fold cross-validation ensures all samples from the same group, such as the same patient or user, appear in only one fold. This prevents leakage when samples within a group are correlated and provides more honest performance estimates for models deployed across different groups.

Q: What is time series cross-validation?
A: Time series cross-validation uses expanding or rolling windows to ensure the validation set always consists of data from future time periods relative to the training set. This respects the temporal ordering constraint and produces realistic performance estimates for models deployed in production.

Q: What is the train-validation-test workflow?
A: The standard workflow fits a model on training data, tunes hyperparameters using validation data, and evaluates final performance on a held-out test set used only once. This three-way split prevents both hyperparameter overfitting to validation data and optimistic bias from evaluating on training data.

Q: What is data-centric AI?
A: Data-centric AI focuses on improving model performance primarily through improving data quality, consistency, and coverage rather than model architecture. It recognizes that for many practical problems, better data provides larger gains than better models, shifting emphasis from model tuning to data curation.

Q: What is annotation quality control?
A: Annotation quality control ensures that labels in training data are accurate and consistent through strategies including annotator training, consensus labeling where multiple annotators label each example, inter-annotator agreement measurement, and systematic review of difficult or low-agreement examples.

Q: What is active learning for annotation?
A: Active learning for annotation uses model uncertainty to select which unlabeled examples would be most informative to annotate next, maximizing learning from each labeled example. It enables building effective training datasets with fewer annotations compared to random sampling.

Q: What is weak supervision?
A: Weak supervision programmatically labels large datasets using heuristic labeling functions that are noisy or imprecise rather than requiring manual annotation of each example. The Snorkel framework aggregates conflicting weak labels using a generative model to produce probabilistic training labels.

Q: What is semi-supervised learning in practice?
A: In practice, semi-supervised learning leverages abundant unlabeled data alongside small labeled datasets using techniques like pseudo-labeling, consistency regularization, and self-training. It is particularly valuable when annotation is expensive and large amounts of unlabeled data are readily available.

Q: What is self-training?
A: Self-training iteratively generates pseudo-labels for unlabeled data using a model trained on labeled data, adds high-confidence pseudo-labeled examples to the training set, and retrains. This bootstrapping process leverages unlabeled data to improve performance when labeled data is scarce.

Q: What is pseudo-labeling?
A: Pseudo-labeling generates soft or hard labels for unlabeled data using model predictions, treating them as if they were ground truth labels during subsequent training iterations. Careful selection of high-confidence examples and gradual incorporation prevent error amplification from incorrect pseudo-labels.

Q: What is consistency regularization?
A: Consistency regularization enforces that model predictions remain stable under data augmentation or small input perturbations, using the prediction discrepancy as an additional unsupervised loss. Methods like UDA and FixMatch apply this principle to leverage unlabeled data for improved generalization.

Q: What are large language models?
A: Large language models are autoregressive transformer-based neural networks trained on massive text datasets with billions to trillions of parameters. They learn rich representations of language enabling generation, comprehension, translation, reasoning, and coding through next-token prediction at scale.

Q: What is pretraining in language models?
A: Pretraining trains a language model on a large general corpus to learn broad language representations before task-specific adaptation. Predicting masked tokens in BERT or next tokens in GPT on internet-scale text produces representations that transfer effectively to downstream NLP and reasoning tasks.

Q: What is the transformer decoder architecture?
A: The transformer decoder uses masked self-attention that prevents each position from attending to future positions, enabling autoregressive generation by ensuring predictions depend only on preceding tokens. Feed-forward layers and residual connections complete the decoder block used in GPT-style language models.

Q: What is the transformer encoder architecture?
A: The transformer encoder uses bidirectional self-attention where each token can attend to all other tokens in both directions, producing rich contextualized representations. Multiple encoder layers are stacked to build hierarchical representations used in BERT-style models for understanding tasks.

Q: What is the encoder-decoder architecture?
A: Encoder-decoder transformers use a bidirectional encoder to represent input sequences and an autoregressive decoder that generates outputs by attending to encoder representations via cross-attention. This architecture is used for machine translation, summarization, and question answering.

Q: What is cross-attention?
A: Cross-attention allows the decoder to attend to encoder representations by using decoder hidden states as queries and encoder outputs as keys and values. It enables the decoder to selectively focus on relevant parts of the encoded input when generating each output token.

Q: What is positional encoding variety?
A: Positional encodings include fixed sinusoidal encodings in the original transformer, learned absolute position embeddings in BERT, relative position encodings like Shaw's method, rotary position embeddings in LLaMA, and ALiBi which adds position-dependent biases to attention scores.

Q: What is the scaling hypothesis?
A: The scaling hypothesis proposes that reliably scaling up model size, data, and compute produces continuous and predictable improvements in language model capabilities. Evidence from scaling laws suggests intelligence may emerge primarily from scale rather than architectural innovations.

Q: What is GPT-3?
A: GPT-3 is a 175 billion parameter autoregressive language model demonstrating remarkable few-shot learning abilities. By conditioning on a handful of examples in the prompt, GPT-3 can perform tasks it was not explicitly trained for, demonstrating that scale enables emergent in-context learning.

Q: What is GPT-4?
A: GPT-4 is a large multimodal language model capable of processing both text and images. It demonstrates strong reasoning, coding, and instruction-following abilities across diverse benchmarks, trained with RLHF and additional safety fine-tuning to improve alignment with human preferences.

Q: What is LLaMA?
A: LLaMA, Large Language Model Meta AI, is a family of open-source language models released by Meta that are competitive with or superior to much larger models through efficient training on high-quality data. It uses rotary position embeddings, pre-normalization, and SwiGLU activations for improved efficiency.

Q: What is Mistral?
A: Mistral is an efficient 7-billion parameter language model using sliding window attention and grouped query attention that achieves performance competitive with much larger models through architectural efficiency. Its strong performance-to-size ratio makes it popular for deployment on consumer hardware.

Q: What is grouped query attention?
A: Grouped query attention divides attention heads into groups that share key and value projections, reducing memory bandwidth requirements during inference compared to full multi-head attention. It achieves similar quality to multi-head attention while enabling faster inference through reduced key-value cache size.

Q: What is sliding window attention?
A: Sliding window attention restricts each token to attend only to a local window of neighboring tokens rather than the full sequence, reducing attention complexity from quadratic to linear in sequence length. Global attention tokens can optionally attend to the entire sequence for long-range dependencies.

Q: What is the attention sink phenomenon?
A: Attention sinks are tokens, particularly the initial tokens in a sequence, that receive disproportionately high attention weights from many other tokens regardless of semantic relevance. Models appear to route attention to these sink tokens as a default, and their removal can disrupt attention patterns.

Q: What is context extension in language models?
A: Context extension techniques adapt models trained on short contexts to handle much longer sequences. Methods include position interpolation that scales position encodings, fine-tuning on longer sequences with modified position bases, and retrieval augmentation to avoid quadratic attention costs.

Q: What is ROPE?
A: Rotary Position Embedding encodes position information by rotating the query and key vectors by position-dependent angles before computing attention scores. The rotation is designed so the attention score between two tokens depends only on their relative position difference, enabling generalization to longer sequences.

Q: What is ALiBi?
A: ALiBi, Attention with Linear Biases, adds a static linear penalty to attention logits based on the distance between query and key positions, without adding any position embeddings. This simple modification enables zero-shot context length generalization at inference time without retraining.

Q: What is constitutional AI training?
A: Constitutional AI fine-tunes models to be helpful, harmless, and honest using a set of written principles as a constitution. The model critiques its own responses for violations of the constitution and revises them, generating training data that is used for supervised learning and preference modeling.

Q: What is supervised fine-tuning?
A: Supervised fine-tuning updates a pretrained language model on a curated dataset of high-quality instruction-following examples, teaching the model to respond helpfully to diverse prompts. SFT is the first stage of RLHF alignment and significantly improves instruction-following over the base pretrained model.

Q: What is the base model versus instruct model?
A: A base language model is trained only on next-token prediction and will continue any text it is given without following instructions. An instruct or chat model is fine-tuned with SFT and RLHF to follow instructions, be helpful, and refuse harmful requests, producing a much more usable assistant.

Q: What is model safety in language models?
A: Model safety in language models ensures the system refuses harmful requests, avoids producing dangerous content, acknowledges uncertainty, and does not assist with illegal activities. Safety training uses RLHF with human preference data rating responses and adversarial red-teaming to identify failure modes.

Q: What is red teaming?
A: Red teaming in AI evaluates system safety and robustness by adversarially probing for harmful outputs, vulnerabilities, and failure modes. Human red teams craft adversarial prompts attempting to elicit problematic behaviors, while automated red teaming scales this process using separate language models.

Q: What is jailbreaking in language models?
A: Jailbreaking is the practice of crafting adversarial prompts that bypass language model safety measures and elicit outputs the model was trained to refuse. Techniques include role-playing scenarios, hypothetical framing, and prompt injection, exploiting the tension between helpfulness and safety.

Q: What is prompt injection?
A: Prompt injection attacks embed adversarial instructions in data a language model processes, causing it to follow the injected instructions instead of the user's intended instructions. It is a significant security concern for language model applications that process untrusted external content.

Q: What is emergent behavior in language models?
A: Emergent behaviors are capabilities that appear in language models only above certain scale thresholds, absent in smaller models and present in larger ones. Examples include multi-step arithmetic, logical deduction, and multilingual capabilities that emerge without explicit training as parameters and data scale up.

Q: What is the MMLU benchmark?
A: Massive Multitask Language Understanding evaluates language model knowledge across 57 academic subjects including STEM, humanities, and professional domains. It tests both breadth and depth of world knowledge, with human expert performance around 89 percent and frontier models now exceeding this.

Q: What is the BIG-Bench benchmark?
A: BIG-Bench, Beyond the Imitation Game benchmark, contains over 200 diverse tasks designed to be challenging for current language models, including tasks requiring reasoning, world knowledge, and complex language understanding. It tracks progress toward more capable AI systems.

Q: What is language model fine-tuning for code?
A: Fine-tuning language models on large code corpora or curated programming tasks produces code-specialized models capable of code completion, generation, debugging, and explanation. Models like Codex, Code LLaMA, and DeepSeek Coder are pretrained or fine-tuned on GitHub repositories.

Q: What is retrieval augmented generation?
A: Retrieval augmented generation enhances language model responses by retrieving relevant documents from a knowledge base and conditioning generation on both the user query and retrieved context. It reduces hallucinations by grounding responses in retrieved factual information and enables knowledge base updates without retraining.

Q: What is multi-modal learning?
A: Multi-modal learning trains models on multiple data modalities simultaneously such as text, images, audio, and video. Shared or aligned representations enable cross-modal understanding, generation, and retrieval, used in tasks like visual question answering, image captioning, and text-to-image generation.

Q: What is visual question answering?
A: Visual question answering requires models to answer natural language questions about images, integrating visual understanding with language comprehension. Models encode image features with visual encoders and answer questions using cross-modal attention between visual and language representations.

Q: What is image captioning?
A: Image captioning generates natural language descriptions of image content, combining visual understanding with language generation. Models encode images into visual features and condition autoregressive language generation on these features, trained on paired image-caption datasets.

Q: What is video captioning?
A: Video captioning extends image captioning to the temporal domain, generating textual descriptions of video content that capture both the visual content and the temporal narrative of events. It requires integrating spatial and temporal information from video frames.

Q: What is audio processing with deep learning?
A: Deep learning processes raw audio waveforms or spectrograms for tasks including speech recognition, music classification, speaker identification, and audio generation. CNNs process spectrograms as 2D images while recurrent networks and transformers model temporal audio dynamics.

Q: What is speech recognition?
A: Automatic speech recognition transcribes spoken audio to text using acoustic models that process audio features and language models that impose linguistic constraints on transcriptions. End-to-end neural models like Whisper directly map mel spectrograms to token sequences.

Q: What is Whisper?
A: Whisper is a large-scale speech recognition model trained by OpenAI on 680,000 hours of multilingual audio data. It handles diverse audio conditions, accents, and languages robustly and can perform speech translation directly from audio to English text.

Q: What is text-to-speech synthesis?
A: Text-to-speech converts written text into natural-sounding speech using deep generative models. Modern neural TTS systems like VITS and Bark produce high-quality expressive speech by modeling the complex acoustic space of human voice and prosody end-to-end.

Q: What is voice cloning?
A: Voice cloning generates speech that matches the voice characteristics of a target speaker from just a few seconds of reference audio. Zero-shot voice cloning using neural speaker adaptation enables personalized TTS without speaker-specific training.

Q: What is optical character recognition?
A: OCR extracts text from images of documents, recognizing characters in natural scenes, printed documents, and handwriting. Modern deep learning approaches combine convolutional feature extraction with sequence-to-sequence models to handle diverse fonts, layouts, and image qualities.

Q: What is document understanding?
A: Document understanding interprets the semantic content of documents combining visual layout understanding with textual comprehension. LayoutLM and similar models incorporate position information and visual features alongside text tokens to parse forms, receipts, and document structures.

Q: What is table understanding?
A: Table understanding enables machine learning models to parse, reason about, and extract information from tabular data presented in documents or databases. Tasks include table question answering, cell classification, and relation extraction using transformer models adapted for structured grid data.

Q: What is information extraction?
A: Information extraction automatically identifies and structures relevant information from unstructured text, producing machine-readable outputs like entities, relationships, and events. It supports knowledge base population, business intelligence, and downstream reasoning tasks.

Q: What is event extraction?
A: Event extraction identifies descriptions of real-world events in text, extracting event types, triggers, and arguments such as who did what to whom, when, and where. It captures the dynamic actions and state changes described in text for knowledge base population and narrative understanding.

Q: What is cross-lingual NLP?
A: Cross-lingual NLP enables models to understand and generate text across multiple languages, leveraging shared representations that capture meaning independently of language. Multilingual models like mBERT and XLM-R are pretrained on text from hundreds of languages simultaneously.

Q: What is machine translation evaluation?
A: Machine translation evaluation uses automatic metrics like BLEU, chrF, and COMET alongside human judgment to assess translation quality. Human evaluation rates fluency and adequacy of translations, while learned metrics trained on human ratings provide more reliable automatic assessment.

Q: What is neural machine translation?
A: Neural machine translation uses sequence-to-sequence transformer models trained on parallel corpora to translate between language pairs. Encoder-decoder architectures with cross-attention replaced phrase-based statistical methods, dramatically improving translation quality for high-resource language pairs.

Q: What is low-resource NLP?
A: Low-resource NLP addresses natural language processing for languages or domains with limited training data. Approaches include cross-lingual transfer from rich-resource languages, multilingual pretraining, data augmentation, and meta-learning to maximize performance from minimal supervised examples.

Q: What is data augmentation for NLP?
A: Data augmentation for NLP creates additional training examples from existing data through transformations like synonym replacement, back-translation, word deletion, random swap, and paraphrase generation. These transformations increase training diversity and improve model robustness to surface variation.

Q: What is back-translation?
A: Back-translation generates additional training pairs by translating target-language sentences back to the source language and pairing the result with the original target. It is widely used for data augmentation in low-resource machine translation and NLP tasks.

Q: What is few-shot learning for NLP?
A: Few-shot learning in NLP adapts models to new tasks or domains from only a handful of labeled examples by leveraging rich pretrained representations. Techniques include prototypical networks, meta-learning, and prompting large language models with a few task demonstrations.

Q: What is named entity linking?
A: Named entity linking connects entity mentions in text to their canonical entries in a knowledge base such as Wikipedia or Wikidata. It disambiguates among entities with the same surface form and enables grounding of textual mentions to structured knowledge.

Q: What is reading comprehension?
A: Machine reading comprehension evaluates model ability to answer questions based on a provided passage, ranging from extracting answer spans to multi-hop reasoning across multiple documents. SQuAD, HotpotQA, and DROP are standard benchmarks testing different reading comprehension abilities.

Q: What is open-domain question answering?
A: Open-domain question answering retrieves answers from a large unstructured knowledge source like Wikipedia without a provided context passage. Systems typically combine a retrieval component that fetches relevant documents with a reading comprehension model that extracts answers.

Q: What is commonsense reasoning?
A: Commonsense reasoning requires understanding and applying everyday knowledge about the world that is rarely stated explicitly in text. Benchmarks like Winogrande, CommonsenseQA, and HellaSwag test whether models possess the basic world knowledge needed to navigate everyday situations.

Q: What is mathematical reasoning in AI?
A: Mathematical reasoning evaluates AI ability to solve algebraic problems, word problems, proofs, and calculation tasks. Chain-of-thought prompting and process supervision providing feedback on intermediate reasoning steps have significantly improved language model performance on math benchmarks.

Q: What is code generation?
A: Code generation uses language models to produce executable source code from natural language descriptions, completing partial code, translating between programming languages, and generating tests. Models trained on code repositories learn syntax, idioms, and common programming patterns.

Q: What is program synthesis?
A: Program synthesis automatically generates programs that satisfy given specifications, which may be provided as input-output examples, natural language descriptions, or formal logical constraints. Neural program synthesis uses seq2seq models while search-based approaches generate and test candidate programs.

Q: What is model editing?
A: Model editing updates specific factual associations within a pretrained language model without retraining the entire model, enabling targeted knowledge corrections and insertions. Methods include ROME and MEMIT which directly modify feed-forward network weights associated with specific facts.

Q: What is continual learning for language models?
A: Continual learning for language models enables updating models with new knowledge without forgetting previously learned information, addressing the challenge that retraining from scratch is prohibitively expensive. Regularization, replay, and parameter-efficient methods are used to mitigate catastrophic forgetting.

Q: What is foundation model?
A: A foundation model is a large model trained on broad data that can be adapted to a wide range of downstream tasks through fine-tuning, prompting, or lightweight adaptation. The term emphasizes that large pretrained models form the foundation upon which many applications are built.

Q: What is multimodal foundation model?
A: A multimodal foundation model processes and generates content across multiple data types including text, images, audio, and video within a unified architecture. Models like GPT-4V and Gemini demonstrate flexible cross-modal reasoning and generation capabilities from large-scale multimodal pretraining.

Q: What is a generalist AI agent?
A: A generalist AI agent can perceive diverse inputs, reason, plan, and take actions across a wide range of tasks without task-specific engineering. Large language models enhanced with tool use, memory, and planning capabilities are current approaches toward generalist agents.

Q: What is tool use in language models?
A: Tool use enables language models to invoke external tools like calculators, search engines, code interpreters, and APIs to augment their capabilities. By generating structured tool calls and processing their results, models can solve problems requiring precise computation or real-time information.

Q: What is the ReAct framework?
A: ReAct interleaves reasoning and acting in language models by alternating thought traces explaining the current reasoning with action calls to tools or APIs. Combining chain-of-thought reasoning with concrete action taking improves performance on knowledge-intensive and agentic tasks.

Q: What is an AI agent?
A: An AI agent perceives its environment, reasons about its goals, plans sequences of actions, and executes them to achieve desired outcomes. Current language model agents use prompting, tool calling, and memory to tackle complex multi-step tasks with minimal human intervention.

Q: What is long-horizon planning in AI?
A: Long-horizon planning requires reasoning about sequences of actions extending far into the future to achieve a goal, dealing with partial observability, uncertainty, and delayed feedback. It is a key challenge for AI agents in complex environments where simple reactive policies are insufficient.

Q: What is memory in AI agents?
A: Memory in AI agents stores information from past interactions and reasoning for use in future decisions. Working memory in the context window provides immediate access to recent information, while long-term memory systems using vector databases enable retrieval of information from prior sessions.

Q: What is the chain of thought reasoning?
A: Chain of thought reasoning prompts language models to generate intermediate reasoning steps before producing a final answer, decomposing complex problems into smaller steps. This significantly improves performance on multi-step arithmetic, logical reasoning, and commonsense reasoning tasks.

Q: What is tree of thoughts?
A: Tree of thoughts extends chain of thought by exploring multiple reasoning paths in a tree structure, evaluating intermediate reasoning states and using search strategies like BFS and DFS to navigate the space of possible reasoning paths. It improves performance on tasks requiring strategic planning.

Q: What is program-aided reasoning?
A: Program-aided reasoning uses language models to generate code that performs the necessary computations for solving mathematical or analytical problems, then executes the code to get precise answers. It combines language model reasoning with the precision and reliability of programmatic computation.

Q: What is self-consistency in language models?
A: Self-consistency improves language model accuracy by generating multiple independent reasoning chains with diverse sampling, then selecting the answer supported by the majority of chains. Marginalizing over multiple reasoning paths reduces sensitivity to individual reasoning errors.

Q: What is automatic prompt optimization?
A: Automatic prompt optimization uses optimization algorithms to search for prompts that maximize language model performance on a target task, replacing manual prompt engineering. Methods include gradient-based soft prompt optimization, evolutionary search over discrete prompts, and using LLMs to generate and improve prompts.

Q: What is the zero-shot chain of thought?
A: Zero-shot chain of thought triggers step-by-step reasoning in language models by appending the phrase let's think step by step to the problem statement without providing any examples. This simple zero-shot instruction significantly improves performance on arithmetic and logical reasoning tasks.

Q: What is multi-agent systems in AI?
A: Multi-agent AI systems use multiple specialized AI agents that communicate, collaborate, and coordinate to solve complex tasks. Architectures include orchestrator-worker patterns, debate between agents, and peer-to-peer collaboration where different agents possess different tools and expertise.

Q: What is the AutoGPT paradigm?
A: AutoGPT and similar autonomous agent systems use language models to self-direct a sequence of actions toward a high-level goal, automatically decomposing goals into subtasks, using tools, and iterating based on results. They extend language model capabilities toward autonomous task completion.

Q: What is responsible scaling?
A: Responsible scaling refers to organizational commitments to evaluate AI systems for dangerous capabilities before deploying or training more powerful models, pausing development if certain capability thresholds are reached. It operationalizes safety commitments during capability scaling.

Q: What is interpretability research?
A: Interpretability research seeks to understand the internal representations and computations of neural networks, identifying circuits responsible for specific behaviors, understanding knowledge representation, and verifying alignment properties. Mechanistic interpretability studies individual components and their interactions.

Q: What is superposition in neural networks?
A: Superposition hypothesis proposes that neural networks represent more features than they have neurons by encoding multiple features in overlapping distributed representations across neurons. Sparse activations allow disambiguation, enabling models to store vastly more information than their size suggests.

Q: What is mechanistic interpretability?
A: Mechanistic interpretability attempts to reverse-engineer specific algorithms and circuits implemented by trained neural networks, identifying which components perform which computations. Techniques include activation patching, probing, and circuit discovery to understand model internals.


# === FINAL_EXTENSION ===

Q: What is encoder-only transformer?
A: An encoder-only transformer processes the entire input sequence bidirectionally and produces contextual representations for every token. It is suited for classification, named entity recognition, and other understanding tasks where the full context is available during inference.

Q: What is decoder-only transformer?
A: A decoder-only transformer generates tokens autoregressively attending only to past tokens using causal masking. It is the architecture of GPT-style language models and is well suited for text generation, code completion, and few-shot learning through prompting.

Q: What is encoder-decoder transformer?
A: An encoder-decoder transformer uses an encoder to produce contextual source representations and a decoder that attends to these representations via cross-attention while generating the target sequence autoregressively. It is used for translation, summarization, and structured prediction.

Q: What is the feed-forward block in transformers?
A: The feed-forward block in a transformer applies two linear transformations with a nonlinear activation between them to each token independently. It is typically twice to four times wider than the model dimension and accounts for most of the model's parameter count.

Q: What is prenorm versus postnorm in transformers?
A: Prenorm places layer normalization before the attention and feed-forward blocks while postnorm places it after. Prenorm improves gradient flow and training stability in deep transformers but postnorm may produce better final representations in some settings.

Q: What is the embedding layer in transformers?
A: The embedding layer maps discrete input tokens to dense continuous vectors of the model dimension. The same embedding matrix is often used for both input embedding and output projection to the vocabulary, tying the input and output representations.

Q: What is the role of the feed-forward layer dimension?
A: The feed-forward layer dimension, typically four times the model dimension, controls how much information can be stored in the network's implicit memory. Larger feed-forward dimensions increase the network's capacity to memorize facts at the cost of increased parameters.

Q: What is the number of attention heads?
A: The number of attention heads in multi-head attention controls how many independent subspaces the model can attend to simultaneously. More heads allow capturing more diverse types of relationships at the cost of a smaller dimensionality per head.

Q: What is the depth of a transformer?
A: The depth of a transformer is the number of stacked encoder or decoder blocks. Deeper models can learn more complex hierarchical representations but are harder to train and more prone to gradient issues, motivating the use of prenorm and careful initialization.

Q: What is the GPT-2 architecture?
A: GPT-2 is a 12 to 48 layer decoder-only transformer with 117M to 1.5B parameters, pretrained on 40GB of internet text. It demonstrated that scaling language models produces strong few-shot generalization and was the first model showing language models can be dangerous if misused.

Q: What is the GPT-3 architecture?
A: GPT-3 is a 175 billion parameter decoder-only transformer that demonstrated remarkable few-shot in-context learning abilities from a single pretrained model without any fine-tuning, establishing the paradigm of large language model prompting.

Q: What is the PaLM architecture?
A: PaLM is a 540 billion parameter language model trained on a mixture of multilingual text, code, and mathematical content using the Pathways distributed training system. It achieved breakthrough performance on reasoning, code, and multilingual benchmarks.

Q: What is LLaMA?
A: LLaMA is a family of open-source language models from Meta ranging from 7B to 65B parameters trained on publicly available data. It demonstrated that models trained on more tokens can match larger models trained on less data, following Chinchilla scaling insights.

Q: What is Mistral?
A: Mistral is a 7B parameter language model using sliding window attention and grouped query attention for efficient inference, achieving performance competitive with larger models while being much faster due to its architectural efficiency optimizations.

Q: What is Mixtral?
A: Mixtral is a mixture of experts model using eight expert feed-forward networks per layer with two selected per token. With 46.7B total parameters but only 12.9B active per token, it achieves strong performance with inference costs comparable to a 12B dense model.

Q: What is Gemma?
A: Gemma is a family of lightweight open-source language models from Google built using techniques from Gemini. The 2B and 7B variants are designed for responsible deployment and achieve strong performance on benchmarks relative to their small size.

Q: What is Qwen?
A: Qwen is a series of large language models from Alibaba Cloud ranging from 0.5B to 72B parameters, pretrained on multilingual data with strong performance in both Chinese and English benchmarks covering reasoning, code, and math.

Q: What is instruction following?
A: Instruction following is the ability of a language model to execute diverse natural language instructions provided in a prompt accurately and reliably. It is developed through instruction tuning on paired instruction-response data and RLHF alignment.

Q: What is system prompt?
A: A system prompt is a set of instructions provided to a language model before the conversation begins that defines its role, capabilities, constraints, and behavior. It shapes the model's persona and response style throughout the subsequent interaction.

Q: What is a chat template?
A: A chat template is a formatted string structure that organizes multi-turn conversations between a user and an assistant into a consistent format expected by the model. Different models use different templates and applying the wrong template degrades performance.

Q: What is kernel density estimation?
A: Kernel density estimation estimates the probability density function of a random variable from a finite data sample by placing a kernel function centered at each observation and summing them. The bandwidth controls the smoothness of the resulting density estimate.

Q: What is the Gaussian kernel?
A: The Gaussian kernel is the most commonly used kernel in density estimation and kernel methods, defined as the standard normal probability density function. It assigns smoothly decaying weights to neighboring points based on their squared distance.

Q: What is density ratio estimation?
A: Density ratio estimation estimates the ratio of two probability densities without estimating each separately. It is used for importance weighting, covariate shift correction, and two-sample testing, and can be formulated as a binary classification problem.

Q: What is importance sampling?
A: Importance sampling estimates expectations under a target distribution by drawing samples from a different proposal distribution and reweighting each sample by the ratio of target to proposal probability. It reduces variance when the proposal covers the target well.

Q: What is stratified sampling?
A: Stratified sampling divides a population into homogeneous subgroups called strata and samples from each stratum independently. It reduces variance compared to simple random sampling when subgroup means differ substantially from the overall mean.

Q: What is Monte Carlo integration?
A: Monte Carlo integration estimates integrals by drawing random samples from a probability distribution and averaging the integrand values. Its convergence rate is independent of dimensionality making it the method of choice for high-dimensional integration.

Q: What is the expectation maximization algorithm for GMMs?
A: EM for Gaussian mixture models alternates between the E-step computing posterior probabilities that each point belongs to each component and the M-step updating component parameters to maximize the expected complete-data log-likelihood. It converges to a local maximum.

Q: What is the Cox proportional hazards model?
A: The Cox proportional hazards model is a regression model for survival data relating the hazard of an event to a linear combination of features through an exponential link. It handles censored observations and estimates relative risk without specifying the baseline hazard.

Q: What is survival analysis?
A: Survival analysis studies the time until an event of interest occurs accounting for censored observations where the event has not occurred by the study end. Machine learning approaches like survival forests and neural networks extend classical models to complex feature spaces.

Q: What is causal discovery?
A: Causal discovery learns the causal structure of a system from observational data, identifying which variables causally influence which others. Constraint-based methods like PC and FCI and score-based methods like GES use conditional independence tests or Bayesian scores to infer graph structure.

Q: What is instrumental variable?
A: An instrumental variable is a variable that affects the treatment variable but affects the outcome only through the treatment, not directly. It enables estimating causal effects of treatment on outcome in the presence of unmeasured confounding variables.

Q: What is regression discontinuity design?
A: Regression discontinuity design estimates causal effects by comparing outcomes of units just above and just below a threshold that determines treatment assignment. The discontinuity in outcomes at the threshold identifies the local average treatment effect.

Q: What is difference in differences?
A: Difference in differences estimates treatment effects by comparing changes over time in treated and control groups, assuming that absent treatment the two groups would have followed parallel trends. It controls for time-invariant confounders and shared time trends.

Q: What is synthetic control?
A: Synthetic control constructs a counterfactual for a treated unit by weighting a combination of control units to match the treated unit's pretreatment characteristics. It enables case studies of policy interventions with a single treated unit and multiple controls.

Q: What is Bayesian optimization?
A: Bayesian optimization uses a surrogate model, typically a Gaussian process, to model the unknown objective function and an acquisition function to select the next evaluation point balancing exploration and exploitation. It is sample-efficient for expensive black-box optimization.

Q: What is Gaussian process?
A: A Gaussian process is a distribution over functions where any finite collection of function values follows a joint Gaussian distribution parameterized by a mean function and covariance kernel. It provides probabilistic predictions with uncertainty estimates for regression and Bayesian optimization.

Q: What is a covariance kernel in GPs?
A: The covariance kernel in a Gaussian process defines the similarity between function values at different inputs. The radial basis function kernel encodes smoothness, the Matérn kernel controls differentiability, and the periodic kernel encodes periodicity.

Q: What is acquisition function?
A: An acquisition function determines which point to evaluate next in Bayesian optimization by balancing exploration of uncertain regions and exploitation of promising ones. Expected improvement, upper confidence bound, and probability of improvement are common choices.

Q: What is multi-fidelity optimization?
A: Multi-fidelity optimization uses cheap approximations of the expensive objective function such as lower-resolution simulations or shorter training runs to guide search. It allocates more evaluations to promising regions at low fidelity and uses high fidelity for final confirmation.

Q: What is neural process?
A: A neural process is a family of models combining neural networks with Gaussian processes to approximate stochastic processes. It is trained to predict distributions over function values at query points given a context set of observations enabling few-shot regression and uncertainty quantification.

Q: What is the Bellman equation?
A: The Bellman equation expresses the value of a state as the immediate reward plus the discounted value of the next state. It provides a recursive formulation enabling dynamic programming and temporal difference learning algorithms to compute value functions.

Q: What is temporal difference learning?
A: Temporal difference learning updates value estimates using the difference between consecutive predictions rather than waiting for the final return. TD methods like Q-learning bootstrap from current estimates enabling online learning without complete episode trajectories.

Q: What is n-step return?
A: N-step return computes the return by unrolling the trajectory for n steps before bootstrapping from the value estimate. It interpolates between one-step TD bootstrapping with low variance but high bias and full Monte Carlo returns with zero bias but high variance.

Q: What is a replay buffer?
A: A replay buffer stores past transitions of state, action, reward, and next state collected during environment interaction. Random sampling from the buffer breaks temporal correlations and enables reuse of past experience, stabilizing off-policy deep reinforcement learning.

Q: What is prioritized experience replay?
A: Prioritized experience replay samples transitions from the replay buffer with probability proportional to their temporal difference error magnitude, focusing learning on surprising transitions that provide the most informative training signal.

Q: What is the discount factor?
A: The discount factor gamma controls the relative importance of immediate versus future rewards, with values close to one giving near equal weight to all future rewards and values close to zero making the agent myopic and focused only on immediate rewards.

Q: What is a policy in reinforcement learning?
A: A policy is a function mapping states to action probabilities or deterministic actions that specifies the agent's behavior. Stochastic policies enable exploration while deterministic policies are more efficient at inference. The optimal policy maximizes expected cumulative discounted reward.

Q: What is a value function?
A: A value function estimates the expected cumulative discounted reward from a given state following a specific policy. State value functions assign values to states while action-value functions also condition on the action taken, enabling policy improvement.

Q: What is an advantage function?
A: The advantage function measures how much better an action is compared to the expected value of the current state under the current policy. Using advantage estimates rather than raw returns in policy gradient reduces gradient variance without introducing bias.

Q: What is generalized advantage estimation?
A: Generalized advantage estimation computes advantage estimates as an exponentially weighted average of multi-step advantage estimates, interpolating between high-variance Monte Carlo estimates and high-bias bootstrapped estimates through a lambda parameter.

Q: What is TRPO?
A: Trust Region Policy Optimization constrains policy updates to a trust region where the new policy is close to the old policy measured by KL divergence. This prevents catastrophically large policy updates while allowing maximum improvement within the trust region.

Q: What is DDPG?
A: Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient extends DQN to continuous action spaces by learning a deterministic policy and a Q-function using actor-critic architecture with experience replay and target networks. It applies gradient ascent directly through the Q-function to update the deterministic policy.

Q: What is SAC?
A: Soft Actor-Critic maximizes both the expected return and the entropy of the policy, encouraging exploration by rewarding the policy for being uncertain. The maximum entropy objective produces more robust policies and enables automatic temperature tuning for the entropy bonus.

Q: What is TD3?
A: Twin Delayed Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient addresses function approximation errors in DDPG by using two critic networks and taking their minimum to reduce overestimation, delaying policy updates relative to critic updates, and adding target policy smoothing noise.

Q: What is reward function design?
A: Reward function design specifies the scalar feedback signal that guides reinforcement learning agents toward desired behaviors. Poorly designed rewards can cause reward hacking and specification gaming requiring careful engineering to align with the true objective.

Q: What is imitation learning?
A: Imitation learning trains agents to mimic expert behavior by learning from demonstrations rather than sparse environment rewards. Behavioral cloning directly supervises the policy on expert actions while inverse reinforcement learning infers the reward function from demonstrations.

Q: What is behavioral cloning?
A: Behavioral cloning trains a policy to mimic expert demonstrations through supervised learning, predicting the expert's action at each observed state. Compounding errors arise because the trained policy encounters states not seen in demonstrations, motivating data aggregation methods.

Q: What is DAgger?
A: Dataset Aggregation iteratively extends the training data by querying the expert for correct actions at states visited by the current policy, not just the original demonstrations. This addresses compounding errors in behavioral cloning by covering the policy's own distribution of visited states.

Q: What is reward shaping?
A: Reward shaping adds potential-based bonus rewards to the environment reward to guide exploration toward states the designer believes are useful. Potential-based shaping preserves the optimal policy of the original reward function while providing denser feedback.

Q: What is model predictive control with neural networks?
A: Model predictive control uses a learned neural network model of system dynamics to plan sequences of actions optimizing a cost function over a receding horizon. At each step only the first action is executed and planning is re-run from the new state.

Q: What is domain-specific augmentation?
A: Domain-specific augmentation applies transformations that preserve label semantics within a particular data domain. For medical imaging this might include elastic deformations and intensity changes while for speech it includes time stretching and pitch shifting.

Q: What is test-time augmentation?
A: Test-time augmentation generates multiple augmented versions of a test example, collects model predictions for each, and aggregates them through voting or averaging. It improves prediction accuracy and calibration at the cost of multiple forward passes per test example.

Q: What is stochastic depth?
A: Stochastic depth randomly drops entire residual blocks during training, effectively training an ensemble of networks with different depths. At inference all blocks are active with their outputs scaled by the block survival probability, improving generalization.

Q: What is DropBlock?
A: DropBlock extends dropout to convolutional networks by dropping contiguous regions of feature maps rather than individual units. Dropping spatial regions is more effective because nearby units in feature maps are correlated and individual dropout rarely affects spatial semantic information.

Q: What is SamplePairing?
A: SamplePairing creates training examples by averaging pairs of images from the same class and using their original label. This simple augmentation is particularly effective for training with limited data and encourages smooth decision boundaries.

Q: What is feature space augmentation?
A: Feature space augmentation applies transformations in the learned feature space rather than the raw input space, generating new examples with the same label as nearby training examples in the embedding space. It can produce more diverse augmentations than input-level transformations.

Q: What is generative augmentation?
A: Generative augmentation uses a generative model like a GAN or diffusion model to synthesize additional training examples for underrepresented classes or challenging scenarios. The quality of synthetic data depends heavily on the generative model's fidelity.

Q: What is back-translation for NLP?
A: Back-translation augments text training data by translating to another language and translating back, producing paraphrases with similar meaning but different surface form. It is particularly effective for low-resource neural machine translation and text classification.

Q: What is synonym replacement?
A: Synonym replacement augments text by randomly replacing words with their synonyms from a thesaurus or word embedding neighbors. It creates diverse training examples preserving the semantic content while varying the vocabulary.

Q: What is random insertion for text?
A: Random insertion augments text by inserting synonyms of randomly chosen non-stop words at random positions in the sentence. It increases vocabulary diversity in training data without significantly changing the semantic content of the original text.

Q: What is random deletion for text?
A: Random deletion augments text by randomly removing individual words with a fixed probability. For short sentences fewer deletions are made to preserve intelligibility. It encourages models to be robust to missing words and noisy incomplete inputs.

Q: What is random swap for text?
A: Random swap augments text by randomly choosing two words in the sentence and swapping their positions. Repeated n times per sentence it creates variations of the original text while preserving most of the semantic content and vocabulary.

Q: What is EDA for NLP?
A: Easy Data Augmentation combines synonym replacement, random insertion, random deletion, and random swap to augment text training data with minimal effort. Applying a random subset of these operations to each training example significantly improves classifier performance with limited data.

Q: What is specAugment?
A: SpecAugment augments mel spectrograms for speech recognition by masking contiguous blocks of time steps and frequency channels. This simple augmentation dramatically improves robustness to noisy and incomplete audio without requiring additional data.

Q: What is mixup for structured data?
A: Mixup for structured data creates training examples by taking convex combinations of pairs of feature vectors and their corresponding labels. Even for tabular data it can smooth decision boundaries and improve calibration, though careful handling of categorical features is required.

Q: What is NDCG?
A: Normalized Discounted Cumulative Gain measures ranking quality by summing relevance scores of retrieved items discounted by their position and normalizing by the ideal ranking. Higher-ranked relevant items contribute more to the score reflecting that users examine top results most carefully.

Q: What is MRR?
A: Mean Reciprocal Rank is the average of the reciprocal ranks of the first relevant item for a set of queries. It rewards systems that place relevant items higher in the ranking and is simple to compute and interpret for information retrieval evaluation.

Q: What is pass at k?
A: Pass at k is an evaluation metric for code generation that measures the probability that at least one of k generated code samples passes all test cases. It accounts for the stochastic nature of generation by evaluating multiple samples per problem.

Q: What is win rate evaluation?
A: Win rate evaluation measures the fraction of pairwise comparisons where a model's outputs are preferred over a baseline by human judges or a strong judge model. It provides a natural summary of preference-based evaluation for generative models.

Q: What is MT-bench?
A: MT-bench is a challenging multi-turn conversation benchmark that evaluates language model instruction following using GPT-4 as an automatic judge to score model responses. It covers diverse task categories and evaluates conversational and reasoning capabilities.

Q: What is Chatbot Arena?
A: Chatbot Arena is a platform that evaluates language models through anonymous pairwise comparisons by human users, computing Elo ratings from preference votes. It provides crowd-sourced human preference rankings reflecting real-world usage patterns.

Q: What is MMLU?
A: Massive Multitask Language Understanding is a benchmark evaluating language model knowledge across 57 subject areas from mathematics and science to social sciences and humanities. It measures both breadth of knowledge and reasoning ability through multiple choice questions.

Q: What is HumanEval?
A: HumanEval is a code generation benchmark containing 164 programming problems with unit tests, measuring a model's ability to generate correct Python functions from docstring descriptions. It is the standard benchmark for evaluating code generation capabilities.

Q: What is GSM8K?
A: GSM8K is a benchmark of 8500 grade school math word problems requiring multi-step arithmetic reasoning. It has become a key benchmark for evaluating mathematical reasoning capabilities in language models and is solved reliably only by sufficiently large models.

Q: What is MATH benchmark?
A: The MATH benchmark contains 12500 competition mathematics problems across five difficulty levels requiring advanced mathematical reasoning. It is significantly harder than GSM8K and distinguishes capabilities between frontier language models.

Q: What is TruthfulQA?
A: TruthfulQA evaluates whether language models generate truthful answers to questions that many humans answer incorrectly due to misconceptions. It measures models' tendency to hallucinate believable but false information rather than admitting uncertainty.

Q: What is HellaSwag?
A: HellaSwag evaluates commonsense natural language inference by requiring models to select the most plausible completion of an activity description from adversarially filtered distractors. It specifically tests the kind of grounded commonsense reasoning language models struggle with.

Q: What is BIG-bench?
A: Beyond the Imitation Game benchmark is a collection of over 200 diverse challenging tasks designed to probe aspects of reasoning that are difficult for language models. It covers tasks from algorithmic reasoning to social intelligence and knowledge.

Q: What is perplexity as evaluation metric?
A: Perplexity measures a language model's ability to predict a held-out text corpus, with lower perplexity indicating better modeling. While useful for comparing models of the same architecture and training setup, cross-model comparisons require careful attention to tokenization differences.

Q: What is human alignment evaluation?
A: Human alignment evaluation assesses whether a model's responses are helpful, honest, and harmless through human rating studies collecting pairwise preferences, absolute quality ratings, and safety assessments. It directly measures the dimensions targeted by alignment training.

Q: What is automated evaluation with LLMs?
A: Automated evaluation with LLMs uses a strong language model like GPT-4 as an evaluator to rate or compare model outputs on dimensions like factual accuracy, helpfulness, and coherence. It scales human evaluation but can inherit the evaluator model's biases.

Q: What is the G-eval framework?
A: G-eval is a framework for NLP evaluation using large language models with chain-of-thought reasoning to score text generation outputs on task-specific dimensions. It generates form-filling evaluation prompts and aggregates token probability outputs as continuous scores.

Q: What is model contamination testing?
A: Model contamination testing checks whether benchmark examples appear in a model's training data by comparing test set n-grams against known training corpora. Contaminated benchmarks over-estimate model generalization and require decontamination or replacement.

Q: What is calibration evaluation?
A: Calibration evaluation measures whether a model's confidence scores accurately reflect the probability of being correct. It uses reliability diagrams, expected calibration error, and maximum calibration error to quantify the alignment between confidence and accuracy.

Q: What is reject option evaluation?
A: Reject option evaluation assesses models that can abstain from predictions by measuring coverage, the fraction of examples classified, alongside accuracy on classified examples. A good reject option system maintains high accuracy while covering most examples.

Q: What is weight tying in language models?
A: Weight tying shares the input embedding matrix with the output projection matrix in language models, reducing total parameters by the vocabulary size times the model dimension. Tying weights enforces consistency between how the model reads and generates tokens.

Q: What is perplexity reduction?
A: Perplexity reduction during training indicates the model is learning to predict text more accurately. Tracking perplexity on a validation set provides a continuous measure of language modeling improvement and can signal when training has converged or begun to overfit.

Q: What is model interpolation?
A: Model interpolation combines predictions from multiple models by taking a weighted average of their output probabilities. Weights can be set manually, optimized on validation data, or learned end-to-end, improving accuracy and robustness over any single model.

Q: What is a joint model?
A: A joint model simultaneously optimizes multiple related tasks sharing parameters and training signals. Joint training often improves performance on all tasks compared to separate models by regularizing through the diversity of training objectives.

Q: What is a pipeline model versus end-to-end?
A: A pipeline model chains separate specialized models each solving a subtask, with errors accumulating across stages. End-to-end models learn all subtasks jointly from raw inputs to final outputs, enabling better integration but requiring more data and careful training.

Q: What is the semantic gap in retrieval?
A: The semantic gap in retrieval refers to the discrepancy between how users express information needs in queries and how relevant documents express the same information. Dense retrieval with learned embeddings helps bridge this gap compared to keyword matching.

Q: What is negative transfer?
A: Negative transfer occurs when training on multiple tasks together hurts performance on one or more tasks compared to training on each task independently. It arises from conflicting gradients or parameter interference between tasks.

Q: What is task sampling in multi-task learning?
A: Task sampling determines how frequently each task is sampled for gradient updates in multi-task training. Temperature-based sampling smooths the task frequency distribution, while proportional sampling matches data quantities, affecting which tasks receive more gradient signal.

Q: What is a benchmark suite?
A: A benchmark suite is a collection of diverse evaluation tasks measuring different aspects of model capability, providing a comprehensive assessment of model strengths and weaknesses across domains, difficulty levels, and task types.

Q: What is red teaming for bias?
A: Red teaming for bias systematically probes model outputs across demographic groups and sensitive topics to identify discriminatory patterns, offensive content generation, and unfair disparate treatment before deployment in sensitive applications.

Q: What is capability evaluation?
A: Capability evaluation measures what a model can do across a range of tasks and domains, assessing both the breadth of task coverage and the depth of performance on each task. It guides understanding of model strengths and limitations.

Q: What is the alignment gap?
A: The alignment gap refers to the difference between what a model optimizes during training and what is actually desired in deployment. Closing the alignment gap requires careful reward specification, human feedback, and evaluation of real-world deployment outcomes.

Q: What is proximal policy gradient?
A: Proximal policy gradient refers to policy gradient algorithms that constrain the policy update magnitude to prevent destructive large steps. PPO achieves this through clipping the probability ratio while TRPO uses an explicit KL divergence constraint.

Q: What is value-based versus policy-based RL?
A: Value-based reinforcement learning derives the policy from a learned value function by selecting the action maximizing the value estimate. Policy-based methods directly parameterize and optimize the policy without explicitly computing value functions.

Q: What is off-policy learning?
A: Off-policy learning optimizes a target policy using data collected by a different behavior policy. This enables learning from historical data, replayed experience, and expert demonstrations without requiring the target policy to collect all its own experience.

Q: What is on-policy learning?
A: On-policy learning optimizes a policy using data collected by the current version of that policy. It is theoretically cleaner but less sample efficient than off-policy learning because collected data can only be used for a single update before becoming stale.

Q: What is curriculum reinforcement learning?
A: Curriculum reinforcement learning progressively increases the difficulty of training environments or tasks, starting with easier problems and gradually introducing harder ones. This mirrors how humans learn complex skills and helps agents avoid getting stuck in local optima.

Q: What is goal-conditioned RL?
A: Goal-conditioned reinforcement learning trains agents to reach different goals by conditioning the policy and reward function on a goal specification. The same policy can pursue different objectives without retraining by changing the goal input.

Q: What is hindsight experience replay?
A: Hindsight experience replay relabels unsuccessful trajectories with the states actually reached as if they were the intended goals, converting failed attempts into successful training examples. This drastically improves learning efficiency in sparse reward goal-reaching tasks.

Q: What is reward modeling?
A: Reward modeling trains a neural network to predict human preferences or task success from state-action trajectories. The learned reward model serves as a proxy for human evaluation during reinforcement learning when direct human feedback is too slow or expensive.

Q: What is the explore-exploit dilemma?
A: The explore-exploit dilemma is the fundamental tension in reinforcement learning between exploring the environment to discover better strategies and exploiting current knowledge to maximize reward. Balancing exploration and exploitation is central to effective reinforcement learning.

Q: What is epsilon greedy exploration?
A: Epsilon greedy exploration selects the current best action with probability one minus epsilon and a random action with probability epsilon. The epsilon parameter controls the exploration rate and is typically annealed over training from high to low values.

Q: What is count-based exploration?
A: Count-based exploration gives bonus rewards for visiting states that have been visited few times, encouraging the agent to explore novel states. Approximate counts using hash functions or density models enable count-based exploration in large continuous state spaces.

Q: What is curiosity-based exploration?
A: Curiosity-based exploration adds an intrinsic reward for prediction errors of a learned dynamics or feature prediction model, rewarding the agent for encountering surprising states where its model is wrong. It drives exploration without requiring environment-specific reward engineering.

Q: What is random network distillation?
A: Random network distillation measures novelty by training a predictor network to match the outputs of a randomly initialized fixed target network. The prediction error serves as an intrinsic curiosity reward, with lower errors indicating more familiar states.



# === DEEP_TECHNICAL_EXTENSION ===

Q: What is the transformer attention complexity?
A: The standard scaled dot-product attention has O(n squared d) time complexity and O(n squared) space complexity where n is sequence length and d is key dimension. This quadratic dependency on sequence length limits the maximum context length transformers can practically handle and motivates efficient attention research.

Q: What is the role of layer normalization in training stability?
A: Layer normalization stabilizes training by normalizing activations to have unit mean and variance before or after each transformer block. It ensures gradient magnitudes remain reasonable throughout the network, prevents activation values from growing or shrinking uncontrollably, and enables higher learning rates without divergence.

Q: How does weight initialization affect training?
A: Weight initialization determines the starting point for gradient descent and strongly influences whether training succeeds or fails. Initialization that produces activations with appropriate scale ensures that neither vanishing gradients preventing early layers from learning nor exploding gradients destabilizing training occur from the very first forward pass.

Q: What is the relationship between model depth and generalization?
A: Deeper models learn hierarchical representations by composing simple transformations into complex ones. Each additional layer can capture increasingly abstract patterns, enabling exponential expressiveness growth with depth. However, deeper models are harder to train, more prone to overfitting, and require careful regularization, normalization, and initialization to realize their theoretical advantages.

Q: What is gradient flow analysis?
A: Gradient flow analysis examines how gradient signals propagate backward through a network during training, identifying layers where gradients vanish or explode. Visualization techniques plot gradient norms per layer, activation statistics, and weight update magnitudes throughout training to diagnose optimization problems and guide architectural improvements.

Q: What is the role of normalization in generalization?
A: Normalization layers like batch normalization and layer normalization improve generalization beyond their training stabilization effects. They act as regularizers by adding noise from batch statistics, reduce covariate shift between layers, and can smooth the loss landscape making it easier for optimization to find flat minima with better generalization properties.

Q: What is neural collapse?
A: Neural collapse is a phenomenon observed in the terminal phase of training where class representations collapse to their class means, class means form an equiangular tight frame with maximum separation, and the classifier weights align with class means. It provides theoretical understanding of how well-trained classifiers organize their representations.

Q: What is grokking in neural networks?
A: Grokking is the phenomenon where a neural network first memorizes training data producing near-zero training loss but high test loss, then suddenly generalizes after extended training. It demonstrates that optimization and generalization can be substantially decoupled and that extended training beyond apparent convergence can produce qualitative behavioral changes.

Q: What is mechanistic interpretability of transformers?
A: Mechanistic interpretability of transformers reverse-engineers specific capabilities to the level of individual attention heads, MLP neurons, and circuits. Research has identified heads specializing in copying, induction, and factual recall, and circuits implementing indirect object identification and mathematical operations in specific model layers.

Q: What is superposition and polysemanticity?
A: Superposition occurs when neural networks represent more features than they have dimensions by encoding multiple features in overlapping directions. This causes polysemanticity where individual neurons activate for multiple unrelated concepts. Sparse autoencoders decompose neural activations to identify monosemantic features hidden within superimposed representations.

Q: What is the lottery ticket in terms of network topology?
A: The lottery ticket hypothesis identifies sparse subnetworks that when trained from their original initialization achieve the same accuracy as the full network. These winning tickets are thought to embody the network topology most suited to the task and are believed to be implicitly identified through magnitude-based pruning after training.

Q: What is adversarial training for robustness?
A: Adversarial training augments the training distribution by generating adversarial examples using PGD or FGSM and training the model to correctly classify them. The model learns to produce smooth predictions in the vicinity of each training example, building robustness to worst-case perturbations at the cost of some accuracy on clean examples.

Q: What is dataset distillation?
A: Dataset distillation learns a small synthetic training set that when used to train a model from scratch produces similar performance to training on the full large dataset. The distilled dataset captures the most essential learning signal, enabling fast model prototyping and neural architecture search.

Q: What is a computational budget for language model inference?
A: The computational budget for language model inference is dominated by the memory bandwidth required to load model weights for each generated token and the FLOPs for attention and feed-forward computation. Longer context and more attention heads increase costs, motivating efficient attention and model compression for deployment.

Q: What is the memory wall in deep learning?
A: The memory wall refers to the growing disparity between compute speed improvements and memory bandwidth improvements in modern hardware. As models grow larger, loading weights from memory increasingly bottlenecks inference performance even when raw compute is sufficient, making memory-efficient model designs and quantization critical for deployment.

Q: What is model parallelism versus expert parallelism?
A: Model parallelism shards the layers of a single model across devices while expert parallelism shards the expert feed-forward networks of a mixture of experts model across devices. Expert parallelism has lower communication overhead because experts for different tokens can be computed independently on different devices.

Q: What is zero redundancy optimizer?
A: ZeRO, Zero Redundancy Optimizer, partitions optimizer states, gradients, and model parameters across data parallel workers rather than replicating them. The three ZeRO stages progressively reduce memory per worker allowing much larger models to be trained without model parallelism overhead.

Q: What is the activation function in large language models?
A: Large language models typically use SwiGLU or GELU activation functions in their feed-forward blocks. SwiGLU combines the Swish activation with a gated linear unit structure that multiplies the activated features with a linear projection, consistently outperforming standard ReLU in language model training.

Q: What is the role of residual connections in transformers?
A: Residual connections in transformers allow the gradient to flow directly from the output to the input of each block, making it easier to learn near-identity transformations in blocks that should not change the representation significantly. They also enable layer outputs to be understood as iterative refinements of an initial embedding.

Q: What is a bottleneck attention in vision transformers?
A: Bottleneck attention is a variation of attention that uses queries of lower dimension than the key-value pairs to reduce computation. By projecting queries to a lower-dimensional space, cross-attention between images and compact memory tokens can be computed much more efficiently than full self-attention.

Q: What is a sparse transformer?
A: A sparse transformer applies attention to only a subset of token pairs using predefined local and strided patterns, reducing attention complexity from quadratic to sub-quadratic or linear in sequence length. Multiple sparse attention layers can propagate information between distant positions through the stack of layers.

Q: What is a memory-efficient attention implementation?
A: Memory-efficient attention implementations like flash attention reorder the attention computation to process blocks of the query, key, value matrices that fit in fast on-chip SRAM rather than loading the full O(n squared) attention matrix. This reduces memory usage from quadratic to linear while computing mathematically identical results.

Q: What is the role of position in transformer output quality?
A: The position of a token significantly influences transformer attention patterns, particularly for long contexts where late positions may receive less attention than early positions. Position encoding design and the maximum training context length both affect how well the model utilizes information at different sequence positions.

Q: What is inference throughput optimization?
A: Inference throughput optimization maximizes the number of tokens or requests processed per second per unit of hardware. Key techniques include continuous batching that fills idle compute during decoding, KV cache management, quantization for faster matrix operations, and kernel fusion that combines operations to reduce memory transactions.

Q: What is continuous batching for LLMs?
A: Continuous batching dynamically adds new requests to a batch as previous requests finish generating their sequences, keeping GPU utilization high throughout the generation process. Unlike static batching that waits for all requests in a batch to complete before starting new ones, it significantly improves throughput.

Q: What is speculation acceptance rate?
A: The speculation acceptance rate in speculative decoding is the fraction of draft model tokens accepted by the verification pass of the large target model. Higher acceptance rates indicate better draft quality and greater speedup. The rate depends on how well the small draft model approximates the target model's distribution.

Q: What is quantization calibration?
A: Quantization calibration determines the optimal quantization ranges for weights and activations using a small calibration dataset run through the model. Calibration techniques include min-max which uses extreme observed values and percentile which ignores outliers, trading off representation accuracy for outlier handling.

Q: What is GGUF format?
