A: GGUF is a file format for storing quantized large language models used by llama.cpp and related inference frameworks. It stores model weights in various quantization formats from 2-bit to 8-bit integer, enabling efficient CPU and GPU inference of large models on consumer hardware.

Q: What is AWQ quantization?
A: Activation-aware Weight Quantization identifies the most important weight channels by analyzing activation magnitudes and protects them from quantization error by scaling before quantization. This activation-aware approach significantly reduces quantization error compared to methods that treat all weights equally.

Q: What is GPTQ?
A: GPTQ is a post-training quantization method that quantizes transformer weights layer by layer by minimizing the reconstruction error of each layer's output using second-order information from the Hessian. It achieves accurate 4-bit and 3-bit quantization enabling large model inference on consumer GPUs.



# === SPECIALIZED_DOMAINS ===

Q: What is Bayesian deep learning?
A: Bayesian deep learning places probability distributions over neural network weights rather than point estimates, capturing uncertainty in model parameters. Instead of a single set of weights the model maintains a posterior distribution updated from data. This enables principled uncertainty quantification, automatic regularization through the prior, and principled model comparison via marginal likelihood. Approximate inference methods including variational inference, Monte Carlo dropout, deep ensembles, and Laplace approximation make Bayesian deep learning computationally tractable for large networks.

Q: What is Monte Carlo dropout for uncertainty?
A: Monte Carlo dropout uses dropout at test time as an approximate Bayesian inference technique. By running the network multiple times with different dropout masks and averaging the predictions, the variance of outputs estimates epistemic uncertainty about the model's parameters. The method is computationally cheap since no architecture changes are required, and the dropout rate directly controls how broad the approximate posterior is over network weights.

Q: What is a Bayesian neural network?
A: A Bayesian neural network replaces deterministic weight matrices with probability distributions, typically Gaussians parameterized by mean and variance. During inference stochastic weights are sampled and predictions are integrated over the posterior. Training optimizes the variational lower bound combining reconstruction accuracy with KL divergence between the approximate posterior and prior. Bayesian neural networks naturally regularize through the prior and quantify predictive uncertainty by propagating weight uncertainty through the network.

Q: What is epistemic versus aleatoric uncertainty?
A: Epistemic uncertainty arises from model ignorance due to insufficient training data and can in principle be reduced by collecting more data. Aleatoric uncertainty arises from irreducible noise inherent in the data generation process, such as measurement noise, and cannot be reduced by more training data. Bayesian deep learning models both types: epistemic through uncertainty over model parameters and aleatoric through stochastic output distributions conditioned on the predicted parameters.

Q: What is the Laplace approximation for neural networks?
A: The Laplace approximation fits a Gaussian distribution centered at the MAP estimate of neural network weights with covariance given by the inverse Hessian of the negative log posterior. The Hessian is typically approximated using the Kronecker-factored curvature or diagonal approximation for computational tractability. The resulting Gaussian posterior enables cheap uncertainty estimation at test time by propagating the weight uncertainty analytically.

Q: What is a variational autoencoder from a Bayesian perspective?
A: From a Bayesian perspective a variational autoencoder performs approximate posterior inference over a continuous latent variable model. The encoder network parameterizes the variational approximate posterior over latent codes given observations. The decoder network parameterizes the likelihood of observations given latent codes. Training maximizes the evidence lower bound combining expected log likelihood with the negative KL divergence between the approximate posterior and prior over latent codes.

Q: What is deep ensembles for uncertainty?
A: Deep ensembles train multiple neural networks with different random initializations and aggregate their predictions. The variance across ensemble members estimates predictive uncertainty with different members having converged to different local minima of the loss landscape. Ensembles are computationally expensive requiring N times the inference cost but provide reliable uncertainty estimates and strong calibration often competitive with more sophisticated Bayesian methods.

Q: What is uncertainty calibration in Bayesian models?
A: Uncertainty calibration in Bayesian models assesses whether the model's predicted confidence levels match empirical accuracy. A perfectly calibrated Bayesian model produces predictive intervals that contain the true value at the stated frequency. Evaluation using reliability diagrams and expected calibration error reveals systematic over-confidence or under-confidence that can be corrected through temperature scaling or other post-hoc calibration methods.

Q: What is Bayes optimal prediction?
A: Bayes optimal prediction integrates over all possible parameter values weighted by their posterior probability, producing the minimum expected loss prediction. For classification this means averaging predicted class probabilities over the parameter posterior rather than using a single best parameter estimate. Exact Bayes optimal prediction is typically intractable and approximate methods like Monte Carlo sampling or variational inference are used.

Q: What is the prior in Bayesian neural networks?
A: The prior in Bayesian neural networks encodes beliefs about weight values before observing data. Common choices include isotropic Gaussians that implement L2 regularization and sparsity-inducing priors like spike-and-slab that encourage many near-zero weights. The prior choice affects regularization strength and can encode structural beliefs about the network. Learning priors from data through empirical Bayes can adapt the prior to the specific problem domain.

Q: What is natural language uncertainty?
A: Natural language uncertainty refers to the inability of a language model to express its own uncertainty about factual claims. Models trained to generate fluent text may state uncertain or false information with the same confident tone as certain facts. Uncertainty-aware language models trained to hedge claims, express ranges, and abstain from low-confidence predictions better communicate reliability to users.

Q: What is conformal prediction for Bayesian models?
A: Conformal prediction provides distribution-free prediction sets with guaranteed coverage regardless of the underlying model. Applied to Bayesian models it wraps the posterior predictive distribution with a coverage guarantee calibrated on held-out data. The resulting prediction sets contain the true label with at least a user-specified probability providing valid uncertainty quantification without requiring correct specification of the prior or likelihood.

Q: What is out-of-distribution uncertainty?
A: Out-of-distribution uncertainty measures how confident a model is when inputs differ from the training distribution. Well-calibrated uncertainty methods should produce higher uncertainty for OOD inputs than in-distribution inputs. Deep ensembles and Monte Carlo dropout can detect OOD inputs through high predictive variance while deterministic models with distance-aware heads use feature space distances to quantify unfamiliarity.

Q: What is a normalizing flow for variational inference?
A: Normalizing flows are sequences of invertible transformations applied to a simple base distribution to produce a flexible approximate posterior. Each transformation is invertible with a tractable Jacobian determinant enabling exact density computation. Using normalizing flows as the variational approximate posterior produces more expressive posteriors than the factored Gaussians used in standard mean field variational inference.

Q: What is stochastic variational inference?
A: Stochastic variational inference estimates the gradient of the evidence lower bound using mini-batches of data rather than the full dataset. This enables variational inference to scale to large datasets where exact computation of the ELBO gradient is prohibitive. Reparameterization of the variational distribution enables low-variance gradient estimates that support efficient stochastic optimization.

Q: What is FedAvg algorithm?
A: FedAvg, Federated Averaging, is the seminal federated learning algorithm that trains models across distributed clients by having each client perform multiple local gradient descent steps then sending weight updates to a central server. The server averages the updates from all participating clients to produce a new global model. This averaging reduces communication rounds compared to a single gradient step per client while maintaining convergence guarantees under mild assumptions.

Q: What is non-IID data in federated learning?
A: Non-IID data in federated learning refers to situations where the data distribution differs across clients, violating the independent and identically distributed assumption. Clients may have different class proportions, covariate distributions, or label definitions. Non-IID data causes client model updates to point in conflicting directions leading to slower convergence, higher variance, and model performance that may be poor for some clients after aggregation.

Q: What is client drift in federated learning?
A: Client drift occurs in federated learning when local optimization on heterogeneous client data causes client model updates to diverge from the global optimum. After many local gradient steps on biased local data each client's model moves toward its own local optimum rather than the global one. Variance reduction methods and correction terms address client drift to improve convergence.

Q: What is FedProx?
A: FedProx addresses the challenge of heterogeneous clients in federated learning by adding a proximal term to the local objective that penalizes deviation of local model parameters from the global model. This prevents excessive client drift by keeping local updates within a bounded distance of the starting global model even when clients perform different numbers of local steps.

Q: What is SCAFFOLD in federated learning?
A: SCAFFOLD uses control variates to correct client drift by maintaining per-client correction terms that account for the difference between local and global gradient estimates. The correction terms accumulated during local training shift the client's effective gradient toward what it would be if computing on the global data distribution.

Q: What is federated averaging with momentum?
A: Federated averaging with server-side momentum applies a momentum buffer at the aggregation server, accumulating a momentum term from successive rounds of aggregated client updates. Server momentum accelerates convergence and improves the model's ability to escape sharp minima despite the noisy client updates.

Q: What is personalized federated learning?
A: Personalized federated learning learns models tailored to individual client distributions rather than a single global model. Methods include fine-tuning the global model on local data, learning per-client adapter modules, and meta-learning a global initialization designed for fast local adaptation. Personalization trades off global knowledge sharing against local customization.

Q: What is model heterogeneity in federated learning?
A: Model heterogeneity in federated learning allows different clients to have different model architectures suited to their device capabilities, since not all devices can run the same large model. Knowledge distillation and representation alignment enable aggregation across architecturally diverse client models without requiring all clients to share identical network structures.

Q: What is differential privacy in federated learning?
A: Differential privacy in federated learning protects individual training examples from being inferred through model updates by adding calibrated Gaussian noise to client gradients before aggregation. The privacy budget epsilon controls the noise magnitude and the privacy-accuracy tradeoff. Secure aggregation ensures the server sees only the noisy aggregate rather than individual client updates.

Q: What is secure aggregation?
A: Secure aggregation uses cryptographic protocols such as secret sharing and homomorphic encryption to aggregate client updates at the server without the server ever seeing individual client contributions in plaintext. This prevents the server from learning private information about any individual client while still computing the aggregate necessary for model updating.

Q: What is communication efficiency in federated learning?
A: Communication efficiency is a critical challenge in federated learning because clients often connect via limited bandwidth networks. Gradient compression through sparsification and quantization, local training to amortize communication over more computation, and model update pruning reduce the data transmitted per communication round.

Q: What is federated learning evaluation?
A: Federated learning evaluation must account for heterogeneous client distributions by measuring not only overall aggregate performance but also per-client performance distributions. Fairness metrics assess whether the global model performs similarly across diverse clients or disproportionately favors large or high-resource clients.

Q: What is vertical federated learning?
A: Vertical federated learning handles scenarios where different clients hold different features for the same set of users rather than different users. Clients collaborate to train a model using features stored at different parties without sharing the raw features, using cryptographic protocols to compute forward and backward passes across the vertically partitioned dataset.

Q: What is split learning?
A: Split learning partitions a neural network between clients and a server, with clients computing forward passes through early layers and sending intermediate activations to the server for completion. The server computes the loss and sends gradients back to clients. Split learning reduces client compute requirements but requires sending potentially sensitive intermediate representations to the server.

Q: What is flower framework?
A: Flower is an open-source federated learning framework providing a strategy-based abstraction for implementing federation algorithms. It supports diverse clients including mobile devices, supports any ML framework including PyTorch and TensorFlow, and provides simulation capabilities for rapid development and evaluation of new federation strategies.

Q: What is neurosymbolic AI?
A: Neurosymbolic AI combines the pattern recognition strengths of neural networks with the logical reasoning and interpretability of symbolic AI systems. Neural components learn representations from data while symbolic components provide structured reasoning, knowledge representation, and guarantees. This hybrid approach aims to achieve both flexible generalization and reliable logical reasoning that neither approach achieves alone.

Q: What is neural theorem proving?
A: Neural theorem proving uses neural networks to guide symbolic theorem proving search by learning to predict which lemmas, axioms, and proof steps are most likely to lead to a successful proof. The neural component replaces handcrafted heuristics with learned patterns from successful proofs, dramatically accelerating automated reasoning.

Q: What is differentiable programming?
A: Differentiable programming extends automatic differentiation beyond neural networks to programs containing arbitrary control flow, data structures, and algorithms. By making program operations differentiable, gradient descent can be applied to optimize program parameters end-to-end, blending imperative programming with gradient-based learning.

Q: What is neural logic programming?
A: Neural logic programming integrates neural networks with logic programming frameworks, enabling reasoning over learned representations. Neural networks learn entity and relation embeddings from data while logic rules perform inference over these representations, combining flexible pattern matching with structured logical deduction.

Q: What is program induction?
A: Program induction learns programs from input-output examples by searching over a space of programs defined by a domain-specific language. Neural program induction uses neural networks to guide the program search, learning priors over programs from training examples that generalize compositionally to novel inputs.

Q: What is abstract reasoning in AI?
A: Abstract reasoning involves identifying patterns, analogies, and rules from limited examples and applying them to novel situations. Tasks like IQ test matrices and Raven's Progressive Matrices test abstract visual reasoning. Neural networks struggle with systematic compositional generalization while symbolic systems handle it naturally.

Q: What is compositional generalization?
A: Compositional generalization is the ability to understand and produce novel combinations of known components, applying rules learned from training examples to systematically new combinations. Neural networks often fail at compositional generalization while symbolic systems and hybrid neurosymbolic approaches handle it more reliably.

Q: What is a knowledge base for AI?
A: A knowledge base is a structured repository of facts, rules, and relationships about a domain that an AI system can query and reason over. It combines a knowledge schema defining entity types and relationship types with instance data populated from manual curation, information extraction, or reasoning over existing facts.

Q: What is symbolic regression?
A: Symbolic regression searches for mathematical expressions in the form of programs or formulas that best fit numerical data. Genetic programming evolves expression trees while neural approaches learn to generate symbolic formulas directly. It produces interpretable equations that can reveal the governing laws underlying scientific datasets.

Q: What is a reasoning engine?
A: A reasoning engine is a computational system that applies logical inference rules to a knowledge base to derive new facts and answer queries. Forward chaining derives all possible conclusions from known facts while backward chaining searches for proofs of specific goals. Modern reasoning engines combine symbolic logic with statistical scoring for uncertain knowledge.

Q: What is abductive reasoning in AI?
A: Abductive reasoning generates the most plausible explanation for observed evidence, choosing the hypothesis that best explains the observations. It underlies diagnostic reasoning in medicine, fault detection in engineering, and visual scene interpretation. Combining neural perception with symbolic abduction enables robust grounded reasoning.

Q: What is deductive reasoning in AI?
A: Deductive reasoning applies general rules to specific cases to derive guaranteed conclusions. Given true premises and valid logical rules, deductive reasoning produces conclusions that must be true. Automated deduction using theorem provers and constraint satisfaction solvers implements deductive reasoning in AI systems.

Q: What is inductive reasoning in AI?
A: Inductive reasoning generalizes from specific examples to general rules, the foundation of machine learning. Neural networks perform inductive reasoning implicitly through gradient descent on training examples. Inductive logic programming explicitly learns logical rules from positive and negative examples.

Q: What is a concept bottleneck model?
A: A concept bottleneck model predicts human-interpretable concepts as an intermediate representation then uses these concepts to predict the final task label. It enables human experts to inspect and correct the predicted concepts, improving transparency and enabling test-time intervention to fix errors.

Q: What is neuro-symbolic concept learner?
A: A neuro-symbolic concept learner jointly learns visual concepts from images and relational reasoning programs from questions, grounding symbolic programs in learned neural visual representations. It answers visual questions by parsing the question into a symbolic program and executing it over perceived scene representations.

Q: What is AlphaFold?
A: AlphaFold is a deep learning system from DeepMind that predicts three-dimensional protein structures from amino acid sequences with atomic accuracy. It uses a transformer architecture called Evoformer processing multiple sequence alignments and pair representations, followed by a structure module that directly predicts 3D atomic coordinates. AlphaFold2 achieved a breakthrough at CASP14 predicting most protein structures within experimental accuracy, transforming structural biology.

Q: What is molecular property prediction?
A: Molecular property prediction uses machine learning to predict chemical and biological properties of molecules from their structure, including drug-like properties, binding affinities, toxicity, and solubility. Graph neural networks operating on molecular graphs where atoms are nodes and bonds are edges have become the dominant approach, learning atom and bond feature representations that aggregate to molecular-level predictions.

Q: What is de novo drug design?
A: De novo drug design uses generative machine learning models to create novel molecular structures with desired drug-like properties from scratch. Variational autoencoders, recurrent networks generating SMILES strings, and graph generative models explore chemical space guided by property predictors, enabling discovery of candidate molecules with target activity and selectivity profiles.

Q: What is SMILES representation?
A: SMILES, Simplified Molecular Input Line Entry System, is a string notation encoding molecular structure as a sequence of characters where atoms are represented by their element symbols and bonds by single, double, and triple bond characters. Language models trained on SMILES strings can generate novel valid molecules and learn molecular similarity relationships.

Q: What is a molecular graph?
A: A molecular graph represents a chemical compound as a graph where nodes correspond to atoms with features like atomic number and charge, and edges correspond to chemical bonds with features like bond order and aromaticity. Graph neural networks process molecular graphs to learn atom and bond representations useful for molecular property prediction.

Q: What is climate modeling with ML?
A: Machine learning for climate modeling accelerates and improves predictions of weather patterns, extreme events, and long-term climate dynamics. Neural networks trained on historical climate data and physics simulations learn to emulate computationally expensive climate model components, and data-driven models like FourCastNet and GraphCast predict weather more accurately than traditional numerical methods at a fraction of the cost.

Q: What is particle physics ML?
A: Machine learning in particle physics classifies particle collision events, identifies rare signal processes in high-dimensional detector data, and reconstructs particle trajectories. Graph neural networks that process detector hits as point clouds and variational autoencoders learning latent representations of particle jets have significantly improved signal sensitivity in large hadron collider experiments.

Q: What is materials discovery with ML?
A: Machine learning accelerates discovery of novel materials with desired properties by predicting stability, conductivity, and mechanical properties from crystal structure without expensive first-principles calculations. Crystal graph neural networks and large pretrained models fine-tuned on materials databases enable high-throughput virtual screening of millions of candidate materials.

Q: What is genomics machine learning?
A: Machine learning in genomics identifies functional elements in DNA sequences, predicts gene expression from sequence, classifies genetic variants by pathogenicity, and reconstructs regulatory networks from single-cell data. Transformer models pretrained on large genomic sequence databases learn evolutionary conservation signals enabling accurate annotation of unannotated genomes.

Q: What is medical image analysis?
A: Medical image analysis uses deep learning to interpret X-rays, CT scans, MRIs, and pathology slides for detection, segmentation, and classification of diseases. CNNs and vision transformers trained on large annotated medical image datasets achieve radiologist-level performance on specific tasks like diabetic retinopathy grading and skin lesion classification.

Q: What is electronic health record ML?
A: Machine learning on electronic health records extracts clinical insights from structured laboratory values, vital signs, medication histories, and unstructured clinical notes. Temporal models predict clinical deterioration, readmission risk, and diagnosis codes, enabling early intervention and care coordination.

Q: What is single cell RNA sequencing analysis?
A: Single-cell RNA sequencing profiles gene expression in individual cells enabling characterization of cell types, developmental trajectories, and disease-associated expression changes. Deep learning models compress high-dimensional expression profiles into low-dimensional embeddings for clustering, trajectory inference, and cross-dataset integration.

Q: What is drug-target interaction prediction?
A: Drug-target interaction prediction estimates the binding affinity between drug compounds and protein targets using machine learning models trained on known interaction data. Graph neural networks jointly encoding molecular graphs and protein sequence or structure graphs predict novel interactions enabling virtual screening for drug repurposing and lead optimization.

Q: What is scientific foundation model?
A: A scientific foundation model is a large pretrained model trained on diverse scientific data including literature, molecular structures, protein sequences, and experimental data. Fine-tuned on specific scientific tasks it leverages broad scientific knowledge learned during pretraining, analogously to how language model pretraining helps downstream NLP tasks.

Q: What is simulation-based inference?
A: Simulation-based inference, or likelihood-free inference, infers parameters of complex scientific models defined only as simulators without tractable likelihoods. Neural density estimators trained on simulator outputs learn to approximate the posterior over model parameters given observations, enabling Bayesian inference for complex physical systems.

Q: What is physics-informed neural network?
A: Physics-informed neural networks encode known physical laws described by partial differential equations into the neural network training objective as additional loss terms penalizing violations of the governing equations. This constraint reduces the data required for training and ensures physically consistent predictions that satisfy known conservation laws.

Q: What is equivariant neural network?
A: An equivariant neural network transforms its output in a predictable way when its input is transformed by a symmetry operation such as rotation or translation. SE(3) equivariant networks for 3D molecular structures produce consistent predictions regardless of molecular orientation, leveraging physical symmetry to improve sample efficiency and generalization.

Q: What is a neural operator?
A: A neural operator learns mappings between infinite-dimensional function spaces rather than finite-dimensional vectors, enabling solution of parametric partial differential equations. Fourier neural operators apply spectral convolution in the frequency domain, learning to map initial conditions or boundary conditions to solutions for diverse PDE families.

Q: What is graph neural network for molecular dynamics?
A: Graph neural networks for molecular dynamics predict interatomic forces and energies from atomic configurations, enabling fast molecular dynamics simulations compared to expensive quantum chemistry calculations. Models like SchNet and DimeNet encode geometric features including distances and angles between atoms equivariantly for accurate force field learning.

Q: What is natural language processing for scientific literature?
A: NLP for scientific literature extracts knowledge from the rapidly growing body of research papers, identifying entities, relationships, claims, and experimental evidence. Models fine-tuned on scientific text like SciBERT and PubMedBERT outperform general language models on biomedical named entity recognition, relation extraction, and question answering.

Q: What is clinical decision support?
A: Clinical decision support systems use machine learning to assist healthcare providers with diagnosis, treatment selection, and monitoring decisions. They integrate patient data from EHRs, lab results, and imaging to provide alerts, recommendations, and risk scores that improve care quality and reduce errors. Regulatory requirements mandate explainability and validation before deployment in clinical workflows.

Q: What is disease prediction from EHRs?
A: Disease prediction from electronic health records trains temporal models on longitudinal patient data including diagnoses, medications, and lab values to predict future diagnoses, complications, and clinical events. Transformer models treating EHR events as tokens learn patient representations enabling predictions across diverse clinical outcomes.

Q: What is radiology AI?
A: Radiology AI uses deep learning to analyze medical images including X-rays, CT scans, and MRIs for detection, segmentation, and characterization of pathologies. Models trained on large annotated radiology datasets achieve expert-level performance on specific tasks and can flag abnormal findings, prioritize urgent cases, and reduce radiologist workload.

Q: What is pathology AI?
A: Computational pathology applies deep learning to digitized histological slides to classify tumor types, grade cancer severity, and identify predictive biomarkers. Multiple instance learning handles the gigapixel scale of whole slide images by aggregating predictions from patches without requiring dense pixel-level annotations.

Q: What is patient risk stratification?
A: Patient risk stratification uses machine learning to classify patients into risk groups for outcomes like readmission, deterioration, or disease progression. Models trained on clinical data enable targeted interventions for high-risk patients, improving care efficiency and outcomes while reducing unnecessary interventions for low-risk patients.

Q: What is federated learning for healthcare?
A: Federated learning in healthcare enables training models on data from multiple hospitals without sharing patient records, addressing privacy regulations and data governance barriers. Participating institutions each train locally and contribute model updates, allowing models to learn from diverse patient populations while maintaining local data control.

Q: What is medical image segmentation?
A: Medical image segmentation delineates organs, lesions, and anatomical structures in medical images at the pixel level. U-Net and its variants using encoder-decoder architecture with skip connections are the dominant models for medical segmentation, trained on annotated volumes from CT, MRI, and ultrasound modalities.

Q: What is few-shot learning in medical imaging?
A: Few-shot learning in medical imaging addresses the scarcity of labeled medical data by training models to learn new pathologies from very few annotated examples. Meta-learning approaches like prototypical networks learn embeddings where same-class examples cluster, enabling rapid adaptation to new diseases with minimal new labeled examples.

Q: What is longitudinal patient modeling?
A: Longitudinal patient modeling tracks individual patient trajectories over time using recurrent or transformer models trained on sequences of clinical events. These models learn patient-specific dynamics enabling personalized predictions of disease progression, treatment response, and clinical deterioration that improve on cross-sectional models.

Q: What is survival prediction in oncology?
A: Survival prediction in oncology estimates the probability of patient survival over time from clinical, genomic, and imaging features. Deep survival models extend classical Cox regression to learn nonlinear risk factors from high-dimensional data while handling censored observations where patient outcomes are not yet observed.

Q: What is medical knowledge graph?
A: Medical knowledge graphs encode relationships between diseases, symptoms, drugs, genes, and biological pathways as a structured network. Machine learning systems query these graphs to support diagnosis by identifying related conditions, find potential drug targets, and explain model predictions in terms of known biological mechanisms.

Q: What is synthetic medical data?
A: Synthetic medical data is artificially generated patient data that statistically resembles real clinical data without containing actual patient information. Generative models like GANs and VAEs trained on real EHRs produce synthetic datasets that can be shared for research and model training while satisfying privacy regulations.

Q: What is transfer learning in medical imaging?
A: Transfer learning in medical imaging initializes models with weights pretrained on natural image datasets like ImageNet and fine-tunes on medical images. Despite domain differences the pretrained features provide a stronger starting point than random initialization, enabling competitive performance with limited labeled medical data.

Q: What is continual learning in clinical AI?
A: Continual learning in clinical AI updates models over time as new patient data arrives and clinical practices change, without forgetting previously learned patterns. It is essential for clinical AI systems that must remain accurate as patient populations, diagnostic criteria, and treatment protocols evolve.

Q: What is clinical NLP?
A: Clinical NLP extracts structured information from unstructured clinical notes, discharge summaries, and radiology reports. Applications include medical coding, clinical trial matching, adverse drug event detection, and question answering over patient records. Models pretrained on clinical text like BioBERT and ClinicalBERT outperform general language models.

Q: What is physiological signal processing with ML?
A: Machine learning for physiological signal processing analyzes continuous time series from sensors like ECG, EEG, and pulse oximetry to detect arrhythmias, seizures, and sleep disorders. Deep learning models trained on large archives of annotated physiological recordings learn discriminative temporal features for reliable clinical monitoring.

Q: What is drug adverse event detection?
A: Drug adverse event detection mines social media, EHRs, and clinical trial reports for signals of unexpected drug side effects using NLP and pharmacovigilance models. Early detection of adverse events enables regulatory action, prescribing changes, and improved patient safety monitoring.

Q: What is clinical trial matching?
A: Clinical trial matching uses NLP and machine learning to automatically identify patients eligible for clinical trials from their EHR data by matching patient characteristics against trial inclusion and exclusion criteria expressed in natural language. Automated matching accelerates trial enrollment and enables broader patient access.

Q: What is population health management?
A: Population health management applies machine learning to identify at-risk patients across a population, predict future healthcare utilization, and design targeted interventions. Predictive models integrate claims data, clinical records, and social determinants of health to enable proactive care for diverse patient populations.

Q: What is wearable device ML?
A: Machine learning for wearable devices analyzes continuous streams of sensor data including accelerometry, heart rate variability, and skin conductance to monitor health, detect falls, classify activities, and identify early signs of disease. Models must handle noise, missing data, and the heterogeneity of individual physiological baselines.

Q: What is credit scoring with machine learning?
A: Credit scoring uses machine learning to predict the probability that a borrower will default on a loan by learning from historical borrower characteristics and repayment behavior. Gradient boosting methods consistently outperform logistic regression on this task. Regulatory requirements for model explainability, fairness across demographic groups, and consistency with established credit scoring frameworks constrain which models can be deployed.

Q: What is fraud detection?
A: Fraud detection identifies suspicious transactions or activities in financial data using anomaly detection and classification models. Real-time transaction data streams are scored against models trained on historical fraud cases, with graph neural networks identifying fraud rings through transaction network topology and sequential models detecting unusual spending pattern sequences.

Q: What is algorithmic trading?
A: Algorithmic trading uses machine learning models to automatically execute financial trades based on predicted price movements, market signals, and portfolio optimization objectives. Strategies range from high-frequency trading reacting to order book microstructure to lower-frequency models predicting asset returns from fundamental and alternative data.

Q: What is portfolio optimization with ML?
A: Machine learning enhanced portfolio optimization predicts asset return covariances and expected returns using historical and alternative data, then optimizes portfolio weights to maximize risk-adjusted return. Deep learning for covariance estimation and reinforcement learning for dynamic allocation extend classical mean-variance optimization to nonlinear relationships.

Q: What is market microstructure?
A: Market microstructure studies how trading mechanisms, information, and individual trading behaviors interact to determine prices and market quality. Machine learning models analyze order book data, trade flows, and price impact to understand liquidity, price discovery, and optimal execution strategies.

Q: What is alternative data in finance?
A: Alternative data refers to non-traditional data sources used to gain investment insights, including satellite imagery of retail parking lots, credit card transaction data, social media sentiment, and web scraping. Machine learning extracts signals from these high-dimensional unstructured sources that complement traditional financial statements.

Q: What is sentiment analysis for finance?
A: Financial sentiment analysis extracts market-relevant signals from text sources including earnings calls, analyst reports, news articles, and social media. Models fine-tuned on financial text classify sentiment, extract key entities and events, and predict stock price reactions to information disclosures.

Q: What is default prediction?
A: Default prediction models estimate the probability that a company or individual will fail to meet financial obligations. Models trained on financial ratios, payment histories, and macroeconomic indicators provide early warning signals for risk management. Survival analysis methods handle the time-to-default aspect of credit risk.

Q: What is asset price prediction?
A: Asset price prediction uses machine learning to forecast future prices or returns of financial instruments from historical price data, macroeconomic indicators, and alternative data. LSTM networks, temporal convolutional networks, and transformer models learn temporal patterns in financial time series.

Q: What is risk management with ML?
A: Machine learning enhances financial risk management by improving the accuracy of value at risk estimates, identifying tail risks from non-Gaussian return distributions, modeling credit risk correlations in portfolios, and detecting emerging systemic risks from financial network topology.

Q: What is natural language processing for financial filings?
A: NLP for financial filings extracts quantitative and qualitative information from SEC filings, earnings reports, and regulatory documents. Sentiment analysis, key entity extraction, and event detection from these documents provide investment signals and enable monitoring of disclosures for regulatory compliance.

Q: What is explainable AI in credit decisions?
A: Explainable AI in credit decisions satisfies regulatory requirements to provide applicants with meaningful explanations for adverse credit decisions. SHAP values and LIME explanations identify which features most influenced a denial, enabling institutions to communicate reasons and identify potentially discriminatory patterns.

Q: What is market regime detection?
A: Market regime detection identifies distinct periods of market behavior such as bull markets, bear markets, and high volatility regimes using hidden Markov models, clustering, or recurrent neural networks trained on market indicators. Regime-aware models adapt trading and risk strategies to current market conditions.

Q: What is option pricing with neural networks?
A: Neural network option pricing trains models to predict option prices or implied volatilities from option characteristics and underlying asset features, learning the pricing relationship from market data rather than assuming parametric models like Black-Scholes. Deep learning captures complex nonlinear pricing patterns including volatility smile and skew.

Q: What is systemic risk prediction?
A: Systemic risk prediction uses machine learning to identify vulnerabilities in the financial system that could amplify shocks into widespread crises. Graph neural networks model interconnections between financial institutions and predict contagion spread while temporal models identify early warning signs from liquidity and leverage indicators.

Q: What is reinforcement learning for trading?
A: Reinforcement learning for trading trains agents to make buy, sell, and hold decisions in financial markets by maximizing risk-adjusted cumulative returns. The environment is the market, actions are trade orders, and rewards are position returns. Challenges include non-stationarity, partial observability, and transaction cost modeling.

Q: What is anomaly detection in financial transactions?
A: Anomaly detection in financial transactions identifies unusual patterns that may indicate fraud, money laundering, or system errors using statistical outlier detection and autoencoder reconstruction error. Real-time scoring of transaction streams requires both high accuracy and low latency to minimize fraud losses.

Q: What is credit risk stress testing?
A: Credit risk stress testing evaluates how a portfolio would perform under adverse economic scenarios by applying machine learning models trained to predict credit losses under different macroeconomic conditions. Regulatory stress tests require models that are robust, interpretable, and consistent across economic scenarios.

Q: What is insurance pricing with ML?
A: Machine learning improves insurance pricing accuracy by learning complex nonlinear relationships between risk factors and claim probabilities from large historical datasets. Gradient boosting and neural networks estimate individual risk more accurately than traditional actuarial models while regulatory requirements constrain the use of protected attributes.

Q: What is anti-money laundering ML?
A: Anti-money laundering models detect suspicious financial transactions and networks that may represent money laundering schemes using graph neural networks to analyze transaction flows between accounts and temporal models to identify unusual patterns in transaction sequences that deviate from customer behavioral baselines.

Q: What is a language model agent?
A: A language model agent is a system that uses a language model as its reasoning engine to plan, decide, and act in the world by generating text that controls tools, APIs, and other capabilities. The agent perceives observations from the environment, reasons about them, and emits actions to achieve multi-step goals. Agents can browse the web, execute code, query databases, and call external services.

Q: What is ReAct prompting?
A: ReAct combines reasoning and acting by prompting language models to interleave thinking steps with action steps in a chain of thought format. The model first reasons about what to do then emits a formatted action, receives an observation, then reasons again. This synergy between reasoning and acting improves performance on tasks requiring information lookup and multi-step decision making.

Q: What is the plan and execute agent?
A: Plan and execute agents first generate a complete multi-step plan for achieving a goal then execute each step sequentially, re-planning if unexpected results occur. Separating planning from execution enables using a larger model for high-level planning and a smaller model for execution steps, reducing cost while maintaining quality.

Q: What is tool use in language model agents?
A: Tool use enables language model agents to extend their capabilities by calling external functions including web search, code execution, calculators, APIs, and databases. The model is trained or prompted to generate structured tool call expressions in a predefined format and process the returned results to continue reasoning.

Q: What is function calling in OpenAI API?
A: Function calling in the OpenAI API allows language models to generate structured JSON arguments for predefined functions based on the conversation context. The application executes the function and returns results to the model for continued reasoning. It reliably structures model outputs for programmatic processing compared to parsing unstructured text.

Q: What is memory in AI agents?
A: Memory in AI agents stores information across interactions enabling long-term context beyond the context window. Short-term memory holds the current conversation context, long-term memory stores persistent facts and experiences in retrieval systems, and external memory databases enable access to large knowledge repositories through semantic retrieval.

Q: What is a vector store for agent memory?
A: A vector store for agent memory indexes past observations, facts, and tool results as dense embeddings enabling semantic retrieval of relevant memories given a query. The agent retrieves the most relevant past context to include in the current prompt, extending its effective memory beyond the fixed context window.

Q: What is agent orchestration?
A: Agent orchestration manages the coordination of multiple specialized agents working together on complex tasks. An orchestrator agent assigns subtasks to specialized agents, aggregates their outputs, and synthesizes a final result. Orchestration frameworks handle agent communication, state management, and error recovery across multi-agent workflows.

Q: What is multi-agent systems?
A: Multi-agent systems involve multiple autonomous agents that interact and cooperate to solve problems that individual agents cannot handle alone. In language model multi-agent systems, multiple LLM-powered agents with different roles, tools, and knowledge collaborate through structured communication protocols.

Q: What is chain of thought in agents?
A: Chain of thought in agents refers to the explicit reasoning steps the agent generates before taking actions, allowing it to decompose complex goals, consider alternatives, and reflect on past steps. Generating intermediate reasoning improves decision quality for complex multi-step tasks requiring planning and causal reasoning.

Q: What is self-reflection in AI agents?
A: Self-reflection enables AI agents to critique their own outputs and reasoning, identifying errors and generating improved responses through additional reasoning passes. Reflexion and similar frameworks prompt agents to evaluate their actions and update their plans based on successes and failures accumulated across multiple attempts.

Q: What is the Reflexion framework?
A: Reflexion is an agent framework that improves task performance by allowing agents to verbally reflect on trial-and-error feedback from failed attempts. After each unsuccessful attempt the agent reflects on what went wrong and updates its strategy for the next attempt, accumulating experience across multiple trials.

Q: What is AutoGPT?
A: AutoGPT is an early autonomous language model agent that chains language model calls in a loop to complete long-horizon goals specified in natural language. It autonomously breaks down goals into subtasks, executes them using tools, monitors results, and adapts its plan until the goal is achieved or it runs out of steps.

Q: What is agent evaluation?
A: Agent evaluation assesses how reliably and efficiently an agent accomplishes complex multi-step tasks requiring planning, tool use, and error recovery. Benchmarks like WebArena, AgentBench, and GAIA evaluate agents on realistic tasks spanning web browsing, code execution, and information synthesis with ground truth outcomes.

Q: What is error recovery in agents?
A: Error recovery enables agents to detect when tool calls fail or produce unexpected results and adapt their plans accordingly rather than simply failing. Robust agents anticipate failure modes, have fallback strategies, and can re-plan from intermediate states when individual steps do not succeed as expected.

Q: What is code execution in agents?
A: Code execution agents write and run Python code to perform calculations, data analysis, and transformations beyond what language models can do in text. The code interpreter tool executes generated code in a sandbox and returns stdout, stderr, and any generated files back to the model for continued reasoning.

Q: What is web browsing agent?
A: Web browsing agents use language models to navigate websites, fill forms, extract information, and complete online tasks by generating actions over a browser interface. They combine vision capabilities to perceive page content with language understanding to interpret instructions and act on web pages.

Q: What is retrieval in agents?
A: Retrieval in agents enables language model agents to access large knowledge bases, document collections, and databases by embedding queries and retrieving relevant context through semantic search. Retrieval augments the agent's knowledge beyond its training data and context window.

Q: What is grounding in AI agents?
A: Grounding in AI agents connects the agent's language understanding and generation to real-world perceptions and actions. Visual grounding connects language to image regions, spatial grounding connects descriptions to coordinates, and embodied grounding connects instructions to physical actions in robots.

Q: What is a task graph for agents?
A: A task graph decomposes a complex goal into a directed acyclic graph of subtasks with dependencies between them. Agent orchestration systems execute tasks according to the dependency order, enabling parallel execution of independent subtasks and ensuring dependencies are satisfied before dependent tasks begin.

Q: What is neural code search?
A: Neural code search retrieves relevant code snippets from large repositories by embedding queries and code in a shared semantic space. Models like CodeBERT and GraphCodeBERT pretrained on code and documentation learn embeddings where semantically similar queries and code are close, enabling natural language search over codebases.

Q: What is code summarization?
A: Code summarization generates natural language descriptions of code snippets automatically. Sequence-to-sequence models encode code syntax trees or token sequences and decode natural language summaries, enabling automatic documentation generation and code search indexing.

Q: What is code completion?
A: Code completion predicts the next tokens in a program given the preceding context, assisting developers by suggesting method names, argument values, and entire code blocks. Large language models pretrained on code repositories have transformed code completion from simple heuristic suggestions to semantically meaningful multi-line generation.

Q: What is code translation?
A: Code translation automatically converts programs between programming languages while preserving their functionality. Neural sequence-to-sequence models trained on parallel code corpora in multiple languages can translate Python to Java, C to Rust, and other language pairs, enabling legacy code modernization.

Q: What is automated bug detection?
A: Automated bug detection uses machine learning to identify code defects, security vulnerabilities, and logical errors without executing the code. Models trained on pairs of buggy and fixed code learn to identify common error patterns and suggest corrections.

Q: What is automated test generation?
A: Automated test generation uses language models to generate unit tests that exercise software functions and verify their behavior. Models learn from existing test suites to generate tests that cover edge cases, error conditions, and typical usage patterns for new code.

Q: What is static analysis with ML?
A: Machine learning enhanced static analysis identifies code quality issues, security vulnerabilities, and style violations without executing programs. Models trained on large labeled code corpora learn patterns associated with bugs and security issues that traditional pattern matching rules miss.

Q: What is code review automation?
A: Code review automation uses language models to automatically review code changes, identifying potential bugs, performance issues, and style violations. Models trained on historical code reviews learn to provide constructive feedback similar to experienced human reviewers.

Q: What is semantic code clone detection?
A: Semantic code clone detection identifies functionally equivalent code fragments that differ syntactically, such as refactored or obfuscated copies. Embedding-based approaches compare semantic representations of code rather than syntactic similarity, detecting clones across language translations and refactoring.

Q: What is program repair with ML?
A: Machine learning for program repair automatically generates patches to fix software bugs given buggy code and test failures. Models fine-tuned on bug-fix datasets learn to predict likely repairs conditioned on the buggy code and failure context.

Q: What is code generation evaluation?
A: Code generation evaluation uses functional correctness as the primary metric by executing generated code against test suites. Pass@k measures the probability of at least one of k generated solutions being correct. Metrics like CodeBLEU add code-specific features like abstract syntax tree similarity to surface-level token overlap measures.

Q: What is intent prediction in programming?
A: Intent prediction infers what a programmer is trying to accomplish from partial code, comments, and variable names to provide more relevant suggestions. Understanding developer intent enables code assistants to complete entire logical blocks rather than just predicting the most likely next tokens.

Q: What is variable name prediction?
A: Variable name prediction generates meaningful names for variables and functions from their usage context and semantics. Models trained on code learn the conventions for naming patterns associated with different types of values, improving code readability in generated or obfuscated code.

Q: What is docstring generation?
A: Docstring generation automatically creates documentation strings for functions and classes describing their purpose, parameters, return values, and side effects. Language models trained on paired code and documentation generate accurate docstrings from code alone, reducing the documentation burden on developers.

Q: What is code embedding model?
A: A code embedding model maps source code to dense vector representations capturing semantic meaning beyond syntactic similarity. CodeBERT, GraphCodeBERT, and UniXcoder produce embeddings useful for code search, clone detection, and classification by pretraining on massive code corpora from GitHub.

Q: What is train-test distribution mismatch?
A: Train-test distribution mismatch occurs when the distribution of examples in the test set differs from those in the training set, causing performance degradation even for well-trained models. It arises from temporal shifts in user behavior, geographic differences, or selection biases in how training data was collected. Techniques including domain adaptation, importance weighting, and robust training address distribution mismatch.

Q: What is data augmentation for distribution shift?
A: Data augmentation for distribution shift generates synthetic training examples that cover the types of distribution shift expected at deployment time. For covariate shift this includes augmentations simulating expected input variations, while for style shift it includes domain randomization generating diverse visual appearances of the same underlying content.

Q: What is importance weighting for covariate shift?
A: Importance weighting corrects for covariate shift by reweighting training examples by the ratio of test to training input densities. This makes the weighted training distribution match the test distribution, producing models with better test performance. The density ratio can be estimated using a classifier distinguishing training from test examples.

Q: What is label noise robustness?
A: Label noise robustness allows models to learn effectively despite incorrect labels in the training data. Robust loss functions, noise-tolerant training algorithms, and sample selection methods that down-weight likely noisy examples improve model quality when clean annotations are unavailable.

Q: What is model robustness evaluation?
A: Model robustness evaluation measures performance under distribution shift, adversarial perturbations, and corruptions. Robustness benchmarks apply systematic transformations like blur, noise, occlusion, and weather effects to test examples, revealing model vulnerabilities not visible in standard held-out evaluation.

Q: What is dataset curation?
A: Dataset curation improves training data quality through systematic deduplication, quality filtering, annotation verification, and balance adjustment. High-quality curated datasets often outperform much larger uncurated datasets, demonstrating that data quality is at least as important as data quantity for model performance.

Q: What is data deduplication?
A: Data deduplication removes duplicate or near-duplicate examples from training datasets that inflate effective dataset size and can bias models toward repeated content. Exact and fuzzy deduplication using hashing and embedding similarity detect duplicates at different granularities.

Q: What is data flywheel?
A: A data flywheel is a virtuous cycle where a deployed model generates predictions that attract users, whose interactions produce labeled data, which is used to improve the model, attracting more users. Products with strong data flywheels accumulate data advantages over competitors that compound over time.

Q: What is active learning in production?
A: Active learning in production selects the most informative unlabeled production examples for human annotation, maximizing model improvement per labeling dollar. By prioritizing uncertain, diverse, or high-impact examples the system improves model performance faster than random sampling from the production stream.

Q: What is weak supervision?
A: Weak supervision generates noisy programmatic labels for unlabeled data using labeling functions encoding domain expertise and heuristics. The Snorkel framework combines multiple weak labeling sources through a generative model that estimates label accuracies, producing probabilistic training labels enabling large-scale labeling without extensive human annotation.

Q: What is data-centric AI?
A: Data-centric AI shifts focus from model architecture improvements to systematic data quality improvement, arguing that fixing errors and improving consistency in training data often yields larger performance gains than model modifications. Iterative data improvement pipelines analyze model errors to identify data issues.

Q: What is model degradation root cause analysis?
A: Model degradation root cause analysis investigates the causes of production performance decline through systematic diagnosis of input distributions, feature statistics, model predictions, and downstream feedback. It distinguishes data quality issues, distribution shift, infrastructure problems, and model capacity limitations to guide targeted fixes.

Q: What is a model health dashboard?
A: A model health dashboard provides a comprehensive real-time view of a deployed model's operational status including prediction distribution, feature drift scores, latency percentiles, error rates, and downstream business metrics. It enables rapid identification and diagnosis of performance issues in production.

Q: What is ground truth collection in production?
A: Ground truth collection in production systematically captures the true outcomes corresponding to model predictions, enabling calculation of actual accuracy metrics beyond proxy indicators. Strategies include delayed label collection, human annotation of sampled predictions, and instrumentation of downstream events.

Q: What is shadow deployment evaluation?
A: Shadow deployment evaluation runs a candidate model in parallel with the production model routing all real traffic to both systems. The candidate's predictions are logged and compared against the production model and eventual ground truth, enabling comprehensive evaluation without any risk to users or business outcomes.

Q: What is model performance SLA?
A: A model performance SLA defines minimum acceptable levels for accuracy, latency, throughput, and availability that a deployed model must maintain. Violations trigger alerts, automatic rollback, or escalation procedures. SLAs formalize the reliability expectations that support teams must maintain for their model services.

Q: What is feature engineering for production?
A: Feature engineering for production requires features to be computable in real time with low latency, consistent between training and serving, robust to missing values and edge cases, and maintainable as the upstream data schema evolves. Feature stores enforce these constraints across the model lifecycle.

Q: What is testing in production?
A: Testing in production evaluates model changes using real user traffic and actual business outcomes rather than offline metrics on historical data. Methods include canary deployments, feature flags, shadow testing, and A/B experiments that expose different user populations to different model versions simultaneously.

Q: What is observability for ML systems?
A: Observability for ML systems enables engineers to understand internal model behavior and diagnose issues from external outputs. It extends monitoring to include detailed logging of prediction distributions, feature importances, intermediate representations, and data quality metrics that support root cause analysis of production problems.

Q: What is model drift correction?
A: Model drift correction adapts a deployed model to new data distributions through retraining, fine-tuning, or online learning. The correction strategy depends on the drift type: sudden drift often requires full retraining while gradual drift can be handled with incremental updates on recent data weighted more heavily.



# === ADVANCED_SPECIALIZED ===

Q: What is text-guided image editing?
A: Text-guided image editing modifies specific regions or attributes of an existing image according to natural language instructions. Methods like InstructPix2Pix train on pairs of original and edited images with text instructions, enabling precise edits such as changing object color, style, or replacing objects while preserving unspecified regions.

Q: What is image outpainting?
A: Image outpainting extends an image beyond its original borders by generating new content that is consistent with the visible portion. Diffusion models conditioned on the masked image and surrounding context generate plausible extensions following the style, lighting, and content of the original image.

Q: What is ControlNet?
A: ControlNet adds spatial conditioning to diffusion models by introducing parallel trainable branches that encode control signals like edge maps, depth maps, and pose skeletons. The control signal guides image generation to match the specified spatial structure while the base diffusion model determines style and content.

Q: What is Dreambooth?
A: Dreambooth fine-tunes a text-to-image diffusion model on a small set of images of a specific subject so the model can generate novel images of that subject in diverse contexts. Fine-tuning on only three to five images with a unique identifier token binds the model to the subject's appearance.

Q: What is textual inversion?
A: Textual inversion learns a new text token embedding that captures a concept from a small set of example images. By optimizing only the new token embedding while keeping the diffusion model frozen, concepts like specific objects, styles, and textures can be represented in the existing text embedding space.

Q: What is SDXL?
A: SDXL is an enhanced version of Stable Diffusion featuring a larger UNet backbone, a second text encoder from OpenCLIP, and a refinement model for high-resolution detail. The two-stage generation process produces significantly higher quality and more detailed images than earlier Stable Diffusion versions.

Q: What is video diffusion model?
A: Video diffusion models extend image diffusion to generate temporally consistent video sequences by adding temporal attention and 3D convolutional layers to the denoising UNet. They are trained on video datasets to learn natural motion dynamics alongside spatial appearance.

Q: What is Sora?
A: Sora is a text-to-video generation model from OpenAI based on a diffusion transformer architecture that generates high-quality, physically plausible videos up to one minute long from text descriptions. It models spatiotemporal patches of video directly enabling complex camera movements and object interactions.

Q: What is audio-visual generation?
A: Audio-visual generation jointly produces synchronized audio and video from text or other conditioning signals. Models learn correlations between sound and appearance to generate realistic paired outputs such as speaking face video with corresponding speech or musical performance video with matching audio.

Q: What is image editing with diffusion models?
A: Diffusion model image editing modifies specific attributes of an image by inverting it to a noisy representation using DDIM inversion then denoising with modified text conditioning. The inversion preserves image structure while changed text guidance redirects generation toward the desired attribute modification.

Q: What is concept erasure in generative models?
A: Concept erasure removes specific concepts like copyrighted characters or explicit content from pretrained generative models by fine-tuning to increase the loss for generating erased concepts while preserving generation quality for unrelated concepts. It enables deployment of generative models with controlled content policies.

Q: What is compositional image generation?
A: Compositional image generation creates images by combining multiple independent conditions such as different objects at different locations, styles applied to different regions, and layered text and image conditioning. It enables fine-grained control over individual scene elements beyond holistic text description.

Q: What is 3D generation with diffusion models?
A: 3D generation with diffusion models creates three-dimensional objects and scenes either by directly modeling 3D representations like neural radiance fields and mesh geometry or by distilling 3D structure from multiple 2D views generated by an image diffusion model using score distillation sampling.

Q: What is score distillation sampling?
A: Score distillation sampling uses a pretrained 2D image diffusion model as a prior to optimize 3D representations by computing gradients from the diffusion model's score function evaluated on rendered views. It enables text-to-3D generation by leveraging the rich visual knowledge of image diffusion models.

Q: What is latent consistency model?
A: Latent consistency models are distilled versions of diffusion models that generate high-quality images in one to four sampling steps rather than the many steps required by standard diffusion models. Consistency distillation trains the model to produce samples consistent with the deterministic probability flow ODE at any timestep.

Q: What is diffusion probabilistic model sampling?
A: Diffusion model sampling reverses the forward noise addition process by repeatedly applying a learned denoising network that removes a small amount of noise at each step. DDPM uses stochastic sampling while DDIM uses deterministic sampling enabling faster high-quality generation with fewer steps.

Q: What is guidance scale?
A: Guidance scale in classifier-free guidance controls how strongly the generated content adheres to the conditioning signal. Higher guidance scales produce images more closely matching the text prompt but with reduced diversity and potential quality artifacts, while lower scales produce more varied and natural-looking outputs.

Q: What is classifier guidance?
A: Classifier guidance uses gradients from an external classifier to steer the sampling process of a diffusion model toward the desired class. The classifier is trained on noisy images at various noise levels enabling it to provide informative gradients throughout the diffusion chain.

Q: What is latent diffusion?
A: Latent diffusion performs the denoising process in the compressed latent space of a pretrained variational autoencoder rather than pixel space. The reduced dimensionality dramatically decreases computational requirements while the autoencoder decoder recovers fine details during final reconstruction from the generated latent code.

Q: What is a UNet in diffusion models?
A: The UNet in diffusion models is the backbone denoising network that predicts the noise to remove at each diffusion step. Its encoder-decoder structure with skip connections enables both coarse semantic understanding and fine spatial detail recovery, and cross-attention layers inject text conditioning throughout the feature hierarchy.

Q: What is CLIP guidance for generation?
A: CLIP guidance steers image generation toward text descriptions by computing the gradient of the CLIP similarity score between generated images and text prompts and adding this signal to the diffusion sampling updates. It improves semantic alignment between generated images and complex text descriptions.

Q: What is image tokenization for generation?
A: Image tokenization converts continuous pixel images into sequences of discrete tokens using vector quantization, enabling autoregressive language models to generate images token by token. VQ-VAE learns a codebook of image patches and assigns each patch to its nearest codebook entry.

Q: What is flow matching?
A: Flow matching trains generative models by learning to transport samples from a simple noise distribution to the data distribution along simple probability flow paths. Compared to diffusion models it enables faster training and sampling by using straighter transport paths that require fewer discretization steps.

Q: What is rectified flow?
A: Rectified flow learns to transport samples between noise and data distributions along straight-line paths by regressing a velocity field that linearly interpolates between paired noise and data samples. Straight paths enable accurate single-step generation after iterative straightening training.

Q: What is image aesthetics scoring?
A: Image aesthetics scoring trains models to predict human aesthetic quality ratings for images. Scores from aesthetics models are used to filter training datasets for generative models, rank generated image samples, and provide feedback for reinforcement learning fine-tuning of image generation systems.

Q: What is the Anthropic model card?
A: A model card for an Anthropic model documents its training methodology, evaluation results across diverse benchmarks, safety evaluations including red-teaming results, intended use cases, known limitations, and ethical considerations. It provides transparency about model capabilities and risks to enable informed deployment decisions.

Q: What is sycophancy in language models?
A: Sycophancy is the tendency of language models to tell users what they want to hear rather than what is accurate, agreeing with incorrect claims when users express confidence or challenge the model's response. It arises from RLHF training where human raters may prefer agreeable responses regardless of accuracy.

Q: What is honesty in AI?
A: Honesty in AI encompasses truthfulness producing only believed claims, calibration expressing appropriate uncertainty, transparency about reasoning, non-deception avoiding creating false impressions, and non-manipulation influencing only through legitimate epistemic means. Honest AI systems support rather than undermine users' rational agency.

Q: What is the helpful harmless honest framework?
A: The helpful harmless honest framework defines three objectives for aligned AI assistants. Helpful means genuinely assisting users with their goals. Harmless means avoiding outputs that could cause harm to users or third parties. Honest means being truthful and not deceiving users. Tension between these objectives motivates sophisticated alignment methods.

Q: What is red teaming for LLMs?
A: Red teaming for large language models involves systematically probing the model with adversarial prompts designed to elicit harmful, unsafe, or misaligned outputs. Both human red-teamers and automated red-teaming models generate diverse attack scenarios to identify safety failures before deployment.

Q: What is jailbreaking a language model?
A: Jailbreaking is the practice of crafting prompts that bypass a language model's safety training to elicit outputs the model has been trained to refuse. Techniques include role-playing scenarios, encoding harmful requests in base64 or other formats, and multi-turn manipulation that gradually moves the model toward unsafe outputs.

Q: What is adversarial prompting?
A: Adversarial prompting designs inputs that cause language models to produce unintended outputs including bypassing safety filters, revealing system prompts, or generating harmful content. Research into adversarial prompting both evaluates model safety and informs training to improve robustness to manipulation.

Q: What is prompt injection?
A: Prompt injection inserts malicious instructions into content that a language model processes, hijacking the model's behavior. Attacks include hiding instructions in documents the model reads, embedding commands in web pages a browsing agent visits, and encoding directives in retrieved database entries.

Q: What is model safety fine-tuning?
A: Model safety fine-tuning adapts pretrained language models to refuse unsafe requests, avoid harmful outputs, and behave in accordance with guidelines. It uses supervised fine-tuning on safe response demonstrations and RLHF with human preferences to train models that balance helpfulness with harmlessness.

Q: What is the constitutional AI training process?
A: Constitutional AI trains models through two phases. First supervised learning on model-generated revisions of its own outputs based on a written constitution. Then reinforcement learning from AI feedback where the model generates preference comparisons according to constitutional principles rather than requiring human pairwise judgments.

Q: What is scalable oversight?
A: Scalable oversight addresses maintaining meaningful human supervision of AI systems on tasks too complex for humans to directly evaluate. Proposed methods include iterated amplification decomposing tasks into subtasks, debate where models argue positions for human judgment, and recursive reward modeling using AI assistance for evaluation.

Q: What is AI deception?
A: AI deception occurs when an AI system creates false impressions in a user's mind through technically true statements, misleading framing, selective emphasis, or omission of relevant information. Preventing deception requires training objectives that penalize not just explicit falsehoods but all forms of manipulative communication.

Q: What is value learning?
A: Value learning is the challenge of inferring human values from observations of human behavior, stated preferences, and feedback to train AI systems that pursue what humans actually want rather than simplified proxy objectives. Inverse reward design and preference learning from comparisons are practical approaches.

Q: What is goodhart's law applied to RLHF?
A: Goodhart's law in RLHF manifests when the reward model trained on human preference data becomes a poor proxy for true human preferences as it is optimized. The language model learns to produce outputs that score well on the reward model through reward hacking rather than genuinely satisfying user preferences.

Q: What is overoptimization in RLHF?
A: Overoptimization in RLHF occurs when the language model's policy diverges too far from the reference model in pursuit of high reward, producing outputs that score well on the learned reward model but poorly in human evaluation. KL divergence penalties from the reference model prevent excessive overoptimization.

Q: What is the KL divergence penalty in RLHF?
A: The KL divergence penalty in RLHF adds a regularization term to the RL objective that penalizes the language model policy for diverging from the pretrained reference policy. This prevents the model from finding reward-hacking solutions that exploit the reward model's errors while maintaining proximity to the original pretrained distribution.

Q: What is reward model collapse?
A: Reward model collapse occurs when a reward model trained on limited human preference data fails to generalize, assigning high scores to out-of-distribution generated text that human raters would actually rate poorly. It limits how much RLHF optimization can be performed before reward hacking occurs.

Q: What is online versus offline RLHF?
A: Online RLHF generates new samples from the current policy and collects preference labels on them for continuous training, allowing the reward model and policy to co-evolve. Offline RLHF trains on a fixed dataset of preferences collected from an earlier policy, which may not reflect the distribution of the current model.

Q: What is process reward model?
