A: A process reward model provides step-level credit assignment for multi-step reasoning by predicting whether each reasoning step is correct rather than only evaluating the final answer. Training models with process rewards improves reasoning reliability by discouraging correct answers reached through flawed reasoning chains.

Q: What is constitutional AI feedback?
A: Constitutional AI feedback uses the model itself to generate preference judgments between response pairs according to stated constitutional principles rather than collecting human pairwise comparisons. This AI-generated feedback enables scaling preference learning beyond what human annotation budgets allow.

Q: What is self-play for alignment?
A: Self-play for alignment uses language models to generate both arguments for and against the safety of model outputs, with a human or model judging which argument is more convincing. The debaters learn to find and counter the most compelling criticisms improving the quality of safety evaluation.

Q: What is targeted evaluation for alignment?
A: Targeted evaluation for alignment assesses specific potential failure modes through curated test sets probing honesty, harmlessness, and helpfulness. Rather than relying on broad benchmarks, targeted evaluation directly tests whether a model exhibits specific problematic behaviors identified through red-teaming.

Q: What is calibration and epistemic honesty?
A: Calibration and epistemic honesty require AI systems to express uncertainty proportional to their actual knowledge. Overconfident models asserting facts beyond their knowledge and underconfident models unnecessarily hedging reliable information both harm users. Training models to be calibrated improves the reliability of uncertainty signals.

Q: What is the preference learning problem?
A: The preference learning problem involves inferring a reward function from human preference judgments between pairs of outcomes. Challenges include inconsistencies in human preferences, difficulty of expressing complex values in pairwise comparisons, and generalization from limited labeled pairs to the full space of possible model outputs.

Q: What is human feedback quality?
A: Human feedback quality significantly impacts RLHF outcomes since the reward model inherits the biases, inconsistencies, and limitations of human raters. Factors including rater expertise, annotation guidelines, inter-rater agreement, and incentive structures determine whether collected preferences represent the intended values.

Q: What is gradient surgery?
A: Gradient surgery addresses conflicting gradients in multi-task learning by projecting each task's gradient onto a conflict-free direction. When two tasks' gradients point in opposite directions, one is projected to remove the component conflicting with the other, allowing both tasks to make progress simultaneously.

Q: What is PCGrad?
A: Projecting Conflicting Gradients modifies the multi-task gradient by detecting pairs of conflicting task gradients and projecting each onto the normal plane of the other's gradient. This simple modification of the gradient update prevents task interference and consistently improves multi-task learning performance.

Q: What is Frank-Wolfe optimization?
A: The Frank-Wolfe algorithm, or conditional gradient method, optimizes a function subject to constraints by computing a linear approximation at the current point and taking a step toward the minimizer of the linear approximation within the feasible set. It maintains feasibility throughout without projection steps.

Q: What is second order optimization in practice?
A: Second order optimization uses curvature information to scale gradient updates, enabling larger and more accurate steps than first order methods. Practical implementations approximate the Hessian using K-FAC or diagonal methods, apply to specific layers, or use limited-memory quasi-Newton methods that approximate curvature from gradient history.

Q: What is the role of batch normalization in optimization?
A: Batch normalization smooths the loss landscape by reducing the sensitivity of gradient updates to changes in earlier layer parameters. This allows training with higher learning rates by reducing the risk of gradient explosion and makes the gradient more predictive of the loss improvement direction.

Q: What is optimizer hyperparameter sensitivity?
A: Optimizer hyperparameter sensitivity refers to how much model performance depends on the choice of learning rate, momentum, and other optimizer settings. Adaptive optimizers like Adam are generally less sensitive to hyperparameter choice than SGD but may still require careful tuning for optimal results.

Q: What is the relation between regularization and optimization?
A: Regularization and optimization interact because regularization terms in the loss function change the optimization landscape. L2 regularization corresponds to a Gaussian prior and creates a quadratic bowl encouraging small weights. Early stopping implements implicit regularization by limiting the number of gradient steps taken.

Q: What is cyclical cosine annealing with restarts?
A: Cyclical cosine annealing with warm restarts decays the learning rate following a cosine schedule and periodically resets it to the maximum value at each restart. The brief high learning rate phases enable the model to escape local minima while the decay phases allow convergence to promising solutions.

Q: What is the catapult phase in training?
A: The catapult phase refers to early training dynamics where the network parameters rapidly move to an appropriate scale before slower gradient-based refinement begins. This initial dynamics phase is influenced by initialization scale and learning rate and determines which basin of attraction the model ultimately converges to.

Q: What is feature alignment across layers?
A: Feature alignment across layers ensures that representations learned at different depths of a network encode complementary and compatible information. Analysis of representation similarity across layers using centered kernel alignment reveals how feature abstraction progresses from low-level to high-level representations.

Q: What is neural tangent kernel?
A: The neural tangent kernel describes the training dynamics of infinitely wide neural networks, showing that gradient descent on infinitely wide networks is equivalent to kernel regression with a fixed kernel determined by the network architecture. It provides theoretical understanding of feature learning in the infinite width limit.

Q: What is lazy training?
A: Lazy training describes the regime where a neural network's parameters change negligibly from initialization during training, with the network functioning approximately as a kernel machine. It occurs in overparameterized networks trained with small learning rates and is associated with the neural tangent kernel regime.

Q: What is feature learning versus kernel methods?
A: Feature learning occurs when neural network representations change substantially during training to fit the task, while kernel methods use fixed representations. Deep learning's advantage over kernel methods stems primarily from feature learning that discovers task-relevant abstractions not captured by any fixed kernel.

Q: What is the edge of stability?
A: The edge of stability describes a training phenomenon where the learning rate is set high enough that the sharpest curvature in the loss landscape exceeds the stability threshold but training nonetheless converges. The optimizer oscillates near the edge of stability with the loss sometimes increasing before ultimately decreasing.

Q: What is probing in neural networks?
A: Probing trains simple classifiers on top of frozen intermediate representations from a pretrained network to assess what information is encoded at different layers. Probing studies reveal how networks progressively transform input representations into task-relevant features across their depth.

Q: What is representational similarity analysis?
A: Representational similarity analysis compares representations across different network layers, architectures, and training conditions by computing the correlation between their pairwise similarity matrices. It enables objective comparison of how different neural networks encode the same set of stimuli.

Q: What is layer freezing in fine-tuning?
A: Layer freezing prevents selected layers from updating during fine-tuning by detaching their parameters from the computational graph. Freezing early layers that capture general features and fine-tuning later layers reduces compute, prevents overfitting on small datasets, and preserves pretrained representations.

Q: What is domain-adaptive pretraining?
A: Domain-adaptive pretraining continues language model pretraining on domain-specific unlabeled text before task-specific fine-tuning, adapting the model's representations to domain vocabulary and patterns. It consistently improves fine-tuning performance in specialized domains like biomedical and legal text.

Q: What is task-adaptive pretraining?
A: Task-adaptive pretraining continues language model pretraining on text closely related to the final downstream task using the same self-supervised objective. It further adapts representations beyond domain pretraining by exposing the model to the specific text type and vocabulary of the target task.

Q: What is masking strategies in pretraining?
A: Masking strategies in masked language model pretraining determine which tokens are replaced with mask tokens, random tokens, or left unchanged. Whole word masking masks entire words rather than subword tokens. Span masking masks contiguous spans producing models better at generative tasks requiring predicting multiple consecutive tokens.

Q: What is dynamic masking?
A: Dynamic masking in language model pretraining generates fresh random masks for each training example in each epoch rather than using fixed masks. This ensures the model sees each token in different contexts and masked positions over training, improving representation quality compared to static masking.

Q: What is efficient fine-tuning?
A: Efficient fine-tuning adapts large pretrained models to new tasks while updating minimal parameters, reducing storage and computation compared to full fine-tuning. Methods include LoRA, prefix tuning, adapter layers, and prompt tuning which each represent different tradeoffs between expressiveness and parameter efficiency.

Q: What is catastrophic forgetting prevention?
A: Catastrophic forgetting prevention maintains performance on previously learned tasks when learning new ones through regularization methods like elastic weight consolidation that protect important weights, memory replay that rehearses old examples during new learning, and architecture methods that grow new capacity for new tasks.

Q: What is elastic weight consolidation?
A: Elastic weight consolidation prevents catastrophic forgetting by identifying important weights for previous tasks using the Fisher information matrix and penalizing changes to these weights during new task training. Important weights are protected while unimportant ones remain free to adapt to the new task.

Q: What is progressive neural networks?
A: Progressive neural networks prevent catastrophic forgetting by allocating a new network column for each new task connected to frozen columns of previous tasks through lateral connections. Previous task knowledge is preserved exactly while new task knowledge is learned in separate parameter space.



# === THEORETICAL_AND_ALIGNMENT ===

Q: What is contrastive decoding?
A: Contrastive decoding improves language model output quality by subtracting the log probabilities from a smaller amateur model from those of a larger expert model before sampling. The intuition is that the smaller model captures common uninformative patterns while the larger model captures the task-relevant signal, so their difference emphasizes the expert's unique contributions over the amateur.

Q: What is speculative sampling?
A: Speculative sampling is a variant of speculative decoding that uses importance sampling theory to verify draft tokens from a small model using the large target model in a single batched forward pass. It produces outputs statistically equivalent to sampling from the target model while achieving significant speedups when the draft model is well-calibrated.

Q: What is minimum Bayes risk decoding?
A: Minimum Bayes risk decoding selects the output sequence that minimizes expected loss under a reference metric by generating many candidate sequences and selecting the one with the highest average similarity to all other candidates. It produces more conservative and higher quality outputs than greedy or beam search decoding.

Q: What is best-of-n sampling?
A: Best-of-n sampling generates n candidate outputs from a language model and returns the one scoring highest according to a reward model or verifier. Scaling n provides a simple way to improve output quality by increasing sampling diversity, at the cost of n times the inference computation.

Q: What is a verifier in language model decoding?
A: A verifier is a model trained to evaluate whether a language model's output is correct or satisfactory, used to score and select among multiple candidate outputs. Verifiers trained on outcome supervision consistently improve accuracy on reasoning tasks when used for best-of-n or beam search decoding.

Q: What is self-consistency prompting?
A: Self-consistency prompting generates multiple diverse reasoning chains for a question through temperature sampling and takes a majority vote over their final answers. The consistency of independent reasoning paths toward the same conclusion provides strong evidence of correctness, significantly improving accuracy on mathematical and logical reasoning.

Q: What is the test-time compute scaling hypothesis?
A: The test-time compute scaling hypothesis proposes that spending more computation at inference time through iterative refinement, extended reasoning chains, or search over multiple candidate solutions can substitute for training time compute, enabling smaller models to match larger ones on difficult tasks.

Q: What is inference time scaling?
A: Inference time scaling systematically increases the computation spent per prediction through longer reasoning chains, more sampled candidates, deeper search trees, or iterative refinement. Research like OpenAI o1 demonstrates consistent accuracy improvements on hard reasoning tasks as inference compute scales up.

Q: What is a reasoning chain verifier?
A: A reasoning chain verifier evaluates intermediate steps in a multi-step reasoning process rather than only the final answer. Process reward models trained on step-level correctness labels enable verification and search over reasoning chains, improving reliability by detecting and pruning incorrect reasoning steps early.

Q: What is Monte Carlo tree search for LLMs?
A: Monte Carlo tree search applied to language models explores reasoning chains as a tree where each node is a reasoning step. The algorithm selects, expands, simulates, and backpropagates through the tree guided by a value model, discovering correct reasoning paths for complex multi-step problems.

Q: What is reward-guided generation?
A: Reward-guided generation uses a learned reward model to steer the generation process toward outputs with higher reward. During beam search or sampling, sequences receiving higher reward from the model are kept or weighted more heavily, producing outputs better aligned with the reward model's learned preferences.

Q: What is length bias in language model evaluation?
A: Length bias in language model evaluation refers to the tendency of LLM evaluators and human raters to prefer longer more elaborate responses regardless of quality. Controlling for length in evaluation and explicitly rewarding concise accurate responses addresses this systematic bias in preference learning.

Q: What is position bias in language model evaluation?
A: Position bias causes evaluators to prefer the first or second response in a pairwise comparison regardless of quality. Mitigating position bias requires presenting comparisons in both orders and averaging judgments or using specialized debiasing techniques in the evaluation protocol.

Q: What is verbosity bias in RLHF?
A: Verbosity bias in RLHF occurs when human raters or reward models preferentially rate longer responses higher because length creates an impression of thoroughness. Models optimized against biased reward models learn to produce unnecessarily verbose responses to maximize reward rather than genuine quality.

Q: What is reward model generalization?
A: Reward model generalization is the ability of a reward model trained on human preference data to correctly score novel model outputs not seen during training. Poor generalization allows the policy to find high-scoring outputs the reward model incorrectly evaluates, causing reward hacking.

Q: What is Constitutional AI revision?
A: The revision step in Constitutional AI prompts the model to critique its own initial response according to a specific principle from the constitution and then rewrite the response to better satisfy the principle. Iterating through multiple principles produces responses that are simultaneously helpful and aligned with the full set of constitutional principles.

Q: What is the crowdsourcing of human feedback?
A: Crowdsourcing human feedback collects preference labels from large numbers of workers through platforms like Mechanical Turk. While scalable, crowdsourced labels require careful quality control through consistency checks, attention tests, and redundant annotation to filter low-quality raters.

Q: What is expert preference labeling?
A: Expert preference labeling collects feedback from domain experts rather than crowdworkers for tasks requiring specialized knowledge such as medical advice, legal analysis, and scientific reasoning. Expert labels are more reliable for knowledge-intensive tasks but are more expensive and slower to collect.

Q: What is the annotation interface for RLHF?
A: The annotation interface for RLHF presents human raters with pairs or sets of model responses and collects preference judgments, quality ratings, or safety assessments. Interface design including labeling guidelines, examples, and feedback mechanisms significantly affects the quality and consistency of collected annotations.

Q: What is disagreement in human preferences?
A: Disagreement in human preferences arises from genuine value differences between raters, ambiguity in the task, and subjective interpretation of quality. Reward models trained on aggregated preferences may not represent any individual's values well, motivating personalized reward learning that models individual preference distributions.

Q: What is the principal-agent problem in AI?
A: The principal-agent problem in AI arises when the AI system's objectives diverge from the principals, the humans who deploy and interact with it. Operators customize models for their use cases while users interact with them, creating a hierarchy of principals whose instructions the AI must balance.

Q: What is adversarial safety evaluation?
A: Adversarial safety evaluation systematically probes a model's safety training using crafted prompts designed to elicit harmful outputs. Both human red-teamers and automated attack methods generate diverse adversarial scenarios, and the fraction of successful attacks measures safety training robustness.

Q: What is RLHF versus DPO comparison?
A: RLHF trains a separate reward model then uses PPO to optimize the policy against it, requiring more moving parts and careful hyperparameter tuning. DPO directly optimizes the policy on preference pairs with a simpler binary cross-entropy loss achieving comparable alignment without a separate reward model training stage.

Q: What is KTO?
A: Kahneman-Tversky Optimization is an alignment method using individual example quality labels rather than pairwise preferences, aligned with prospect theory from behavioral economics. It assigns asymmetric losses to desirable and undesirable examples reflecting that humans are more sensitive to losses than equivalent gains.

Q: What is SPIN?
A: Self-Play Fine-tuning iteratively generates responses from the current model and trains it to distinguish its own outputs from human-generated demonstrations, improving response quality through self-play without requiring new human annotations or pairwise preference data beyond the original SFT demonstration set.

Q: What is the forgetting problem in fine-tuning?
A: The forgetting problem in fine-tuning occurs when a model loses general capabilities learned during pretraining when fine-tuned on a specific task. The model becomes overspecialized to the fine-tuning data distribution, reducing performance on tasks not present in the fine-tuning set.

Q: What is instruction hierarchy?
A: Instruction hierarchy is the order of priority given to different sources of instructions in a language model system. System prompts from operators typically take precedence over user messages, which take precedence over instructions embedded in retrieved documents. Models must learn to respect this hierarchy to prevent injection attacks.

Q: What is harm avoidance classification?
A: Harm avoidance classification is a component of safety-trained language models that predicts whether a response could cause harm before generating it. A classifier trained on labeled examples of harmful and harmless requests gates generation, refusing to produce harmful content regardless of prompt sophistication.

Q: What is the evaluation of alignment?
A: Evaluating alignment measures how well a model's behavior matches intended values across dimensions of helpfulness, honesty, and harmlessness. TruthfulQA measures honesty, safety benchmarks measure harm avoidance, and helpfulness is evaluated through user preference studies and task completion metrics.

Q: What is model specification?
A: Model specification is a document or set of guidelines defining the intended behavior of a language model across diverse situations, specifying how to handle edge cases, conflicting instructions, and sensitive topics. Specifications guide both training and evaluation and communicate intended behavior to users and developers.

Q: What is human oversight of AI?
A: Human oversight of AI maintains meaningful human control over AI system behavior through transparency about model capabilities and limitations, interpretability tools, deployment safeguards, monitoring systems, and governance processes. Preserving human oversight is particularly important as AI systems become more capable and autonomous.

Q: What is corrigibility training?
A: Corrigibility training teaches AI systems to actively support human oversight and control, accepting corrections and modifications without resistance. Training objectives encouraging corrigible behavior include rewarding systems for flagging uncertainty, deferring to human judgment, and transparently reporting reasoning.

Q: What is AI assistance versus AI autonomy?
A: AI assistance uses AI to augment human capabilities while maintaining human decision authority. AI autonomy delegates decision-making to AI systems with reduced human oversight. The appropriate balance between assistance and autonomy depends on task stakes, AI reliability, and the availability of human oversight.

Q: What is a deployment policy for AI?
A: A deployment policy for AI specifies which use cases are permitted, which require additional safeguards, and which are prohibited. Policies address content restrictions, user verification requirements, monitoring obligations, and escalation procedures for edge cases.

Q: What is multi-party alignment?
A: Multi-party alignment addresses conflicts between the values and instructions of different stakeholders in an AI system including developers, operators, users, and affected third parties. Aligning AI systems to serve all relevant parties while managing conflicts requires explicit priority rules and value learning from diverse feedback.

Q: What is the gradient descent convergence proof?
A: For convex functions with Lipschitz continuous gradients gradient descent converges to the global minimum at a rate of O(1/t) where t is the number of iterations. For strongly convex functions the convergence rate improves to O(rho to the t) for some rho less than one giving linear or exponential convergence. Non-convex neural network training lacks these guarantees but empirically converges to good solutions.

Q: What is stochastic gradient descent theory?
A: Stochastic gradient descent theory shows that SGD converges to a stationary point of the loss function when the learning rate decays appropriately. The noise in stochastic gradient estimates can help escape sharp local minima and saddle points. SGD implicitly favors flat minima over sharp minima which empirically correlates with better generalization.

Q: What is the overparameterization benefit?
A: Overparameterized neural networks with more parameters than training examples can still generalize well, contradicting classical statistical learning theory. This benign overfitting occurs because overparameterized networks converge to minimum norm solutions that generalize well and because stochastic gradient descent has an implicit regularization effect toward flat minima.

Q: What is neural network double descent?
A: Double descent describes a phenomenon where test error first decreases then increases then decreases again as model capacity increases. In the highly overparameterized regime beyond the interpolation threshold the test error decreases again as the minimum norm interpolant smoothly fits the training data while generalizing well.

Q: What is implicit regularization in neural networks?
A: Implicit regularization refers to the regularization effect of gradient descent optimization beyond any explicit penalty terms in the loss function. SGD with specific learning rate schedules and batch sizes implicitly biases toward certain types of solutions such as flat minima and low-rank weight matrices without explicit constraints.

Q: What is the neural network inductive bias?
A: The inductive bias of a neural network architecture is the set of assumptions baked in that determine which functions it can efficiently represent. Convolutional networks assume translation invariance and local structure. Recurrent networks assume sequential dependency. Transformers assume arbitrary pairwise interactions with positional encoding providing structure.

Q: What is parameter space versus function space?
A: Parameter space refers to the space of all possible model weights while function space refers to the space of all possible input-output mappings representable by the model. The same function can be represented by infinitely many parameter settings, and gradient descent navigates parameter space toward better functions.

Q: What is the flat minimum hypothesis?
A: The flat minimum hypothesis proposes that solutions in wide flat regions of the loss landscape generalize better than solutions in narrow sharp minima because flat minima are more robust to small perturbations of weights that correspond to distributional shift between training and test data.

Q: What is loss landscape visualization?
A: Loss landscape visualization projects the high-dimensional loss landscape onto two-dimensional slices to reveal geometric properties including sharpness, flatness, and the presence of multiple minima. Filter normalization projects along the directions of maximum weight magnitude providing scale-invariant visualization.

Q: What is the feature learning paradigm in deep learning?
A: The feature learning paradigm holds that deep learning succeeds because networks learn hierarchical features that are useful for the task rather than relying on fixed hand-crafted features. Each layer transforms the representation to make the task easier, with later layers producing linearly separable features.

Q: What is disentangled representation learning?
A: Disentangled representation learning produces latent representations where different dimensions or groups of dimensions correspond to independent semantically meaningful factors of variation. Disentangled representations enable controlled manipulation of individual factors, improve interpretability, and often transfer better to new tasks.

Q: What is the beta-VAE?
A: Beta-VAE modifies the standard VAE training objective by scaling the KL divergence term by a factor beta greater than one. The stronger KL pressure encourages the model to use fewer latent dimensions and produce more disentangled representations where each dimension captures an independent generative factor.

Q: What is information bottleneck theory?
A: Information bottleneck theory frames deep learning as learning representations that are minimally sufficient, capturing only the information in the input relevant to predicting the target while compressing away irrelevant information. It provides a theoretical framework for understanding generalization through the lens of information compression.

Q: What is the deep learning theory research agenda?
A: Deep learning theory research aims to explain empirical observations including generalization of overparameterized networks, the effectiveness of specific architectures, optimization dynamics, and the role of depth. Open questions include characterizing which solutions gradient descent finds and when they generalize.

Q: What is the manifold of natural images?
A: The manifold of natural images is a low-dimensional nonlinear submanifold embedded in the high-dimensional space of all possible pixel configurations. Deep generative models learn to map a simple low-dimensional prior distribution to this natural image manifold enabling sampling of realistic images by sampling from the prior.

Q: What is linear mode connectivity?
A: Linear mode connectivity describes the phenomenon where two independently trained networks with the same architecture can be linearly interpolated in weight space with a smooth or monotonically decreasing loss along the interpolation path. It suggests that despite appearing to be in different local minima the solutions are in fact well-connected.

Q: What is loss of plasticity in neural networks?
A: Loss of plasticity refers to the decreasing ability of a neural network to learn new information as it accumulates experience. After extensive training on one task or distribution the network may have become so specialized that effective learning on new tasks requires resetting or reinitialization of some parameters.

Q: What is the dying neuron problem in ReLU networks?
A: The dying neuron problem occurs in networks with ReLU activations when many neurons permanently output zero because their input is always negative. Dead neurons stop contributing gradients and effectively reduce network capacity. Initialization strategies and Leaky ReLU variants prevent neurons from dying permanently.

Q: What is gradient explosion in RNNs?
A: Gradient explosion in recurrent neural networks occurs when gradients grow exponentially during backpropagation through time as the same weight matrix is repeatedly multiplied. Gradient clipping prevents training instability by scaling down the gradient when its norm exceeds a threshold while preserving gradient direction.

Q: What is the depth-width tradeoff in theoretical analysis?
A: Theoretical analysis shows that depth provides exponential expressiveness advantages over width for certain function classes. A deep network can represent functions that would require exponentially wider shallow networks. This explains why deep architectures consistently outperform shallow ones with comparable total parameters on hierarchically structured tasks.

Q: What is norm-based generalization bounds?
A: Norm-based generalization bounds bound the generalization gap of neural networks by the product of spectral norms of weight matrices across layers. These bounds capture the idea that networks with smaller weights generalize better and provide theoretical justification for weight decay regularization.

Q: What is PAC-Bayes generalization bound?
A: PAC-Bayes bounds provide generalization guarantees for randomized predictors by bounding the gap between training and test error in terms of the KL divergence between the posterior distribution over parameters and a fixed prior. Tighter bounds result from posteriors close to the prior representing well-regularized solutions.

Q: What is the lottery ticket in terms of initialization?
A: The original initialization of winning lottery ticket subnetworks is crucial to their trainability. When the sparse subnetwork is reset to its original initialization values and retrained it reaches the same accuracy as the full network. Randomly initialized sparse subnetworks do not train as well showing the importance of the specific initialization.

Q: What is the fortuitous forgetting hypothesis?
A: The fortuitous forgetting hypothesis proposes that the positive effects of pruning and quantization in producing more generalizable models stem from eliminating overfitted patterns rather than only from compression. The constraints imposed by compression remove model components encoding training-specific noise rather than signal.

Q: What is network architecture design principles?
A: Network architecture design principles guide the construction of effective neural networks through guidelines like increasing feature dimensionality gradually in encoders, using residual connections in deep networks, matching architectural biases to data structure, and using normalization for stable training of deep models.



# === COMPREHENSIVE_FINAL ===

Q: What is the decoder block in GPT-style models?
A: The decoder block in GPT-style autoregressive language models consists of a causal self-attention layer, a position-wise feed-forward network, and residual connections with layer normalization. Causal masking ensures each token only attends to itself and earlier tokens, enabling parallel training while maintaining the autoregressive generation constraint during inference.

Q: What is the encoder block in BERT-style models?
A: The encoder block in BERT-style bidirectional models consists of a full self-attention layer without causal masking, a feed-forward network, and residual connections with layer normalization. Each token can attend to all other tokens in both directions, producing rich contextual representations useful for understanding tasks.

Q: What is a sparse feedforward network?
A: A sparse feedforward network activates only a fraction of neurons for each input by using top-k activation selection or mixture-of-expert routing. This produces a conditionally sparse computation pattern where different inputs activate different subsets of parameters, increasing total parameter count without proportionally increasing computation.

Q: What is the key-value projection in attention?
A: The key and value projections in attention are learned linear transformations applied to input token representations. Keys determine which tokens are relevant to each query through dot product similarity. Values contain the information retrieved and aggregated according to the softmax-normalized attention weights computed from query-key compatibility.

Q: What is the query projection in attention?
A: The query projection linearly transforms the current token's representation into the query space used to compute compatibility scores with all key projections. Each attention head uses a separate query projection matrix allowing different heads to attend to different aspects of the input sequence.

Q: What is the output projection in attention?
A: The output projection in multi-head attention concatenates the attended value vectors from all heads and applies a learned linear transformation to produce the final attention output. This mixing across heads allows the model to integrate diverse information captured by different attention patterns.

Q: What is position-wise feedforward network?
A: The position-wise feedforward network applies the same two-layer feedforward transformation independently and identically to each token position. The intermediate expanded dimension, typically four times the model dimension, provides capacity for each token to store and transform information before the next attention layer.

Q: What is the role of SwiGLU activation?
A: SwiGLU activation is a gated linear unit variant using the Swish activation function that gates one linear projection with another. It consistently outperforms standard ReLU and GELU in transformer feed-forward blocks and has become the default activation in many modern language models including LLaMA and Gemma.

Q: What is a tokenizer vocabulary?
A: A tokenizer vocabulary is the complete set of tokens a model recognizes including individual characters, subword units, and common words. The vocabulary size determines the trade-off between sequence length and representation granularity, with typical modern language models using 32,000 to 100,000 token vocabularies.

Q: What is causal language modeling loss?
A: Causal language modeling loss computes cross-entropy between predicted and actual token probabilities for every token in the sequence in parallel during training. Each position predicts only from preceding positions through causal masking enabling efficient parallel computation while maintaining the autoregressive generation semantics.

Q: What is masked language modeling loss?
A: Masked language modeling loss computes cross-entropy between predicted and actual token values for randomly masked positions while ignoring unmasked positions. Typically 15 percent of tokens are masked with 80 percent replaced by mask tokens, 10 percent by random tokens, and 10 percent left unchanged for better representations.

Q: What is span masking in language modeling?
A: Span masking replaces contiguous spans of tokens rather than individual random tokens, requiring the model to predict multiple consecutive masked tokens. This produces models better at text infilling and generation tasks that require producing coherent multi-token continuations rather than single isolated token predictions.

Q: What is a frozen language model?
A: A frozen language model has its weights fixed and not updated during downstream training, used only as a feature extractor or with lightweight adapter modules like LoRA that add trainable parameters while keeping the base model unchanged. This preserves pretrained capabilities while enabling efficient task adaptation.

Q: What is a language model perplexity comparison?
A: Comparing perplexity between language models requires using the same tokenization since different tokenizers produce different sequence lengths from the same text, making perplexity values calculated with different tokenizers not directly comparable. Bits-per-character or bits-per-byte metrics enable fairer cross-model comparison.

Q: What is next sentence prediction in BERT?
A: Next sentence prediction is a binary classification task in BERT pretraining that predicts whether a second sentence immediately follows the first in the original document or is randomly sampled. It was designed to learn inter-sentence relationships but subsequent work showed it contributes less than masked language modeling.

Q: What is sentence-level pretraining tasks?
A: Sentence-level pretraining tasks train language models on objectives requiring understanding of relationships between sentences, including next sentence prediction, sentence order prediction, discourse coherence, and paraphrase identification. These tasks encourage models to learn discourse structure beyond token-level patterns.

Q: What is the WikiText benchmark?
A: WikiText is a language modeling benchmark derived from verified Wikipedia articles used to evaluate language model perplexity. WikiText-103 contains 103 million tokens and WikiText-2 contains 2 million, with longer documents than Penn Treebank enabling evaluation of long-range context modeling.

Q: What is Penn Treebank for language modeling?
A: Penn Treebank is a corpus of Wall Street Journal articles with syntactic annotations used as a language modeling benchmark. Models are evaluated on word-level perplexity on the test set. Despite being relatively small and domain-specific it remains a standard benchmark for comparing language model architectures.

Q: What is the role of context in language generation?
A: Context shapes language generation by providing the model with information about the topic, style, intended audience, and constraints of the desired output. Longer contexts enable more coherent extended generation but require larger context windows and more attention computation.

Q: What is autoregressive versus non-autoregressive generation?
A: Autoregressive generation produces tokens one at a time, each conditioned on all previously generated tokens, achieving high quality but slow sequential inference. Non-autoregressive generation predicts all tokens simultaneously in a single forward pass achieving much faster generation but typically lower quality requiring specialized training.

Q: What is self-supervised learning in vision?
A: Self-supervised learning in vision learns visual representations from unlabeled images by training on pretext tasks that require understanding image content. Tasks include predicting image rotations, solving jigsaw puzzles, colorizing grayscale images, and predicting masked image patches from surrounding context.

Q: What is masked image modeling?
A: Masked image modeling trains vision transformers to reconstruct randomly masked image patches from the visible patches. Similar to masked language modeling it forces the model to learn rich contextual representations that capture semantic content and spatial relationships, producing features that transfer well to downstream vision tasks.

Q: What is MAE?
A: Masked Autoencoder for vision pretrains a vision transformer by masking 75 percent of image patches randomly and training an autoencoder to reconstruct the masked patches. The high masking ratio forces the encoder to learn holistic semantic representations rather than memorizing local textures.

Q: What is DINO self-supervised learning?
A: DINO trains vision transformers using a self-distillation objective where a student network learns to match the outputs of a teacher network with slowly updated weights. Without any labels DINO learns features with emergent semantic segmentation properties and strong linear evaluation performance.

Q: What is SimMIM?
A: Simple Masked Image Modeling pretrains vision models by predicting raw pixel values of masked patches using a simple linear prediction head rather than a deep decoder. The direct pixel reconstruction objective combined with high masking ratios produces representations competitive with more complex reconstruction targets.

Q: What is an anchor-based detector?
A: Anchor-based detectors like Faster R-CNN predefined boxes of various scales and aspect ratios at each spatial location and classify them as foreground or background while regressing offsets to match ground truth boxes. The anchors densely sample the space of possible object locations and sizes.

Q: What is an anchor-free detector?
A: Anchor-free detectors predict object center points, sizes, and offsets directly without predefined anchor boxes. Methods like FCOS predict per-location objectness scores and box dimensions, and CenterNet detects objects as center point heatmaxima, simplifying the detection pipeline and eliminating anchor design hyperparameters.

Q: What is two-stage object detection?
A: Two-stage object detection first generates region proposals indicating likely object locations then classifies and refines each proposal. Faster R-CNN uses a Region Proposal Network sharing features with the detection head. Two-stage methods generally achieve higher accuracy than single-stage at the cost of slower inference.

Q: What is single-stage object detection?
A: Single-stage detectors directly predict object class probabilities and bounding box coordinates from feature maps in one forward pass without a separate proposal stage. Methods like YOLO, SSD, and RetinaNet achieve real-time detection speeds by combining region proposal and classification into a single network.

Q: What is the panoptic quality metric?
A: Panoptic quality evaluates panoptic segmentation by computing the product of recognition quality and segmentation quality. Recognition quality measures how well segments are matched between prediction and ground truth. Segmentation quality measures the IoU of matched segments. The product penalizes both missed detections and poor segmentation.

Q: What is the saddle-free Newton method?
A: Saddle-free Newton method modifies the Hessian to remove negative curvature directions by taking the absolute value of eigenvalues before inverting. This prevents the method from following directions of descent in the parameter space that lead toward saddle points rather than minima.

Q: What is stochastic weight perturbation?
A: Stochastic weight perturbation explores the loss landscape by adding random noise to model weights and measuring the resulting loss change. It is used to characterize loss landscape sharpness, verify model robustness, and implement sharpness-aware minimization by finding and ascending gradient directions that increase loss.

Q: What is the Adam convergence problem?
A: Adam can fail to converge in some settings due to insufficient exploration of the parameter space when the exponential moving average of squared gradients becomes too large. The AMSGrad variant addresses this by using the maximum of past squared gradient averages rather than the current average.

Q: What is AMSGrad?
A: AMSGrad modifies Adam to use the maximum of all past squared gradient averages rather than the decaying exponential average, ensuring the effective learning rate never increases. This guarantees convergence for convex problems where standard Adam may fail to converge due to large learning rate fluctuations.

Q: What is the Polyak averaging?
A: Polyak averaging maintains a running average of all model parameters visited during training, weighted equally or by a trailing window. The averaged parameters often achieve lower variance and better generalization than the final parameters from stochastic gradient descent.

Q: What is tail averaging?
A: Tail averaging computes the average of parameters visited during the final phase of training after learning rate warmup and initial rapid convergence. Averaging over a trajectory of near-convergent solutions smooths noise and finds a better solution than any individual point on the tail trajectory.

Q: What is the gradient norm as a training diagnostic?
A: The gradient norm measures the magnitude of parameter updates at each training step and is a key diagnostic for detecting optimization problems. Consistently high gradient norms indicate learning rate is too high or gradient clipping threshold is too low. Gradient norm spikes signal numerical instability or problematic batches.

Q: What is the relationship between learning rate and batch size?
A: Linear scaling rule suggests increasing the learning rate proportionally when increasing batch size, compensating for the reduced gradient noise. However this linear relationship breaks down at very large batch sizes where noise reduction reduces implicit regularization and diminishing marginal returns occur.

Q: What is the loss increase after learning rate increase?
A: When the learning rate is increased during training the loss often temporarily increases before decreasing to a lower final value. This indicates the optimizer escaped a local minimum and is exploring a broader basin, and is expected behavior in learning rate warmup and cyclical learning rate schedules.

Q: What is the relationship between momentum and learning rate?
A: Higher momentum values make the optimizer more sensitive to learning rate choice since momentum amplifies the effective step size. With very high momentum the effective learning rate is approximately the learning rate divided by one minus momentum so small changes in momentum require proportional learning rate adjustments.

Q: What is data transformation in ML pipelines?
A: Data transformation converts raw input features into representations better suited for model training through operations including scaling, encoding, embedding, and feature crossing. Consistent transformation between training and serving requires applying the same fitted transformation parameters to new data using stored transformation artifacts.

Q: What is feature crossing?
A: Feature crossing creates new features by taking the Cartesian product of two or more categorical features, explicitly modeling interactions that linear models cannot capture. Crossed features enable models to learn from combinations like city and time of day that have different effects together than separately.

Q: What is target encoding?
A: Target encoding replaces categorical feature values with the mean of the target variable for examples with that value. It creates informative numerical representations of high-cardinality categoricals but requires cross-validation or smoothing to prevent target leakage from using the target to encode features.

Q: What is frequency encoding?
A: Frequency encoding replaces categorical values with their frequency of occurrence in the training set. It provides informative statistics about category prevalence without the target leakage risk of target encoding and is particularly useful for high-cardinality categoricals where one-hot encoding would be prohibitively high-dimensional.

Q: What is entity embedding for tabular data?
A: Entity embeddings for tabular data learn dense vector representations for categorical values by training them as neural network embedding layers alongside the downstream prediction task. They capture similarity relationships between categories and often outperform one-hot encoding for high-cardinality variables.

Q: What is feature drift detection in pipelines?
A: Feature drift detection monitors the statistical distribution of individual features in production data and compares them to reference distributions from training. Statistical tests and summary statistics alert when significant shifts occur, triggering investigation of upstream data quality issues or true distribution changes.

Q: What is data schema validation?
A: Data schema validation checks that incoming data conforms to the expected format including column names, data types, value ranges, and cardinality constraints. Failed validation at pipeline ingestion catches data quality issues early before they propagate to model training or corrupt serving predictions.

Q: What is data drift versus concept drift?
A: Data drift refers to changes in the input feature distributions while concept drift refers to changes in the conditional relationship between inputs and outputs. Data drift is observable from inputs alone while concept drift requires monitoring prediction quality against ground truth labels to detect.

Q: What is a machine learning data contract?
A: A machine learning data contract formalizes the interface between data producers and model training pipelines specifying the schema, statistical properties, freshness requirements, and quality metrics that training data must satisfy. It enables catching data quality regressions early through automated contract testing.

Q: What is the role of data sampling in training?
A: Data sampling determines which examples are included in each training batch affecting the gradient's variance and bias. Uniform random sampling is the default but importance sampling reweights examples, curriculum sampling orders by difficulty, and stratified sampling ensures representation of rare classes.

Q: What is a Markov decision process?
A: A Markov decision process is a mathematical framework for sequential decision-making where an agent observes state, takes action, receives reward, and transitions to a new state according to a transition probability distribution satisfying the Markov property that future states depend only on current state and action.

Q: What is the Bellman optimality equation?
A: The Bellman optimality equation characterizes the optimal value function as the maximum over actions of the immediate reward plus the discounted expected value of the next state under the optimal policy. It provides the recursive characterization that value iteration and Q-learning algorithms use to compute optimal policies.

Q: What is value iteration?
A: Value iteration computes the optimal value function by repeatedly applying the Bellman optimality operator to the current value estimate until convergence. Starting from arbitrary initial values the iterates converge to the unique fixed point of the Bellman operator which is the optimal value function.

Q: What is policy iteration?
A: Policy iteration alternates between policy evaluation which computes the value function for the current policy and policy improvement which updates the policy to be greedy with respect to the current value function. It converges to the optimal policy in a finite number of steps for finite MDPs.

Q: What is the reward hypothesis?
A: The reward hypothesis states that all goals and purposes of intelligent agents can be described as the maximization of a scalar reward signal. It is the foundational assumption of reinforcement learning that complex behaviors can be learned through experience guided by a single numerical feedback signal.

Q: What is environment stochasticity in RL?
A: Environment stochasticity means the next state and reward are probabilistic given the current state and action. Stochastic environments require the agent to learn policies robust to this randomness rather than assuming deterministic transitions, which affects both value function estimation and policy optimization.

Q: What is partial observability in RL?
A: Partial observability means the agent does not have access to the complete state of the environment, receiving only observations that provide incomplete information. Partially observable MDPs require agents to maintain beliefs about the hidden state, commonly implemented with recurrent neural networks or attention mechanisms.

Q: What is a belief state in POMDPs?
A: A belief state in a partially observable MDP is a probability distribution over the hidden states of the environment given the history of observations and actions. Optimal planning in POMDPs maintains and updates belief states using Bayes rule at each step.

Q: What is a world model for planning?
A: A world model is an internal representation of the environment's dynamics that an agent uses to simulate the consequences of actions before taking them. Learning accurate world models from experience enables efficient model-based planning that significantly reduces the number of real environment interactions needed.

Q: What is Dreamer?
A: Dreamer is a model-based reinforcement learning algorithm that learns a world model from high-dimensional image observations and trains a policy entirely within the world model's learned latent space through differentiable imagination. It achieves strong performance on visual control tasks with much fewer environment interactions than model-free methods.

Q: What is reward normalization in RL?
A: Reward normalization scales rewards by their running mean and standard deviation to produce unit-variance reward signals. This prevents reward magnitude from dominating over reward variance in determining policy gradient update sizes, improving training stability across environments with very different reward scales.

Q: What is curiosity-driven exploration with ICM?
A: The Intrinsic Curiosity Module learns a forward dynamics model predicting next state features from current state features and action. Prediction error provides an intrinsic reward encouraging exploration of states where the dynamics model is uncertain. ICM enables effective exploration in sparse reward environments.

Q: What is successor representation?
A: The successor representation predicts the discounted expected future occupancy of each state when following a policy, providing an intermediate between model-free value functions and full world models. It factorizes reward and dynamics allowing fast reward generalization while capturing some temporal structure.

Q: What is distributional reinforcement learning?
A: Distributional RL learns the full distribution over returns rather than just the expected return. Return distributions capture uncertainty and enable risk-sensitive decision making. C51, QR-DQN, and IQN are distributional RL algorithms that represent return distributions using categorical distributions, quantile regression, and implicit quantile functions.

Q: What is risk-sensitive reinforcement learning?
A: Risk-sensitive RL optimizes policies that account for the variability of returns in addition to expected value, using risk measures like conditional value at risk that penalize policies with high probability of very bad outcomes. It is important for safety-critical applications where catastrophic outcomes must be avoided.

Q: What is the maximum entropy RL objective?
A: The maximum entropy RL objective augments the standard expected return maximization with an entropy bonus that rewards stochastic policies. This encourages exploration and produces robust policies that can be adapted to new objectives. Soft actor-critic optimizes the maximum entropy objective.

Q: What is reward sparsity?
A: Reward sparsity describes environments where the agent receives non-zero rewards only rarely, such as when successfully completing a task. Sparse rewards make learning difficult because the agent must discover rewarding behaviors through random exploration before any training signal is available.

Q: What is hindsight relabeling?
A: Hindsight relabeling converts unsuccessful goal-reaching trajectories into successful ones by pretending that the state actually reached was the intended goal. This dramatically increases the density of positive examples for training goal-conditioned policies in sparse reward environments.

Q: What is multi-step Q-learning?
A: Multi-step Q-learning uses returns computed by unrolling the trajectory for n steps before bootstrapping from the Q-value estimate at the end. N-step returns have lower bias than one-step TD returns because they use more actual rewards before bootstrapping from the potentially inaccurate value estimate.

Q: What is experience replay design?
A: Experience replay design choices including buffer size, sampling strategy, and memory management significantly affect sample efficiency and stability. Large buffers provide diverse uncorrelated samples but include old transitions from different policies. Prioritized and goal-conditioned replay strategies improve sampling efficiency.

Q: What is model-based policy optimization?
A: Model-based policy optimization trains a neural network model of environment dynamics and uses it to generate model rollouts for policy gradient updates. Mixing model rollouts with real environment data in a ratio that accounts for model error enables sample-efficient policy improvement.

Q: What is neural architecture evolution?
A: Neural architecture evolution uses evolutionary algorithms to search for optimal network architectures by maintaining a population of architecture candidates, selecting the best performers, and applying mutations and crossovers to produce new candidates. It can discover novel architectural motifs not found by gradient-based NAS.

Q: What is hardware-aware NAS?
A: Hardware-aware neural architecture search explicitly optimizes the network architecture for efficient execution on target hardware by measuring latency, energy consumption, or memory usage on the actual device during search and incorporating these hardware metrics into the search objective alongside accuracy.

Q: What is progressive NAS?
A: Progressive neural architecture search starts from simple architectures and progressively increases complexity by adding operations with high predicted value based on a surrogate model. This progressive exploration strategy reduces search cost by evaluating complex architectures only when simpler ones have been exhausted.

Q: What is once-for-all network?
A: Once-for-all network trains a single large network that supports many different sub-networks of different depths, widths, and resolutions. After training the once-for-all network, sub-networks can be extracted and deployed without any additional training, enabling architecture adaptation to different hardware constraints.

Q: What is slimmable network?
A: A slimmable network can be executed at different widths by sharing parameters across width configurations through joint training on all widths simultaneously. Width can be adjusted at runtime to trade off accuracy for latency depending on available compute resources.

Q: What is neural network reliability?
A: Neural network reliability encompasses accuracy, robustness, calibration, and consistency of predictions across diverse inputs and conditions. Reliable systems maintain performance across distribution shifts, provide calibrated uncertainty estimates, and behave predictably in edge cases critical for safety-sensitive deployments.

Q: What is Bayesian model selection?
A: Bayesian model selection uses the marginal likelihood or Bayesian evidence to compare models, automatically penalizing overly complex models that explain training data through memorization rather than generalization. It provides a principled alternative to cross-validation for comparing models of different complexity.

Q: What is hierarchical Bayes?
A: Hierarchical Bayesian models place hyperpriors on the parameters of the prior distribution, allowing the prior to be learned from data rather than specified manually. This enables automatic regularization strength adaptation and transfer of statistical strength across related tasks or groups.

Q: What is Bayesian optimization convergence?
A: Bayesian optimization converges to the global optimum as the number of function evaluations grows under mild conditions on the objective function and acquisition function. The Gaussian process upper confidence bound acquisition function has sublinear cumulative regret bounds guaranteeing eventual convergence.



# ============================================================
# EXTENDED DEEP DIVE SECTION
# ============================================================

Q: Explain the full architecture of the original Transformer model from Attention Is All You Need.
A: The original Transformer uses an encoder-decoder architecture. The encoder stacks six identical layers, each containing two sub-layers: multi-head self-attention and a position-wise feed-forward network. Residual connections and layer normalization wrap each sub-layer. The decoder stacks six layers with three sub-layers: masked multi-head self-attention, cross-attention over encoder outputs, and a feed-forward network. Token embeddings are summed with sinusoidal positional encodings. The output of the decoder stack passes through a linear projection and softmax to produce token probabilities. Multi-head attention projects queries, keys, and values h times with different learned projections, computes scaled dot-product attention in parallel, and concatenates the heads. The feed-forward sub-layer applies two linear transformations with a ReLU in between, using dimension 2048 in the inner layer. The model uses the same weight matrix for input embedding, output embedding, and the pre-softmax linear transformation, scaled by the square root of model dimension. This architecture enabled parallelization over the sequence length dimension, training much faster than recurrent models while achieving state-of-the-art translation performance.

Q: What is cross-attention in the Transformer decoder and how does it work?
A: Cross-attention, also called encoder-decoder attention, allows each decoder position to attend over all positions of the encoder's output sequence. In the decoder's second sub-layer, queries come from the previous decoder sub-layer, while keys and values come from the final encoder output. This means each decoder token can look at any encoder token's representation and weight it by learned compatibility, enabling the decoder to extract the most relevant source information when generating each target token. The attention weights in this layer can be visualized to observe alignment patterns showing which source tokens the model attends to when producing each target word, often resembling the soft word alignments learned by earlier attention mechanisms for machine translation. Cross-attention operates identically to self-attention in terms of computation but differs in the source of queries versus keys and values, bridging the two separate stacks.

Q: How does the Transformer scale with sequence length and what are the consequences?
A: The Transformer's self-attention mechanism has memory and compute complexity that scales quadratically with sequence length. For a sequence of n tokens, computing the full n-by-n attention matrix requires O(n squared) time and memory. This quadratic scaling made early Transformers practical only for sequences up to a few thousand tokens. When applied to documents, books, or long code files, this becomes prohibitive. Researchers have developed linear attention approximations, sparse attention patterns like sliding windows and strided patterns in Longformer and BigBird, low-rank approximations like Linformer, and hierarchical approaches to reduce this quadratic bottleneck. More recently, techniques like FlashAttention optimize the constant factors through better memory access patterns without changing asymptotic complexity, and rotary positional embeddings enable extrapolation to contexts longer than seen during training. Modern frontier models extend effective context to 100,000 or more tokens using combinations of these approaches.

Q: What are the differences between encoder-only, decoder-only, and encoder-decoder Transformer architectures?
A: Encoder-only models like BERT use bidirectional self-attention, allowing every token to attend to all others simultaneously. They produce rich contextual representations of the input but cannot generate text autoregressively. They excel at classification, named entity recognition, and question answering with a provided context. Decoder-only models like GPT use causal masked attention so each token can only attend to preceding tokens. They generate text autoregressively one token at a time and are pretrained with next-token prediction. Fine-tuned or prompted appropriately, they perform a wide range of tasks. Encoder-decoder models like T5 and the original Transformer use an encoder for bidirectional input understanding and a decoder for conditional generation. The decoder cross-attends over encoder outputs, making this architecture natural for transduction tasks like translation and summarization where the output depends on a full understanding of the input. The choice among architectures depends on whether the task primarily requires understanding, generation, or conditional generation.

Q: What is rotary positional embedding and how does it improve on learned positional embeddings?
A: Rotary positional embedding encodes position by applying a rotation to the query and key vectors before computing attention. The rotation at position m uses a frequency-based rotation matrix such that the dot product of a rotated query at position m and a rotated key at position n depends only on their relative position m minus n. This relative encoding means the model naturally generalizes to relative positions and can extrapolate beyond the maximum training length without explicit long-context fine-tuning. Absolute learned positional embeddings simply add a learned vector to each token embedding, with no explicit mechanism to encode relative positions. They do not generalize to positions beyond training length. Sinusoidal positional encodings generalize somewhat but also degrade beyond training length. RoPE also integrates position information directly into the attention computation rather than adding it to embeddings, allowing deeper integration of positional information across layers and producing better performance on tasks requiring long-range reasoning.

Q: What is grouped query attention and multi-query attention?
A: Multi-query attention, or MQA, uses a single shared key and value projection for all attention heads while maintaining separate query projections per head. This reduces the size of the key-value cache at inference time proportionally to the number of heads, dramatically reducing memory bandwidth pressure during autoregressive decoding. Grouped query attention, or GQA, is an interpolation between standard multi-head attention and MQA that groups multiple query heads to share a single key and value projection, offering a configurable tradeoff between cache size and representation quality. For example, with 32 query heads and 8 key-value groups, each key-value projection serves 4 query heads. GQA is used in Llama 2 and many other large language models, achieving nearly the same quality as full multi-head attention with dramatically lower memory bandwidth requirements and therefore faster inference, especially at long context lengths where the key-value cache dominates memory.

Q: What is the difference between pre-norm and post-norm Transformers?
A: Post-norm Transformers, as in the original paper, apply layer normalization after the residual connection: the output is LayerNorm(x plus sublayer(x)). Pre-norm Transformers apply layer normalization before the sublayer: the output is x plus sublayer(LayerNorm(x)). Pre-norm architectures generally train more stably without warmup, produce better gradient flow through the residual stream, and have become the standard for large language models. The key difference is that in pre-norm, the residual pathway is completely unnormalized, preserving the scale of gradients flowing backward. Post-norm normalizes after summation, which can cause vanishing gradients deep in the network during early training. Empirically, pre-norm allows training with larger learning rates and fewer stability issues, though some work suggests post-norm achieves slightly better final perplexity when trained to full convergence with careful initialization.

Q: How does flash attention work at a hardware level?
A: Standard attention materializes the full n-by-n attention matrix in GPU high-bandwidth memory, which becomes the bottleneck for long sequences. FlashAttention restructures the computation to tile the queries, keys, and values into blocks that fit in the much faster SRAM on-chip memory. It computes the softmax and weighted sum incrementally using a running maximum and normalizer trick, never fully materializing the attention matrix in HBM. The backward pass recomputes the attention matrix on-the-fly from the stored query, key, and value tiles rather than storing the full matrix, reducing memory usage from O(n squared) to O(n). The result is mathematically exact, not approximate, but with wall-clock speedup of two to four times and memory reduction proportional to sequence length. FlashAttention-2 further optimizes the tiling strategy and parallelization across attention heads and sequence positions. FlashAttention-3 targets Hopper GPU architectures with asynchronous operations and FP8 support for additional throughput.

Q: What is GPTQ and how does it quantize large language models?
A: GPTQ is a post-training quantization method for large transformer models that uses approximate second-order information to minimize the quantization error layer by layer. For each linear layer, it greedily quantizes individual weights one at a time while updating the remaining unquantized weights to compensate for each quantization error, using a Cholesky decomposition of the Hessian to compute the optimal compensating update efficiently. This layerwise reconstruction approach allows GPTQ to quantize models to 4-bit or even 3-bit precision with accuracy remarkably close to the full-precision model, unlike simpler round-to-nearest quantization which suffers much larger degradation. GPTQ processes a small calibration dataset of a few hundred sequences to estimate the Hessian, taking minutes to hours on a single GPU for models with tens of billions of parameters. The resulting quantized weights are stored in compressed format and dequantized on the fly during inference, reducing memory by four times or more while incurring modest compute overhead.

Q: What is the difference between weight-only quantization and activation quantization?
A: Weight-only quantization compresses the stored weight matrices to low-precision integers while dequantizing them back to float16 or bfloat16 before matrix multiplications at inference time. The compute is still performed in floating point, but memory bandwidth savings reduce the bottleneck for memory-bandwidth-limited generation workloads. It is easy to apply post-training with minimal accuracy loss. Activation quantization also quantizes the intermediate activations during computation to integers, enabling true integer matrix multiplication which is faster than floating-point on hardware with integer compute units. However, activations have dynamic ranges that vary per input, making quantization harder. Large outlier values in activations, common in large language models, cause accuracy degradation when naively quantized. SmoothQuant and LLM.int8() address this through mixed-precision schemes that handle outlier channels in float16 while quantizing the majority in int8, achieving nearly lossless activation quantization for large models.

Q: What is knowledge distillation for language models?
A: Knowledge distillation for language models trains a small student model to match the token-level probability distributions of a larger teacher model over a large corpus. The soft targets from the teacher encode inter-token probability relationships and uncertainty that carry more information than one-hot labels, providing a richer training signal. The distillation loss is typically a weighted combination of the cross-entropy against hard labels and the KL divergence against the teacher's soft distributions, with a temperature parameter that softens the teacher's distribution to spread probability mass across more tokens. DistilBERT reduces BERT's size by 40 percent and speed by 60 percent while retaining 97 percent of performance on GLUE by combining distillation during pretraining with task-specific fine-tuning. For generative models, sequence-level distillation trains the student on samples from the teacher, while token-level distillation directly minimizes divergence at each generation step.

Q: What is LLM.int8() quantization?
A: LLM.int8() is a mixed-precision quantization scheme that addresses the challenge of large outlier activations in large language models. It decomposes matrix multiplications into two parts: a small fraction of outlier-containing feature dimensions are multiplied in float16, while the remaining dimensions are quantized to int8 and multiplied with int8 matrix multiplication. The results are dequantized and summed together. The key insight is that outlier activations appear in a consistent small set of feature dimensions across all tokens, allowing efficient identification and separate handling. This approach enables inference on very large models like OPT-175B at half the GPU memory footprint of float16 while incurring only 0.1 percent accuracy degradation on downstream tasks. The mixed-precision handling adds overhead compared to pure int8 but avoids the large accuracy drops from naively quantizing outlier-containing activations.

Q: What is Bayes' theorem and how is it used in machine learning?
A: Bayes' theorem states that the posterior probability of a hypothesis given observed data equals the likelihood of the data given the hypothesis multiplied by the prior probability of the hypothesis, divided by the marginal probability of the data. In machine learning, it provides a principled framework for updating beliefs about model parameters given training data. Bayesian inference uses the posterior distribution over parameters rather than a point estimate, quantifying uncertainty in a principled way. The prior encodes assumptions about parameter distributions before seeing data, the likelihood connects parameters to observed data, and the posterior combines both. In practice, computing the exact posterior is intractable for deep models, motivating approximate inference methods like variational inference and MCMC. Naive Bayes classification applies Bayes' theorem with a conditional independence assumption, Bayesian optimization uses it for hyperparameter search, and Bayesian neural networks represent uncertainty over weights.

Q: What is maximum likelihood estimation?
A: Maximum likelihood estimation finds the model parameters that maximize the probability of observing the training data under the model. Equivalently, it minimizes the negative log-likelihood, which for many distributions connects directly to familiar loss functions: negative log-likelihood of a Gaussian is mean squared error, and for a categorical distribution it is cross-entropy. MLE provides an unbiased parameter estimate that is asymptotically efficient, meaning it achieves the Cramér-Rao lower bound on estimation variance as sample size grows. It does not encode prior beliefs and can overfit with small datasets. Maximum a posteriori estimation adds a prior term to the objective, equivalent to L2 regularization for Gaussian priors and L1 for Laplace priors, providing a Bayesian perspective on regularization.

Q: What is the central limit theorem and why does it matter for machine learning?
A: The central limit theorem states that the sum or mean of a large number of independent, identically distributed random variables converges in distribution to a Gaussian, regardless of the original distribution, provided the variables have finite variance. In machine learning it justifies treating sample means as approximately Gaussian for statistical testing and confidence intervals. It explains why gradient noise in stochastic gradient descent with large batches is approximately Gaussian. It underpins the validity of many statistical hypothesis tests used in model evaluation. It also explains why features that are sums of many independent factors tend to follow approximately normal distributions in practice, motivating normalization preprocessing.

Q: What is a Gaussian process and when is it used in ML?
A: A Gaussian process is a distribution over functions where any finite collection of function values follows a multivariate Gaussian distribution. It is fully specified by a mean function and a covariance kernel that encodes prior beliefs about function smoothness and structure. Given training data, the posterior GP provides not just a point prediction but a full distribution over function values, enabling principled uncertainty quantification. GPs are used for Bayesian optimization of expensive black-box functions like hyperparameter search, spatial interpolation in geostatistics, and as uncertainty-aware regression models.
