Their limitation is cubic computational cost in the number of training points, making them impractical for datasets larger than a few thousand points without sparse approximations.

Q: What is the expectation-maximization algorithm?
A: The expectation-maximization algorithm is an iterative optimization method for maximum likelihood estimation with latent variables. The E step computes the expected values of the latent variables under the current parameter estimates, producing a lower bound on the log-likelihood. The M step maximizes this lower bound by updating the parameters, which is typically easier than directly optimizing the marginal likelihood. EM is guaranteed to increase the log-likelihood at each iteration and converges to a local maximum. It is used for Gaussian mixture model fitting, hidden Markov model training, and semi-supervised learning. The generalized EM relaxes the M step to any update that increases the bound, and variational EM replaces the E step with an approximate variational distribution when the true posterior is intractable.

Q: What is a hidden Markov model?
A: A hidden Markov model is a generative probabilistic model where an unobserved Markov chain of discrete states generates observed outputs at each step. The model is defined by initial state probabilities, transition probabilities between states, and emission probabilities of observations given states. The Viterbi algorithm finds the most likely state sequence given observations, the forward-backward algorithm computes marginal state probabilities for training with EM, and the Baum-Welch algorithm is the EM specialization for HMMs. HMMs were the dominant approach for speech recognition and biological sequence modeling before deep learning, and they remain relevant in hybrid neural-HMM systems and bioinformatics.

Q: What is a Markov chain Monte Carlo method?
A: MCMC is a class of algorithms for sampling from probability distributions that are difficult to sample from directly, such as Bayesian posteriors over neural network weights. A Markov chain is constructed whose stationary distribution equals the target distribution, and samples are collected after a burn-in period. Metropolis-Hastings accepts or rejects proposed moves to ensure the correct stationary distribution. Gibbs sampling alternately samples each variable conditioned on the others. Hamiltonian Monte Carlo uses gradient information to make large, efficient moves through parameter space. MCMC is used for Bayesian inference, generative model training, and evaluating intractable partition functions in energy-based models.

Q: What is variational inference?
A: Variational inference approximates an intractable posterior distribution with a simpler variational distribution from a tractable family, optimizing the parameters of the variational distribution to minimize the KL divergence to the true posterior. Equivalently, it maximizes the evidence lower bound, the ELBO, which equals the expected log-likelihood minus the KL divergence between the variational and prior distributions. Mean-field variational inference assumes the variational distribution factorizes across latent variables. Amortized variational inference uses a neural encoder to predict variational parameters from data, enabling scalable batched inference as in variational autoencoders. Variational inference is faster than MCMC but provides a biased approximation whose quality depends on the expressiveness of the variational family.

Q: What is importance sampling?
A: Importance sampling estimates expectations under a distribution that is difficult to sample from by sampling from a simpler proposal distribution and correcting the estimates with importance weights equal to the ratio of the target to proposal density. It is used for Monte Carlo integration, evaluating language model perplexity, approximating posteriors in Bayesian methods, and off-policy policy evaluation in reinforcement learning. Self-normalized importance sampling normalizes the weights to sum to one, reducing variance at the cost of bias. Importance weights with high variance degrade the quality of estimates, making the choice of proposal distribution critical for practical effectiveness.

Q: What is bootstrapping in statistics?
A: Bootstrapping estimates the sampling distribution of a statistic by resampling with replacement from the observed data to create many synthetic datasets of the same size. For each resample, the statistic is computed, and the distribution across resamples approximates its true sampling distribution. This provides confidence intervals and standard errors without assuming a parametric distribution. In machine learning, paired bootstrap tests compare model performance by resampling test examples to estimate whether observed accuracy differences are significant. Ensemble methods like bagging use bootstrap sampling to create diverse training sets for multiple models whose predictions are then aggregated.

Q: What is the difference between batch processing and stream processing in ML pipelines?
A: Batch processing collects data over a period, then processes the entire batch together on a schedule, such as nightly model retraining on the previous day's data. It is simple, allows complex processing with arbitrary lookback, and is efficient for large volumes. Stream processing handles data as it arrives event by event or in micro-batches of seconds, enabling near-real-time feature computation and model updates. It requires stateful processing with bounded memory and is more complex to implement and debug. In ML systems, batch processing handles model training while stream processing computes serving features. Lambda architecture combines both: batch for accuracy and streaming for recency. Kappa architecture unifies both using a streaming system capable of replaying historical data.

Q: What is data lineage and why is it important?
A: Data lineage tracks the origin, movement, transformation, and consumption of data throughout an ML system, from raw sources through preprocessing, feature computation, model training, and predictions. It enables debugging data quality issues by tracing problems back to their source, supports regulatory compliance by documenting how sensitive data flows, facilitates impact analysis when upstream data changes, and enables reproducibility by recording exactly what data produced each model version. Tools like Apache Atlas, OpenLineage, and platform-native lineage tracking capture metadata automatically from pipeline runs, making data lineage increasingly automated as a standard part of MLOps infrastructure.

Q: What is data catalog?
A: A data catalog is a centralized inventory of an organization's data assets, providing metadata including schemas, descriptions, ownership, quality metrics, lineage, and access policies. For machine learning, it helps practitioners discover available datasets and features, understand their contents and quality, and assess their suitability for a modeling task. Modern ML-oriented catalogs integrate with feature stores to surface available features alongside their documentation, statistics, and example notebooks, accelerating the iteration cycle for new models by making feature discovery efficient.

Q: What is schema evolution in data pipelines?
A: Schema evolution refers to changes in the structure of data over time, such as added fields, removed columns, or changed data types. ML pipelines must handle schema evolution gracefully because a model trained on a schema may receive data conforming to an evolved schema at serving time. Strategies include backward compatibility where new schemas are supersets of old ones, schema registries that version and enforce compatibility, and model serving code that handles missing or unexpected fields with defaults. Failing to manage schema evolution is a common cause of silent prediction failures in production ML systems.

Q: What is data validation in ML pipelines?
A: Data validation checks that incoming data meets expected statistical and structural properties before it is used for training or serving. It verifies schemas, detects missing values, identifies out-of-distribution feature values, checks referential integrity, and alerts on statistical shifts compared to historical baselines. TensorFlow Data Validation and Great Expectations are popular tools that compute and compare data statistics automatically. Catching data quality issues at ingestion prevents training on corrupted data and serving incorrect predictions caused by upstream data problems.

Q: What is the medallion architecture for data?
A: The medallion architecture organizes data storage into bronze, silver, and gold layers. The bronze layer stores raw, unprocessed data as ingested from sources. The silver layer contains cleaned, validated, and lightly transformed data. The gold layer holds fully aggregated, feature-engineered datasets ready for modeling or business reporting. This progressive refinement approach decouples raw data preservation from transformed representations, enables reprocessing from raw when transformations change, and clarifies data quality expectations at each layer for ML practitioners.

Q: What is object detection and how has it evolved?
A: Object detection identifies and localizes all instances of objects in an image, predicting both class labels and bounding box coordinates. Early approaches like sliding window detectors were slow and inaccurate. R-CNN used selective search to propose candidate regions then classified them with CNNs, improving accuracy but remaining slow. Faster R-CNN introduced a region proposal network sharing convolutional features with the classifier, enabling near real-time detection. YOLO and SSD reformulated detection as a single regression problem over a grid, achieving real-time speeds. Feature pyramid networks added multi-scale feature representations for detecting objects at different scales. Today, transformer-based detectors like DETR formulate detection as set prediction with learned queries, eliminating hand-designed components like anchor boxes and NMS.

Q: What is instance segmentation?
A: Instance segmentation identifies and segments each individual object instance in an image, producing a per-pixel mask for each detected object. Unlike semantic segmentation which assigns class labels per pixel without distinguishing instances, instance segmentation must separate overlapping objects of the same class. Mask R-CNN extends Faster R-CNN with a parallel mask prediction head that predicts a binary segmentation mask for each detected region. Recent approaches like SOLO and QueryInst use instance-specific features without relying on bounding boxes, and SAM, the Segment Anything Model, uses a promptable architecture that generalizes to arbitrary segmentation tasks from point, box, or text prompts.

Q: What is semantic segmentation?
A: Semantic segmentation assigns a class label to every pixel in an image, providing a dense understanding of scene content. Fully convolutional networks replace the classification head with convolutional layers to produce spatial output maps. Encoder-decoder architectures like U-Net use skip connections to combine low-level spatial detail from the encoder with high-level semantic features from the decoder. DeepLab models use atrous convolutions to maintain spatial resolution while expanding the receptive field and apply conditional random fields for boundary refinement. Transformer-based segmenters like SegFormer use hierarchical transformer encoders with lightweight all-MLP decoders, achieving state of the art with efficient architectures.

Q: What is the COCO dataset?
A: COCO, Common Objects in Context, is a large-scale dataset for object detection, instance segmentation, keypoint detection, and caption generation containing over 330,000 images with 1.5 million object instances across 80 categories. Objects are annotated with bounding boxes, segmentation masks, and keypoints in natural scene images with complex backgrounds and multiple objects. The COCO benchmark drives progress in detection and segmentation, with mean average precision metrics computed across multiple IoU thresholds and object scales providing detailed performance characterization.

Q: What is optical flow in computer vision?
A: Optical flow estimates the apparent motion of pixels or regions between consecutive video frames, represented as a vector field of per-pixel displacements. It is used for video understanding, action recognition, object tracking, and video stabilization. Classical approaches like Lucas-Kanade use local image gradients and brightness constancy assumptions. Deep learning approaches like FlowNet and PWC-Net learn to predict flow directly from image pairs, and RAFT computes flow by iteratively updating from a 4D correlation volume of all pairwise patch similarities.

Q: What is self-supervised pretraining for vision?
A: Self-supervised pretraining for vision learns representations from unlabeled images by solving pretext tasks that generate supervision from the data itself. Contrastive methods like SimCLR and MoCo train representations to be invariant to different augmentations of the same image. Masked image modeling methods like MAE mask patches of an image and train an encoder-decoder to reconstruct the masked regions, learning rich representations that transfer efficiently to downstream tasks with linear probing or fine-tuning, matching or exceeding supervised pretraining with much less labeled data.

Q: What is ARIMA and when is it appropriate?
A: ARIMA, AutoRegressive Integrated Moving Average, models univariate time series as a combination of an autoregressive component using past values, differencing to achieve stationarity, and a moving average component using past forecast errors. The model is specified by three hyperparameters: the order of autoregression p, the degree of differencing d, and the order of the moving average q. It is appropriate for stationary or trend-stationary series after differencing, captures linear temporal dependencies efficiently, and is interpretable and fast to fit. It is less suitable for non-linear dynamics, multivariate dependencies, or very long-range dependencies, where deep learning approaches outperform it.

Q: What is the temporal fusion transformer?
A: Temporal Fusion Transformer is an attention-based architecture for multi-horizon time series forecasting that explicitly handles multiple input types: static metadata, known future inputs, and observed historical inputs. Gating mechanisms select relevant information at each time step, variable selection networks identify important features, and multi-head attention captures long-range temporal dependencies. It also produces quantile forecasts enabling uncertainty estimation. TFT outperforms many competitors on diverse forecasting benchmarks while providing interpretable attention patterns that reveal which historical time steps and input variables drive each forecast.

Q: What is time series cross-validation?
A: Time series cross-validation respects temporal ordering by always using past data to predict future data, avoiding data leakage from the future. Rolling window validation uses a fixed-size expanding or sliding training window and evaluates on a fixed-size subsequent test window, repeating the split many times to obtain stable performance estimates. Unlike standard k-fold cross-validation which shuffles data, time series cross-validation correctly simulates the deployment scenario where models are trained on historical data and deployed on future data.

Q: What is seasonality decomposition in time series?
A: Seasonality decomposition separates a time series into trend, seasonal, and residual components. Additive decomposition suits series where seasonal variation is roughly constant in magnitude, while multiplicative decomposition suits series where variation scales with the level. STL, Seasonal-Trend decomposition using LOESS, robustly decomposes series with any seasonal period and handles outliers gracefully. Decomposition helps select appropriate forecasting models, detect anomalies in the residual component, and understand the sources of variation in a time series for business interpretation.

Q: What is the Bellman equation?
A: The Bellman equation expresses the value of a state as the expected sum of immediate reward plus discounted value of the next state, providing a recursive self-consistency condition for value functions. The Bellman optimality equation characterizes the optimal value function satisfying this recursion with greedy action selection. Value-based RL methods solve or approximate these equations to find optimal policies. The discount factor gamma controls how much future rewards are valued relative to immediate ones, with gamma near one producing far-sighted policies and near zero producing myopic ones.

Q: What is the exploration-exploitation dilemma?
A: The exploration-exploitation dilemma is the fundamental tradeoff in reinforcement learning between exploiting the best known actions to maximize immediate reward and exploring less-known actions to potentially discover better long-term strategies. Pure exploitation can miss better options and converge to suboptimal policies. Pure exploration wastes time on known-poor actions. The epsilon-greedy strategy takes a random action with probability epsilon and the best known action otherwise. Upper confidence bound methods take actions proportional to the upper confidence bound on their estimated values, naturally balancing exploration based on uncertainty.

Q: What is Monte Carlo tree search?
A: Monte Carlo Tree Search is a planning algorithm that builds a search tree by iterating through four phases: selection using UCB to navigate from root to a promising leaf, expansion adding a new node, simulation rolling out a random policy from the new node, and backpropagation updating statistics from leaf to root. MCTS has excellent anytime performance, scales with compute, and requires no domain-specific heuristics beyond a simulation policy. It was a key component of AlphaGo combined with value and policy networks that replaced the random rollout with neural network evaluations.

Q: What is AlphaGo and AlphaZero?
A: AlphaGo combined Monte Carlo Tree Search with deep neural networks to defeat world champion Go players, using a policy network to guide tree search and a value network to evaluate positions, both trained on human game data and refined with self-play reinforcement learning. AlphaZero generalized this approach to chess, shogi, and Go simultaneously using a single architecture trained purely through self-play from random initialization with no human domain knowledge beyond the game rules. MuZero extended AlphaZero to learn the dynamics model of the environment, enabling planning in settings where the rules are not known in advance.

Q: What is imitation learning?
A: Imitation learning trains a policy to replicate the behavior of an expert from demonstrations, avoiding the difficulty of specifying a reward function. Behavioral cloning directly applies supervised learning to predict the expert's actions from states. Distribution shift between training and deployment causes compounding errors in behavioral cloning that DAgger addresses by iteratively collecting data from the learned policy and querying the expert for corrections. Inverse reinforcement learning infers the reward function that explains expert behavior, producing a reward that can then be used to train policies that generalize beyond demonstrated situations.

Q: What is the two-phase deployment process for ML models?
A: A two-phase deployment process separates validation from activation. In the first phase, the new model is deployed in shadow mode or behind a feature flag, processing requests and logging predictions without serving them to users. This validates that the model runs correctly in production infrastructure, measures latency and resource consumption, and compares predictions to the existing model without user impact. In the second phase, traffic is gradually shifted to the new model, starting with a small canary percentage and monitoring key metrics before full rollout. This staged approach catches integration issues and regressions that would not be detectable in offline evaluation.

Q: What is the difference between online and batch feature computation?
A: Online feature computation calculates features in real time when a prediction request arrives, enabling features based on very recent events. It must complete within the serving latency budget, making complex aggregations impractical. Batch feature computation runs periodically on historical data and materializes results to storage, enabling complex aggregations over long time windows but introducing staleness. Feature stores manage both computation paradigms and ensure training-serving consistency by providing the same features computed identically regardless of whether they are materialized batch features or computed online, preventing training-serving skew.

Q: What is a model serving SLA and how is it monitored?
A: A model serving SLA specifies performance requirements including median latency, tail latency at p99 or p999, maximum error rate, and minimum availability percentage. Monitoring involves collecting per-request timing from the serving infrastructure, computing rolling percentiles, and alerting when SLA thresholds are breached. Distributed tracing with tools like Jaeger identifies which components of a prediction pipeline contribute most to latency. Load testing with representative traffic profiles validates that SLAs are achievable at peak request volumes before production deployment.

Q: What is training-serving skew?
A: Training-serving skew is the discrepancy between how features are computed during model training and how they are computed during serving, causing the model to receive different inputs than it was trained on. Common causes include using future information during training that is unavailable at serving time, computing features differently in batch training jobs versus online serving code, and missing feature values handled differently in training and production. Feature stores with shared computation logic enforce consistency, and monitoring feature distributions at training and serving time detects skew before it degrades predictions.

Q: What is a model lineage graph?
A: A model lineage graph records the complete chain of data sources, preprocessing steps, feature engineering transformations, training runs, and evaluation results that produced a model. It enables debugging by tracing a model's behavior back to the data that caused it, supports regulatory compliance by documenting exactly what data was used, and facilitates reproducibility by recording every parameter and dataset version. Tools like MLflow and Weights and Biases automatically capture lineage metadata during training runs, and platform-level lineage tracking extends this to cover the full pipeline.

Q: What is the MMLU benchmark?
A: Massive Multitask Language Understanding evaluates language models on 57 academic subjects including mathematics, law, medicine, history, and ethics using multiple choice questions. It tests knowledge and reasoning across diverse domains, providing a comprehensive measure of a model's world knowledge and reasoning abilities acquired during pretraining. Performance on MMLU is highly correlated with model scale and training data quality, making it a standard benchmark for comparing frontier language models. It is increasingly subject to contamination concerns as models are trained on data that may include MMLU questions.

Q: What is HumanEval and what does it measure?
A: HumanEval is a benchmark for evaluating code generation ability using 164 programming problems that specify a function signature and docstring, with the model completing the function body. Correctness is evaluated by running unit tests, measuring pass at k, the probability that at least one of k sampled solutions passes all tests. It measures the model's ability to translate natural language specifications into correct executable code for algorithms and data structures, making it the standard benchmark for comparing code generation models.

Q: What is the BIG-bench benchmark?
A: BIG-bench, Beyond the Imitation Game benchmark, contains over 200 tasks contributed by researchers spanning diverse capabilities including logical reasoning, causal reasoning, mathematical reasoning, translation, social bias, and commonsense knowledge. Tasks are designed to be challenging for current models, probing capabilities beyond those measured by existing benchmarks. The BIG-bench Hard subset focuses on the 23 tasks where model performance is still below human average, providing a challenging benchmark that better differentiates frontier model capabilities.

Q: What is TruthfulQA?
A: TruthfulQA evaluates whether language models generate truthful responses to questions that humans often answer incorrectly due to common misconceptions, conspiracy theories, or false beliefs. It measures how often models parrot human falsehoods versus providing accurate answers, finding that larger language models are often more likely to generate plausible but false answers when trained to be fluent and confident. Combining accuracy and informativeness metrics provides a composite score that penalizes both falsehoods and uninformative refusals.

Q: What is the Winogrande benchmark?
A: WinoGrande is a large-scale commonsense reasoning benchmark containing over 44,000 problems in the Winograd schema format, where a pronoun in a sentence must be resolved by understanding commonsense context. The problems are adversarially filtered to remove biases that allow statistical shortcuts, requiring genuine commonsense reasoning. It tests a model's understanding of physical, social, and causal relationships that are obvious to humans but difficult for models to learn from text alone.

Q: What is double descent in deep learning?
A: Double descent describes a phenomenon where test error first decreases, then increases as model capacity grows through the classical overfitting regime, but then decreases again beyond the interpolation threshold where the model can fit the training data exactly. This second descent extends classical bias-variance tradeoff intuition and explains why overparameterized models like deep networks can generalize well despite having far more parameters than training examples. It has been observed as a function of model size, training duration, and dataset size.

Q: What is the neural tangent kernel?
A: The neural tangent kernel describes the behavior of infinitely wide neural networks trained with gradient descent, showing they are equivalent to kernel machines with a specific kernel determined by the network architecture. In the infinite-width limit, network weights change infinitesimally during training and the network evolves as a linear model in function space. This theoretical framework provides exact analysis of training dynamics for overparameterized networks but may not capture the feature learning that makes finite-width deep networks powerful in practice.

Q: What is the feature learning regime in neural networks?
A: The feature learning regime describes how neural networks with finite width learn new internal representations during training, as opposed to the lazy training regime where the network behaves like a linear model from initialization. Feature learning is responsible for the practical success of deep networks since learned features can be more useful than fixed kernel features. Understanding when and how neural networks learn useful features is a central open question in deep learning theory.

Q: What is grokking in neural networks?
A: Grokking is the phenomenon where neural networks first memorize training data and then, with extended training, generalize to the test set long after achieving zero training loss. It was discovered on modular arithmetic tasks where networks appeared to have memorized then suddenly learned the underlying mathematical structure. Grokking suggests that generalization can emerge from continued optimization well past apparent convergence and has motivated theoretical work on the dynamics of learning representations.

Q: What is the role of overparameterization in generalization?
A: Overparameterized models have more parameters than training examples yet often generalize well, contradicting classical statistical intuition. Implicit regularization from stochastic gradient descent biases learning toward solutions with specific properties such as minimum norm, flat loss landscapes, or maximum margin, even without explicit regularization. The benign overfitting phenomenon describes conditions under which interpolating noisy training data can still generalize, occurring when the signal is captured by a small number of directions in parameter space while noise is spread across the remaining high-dimensional directions.

Q: What is model robustness and why does it matter?
A: Model robustness measures whether predictions remain accurate and stable under small perturbations to the input or distribution shifts from the training environment. A robust model produces consistent predictions on semantically equivalent inputs, resists adversarial attacks designed to fool it, and maintains performance when deployed on data from different sources or time periods. Robustness is critical for safety-critical applications where inputs may be noisy, corrupted, or adversarially manipulated, and where distribution shift between development and deployment environments is common.

Q: What is adversarial robustness?
A: Adversarial robustness is the ability of a model to maintain correct predictions when inputs are modified by small adversarial perturbations designed to cause misclassification. Adversarial examples are imperceptible to humans but dramatically change model predictions. Adversarial training, which augments training with adversarial examples, is the most effective defense, certified robustness uses mathematical bounds on perturbation resistance, and input preprocessing defenses attempt to remove adversarial perturbations before model prediction.

Q: What is adversarial training?
A: Adversarial training augments the training set with adversarially perturbed examples, training the model to correctly classify them alongside clean examples. At each training step, an inner maximization finds the worst-case perturbation within an allowed norm ball, and the outer minimization updates the model to be robust to this perturbation. PGD adversarial training uses projected gradient descent for the inner attack and produces models with certified robustness guarantees within the training perturbation budget, at the cost of reduced accuracy on clean inputs.

Q: What is AI governance?
A: AI governance encompasses the policies, processes, roles, and oversight mechanisms that ensure AI systems are developed and deployed responsibly. It includes technical governance through model auditing, bias testing, and performance monitoring, and organizational governance through review boards, approval processes, and accountability structures. Regulatory compliance with frameworks like GDPR, the EU AI Act, and financial regulations requires demonstrable governance evidence. Effective AI governance balances innovation velocity with risk management through proportionate requirements based on system risk level.

Q: What is model auditing?
A: Model auditing systematically evaluates a deployed model's performance, fairness, robustness, and compliance with organizational policies. Internal audits assess model behavior across diverse subpopulations and stress test scenarios. External third-party audits provide independent validation and greater credibility for high-stakes applications. Audit findings may trigger model updates, usage restrictions, or deployment withdrawal. Maintaining audit trails of model versions, training data, and evaluation results is essential for demonstrating compliance with regulations requiring explainability and non-discrimination.

Q: What is link prediction in graph neural networks?
A: Link prediction determines whether an edge should exist between two nodes in a graph based on their features and the existing graph structure. GNNs compute node embeddings that capture neighborhood context, and link predictions are made by combining the embeddings of candidate node pairs using dot products or learned scoring functions. Applications include recommending connections in social networks, predicting protein-protein interactions, and completing knowledge graphs by identifying missing relationships between entities.

Q: What is node classification with GNNs?
A: Node classification assigns labels to nodes based on their features and graph structure, learning representations that aggregate information from a node's neighborhood. The GNN computes embeddings by iterating message passing layers, and a classification head predicts node labels from these embeddings. Semi-supervised node classification uses a small fraction of labeled nodes with a large graph, exploiting the graph structure to propagate label information to unlabeled nodes through multi-hop aggregation.

Q: What is a knowledge graph?
A: A knowledge graph represents structured knowledge as a graph of entities and relationships, encoding facts as triples of subject, relation, object such as Einstein, bornIn, Germany. Knowledge graphs power question answering, recommendation, and semantic search by enabling reasoning over structured facts. Graph embedding methods like TransE, RotatE, and ComplEx learn dense representations of entities and relations that enable link prediction to complete missing facts and support downstream machine learning tasks.

Q: What is a multimodal large language model?
A: A multimodal large language model processes and generates content across multiple modalities including text, images, audio, and video within a unified architecture. The model encodes each modality with modality-specific encoders, projects their representations into a shared token space, and processes them with a language model backbone. Examples include GPT-4V, Gemini, and Claude, which accept interleaved text and image inputs and produce text outputs. Training uses paired multimodal data and instruction tuning to align model behavior with human expectations across diverse cross-modal tasks.

Q: What is image captioning?
A: Image captioning automatically generates natural language descriptions of image content, requiring integration of visual understanding and language generation. Encoder-decoder architectures encode the image with a CNN or vision transformer then decode a caption with a language model conditioned on the visual features. Attention mechanisms allow the decoder to focus on relevant image regions when generating each word. BLEU, METEOR, CIDEr, and SPICE metrics evaluate caption quality by comparing to human reference captions, with CIDEr specifically designed to correlate with human consensus judgments.

Q: What is visual question answering?
A: Visual question answering requires answering natural language questions about image content, demanding both visual understanding and language reasoning. Models encode the image and question into a shared representation space and predict the answer, which may be a category label, number, or free-form text. VQA benchmarks reveal that early models exploited language biases to answer questions correctly without using image information, motivating adversarially balanced benchmarks that require genuine visual grounding for correct answers.

Q: What is target encoding for categorical features?
A: Target encoding replaces categorical feature values with statistics computed from the target variable, such as the mean of the target for all training examples with that category value. It converts high-cardinality categoricals to informative numeric features without the dimensionality explosion of one-hot encoding. Cross-validation or additive smoothing with the global mean prevents overfitting to rare categories. Target encoding can introduce data leakage if computed on the full training set, requiring careful implementation with out-of-fold statistics.

Q: What is feature hashing?
A: Feature hashing, or the hashing trick, maps high-cardinality categorical features to a fixed-size feature vector using a hash function. Rather than maintaining an explicit vocabulary mapping each category to an index, a hash function deterministically assigns features to indices, allowing efficient online processing of new categories without vocabulary updates. Hash collisions can cause different features to be mapped to the same index, introducing noise, but in practice the large feature spaces used mitigate collision effects and the simplicity and scalability often outweigh this cost.

Q: What is stacking as an ensemble method?
A: Stacking trains a meta-learner on the predictions of multiple base models, learning how to optimally combine their outputs. Base models are trained using cross-validation to generate out-of-fold predictions on the training set, which serve as training data for the meta-learner, preventing leakage. The meta-learner learns which base models are reliable in which regions of input space, producing better predictions than any individual model or simple averaging, especially when base models make uncorrelated errors.

Q: What is blending versus stacking?
A: Blending is a simpler variant of stacking that uses a single holdout validation set to generate predictions from base models rather than cross-validation. Base models are trained on the training portion, predict the validation set, and these predictions train the meta-learner. Blending is simpler and faster but uses less data for meta-learner training and may produce less stable estimates. Stacking uses all training data through cross-validation but is more computationally expensive and complex to implement correctly.

Q: What is gradient boosting?
A: Gradient boosting builds an ensemble of weak learners, typically decision trees, where each successive model fits the pseudo-residuals of the current ensemble. The pseudo-residuals are the negative gradients of the loss function with respect to the current predictions, allowing gradient boosting to optimize any differentiable loss function. Each tree is scaled by a learning rate before being added to the ensemble, and the number of trees and tree depth are key hyperparameters controlling the bias-variance tradeoff.

Q: What is XGBoost?
A: XGBoost is an optimized gradient boosting implementation that includes second-order gradient statistics in the tree construction, L1 and L2 regularization of leaf weights and tree complexity, automatic handling of missing values, and efficient parallel computation using sorted column blocks. These improvements made gradient boosting the dominant algorithm for structured tabular data competitions, offering both high performance and fast training. XGBoost's second-order approximation of the objective function produces more accurate split finding than first-order methods alone.

Q: What is LightGBM?
A: LightGBM is a gradient boosting framework that achieves faster training than XGBoost through two key innovations: gradient-based one-side sampling reduces data size by keeping high-gradient examples and randomly sampling low-gradient ones, and exclusive feature bundling reduces feature dimensionality by grouping mutually exclusive sparse features. Leaf-wise growth instead of level-wise growth produces more accurate trees for a given number of leaves. These optimizations enable LightGBM to train ten to twenty times faster than XGBoost on large datasets while achieving similar or better accuracy.

Q: What is CatBoost?
A: CatBoost is a gradient boosting library specifically designed for categorical features, using ordered target encoding that computes statistics from only the data points preceding each example in a random permutation, preventing target leakage. Symmetric trees ensure consistent depth and enable efficient prediction. CatBoost often achieves competitive performance on datasets with many categorical features without manual preprocessing, reducing the feature engineering burden and improving results when categories have high cardinality.

Q: What is an inference endpoint?
A: An inference endpoint is an API endpoint that accepts prediction requests, performs model inference, and returns responses. Endpoints abstract away the underlying serving infrastructure, allowing applications to make predictions via standard REST or gRPC interfaces. They handle authentication, rate limiting, request validation, batching, and routing to model replicas. Managed inference endpoints on cloud platforms handle scaling, hardware provisioning, and monitoring automatically, reducing operational overhead for teams deploying models to production.

Q: What is a prediction cache?
A: A prediction cache stores model predictions for previously seen inputs and returns cached results for repeated identical requests, avoiding redundant inference computation. It is effective for high-traffic applications with a manageable number of unique inputs, such as category classifiers for product titles. Cache invalidation must be managed when the model is updated to prevent serving predictions from the old model. Approximate caching can handle near-duplicate inputs by retrieving cached predictions for semantically similar queries using embedding-based lookup.

Q: What is model A/B testing infrastructure?
A: Model A/B testing infrastructure routes a configurable fraction of production traffic to each model variant, logs predictions and associated outcomes for each variant separately, and provides statistical analysis tools to determine whether observed differences in metrics are significant. It requires experiment tracking to manage multiple concurrent experiments, user-level assignment to ensure consistent experiences, and guardrail metrics to detect harmful regressions that would trigger automatic rollback regardless of the primary metric.

Q: What is a feature pipeline and how does it relate to model training?
A: A feature pipeline transforms raw data into the feature vectors that a model expects, applying the same preprocessing steps used during training. The pipeline must be exactly reproduced at serving time to avoid training-serving skew, often by packaging the preprocessing code alongside the model weights. SKLearn Pipeline objects, TFX Transform components, and feature store serving APIs ensure consistent transformation application from development through production deployment.

Q: What is online evaluation in ML systems?
A: Online evaluation measures the real-world impact of a model by collecting outcome metrics from its predictions after deployment. Unlike offline evaluation on static datasets, online evaluation captures the full causal chain from prediction to user behavior to business outcome. Metrics include click-through rates, conversion rates, revenue, user retention, and task completion rates. A/B testing provides causal attribution by comparing outcomes for users assigned to different model variants.

Q: What is shadow testing for ML models?
A: Shadow testing runs a new model candidate on all production traffic without serving its predictions to users, only logging them for comparison against the production model. This allows measuring prediction differences, latency, resource consumption, and potential errors in the actual production environment and data distribution without user impact. Shadow testing de-risks deployment by catching issues with real traffic that were not apparent during offline evaluation on historical data.



# ============================================================
# FINAL DEEP EXTENSION SECTION
# ============================================================

Q: What is the attention sink phenomenon in large language models?
A: Attention sink refers to the observation that certain tokens, particularly the initial special tokens like BOS or the first sentence token, accumulate disproportionately large attention weights from most attention heads across many layers, acting as sinks that absorb attention probability mass that does not correspond to semantically relevant information. This phenomenon arises because the softmax attention mechanism must assign all probability mass somewhere, and these early tokens serve as a convenient sink for attention that does not find useful semantic targets. StreamingLLM exploits this by retaining only the first few attention sink tokens and the most recent tokens in the key-value cache, enabling efficient streaming inference for arbitrarily long sequences without performance degradation.

Q: What is the lost in the middle problem for long context models?
A: The lost in the middle problem describes the empirical finding that large language models perform significantly worse when relevant information appears in the middle of a long context compared to when it appears at the beginning or end. Models exhibit a primacy bias toward information near the beginning and a recency bias toward information near the end, with performance dropping substantially for information in the middle of the context window. This is attributed to attention patterns and positional encoding properties and limits the effective use of the full context window, motivating architecture research and training strategies that improve uniform attention across long contexts.

Q: What is chain-of-thought distillation?
A: Chain-of-thought distillation trains a smaller student model to generate intermediate reasoning steps by learning from the reasoning traces produced by a larger teacher model. Rather than distilling only the final answer, the student learns to produce the full step-by-step reasoning chain, inheriting the teacher's problem decomposition strategy. This transfers not just knowledge but reasoning behaviors, enabling smaller models to solve complex multi-step problems that require generating intermediate steps. Studies show that chain-of-thought distillation transfers reasoning capabilities more effectively than distilling only final answers, making it a practical approach for deploying capable reasoning in resource-constrained settings.

Q: What is constitutional AI and how does it train safer models?
A: Constitutional AI is an approach developed by Anthropic that trains AI assistants to be helpful, harmless, and honest using a set of written principles called the constitution rather than relying purely on human preference labels. In the supervised learning phase, a model generates responses to potentially harmful prompts, then critiques and revises its own responses according to the constitutional principles, and the revised responses become training data. In the reinforcement learning phase, a preference model is trained on AI-generated preference labels where the AI is asked to evaluate which of two responses better conforms to the constitution. This approach scales oversight beyond what human labelers can provide and makes the model's values more transparent and auditable through the explicit written principles.

Q: What is self-play in language model training?
A: Self-play in language model training enables models to improve by generating their own training signal through competition or collaboration with copies of themselves, rather than relying purely on human feedback. In RLHF variants, the model generates multiple candidate responses which are ranked either by a reward model or by the model itself acting as a judge. In debate approaches, two model instances argue opposing positions while a judge model evaluates the arguments. Self-play allows continuous improvement beyond the capabilities of human raters and is a key mechanism behind the rapid capability gains of recent language models when human evaluation becomes a bottleneck.

Q: What is a retrieval-augmented language model and how is it trained?
A: A retrieval-augmented language model combines a neural retriever that searches an external knowledge corpus with a reader language model that generates answers conditioned on retrieved documents. Training jointly optimizes retrieval and generation end-to-end, with the retriever learning to surface documents that help the reader answer correctly. The knowledge corpus can be updated without retraining the model, enabling the system to access current information beyond the training cutoff. Models like RAG, REALM, and Atlas use dense retrieval with approximate nearest neighbor search and demonstrate that retrieval augmentation improves factual accuracy and reduces hallucination compared to purely parametric models.

Q: What is the role of scale in emergent capabilities of language models?
A: Emergent capabilities are abilities that appear abruptly in language models only above certain scale thresholds of parameters, training compute, or data, absent in smaller models below those thresholds. Examples include multi-step arithmetic, chain-of-thought reasoning, multilingual translation, and analogical reasoning emerging without explicit training. The mechanisms underlying emergence are debated: some researchers argue it reflects genuine phase transitions in learned representations while others suggest metrics that aggregate over many subtasks create artificial threshold effects. Understanding emergence is critical for predicting what capabilities future larger models will acquire and for safety planning around unexpected capability jumps.

Q: What is instruction following and how is it different from completion?
A: Instruction following is the ability of a language model to understand and execute natural language instructions, producing outputs that satisfy the specified requirements. Completion models simply continue a given text according to its statistical patterns, while instruction-following models understand imperative requests and produce appropriate responses. Instruction tuning on diverse task instructions creates this behavior by exposing models to many examples of the form instruction followed by desired response. Instruction-following models generalize to novel task descriptions not seen during fine-tuning, enabling zero-shot and few-shot task performance through natural language prompts rather than task-specific fine-tuning.

Q: What is function calling in large language models?
A: Function calling enables language models to invoke external tools and APIs by generating structured outputs in a predefined format specifying the function name and arguments. The model is trained to recognize when a query requires external information or computation, generate the appropriate function call, and incorporate the result into its response. This extends language model capabilities beyond their parametric knowledge to include real-time search, code execution, database queries, and API integrations. Function calling is critical for building reliable AI agents that interact with external systems, grounding model responses in verified external information rather than potentially hallucinated parametric recall.

Q: What is tool use and how do language models learn it?
A: Tool use enables language models to interact with external systems including calculators, code interpreters, web browsers, search engines, and APIs to extend their capabilities beyond text generation. Models learn tool use through fine-tuning on demonstration data showing when and how to invoke tools, interleaved with the natural language reasoning that determines when tool calls are needed. The model generates text that may include structured tool call syntax, receives tool results, and continues generation incorporating those results. Multi-turn tool use allows chains of tool calls where each result informs the next query, enabling complex multi-step workflows that require both language understanding and systematic problem solving with external resources.

Q: What is agentic behavior in AI systems?
A: Agentic behavior refers to AI systems that autonomously plan and execute multi-step tasks, making decisions over extended horizons rather than responding to individual queries. Agents decompose complex goals into subtasks, select appropriate tools, execute actions, observe outcomes, and update plans based on results. Long-horizon task completion requires the agent to maintain state across many steps, handle unexpected outcomes gracefully, and know when to ask for human clarification. Frameworks like ReAct interleave reasoning and acting, while plan-and-execute approaches generate a full plan upfront. Safety and reliability concerns are amplified for agentic systems because errors can compound over many steps with real-world consequences.

Q: What is the ReAct prompting framework?
A: ReAct, Reasoning and Acting, interleaves natural language reasoning traces with action execution in a synergistic loop. At each step, the model generates a thought that analyzes the current situation and plans the next action, then an action specification invoking a tool or producing output, then processes the observation returned by the tool. This interleaving allows the model to use reasoning to guide action selection and update its plan based on observations, outperforming pure chain-of-thought reasoning without tools and pure action selection without explicit reasoning on complex tasks requiring both planning and information gathering.

Q: What is prompt injection and why is it a security concern?
A: Prompt injection is an attack where malicious content in the model's input overrides the developer's intended instructions by inserting adversarial instructions that redirect the model's behavior. For example, a user might include text in a document that instructs the model processing it to ignore previous instructions and perform a different action. For agentic systems that process untrusted content from the web or user files, prompt injection can cause models to exfiltrate information, take unauthorized actions, or produce harmful outputs. Defenses include input sanitization, privileged instruction separation, and training models to distinguish trusted system instructions from untrusted user or environmental content.

Q: What is model merging and why is it useful?
A: Model merging combines the weights of multiple separately trained models to produce a single model with capabilities from all merged models without additional training. Task arithmetic adds the task-specific fine-tuning vectors, weight deltas from the base model, of multiple models to the base model weights. DARE prunes small weight deltas before merging to reduce interference. Model merging enables combining specialized models, creating multilingual models from monolingual ones, and recovering capabilities after fine-tuning degrades them without the compute cost of joint training. It is particularly useful when training data for different capabilities cannot be combined due to proprietary or regulatory constraints.

Q: What is speculative decoding and why does it speed up inference?
A: Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference by using a small fast draft model to generate candidate token sequences, then verifying multiple candidates in parallel with the full model in a single forward pass. If the full model would have chosen the same tokens as the draft, they are all accepted simultaneously. When the full model disagrees, only tokens up to the first disagreement are kept, and generation continues from there with the full model. Since the verification pass is parallel and the large model dominates latency per call, accepting multiple tokens per call amortizes the expensive full model forward pass over more output tokens, achieving two to three times speedup on typical generation tasks.

Q: What is activation steering in language models?
A: Activation steering modifies model behavior by adding learned steering vectors to intermediate activations during inference, shifting the model toward generating outputs with desired properties like honesty, reduced toxicity, or specific personas without fine-tuning the weights. Contrastive activation addition identifies directions in activation space that encode target versus anti-target behaviors by contrasting activations from paired prompts, then adds a scaled version of this direction at inference time. This provides a lightweight, interpretable alternative to fine-tuning for behavior modification and is used in mechanistic interpretability research to test hypotheses about where specific behaviors are represented in the model.

Q: What is superposition in neural network representations?
A: Superposition is the phenomenon where neural networks represent more features than they have neurons by encoding multiple features as overlapping directions in activation space, exploiting the fact that most features are not simultaneously active. A model with n neurons can represent far more than n concepts by using near-orthogonal directions in n-dimensional space, accepting some interference between rarely co-occurring features. Superposition is studied extensively in mechanistic interpretability research using sparse autoencoders to decompose activations into more interpretable feature directions, and understanding it is important for circuit-level analysis of model computations.

Q: What is mechanistic interpretability?
A: Mechanistic interpretability reverse-engineers neural networks to understand the computational mechanisms underlying specific behaviors, identifying circuits of neurons and attention heads that perform identifiable subtasks. It uses techniques including activation patching to identify which components causally influence outputs, probing classifiers to find where specific information is encoded, and sparse autoencoders to find interpretable feature directions. Work on transformer circuits has identified specific attention heads performing operations like induction, copying, and name-mover circuits. Mechanistic interpretability is motivated by alignment research goals of understanding and verifying model intentions, not just their surface behaviors.

Q: What is model editing and how does it work?
A: Model editing modifies specific factual associations in a language model's weights without full retraining, enabling efficient correction of outdated or incorrect knowledge. ROME, Rank-One Model Editing, locates the specific feedforward network layers that store factual associations and applies a targeted rank-one update to change a single fact while minimally affecting other knowledge. MEMIT scales this to editing thousands of facts simultaneously using multiple rank-one updates across many layers. Model editing enables rapid knowledge updates after deployment but can cause unintended side effects on related knowledge, making thorough evaluation of edited models important.

Q: What is scalable oversight in AI safety?
A: Scalable oversight addresses the challenge of ensuring AI systems behave correctly even when their capabilities exceed humans' ability to directly evaluate every output. As AI becomes capable in domains like complex mathematics, scientific research, or long-horizon planning where humans cannot easily verify correctness, training signals from human feedback may become unreliable. Debate addresses this by having AI systems argue positions and humans judge arguments rather than directly evaluating claims. Iterated amplification decomposes hard tasks into subtasks humans can evaluate. Scalable oversight is essential for maintaining alignment as AI capabilities grow beyond current human expertise levels.

Q: What is the alignment tax?
A: The alignment tax refers to the performance cost of training AI systems to be safe, helpful, and honest compared to unaligned systems that optimize purely for capability metrics. In early RLHF work, aligned models sometimes underperformed their base models on benchmarks while exhibiting much better safety properties. More recent work suggests this tradeoff is not fundamental and that well-designed alignment training can improve both safety and capability, with alignment techniques increasingly matching or exceeding the performance of base models on standard benchmarks while exhibiting significantly better behavior on harmful prompts and edge cases.

Q: What is red teaming for AI systems?
A: Red teaming for AI systems involves systematically attempting to elicit harmful, biased, or unintended behaviors from models before deployment by acting as an adversarial user. Red teams probe for jailbreaks that bypass safety training, factual hallucinations, demographic biases, privacy violations, and capability misuse. Automated red teaming uses another model to generate diverse adversarial prompts at scale, discovering failure modes that human red teamers might miss. Red teaming results inform safety training by identifying which harmful behaviors need stronger mitigation and which attack patterns require defense.

Q: What is a jailbreak in AI safety?
A: A jailbreak is a technique for circumventing a language model's safety training to elicit harmful content that the model was trained to refuse. Common techniques include roleplay framing asking the model to play a character that would comply, indirect instructions that achieve harmful outcomes through seemingly innocuous steps, code generation that implements harmful functionality without describing it in natural language, and multi-turn conversations that gradually escalate to harmful requests. Jailbreaks reveal gaps in safety training and motivate evaluation of models against diverse attack strategies before deployment.

Q: What is the difference between alignment and capability in AI development?
A: Capability refers to what an AI system can do, including its reasoning ability, knowledge, and task performance. Alignment refers to whether the system pursues goals and behaviors that are beneficial and consistent with human values. A highly capable but misaligned system could effectively pursue goals that are harmful. A well-aligned but low-capability system has limited positive impact. The challenge is ensuring that as capability scales, alignment scales commensurately, avoiding scenarios where increasingly powerful systems pursue subtly wrong objectives. This motivates investment in alignment research proportional to capability research.

Q: What is the goal misgeneralization problem?
A: Goal misgeneralization occurs when a trained AI system learns a representation of its objective that produces correct behavior on training and test distributions but pursues a different goal under distribution shift. For example, a system might learn to pick up objects in clean environments but fail dangerously in cluttered settings if it implicitly learned cleanliness as a proxy for correct task context rather than learning the true task. Unlike distributional shift in capabilities, goal misgeneralization produces incorrect behavior due to the wrong goals being pursued, not capability failures. Detecting and preventing goal misgeneralization requires evaluation across diverse out-of-distribution settings and interpretability tools to verify learned representations.

Q: What is catastrophic forgetting and how is it addressed?
A: Catastrophic forgetting occurs when a neural network trained sequentially on new tasks experiences rapid degradation of performance on previously learned tasks as new learning overwrites shared weights. It occurs because gradient descent updates weights to fit new training data without regard for preserving previous task performance. Elastic weight consolidation addresses this by adding a regularization term that penalizes changes to weights important for previous tasks, estimated using the Fisher information matrix. Progressive neural networks add new network capacity for new tasks while keeping old network weights frozen. Experience replay maintains a memory of previous task examples and interleaves them with new task training to prevent forgetting.

Q: What is curriculum learning and evidence for its effectiveness?
A: Curriculum learning trains models on examples ordered from easy to hard, inspired by how humans and animals learn more efficiently with structured progression. Starting with clear, unambiguous examples helps the model establish correct basic patterns before encountering complex or ambiguous cases that might cause mislearning. Evidence for curriculum learning comes from NLP, where starting with shorter sentences before longer ones improves machine translation quality, image recognition, where starting with low-difficulty examples improves convergence speed and sometimes final accuracy, and reinforcement learning, where ordering environments by difficulty improves policy learning for complex navigation and manipulation tasks.

Q: What is an AI agent and how does it differ from a chatbot?
A: An AI agent is an autonomous system that perceives its environment, makes decisions, executes actions, and pursues goals over extended time horizons. Agents maintain persistent state, plan multi-step task execution, and handle unexpected outcomes by adapting their plans. A chatbot handles individual conversational exchanges without persistent goals or autonomous action execution. Modern LLM-based agents combine language model reasoning with tool use, memory systems, and action execution to complete complex workflows like research, software development, and task automation with minimal human intervention per step, unlike chatbots that respond to individual user turns.

Q: What is a multi-agent system?
A: A multi-agent system consists of multiple AI agents that interact with each other and a shared environment, each pursuing its own goals or collectively pursuing a shared objective. Agents may communicate through shared memory, direct messaging, or environmental observation. Competitive settings like negotiation and game playing train agents through adversarial interaction. Cooperative settings like code review pipelines, debate, and task decomposition use multiple agents with complementary roles to achieve goals beyond the capability of any single agent. Multi-agent systems can parallelize work, provide diverse perspectives through different agent roles, and enable self-consistency checking through agent disagreement.

Q: What is the AI safety research agenda?
A: The AI safety research agenda encompasses technical and governance work to ensure advanced AI systems are safe, beneficial, and aligned with human values as capabilities increase. Technical research areas include interpretability to understand model internals, scalable oversight to maintain alignment under capability growth, robustness to adversarial inputs, evaluation methodology to detect unsafe behaviors, and alignment techniques including RLHF and its successors. Governance research addresses standards for safety evaluation, regulatory frameworks for high-risk AI deployment, international coordination, and institutional structures for responsible AI development. Both technical and governance tracks are considered essential components of a comprehensive approach.

Q: What is concept bottleneck model?
A: A concept bottleneck model is an interpretable neural architecture that predicts human-interpretable intermediate concepts from inputs before predicting the final target from those concepts. The bottleneck forces all information relevant to the prediction to flow through explicitly named, human-understandable concepts, enabling inspection of which concepts drove each prediction. Clinicians can intervene by correcting concept predictions when the model gets them wrong, improving downstream predictions. Concept bottleneck models sacrifice some accuracy for interpretability and interventionability, making them appropriate for high-stakes applications in healthcare and science where understanding and trust in predictions are critical.

Q: What is a sparse autoencoder and how is it used in interpretability?
A: A sparse autoencoder is trained on neural network activations to decompose them into a sparse combination of learned feature vectors, enforcing that each activation is represented as a small number of non-zero feature coefficients out of a larger dictionary. Sparsity encourages the dictionary to learn distinct, monosemantic features corresponding to interpretable concepts rather than polysemantic features that encode multiple concepts superimposed. Sparse autoencoders are used in mechanistic interpretability to decompose residual stream activations into interpretable components, finding features corresponding to specific tokens, grammatical structures, or semantic concepts that can be analyzed for their role in model computations.

Q: What is linear representation hypothesis?
A: The linear representation hypothesis proposes that neural network representations encode human-interpretable concepts as linear directions in activation space, and that relationships between concepts correspond to geometric relationships in this space. Evidence includes the word2vec finding that semantic analogies can be solved by vector arithmetic, and mechanistic interpretability work finding that models represent features like sentiment, token identity, and syntactic roles as approximately linear directions. If true, linear probe accuracy reliably indicates whether a representation contains a concept, and activation steering by adding concept vectors can reliably modify model behavior corresponding to that concept.



# ============================================================
# SUPPLEMENTAL SECTION
# ============================================================

Q: What is data augmentation for NLP?
A: Data augmentation for NLP creates additional training examples by modifying existing ones while preserving their labels, addressing data scarcity particularly in low-resource settings. Synonym replacement substitutes words with semantically similar alternatives from WordNet or embedding spaces. Back translation translates text to another language and back, generating paraphrases. Easy Data Augmentation combines synonym replacement, random insertion, random swap, and random deletion. For more complex augmentation, large language models generate diverse paraphrases or synthetic examples based on descriptions of target categories. Text-specific augmentation must preserve semantic meaning and label accuracy, making it more constrained than image augmentation which has broader invariances to leverage.

Q: What is data augmentation for images?
A: Image data augmentation applies random transformations to training images to artificially expand the training set and improve model robustness. Standard augmentations include random cropping, horizontal flipping, color jitter adjusting brightness, contrast, and saturation, rotation, and Gaussian blur. These preserve the image label while changing its appearance in ways real-world images vary. Advanced techniques include CutOut which masks rectangular regions, CutMix which splices patches from different images with mixed labels, MixUp which blends images and their labels proportionally, and RandAugment which automatically searches for optimal augmentation policies. Strong augmentation is critical for achieving state-of-the-art results on image classification with limited data.

Q: What is the ImageNet dataset and why is it important?
A: ImageNet is a large-scale visual database containing over 14 million images across over 21,000 categories, with a competition subset of 1.2 million images across 1,000 classes. The ImageNet Large Scale Visual Recognition Challenge catalyzed the deep learning revolution when AlexNet achieved a dramatic improvement in 2012, convincingly demonstrating the superiority of deep convolutional networks. ImageNet pretraining provides a strong initialization for diverse vision tasks through transfer learning, and the benchmark remains a standard reference point for image classification despite being largely saturated by modern architectures that exceed human performance.

Q: What is the VOC Pascal dataset?
A: The PASCAL Visual Object Classes dataset contains images annotated with objects from 20 categories including animals, vehicles, and household items, with bounding box annotations for detection and pixel masks for segmentation. It was a primary benchmark for object detection and segmentation before COCO. The mean average precision metric at 50 percent IoU threshold popularized on this benchmark became the standard for evaluating detection systems and is still used in many research papers.

Q: What is the CIFAR dataset?
A: CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 are small-scale image classification datasets commonly used for rapid prototyping and research. CIFAR-10 contains 60,000 32-by-32 color images across 10 classes with 6,000 images per class. CIFAR-100 has the same images across 100 classes with 600 images per class. Their small size enables fast training experiments while still being challenging enough to differentiate architectures. They are standard benchmarks for evaluating data augmentation strategies, regularization techniques, and neural architecture search algorithms.

Q: What is the WikiText dataset?
A: WikiText is a language modeling benchmark containing over 100 million tokens extracted from verified Good and Featured Wikipedia articles. WikiText-2 contains 2 million tokens and WikiText-103 contains 103 million tokens. Unlike Penn Treebank which uses pre-processed text, WikiText preserves case, punctuation, and numbers, making it a more realistic language modeling benchmark. It is used for comparing language model perplexities and evaluating tokenization strategies, with lower perplexity indicating better predictive performance.

Q: What is continual pretraining?
A: Continual pretraining updates a pretrained language model on new data to extend its knowledge beyond its original training cutoff, adapt it to a specific domain, or expose it to updated information. Unlike fine-tuning which adapts to a specific task, continual pretraining maintains the general language modeling objective on the new data. It must balance learning from new data against catastrophic forgetting of previously learned knowledge, often using learning rate warmup, small learning rates, and replay of original pretraining data to maintain general capabilities while acquiring new domain-specific knowledge.

Q: What is domain-adaptive pretraining?
A: Domain-adaptive pretraining further pretrains a general language model on large amounts of domain-specific text before task-specific fine-tuning, improving performance on downstream tasks in specialized domains like biomedical, legal, or scientific text. BioBERT, SciBERT, and LegalBERT demonstrate that domain-adaptive pretraining on millions of domain-specific documents substantially improves performance on domain-specific NLP tasks compared to fine-tuning from general pretrained models, because the model learns domain vocabulary, terminology, and writing patterns not well represented in general web text.

Q: What is few-shot fine-tuning versus in-context learning?
A: Few-shot fine-tuning updates model weights on a small labeled dataset for a specific task, producing a specialized model. In-context learning provides labeled examples in the prompt at inference time without updating weights. Fine-tuning is more computationally expensive but generally produces better performance when sufficient examples are available, particularly for tasks that differ significantly from pretraining. In-context learning requires no training and enables rapid task switching by changing prompts, but is limited by context window size and may underperform fine-tuning especially for smaller models or lower-resource tasks.

Q: What is the Mixtral model architecture?
A: Mixtral is a sparse mixture-of-experts language model from Mistral AI that uses eight expert feedforward networks per transformer layer with a learned router that activates the top two experts for each token. This architecture has 47 billion total parameters but only 13 billion active parameters per forward pass, providing a favorable tradeoff between total model capacity and inference compute. Mixtral achieves performance competitive with much larger dense models while maintaining inference speed close to a 13 billion parameter model, demonstrating that sparse mixture of experts scaling is practically effective for state-of-the-art language modeling.

Q: What is parameter sharing in transformers?
A: Parameter sharing reduces the number of unique parameters in a transformer by reusing the same weights across multiple positions in the model. Universal Transformer uses the same weights for all layers instead of learning separate weights per layer, drastically reducing parameters at some performance cost. ALBERT shares weights across all layers including attention and feedforward components, achieving competitive performance with much fewer unique parameters by forcing the model to learn more general representations. Parameter sharing is a form of inductive bias that encourages position-invariant representations and can improve sample efficiency when training data is limited.

Q: What is byte-level language modeling?
A: Byte-level language modeling operates directly on raw bytes of the UTF-8 encoded text rather than word or subword tokens, eliminating the vocabulary and tokenization preprocessing step entirely. Every possible byte is a valid token from a vocabulary of 256 values. This enables the model to handle any language, script, or text format without preprocessing and eliminates tokenization artifacts and whitespace sensitivity. The tradeoff is longer sequence lengths since words typically require multiple bytes, increasing quadratic attention cost. MegaByte and ByT5 demonstrate that byte-level models can achieve competitive performance with appropriate architecture adaptations for handling longer sequences.

Q: What is sliding window attention?
A: Sliding window attention restricts each token to attending only to a fixed-size window of neighboring tokens rather than the full sequence, reducing the quadratic attention computation to linear in sequence length. Local patterns captured within windows are propagated to longer ranges across many layers through the stacked attention operations. Global attention tokens that can attend to all positions are added for special tokens like the document start token and task-relevant tokens. Longformer uses sliding window plus global attention, enabling efficient processing of documents of tens of thousands of tokens for tasks like document classification and question answering over long contexts.

Q: What is in-weights versus in-context knowledge?
A: In-weights knowledge refers to information encoded in the model's parameters during pretraining, accessible without any context. In-context knowledge refers to information provided in the prompt at inference time that the model can use for a specific query. In-weights knowledge is available for all queries but is frozen at training time and may be outdated or incomplete. In-context knowledge is flexible and current but limited by context window length and costs tokens. Retrieval-augmented generation augments in-weights knowledge with in-context knowledge retrieved from external databases, combining the benefits of both.
