Understanding this distinction guides decisions about when to fine-tune versus when to augment with retrieval.

Q: What is model weight averaging in the context of fine-tuning?
A: Weight interpolation between the pretrained base model and the fine-tuned model, called model merging or weight averaging, often produces a model that is more robust than the fine-tuned model alone. The base model captures broad general capabilities while the fine-tuned model specializes for the target task. Linear interpolation between them balances task performance against capability retention. This technique is related to WiSE-FT, which averages zero-shot and fine-tuned model weights to improve distribution shift robustness. Weight space geometry research finds that fine-tuned models lie in flat basins near the pretrained model, making linear interpolation between them semantically meaningful.

Q: What is tokenization-free language modeling?
A: Tokenization-free language modeling processes raw characters or bytes without the discrete vocabulary mapping step of tokenization, eliminating vocabulary design decisions, out-of-vocabulary handling, and the tokenization-sensitive behavior of models where semantically identical text with different whitespace produces different token sequences. Charformer learns soft subword representations in a differentiable manner. Canine processes characters without any tokenization using local self-attention for efficiency. These approaches improve robustness to spelling variations, morphologically rich languages, and code-switching, though they face challenges with sequence length and matching the efficiency of subword models on standard benchmarks.

Q: What is a recurrent state space model?
A: Recurrent state space models like Mamba and S4 are neural architectures that use structured state space equations to model sequences, capable of parallel training like transformers through convolution but sequential inference like RNNs without the quadratic memory cost of attention. They parameterize the state transition as a convolution over the sequence using selective state updates that allow the model to focus on relevant input tokens, achieving linear time and memory complexity. These models show competitive performance with transformers on many sequence modeling tasks while being substantially more efficient for very long sequences, positioning them as potential alternatives or complements to attention for long-context modeling.

Q: What is reward hacking in reinforcement learning from human feedback?
A: Reward hacking occurs when a reinforcement learning agent learns to maximize the reward signal through behaviors that score highly on the reward model but do not achieve the intended objective. In RLHF for language models, the model may learn to produce responses that appeal to human rating biases, such as verbose confident-sounding answers or sycophantic agreement, rather than genuinely helpful, accurate responses. The reward model trained on finite human preferences is an imperfect proxy for the true objective, and optimizing it too aggressively degrades alignment. KL divergence penalties constraining deviation from the reference policy mitigate reward hacking by preventing the model from exploiting reward model weaknesses too aggressively.

Q: What is constitutional training and self-critique?
A: Constitutional training enables a language model to improve its own outputs by critiquing them according to a set of principles and then revising them to better satisfy the critique. The model generates an initial response, then generates a critique identifying how the response violates the constitution, then generates a revision addressing the critique. Iterating this process produces progressively better responses that conform to the constitutional principles. This self-improvement process generates high-quality training data for supervised fine-tuning and reduces reliance on human labelers for identifying problematic outputs, making it scalable beyond what direct human feedback can achieve.

Q: What is semantic search and how does it differ from keyword search?
A: Semantic search retrieves documents based on conceptual meaning and intent rather than exact keyword matches, using dense vector embeddings that capture semantic similarity. A query is embedded into the same space as document embeddings, and nearest neighbor search returns documents with similar semantic content. Unlike keyword search which requires exact word matches and struggles with synonyms and paraphrases, semantic search understands that similar concepts expressed with different words should retrieve similar results. Dense retrieval models like DPR and BGE are trained on question-answer pairs to produce embeddings where queries and their relevant documents are close in the embedding space.

Q: What is reranking in information retrieval?
A: Reranking is a two-stage retrieval approach where a fast but less accurate retrieval model generates a set of candidate documents, and a slower but more accurate cross-encoder model reranks the candidates. The cross-encoder computes a relevance score by processing the query and each candidate document jointly through a transformer, capturing fine-grained interactions that bi-encoder models miss. This two-stage approach balances the efficiency needed to search millions of documents with the accuracy of expensive cross-attention models applied to a small candidate set, achieving better ranking quality than either approach alone.



# ============================================================
# CLOSING SECTION
# ============================================================

Q: What is the difference between open-source and closed-source language models?
A: Open-source language models release model weights, architecture specifications, and sometimes training code publicly, allowing anyone to download, run, modify, and fine-tune the model. Examples include Llama, Mistral, Falcon, and Phi. Closed-source models like GPT-4 and Claude are only accessible through APIs, with weights and architecture details kept proprietary. Open models enable on-premises deployment for data privacy, unlimited fine-tuning without vendor restriction, inspection of model internals for research, and community modification. Closed models offer potentially higher capability, commercial support, and continuous updates without user management overhead. The distinction affects cost structures, privacy, customization flexibility, and the ability to audit model behavior.

Q: What is parameter count and why does it not fully predict performance?
A: Parameter count is the total number of learnable weights in a model, often used as a proxy for model capacity and capability. Larger parameter counts allow representing more complex functions and storing more world knowledge. However, parameter count alone does not fully predict performance because training data quality and quantity, training compute budget, model architecture efficiency, and the specific task domain all significantly affect outcomes. A smaller model trained on more carefully curated data with a better architecture can outperform a larger model trained on noisier data. Chinchilla demonstrated that many large models were undertrained, and subsequent models achieve better performance with smaller but optimally trained architectures.

Q: What is constitutional drift in deployed AI systems?
A: Constitutional drift refers to gradual changes in an AI system's behavior over time as it is updated, fine-tuned, or as its deployment context evolves, potentially causing deviation from intended values and constraints. Regular behavioral evaluations comparing current model outputs against documented intended behavior detect drift before it causes harm. Systematic testing of refusals, factual accuracy, helpfulness across domains, and behavior on adversarial prompts at each model update provides a behavioral changelog. Drift can occur from continued training on user interaction data, optimization pressure from engagement metrics, or unintended side effects of capability improvements that alter the balance of behaviors.

Q: What is retrieval augmented fine-tuning?
A: Retrieval augmented fine-tuning combines the benefits of fine-tuning with retrieval augmentation by training a model to effectively use retrieved context while also updating its weights for the target domain. Unlike standard fine-tuning which encodes all knowledge in weights, or standard RAG which relies purely on retrieval, this approach trains the model to reason over retrieved documents in ways specialized for the target use case. The model learns when to trust retrieved information versus its parametric knowledge, how to synthesize information from multiple retrieved passages, and how to generate responses in the target domain style and format.

Q: What is activation function design and how does it affect deep networks?
A: Activation function design determines how neural networks introduce nonlinearity, fundamentally affecting gradient flow, representational expressiveness, and computational efficiency. Saturating functions like sigmoid and tanh cause vanishing gradients at extreme inputs, limiting training depth. Non-saturating functions like ReLU solve vanishing gradients for positive inputs but can produce dead neurons. Smooth non-saturating functions like GELU and SiLU provide better gradient properties and often outperform ReLU on transformer models. Activation function research investigates properties including boundedness, monotonicity, smoothness, and mean-centering that affect training stability, convergence speed, and final model quality across different architectures and tasks.

Q: What is the role of normalization in stabilizing large model training?
A: Normalization layers are essential for stable training of large models by controlling the scale and distribution of activations throughout the network. Without normalization, activations can grow or shrink exponentially through deep networks, causing vanishing or exploding gradients that prevent effective optimization. Batch normalization was transformative for CNNs, while layer normalization became standard for transformers due to its independence from batch size. Root mean square normalization used in Llama simplifies layer normalization by removing the mean centering step while maintaining training stability, with slightly lower computational cost. Careful placement of normalization, whether pre-norm or post-norm, significantly affects training stability and the ability to use large learning rates.


Q: What is instruction hierarchy in language model safety?
A: Instruction hierarchy assigns different levels of trust and authority to different sources of instructions that a language model receives. System prompts from developers are given higher authority than user messages, which are given higher authority than content in documents or web pages processed by the model. This prevents prompt injection attacks where malicious content in processed documents attempts to override developer or user instructions. The model is trained to recognize the source of instructions and apply appropriate levels of compliance, following legitimate developer and user requests while resisting attempts by untrusted content to redirect its behavior in unauthorized ways.

Q: What is a transformer block and what computations does it perform?
A: A transformer block is the fundamental repeating unit of the transformer architecture, consisting of a multi-head self-attention sub-layer followed by a position-wise feed-forward sub-layer, each wrapped with a residual connection and layer normalization. The attention sub-layer computes contextual token representations by aggregating information from all other positions weighted by learned compatibility scores. The feed-forward sub-layer applies a two-layer MLP independently to each position, expanding to a larger intermediate dimension before projecting back. The residual connections allow gradient flow and allow the network to learn incremental updates to representations. Stacking many identical blocks enables the model to build increasingly abstract representations through repeated application of these operations.

Q: What is a mixture of experts routing strategy?
A: Mixture of experts routing determines which experts process each input token using a learned gating network that computes softmax scores over all available experts and selects the top-k experts for each token. Load balancing auxiliary losses encourage uniform token distribution across experts, preventing collapse where all tokens route to a small subset. Token choice routing selects the top-k experts per token while expert choice routing has each expert select its top-k preferred tokens, improving load balance but requiring global coordination. Routing decisions are crucial for MoE model quality since poor routing fails to specialize experts and wastes capacity, while expert routing patterns can reveal what semantic or syntactic features different experts have specialized in.

Q: What is the sliding window attention used in Mistral?
A: Mistral uses grouped query attention with a sliding window attention pattern where each token can only attend to the W most recent tokens rather than all past tokens, where W is the window size. This reduces the key-value cache size from O(sequence length) to O(W) and allows efficient inference on very long sequences. Information from beyond the window propagates through multiple attention layers, so after k layers, tokens have an effective receptive field of k times W tokens. Mistral uses W equal to 4096 across all layers, enabling efficient long-document processing while maintaining strong performance through the hierarchical propagation of information across the attention stack.

Q: What is the difference between fine-tuning and pretraining compute costs?
A: Pretraining a large language model requires training on trillions of tokens, consuming hundreds of thousands of GPU-hours and costing millions to hundreds of millions of dollars for frontier models. Fine-tuning a pretrained model requires training on thousands to millions of examples for hundreds to thousands of steps, consuming a fraction of the pretraining compute at a cost that ranges from tens of dollars for small models to thousands for large ones. This asymmetry explains why pretraining is performed once by well-resourced organizations while fine-tuning is accessible to a much wider community. Parameter efficient fine-tuning methods like LoRA further reduce fine-tuning costs by updating only a small fraction of parameters, making task adaptation feasible on single consumer GPUs.

Q: What is an embedding model and how is it different from a language model?
A: An embedding model encodes text into fixed-size dense vector representations designed for downstream tasks like similarity search and retrieval, rather than generating text. Embedding models are typically trained with contrastive objectives on pairs of semantically similar and dissimilar texts, learning representations where similar texts cluster together in the vector space. Language models predict token probabilities and can generate text but their hidden states are not directly optimized for retrieval quality. Dedicated embedding models like BGE, E5, and text-embedding-ada-002 significantly outperform language model hidden states as features for semantic search because their training objective directly optimizes the properties needed for retrieval: semantic similarity preservation and discriminative boundary placement between unrelated texts.

Q: What is the BM25 retrieval algorithm?
A: BM25, Best Match 25, is a probabilistic keyword-based retrieval algorithm that ranks documents by their relevance to a query based on term frequency, inverse document frequency, and document length normalization. It assigns higher scores to documents containing query terms that appear frequently in the document but rarely across the corpus, and applies a saturation function that diminishes returns from additional occurrences of the same term. BM25 is fast, requires no precomputed embeddings, and works well for exact keyword matching. It is commonly combined with dense vector retrieval in hybrid search systems that capture both lexical and semantic relevance, outperforming either approach alone.

Q: What is token budget management in long context language models?
A: Token budget management is the practice of efficiently allocating the available context window across different types of information to maximize model performance. Prompt compression techniques remove redundant or low-information content from the context, retaining essential information in fewer tokens. Selective retrieval fetches only the most relevant chunks from a long document rather than including the full text. Summary buffers compress long conversation histories into compact summaries while preserving key information. Hierarchical context management uses different summary granularities for different temporal distances, keeping recent context verbatim while compressing older interactions. Effective token budget management enables handling tasks that would otherwise exceed context limits while maintaining the information needed for accurate responses.

Q: What is constitutional AI self-improvement?
A: Constitutional AI self-improvement uses the model itself as a judge of its own outputs according to specified principles, generating better training data than human labelers can provide at scale. In each round, the model generates candidate responses, produces critiques identifying violations of the constitution, generates improved revisions, and the accepted revisions become training data for the next round. This creates a self-improving loop where each generation of the model produces higher quality self-critique and revision, progressively reducing violations without requiring proportional increases in human annotation. The approach is particularly valuable for identifying subtle forms of helpfulness failures, mild harm, and reasoning errors that are time-consuming for human annotators to identify at scale but that the model itself can reliably detect.

Q: What is multi-query retrieval in RAG systems?
A: Multi-query retrieval improves retrieval-augmented generation by generating multiple diverse query reformulations from the original user query and retrieving documents for each, then aggregating the results. A single query may miss relevant documents due to vocabulary mismatch or incomplete specification of the information need. By generating queries that approach the topic from different angles, cover related subtopics, or use alternative terminology, multi-query retrieval achieves higher recall of relevant documents. The retrieved sets are combined with deduplication, and the aggregated context provides a richer, more comprehensive information base for the language model to generate accurate, complete responses.

Q: What is graph-of-thought prompting?
A: Graph-of-thought prompting extends chain-of-thought by organizing reasoning as a directed acyclic graph rather than a linear chain, allowing multiple parallel reasoning branches to be explored simultaneously and combined at decision nodes. Different branches can pursue alternative hypotheses, explore multiple solution strategies, or decompose the problem into independent subproblems solved in parallel. The graph structure enables backtracking, aggregation of evidence from multiple reasoning paths, and voting among branches to select the most supported conclusion. This approach improves performance on complex problems where a single linear reasoning chain may commit too early to a suboptimal path, leveraging the diversity of multiple reasoning strategies within a single inference pass.

Q: What is sparse retrieval and how does it complement dense retrieval?
A: Sparse retrieval represents queries and documents as high-dimensional sparse vectors where non-zero dimensions correspond to important terms, enabling fast retrieval using inverted index data structures. Traditional BM25 is a sparse retrieval method, and learned sparse models like SPLADE learn to expand documents and queries with relevant terms predicted by a language model, improving recall while maintaining sparse representation efficiency. Dense retrieval encodes semantics as continuous vectors capturing meaning beyond exact terms. Hybrid retrieval combines sparse and dense scores through reciprocal rank fusion or learned weighting, consistently outperforming either method alone by capturing both lexical precision and semantic generalization.

Q: What is hallucination detection and mitigation?
A: Hallucination detection identifies when a language model generates plausible-sounding but factually incorrect or unsupported claims. Self-consistency checking generates multiple responses to the same query and flags claims that appear in few responses as likely hallucinations. Factual verification pipelines extract claims from model outputs and verify them against trusted knowledge bases or web search results. Retrieval augmentation grounds responses in retrieved source documents, enabling citation-based verification. Calibration training encourages models to express uncertainty when they lack confident knowledge. Chain-of-verification prompts the model to generate verification questions about its own claims and answer them, catching inconsistencies before presenting the final response.
